# THE MAN

---

The life and work of Leonardo have always been, by a kind of fatality, shrouded in mystery, and they largely remain so even now, despite archival research and the publication of his manuscripts.

With his few works of painting and sculpture either ruined or disappeared, we intuit his supreme art more than anything else from his sketches and the enthusiastic judgments of his contemporaries.

As for his scientific knowledge—unrecognized or poorly understood for long centuries and only now being explored for its true value through the examination of sources—it fills us with admiration, though it is painful and astonishing to note that it had, one might say, no influence on the scientific movement of the <term>Cinquecento</term> <note>The 1500s, or the High Renaissance period in Italy.</note>.

His destiny as an artist and scientist is strange; while we certainly cannot accuse him of impotence <note>In this context, a lack of creative power or ability to produce.</note>, we nevertheless feel that the primary reason why He did not

<gloss>Leonardo da Vinci, painting, sculpture, the 1500s, scientist, manuscripts, archival research</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci as a figure of enduring mystery, noting that while his physical artworks are largely lost or damaged, his scientific genius is only now being properly understood despite its lack of influence on his own century.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Renaissance science, art history, Cinquecento, manuscripts, scientific legacy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the psychological and intellectual profile of Leonardo da Vinci, moving from the external mystery of his life to the internal mystery of his character and the perceived "incompleteness" of his work.</meta>

leave behind great works of art and science lies, pro-
bably, not in external circumstances—mere games of
fate—but in the temper of his character which, of all
the Leonardesque mysteries, is certainly the greatest.

We know the artist imperfectly, the scientist even less
surely, but worst of all, the man.

His scientific notes are, naturally, im-
personal; his art is inspired by profound objective
observation rather than intimate passion; the accounts of the
earliest biographers are scarce and entirely
superficial, and his few personal recollections are incredibly dry.
By the very nature of things, an
essay that seeks to evoke the soul of Leonardo
can be nothing more than an interesting, in-
genious attempt—of a daring that almost borders on temerity—but
a sketch and nothing more.

Of this arduous sketch, we shall seek the
essential lines especially in his prose, in the certainty
that, if much is missing, at least there will be nothing
too far from the truth, and in the hope that
some good and new observation may result.

Recently, someone accused Leonardo of, shall we say,
scientific impotence, because his theories,
not translated into practice, could not impose themselves upon
his contemporaries. One might object: he lacked the
means to be able to explain and apply the truths he
intuited; but this is only partially valid, since the same
thing repeats itself exactly for the works of art that He conceives
and does not translate into action, sketches and abandons even
after long years of study, and for the treatises of which
nothing remained but fragments: for these, he certainly
did not lack the means!

And one cannot even speak of inconstancy, nor accuse him

<gloss>Leonardo da Vinci, Leonardesque mysteries, scientific impotence, treatises, inconstancy</gloss>

<summary>The author argues that the greatest mystery of Leonardo is his character, noting that his failure to complete many projects was not due to a lack of means or "inconstancy," but something deeper in his nature.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, psychology, scientific theory, artistic process, biography, Renaissance intellect</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuing the defense of Leonardo da Vinci's character against accusations of inconstancy or "scientific impotence" mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

with Sabba Castiglione <note>A 16th-century knight and writer who knew Leonardo in Milan</note> of "natural lightness and volatility of talent"; no, because every page of his manuscripts proves to us day by day, hour by hour, that his life was entirely dedicated to a single purpose of intense observation and reflection, to reach refinements of art or scientific truths not yet discovered, and he never failed in this noble aim.
Rather, one might say:

> that always the man, in whom thought springs
> upon thought, distances the goal from himself,
> because the heat of the one weakens the other.
<note>A quotation from Dante Alighieri's Purgatorio, Canto V, lines 16-18, explaining how one thought can distract from another.</note>

From observation to observation, from reflection to reflection, the horizon widened for Leonardo, who—driven by the greedy curiosity of the investigator—abandoned, without regret, the brilliant idea that had flashed before him, more eager for theory than for practice, more for new truths than for systematic and patient application.
Thus he preferred theorizing about art to painting or sculpting; thus, for an analogous psychological reason, he preferred sketches caught from life or the outlines of fantastic compositions to the diligent brushwork required to complete a portrait or a fresco.
Indecision, uncertainty, apathy? Not at all! One must be very bold to affirm this of one who knew how to conceive the *Battle of Anghiari* <note>A lost mural commission for the Palazzo Vecchio in Florence</note> and finish its cartoon <term>cartoon: a full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco</term> in a relatively very short time; one must be confusing the impotence that lingers to over-refine with the dissatisfaction that is the highest respect for art and a sharp spur to progress—the indecision born of ste- <meta>catchword: ste- (likely "sterility")</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Leonardo's unfinished works were not the result of a flighty character, but rather a mind so fertile that new theories and observations constantly superseded the practical execution of his projects.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Sabba Castiglione, Dante Alighieri, Battle of Anghiari, artistic theory, psychological profile, scientific observation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Continuing the defense of Leonardo’s creative process, specifically addressing the delays in his major commissions like the Sforza monument.</meta>

...painful sterility of mind with the meditation that is the necessary consequence of prodigious fertility.

It was not due to impotence—as Ludovico "the Moor" <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), the Duke of Milan and Leonardo's patron</note> had shown he believed in a moment of ill-humor—that He labored for long years (and then completed the first model in a single month, August 1489!) on the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza. Rather, it was because, always driven by the desire for new research into the anatomy of the horse and by new artistic visions, he wanted to achieve an artistic and scientific truth that no one had dreamed of before: a supreme ideal and high ambition at the same time. It was not "out of shame," as Michelangelo mocked, that He abandoned that sculpture, but because he was forced by the sudden collapse of political events.

Regarding the abandoned *Horse*, the *Last Supper* <note>original: "Cenacolo"</note>, and the *Battle of Anghiari*—works that were perishing before his very eyes—Leonardo does not have a single word of sorrow in his manuscripts. He only notes dryly, referring to the first, amidst a crowd of notes that are obscure and dry to us, but certainly very bitter for him:
> "The Duke lost his State, his property, and his liberty, and no work was finished for him."

<margin>Interesting observation on Leonardo's creative energy and process</margin>
He probably felt pain, but he kept it, according to his custom, locked within himself; and probably it also passed quickly, because Leonardo's interest and love for his works lasted only as long as the period of creation. Afterward, He became disinterested in them, almost as if they no longer belonged to him, as happens with many temperaments of vigorous creative energy.

In fact, while before the artistic vision has reached its expressive power in his imagination and—

<summary>This page argues that Leonardo's unfinished works resulted from his pursuit of scientific perfection and external political turmoil rather than lack of skill, noting his detached emotional response to the loss of his masterpieces.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Sforza Horse, Last Supper, Battle of Anghiari, Michelangelo, creative psychology, Milanese history</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion on Leonardo’s creative psychology, specifically how his interest in a work often waned once the intellectual problem of its composition was solved.</meta>

...the harmony of composition toward which He strives, his sketches and studies are continuous; then, having reached the goal he aimed for, he does not translate it onto the canvas, or if he does, he does so with great impatience. Before the unfinished "Adoration of the Magi," or the cartoon of "Saint Anne," or the drawing of the "Madonna of the Yarnwinder," which he never painted, He probably thought (like Alberti <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), a foundational Renaissance humanist and architect</note> regarding the direction of building projects) that "to ordain is a lordly work, to labor is a servile act," and he entrusted to his disciples—so that they might execute them under his direction—the <gloss>canvases</gloss> for which he was continually requested, such as the "Litta Madonna," the "Holy Family" in the Hermitage Museum, the "Virgin of the Scales" in the Louvre, etc.

We note further, almost by way of concluding these observations opposed to the accusation of impotence and inconstancy, that Leonardo aimed above all in life to satisfy himself, tasting the admirable pleasures of the contemplation of things and of meditation; He is a noble epicurean of the intellect who wants to enjoy the life of the spirit with full liberty, not an ambitious man, stubborn and inconstant according to the suggestions of an excessive desire for honors and fame.

<margin>about the fables</margin>
In a study of this nature, I believe that one can only make use of the maxims, fables, prophecies, and witticisms of Leonardo with the greatest caution. These were either devised by Him or transcribed from previous authors to serve—at least for the most part—for **allegorical paintings and chivalric emblems**; but, however much one must proceed with leaden feet <note>meaning to proceed with extreme caution</note> before drawing conclusions from them regarding the character and intimate feelings of the artist,

<summary>This page argues that Leonardo's tendency to leave works unfinished stemmed from an intellectual preference for design over manual execution, viewing the latter as "servile." It portrays him as an "epicurean of the intellect" who prioritized personal discovery and spiritual freedom over fame.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Leon Battista Alberti, artistic process, Adoration of the Magi, Saint Anne, Madonna of the Yarnwinder, Litta Madonna, Hermitage Museum, Louvre, allegorical paintings, chivalric emblems, fables</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding Leonardo’s intellectual character, specifically refuting the idea that his unfinished works were the result of a lack of discipline, and instead suggesting they stemmed from a preference for mental contemplation over manual labor.</meta>

<margin>[</margin> Still, it cannot be silenced that the majority of the fables and many of the maxims are directed against ambition, restlessness, and inconstancy (the latter two Leonardo almost always considers the daughters of the first); and it cannot be denied that the maxim: "In the contemplation of things lies the calm and the pleasure of life," and the other against the ambitious who ruin their own lives by not understanding their utility and beauty, are profoundly his own—I would dare to say, entirely sprouted from his ideal.

Furthermore, after having—I repeat—premised that his maxims should not be taken as autobiographical notes, I maintain that it is quite difficult to find a disagreement between the moral ideas upon which he most insists and what we know of his life. He speaks against the love of money, and not a single act reveals him to be a miser (the Latin nursery rhyme <note>original: "filastrocca latina"</note> placed under the note regarding the loan of 13 florins <term>florin: a gold coin struck in Florence, the standard currency of the Renaissance</term> to Salai <note>Gian Giacomo Caprotti, known as Salai, Leonardo's pupil and companion</note> I believe should be considered as a kind of prophecy and playful warning toward himself), but many show him to be indifferent or lavish; he speaks against fashion which changes continually and falls from one exaggeration into the opposite exaggeration, and we know that he always wore a rich, elegant, but dignified and unique style of dress; he speaks against sensual passions, and not a trace of one remains in his writings or those of his contemporaries, so well did he know how to conquer or hide them; he speaks exalting solitude, the mother of profound thoughts, and lives the majority of his time alone; he gives advice on elegant and wise conversation and acquires a reputation as a most attractive speaker.

The only fact that contrasts with his writings is

<summary>The author argues that Leonardo da Vinci's personal life largely mirrored the moral philosophy found in his writings, specifically regarding his disdain for ambition, greed, and fleeting fashions.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Salai, florins, maxims, fables, ambition, restlessness, inconstancy, solitude, Renaissance ethics</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s character, moving from his personal habits and moral maxims to his complex relationship with religious institutions and metaphysical philosophy.</meta>

the testamentary provisions for his funeral, in which a large part is given to the ceremonies of worship—once despised in his *Prophecies*—and faith is placed in the intercession of the venal <note>purchased or mercenary</note> prayer of ecclesiastics, which he had previously stigmatized as useless and deceptive. Certainly, such a will is striking in one who dared to write freely: "Of the selling of Paradise. — An infinite multitude will publicly and peacefully sell things of the greatest price, without the license of the master of those things, and which were never theirs, nor in their power, and human justice will not provide for this." These are words that, if they do not exactly reek of the Reformation <note>The 16th-century religious movement that led to Protestantism</note>, certainly do not smell of sanctimony either, and they show a confident independence of spirit.

<margin>against abstract speculation</margin>
Da Vinci was not openly an unbeliever: "I leave aside the crowned letters" <note>original: "lettere incoronate" - a reference to sacred or divinely inspired texts</note>, he declares, much like several of his contemporaries who were irreligious to the marrow, yet satisfied with an apparent deference that allowed them to keep the framework of their destructive theories intact. But as soon as he can, he launches an assault on "mental things that have not passed through the senses," judging them vain and harmful, born of weakness and poverty of intellect. That is to say, along with necromancy <note>the practice of communicating with the dead</note> and alchemy, he condemns metaphysics and, implicitly, theology, which seek to define what soul and life are—unprovable things—and to analyze the mind of God, in which the universe is included, shredding it as if it could be anatomized. These are things to be left, he concludes ironically, "in the minds of the friars, fathers of the people, who by inspiration know all secrets."

But even while mocking abstract speculation, and

<summary>This page examines the contradiction between Leonardo da Vinci's traditional religious funeral arrangements and his biting written critiques of the Church's sale of spiritual favors. It highlights his rejection of metaphysics and theology in favor of empirical knowledge derived from the senses.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Last Will and Testament, religious hypocrisy, Reformation, metaphysics, theology, empiricism, friars, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s philosophical stance, specifically his rejection of abstract metaphysics in favor of empirical experience, while exploring his nuanced views on the soul and the divine.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

proclaiming (to his glory) the high value of expe-
rience for the progress of human knowledge,
He is neither an atheist nor a materialist, even if
he denies, as he does, the life of the spirit separate from
the body among the elements, and doubts quite clearly
of life beyond the grave.

There remain of Him some sketches of prayer, some
cries of astonished admiration before the
first cause of the world; there remain of Him some
sentences that affirm his faith in the power of the
spirit:
> "The senses are earthly; reason stands outside of them when it contemplates."
— 
> "Our body is subject to heaven, and heaven is subject to the spirit." 
And how differently could he have thought, he who
consecrated his whole life to the pure joys of the intellect,
who disdained the medieval chimeras that still or-
namented themselves pompously with the name of science, but
was entirely taken by the new ideals of art and
science?

<margin>Interesting point</margin>
Truly He was the knight of the Idea, so
faithful to her that men, from his contemporaries down
to us, reproached him for having neglected prac-
tice, for having given little more than sketches and fragments,
instead of paintings and treatises.

<margin>read ? + reflect</margin>
It would be strange to want to construct, with the few
passages that Da Vinci has left us, a Vincian
spiritualist philosophy, but it also seems strange — at least
to me — to deny the name of philosopher to one who saw so
deeply into universal life and with such an ample,
dominating gaze, only because in opposition to the sophis-
tries, the pedantic utopias, and the chimerical superstitions
of the Middle Ages, he proclaimed as the basis of the Re- <note>The text breaks here mid-word; likely "Rinascimento" (Renaissance).</note>

<summary>This page argues that while Leonardo da Vinci rejected traditional metaphysics and doubted the afterlife, his devotion to the "Idea" and the intellect makes him a true philosopher rather than a mere materialist. The author defends Leonardo's fragmented output as the result of his unwavering commitment to intellectual ideals over practical completion.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, empiricism, spiritualism, philosophy of art, Middle Ages vs Renaissance, human knowledge, first cause</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo’s philosophical transition from the Middle Ages to the Renaissance, specifically focusing on his reliance on empirical experience over abstract dogma.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

newly built edifice of human thought: positive experience.

Returning to the question of the will <note>Referring to Leonardo’s last will and testament, which included religious provisions that some scholars find inconsistent with his scientific skepticism.</note>, let us say that we would prefer a Vinci who remained faithful, even in his later years and final days, to his early contempt for greedy prelates and outward rituals; however—even assuming it were possible to escape the testamentary customs of that time—it would be inhumane to turn this into an accusation against him. It is well known that these returns to the faith of one's childhood are common even in our own day, and were then more understandable and justifiable than ever.

A far more serious enigma of Leonardo’s soul is his apparent coldness in the face of any event.

His father dies; Ludovico "the Moor" <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), Duke of Milan and Leonardo’s long-time patron.</note> loses his state and his life; his servant robs him openly with incredible impudence; his friend Giacomo Andrea of Ferrara is executed; and He notes the facts dryly, neither sorrowful, nor angry, nor dejected.

When the demands of art or science predominated, it is easy to understand how Leonardo silenced every sentiment that might disturb serene observation; but in other cases? Note, for example, the serene indifference with which he observes the corpse of Baroncelli <note>Bernardo di Bandino Baroncelli, executed for the murder of Giuliano de' Medici during the Pazzi Conspiracy.</note>, hanged on December 19, 1479, in Florence. He almost certainly had the task of portraying him, as a perpetual execration and a warning to the partisans of the Pazzi; therefore—next to the pen drawing—there is a note regarding the clothes and their color, compiled with completely calm accuracy. And, as a fine proof of the

<summary>This page examines the contradictions in Leonardo da Vinci's character, specifically his return to religious tradition in his final will and his unsettling emotional detachment when recording personal tragedies or violent deaths.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Pazzi Conspiracy, Bernardo Baroncelli, Giacomo Andrea da Ferrara, emotional detachment, funeral rites, scientific observation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s emotional detachment and his intellectual relationships, following the description of his sketches of the executed Baroncelli.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

his indifference as a scientist, one should read the description He provides of the cadaveric section <term>cadaveric section: an early term for an autopsy or anatomical dissection</term> of the old centenarian whom he had attended to in the Hospital of Santa Maria Nova. But—I repeat—what of the other cases?

Numerous are the acquaintances and friendships that He recalls in his manuscripts, but almost all of them have a distinctly intellectual character. It seems that it is not men who interest him, but only philosophical, scientific, and artistic discussions, the loan of a desired book, or the explanation of a theorem. And yet, He who reveals himself in his paintings and sketches as an acute observer of the expression of feelings must have been a profound connoisseur of the souls of his friends!

The fables, the mottos <note>original: "motti"</note>, the jokes <note>original: "facezie"</note>, and above all the caricatures show him to be ironic toward the human race; in Him, one does not find the wild solitude, the rushing anger, or the desperate gloom of Michelangelo <note>original: "Michelangiolo"</note>, despite his bitter, often pessimistic vision of the world, which we shall discuss shortly.

<margin>Leonardo's restraint and lack of malice toward those who wronged him.</margin> With his brothers, he maintains good relations even after they attempted to deprive him of his inheritance; he notes without anger—indeed, almost with the interest sparked by a curious thing—the rascality of his servant Giacomo <note>Giacomo Caprotti, known as Salai, Leonardo's pupil and servant known for his mischievous behavior.</note>; he protests, but calmly, against the arrogance of Giovanni degli Specchi; finally, he finds an accent of sorrow rather than indignation against the latter only when, through his slanders and instigations, the Pope forbids him from practicing anatomy. He reserves his bitter, ironic invective only for when the students, with their clamor, interrupt and prevent his cadaveric sections: it is not material interests that re-

<summary>This page explores Leonardo da Vinci's intellectualized social life and his emotional restraint, contrasting his calm irony with Michelangelo's legendary volatility. It highlights how Leonardo only showed true distress when his scientific pursuits, specifically his anatomical studies, were obstructed.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Santa Maria Nova, Michelangelo, anatomy, Salai, Giovanni degli Specchi, emotional detachment, Renaissance psychology</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo’s emotional restraint and his reaction to those who obstructed his intellectual pursuits, contrasting his calm demeanor with the typical passions of his contemporaries.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<sig>2</sig>

...strove to disturb him, but rather the obstacles placed foolishly or wickedly in the way of acquiring knowledge. Then, proud in His eloquence, is the apology He makes for the man of wisdom—that is, for himself—rising up as a judge and condemner of the vile human herd.

These are the only hints that sound of resentment in all the Leonardesque manuscripts; not even the ungenerous enmity of <term>Michelangelo</term> <note>original: "Michelangiolo"</note> finds an echo there. Moreover, even in the account of the <term>Anonymous</term> <note>The "Anonimo Gaddiano," an early biographer of Leonardo.</note>, <term>Leonardo</term> blushes but remains silent, as if disdaining to respond to the unjust mockery. <insert>Not indifference, not cowardice—both impossible; perhaps a superb mastery of himself?</insert>

"One can have no greater nor lesser mastery than that over oneself" — He wrote, and one would be happy to be able to say that such a beautiful, fierce sentiment was drawn from his own life, but alas! He took it, instead, almost word for word from chapter 32 of the *<term>Flower of Virtue</term>* <note>original: "Fiore di Virtù," a popular medieval collection of moral maxims.</note>.

In any case, it is certain that <term>Leonardo</term> locked within his heart the mystery of his dearest feelings, nor did he ever, as far as we know, abandon himself to outbursts of indignation, to profound dejection, or to turbulent passions.

It is not possible, I believe, to speak seriously of a love for <term>Monna Lisa del Giocondo</term>, because no trace of it remains, neither in the famous stained page of the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> <note>original: "Codice Atlantico"</note>, nor much less in the <term>Treatise on Painting</term> <note>original: "trattato della Pittura"</note>. Moreover, if that impenetrable ink blot was indeed made intentionally by <term>Leonardo</term> to hide from the curious the venting of a moment of passion, what better proof that He...

did he consider such confessions as weaknesses and feel ashamed of them? And what better proof is there that we attempt in vain to lift the veil of his mysterious spirit? For, with firm purpose, He would have remained silent or erased from his manuscripts the memory of everything that truly impassioned him as a man, writing only what interested him as a scientist and artist.

<margin>d. interpretation of Leonardo</margin>
Leonardo! — Here is how, in total opposition to those who accuse him of impotence, of <term>abulia</term> <note>A clinical lack of willpower or decisiveness.</note>, and of inconstancy, <term>D'Annunzio</term> <note>Gabriele D'Annunzio (1863–1938), a famous Italian poet and decadent novelist.</note> and many passionate scholars of <term>Vincian</term> <note>Pertaining to Leonardo da Vinci.</note> matters see the Great Man: a demigod who, having abandoned earthly cares, roams with a serene brow and an Olympian gaze through the realms of art and science.

And perhaps they are wrong: they mistake for tranquility what was a harsh restraint of the self, and for indifference what was <note>underlined in original</note> bitter contempt won after hard trials. In fact, in his writings there remain traces, hitherto unnoticed and unstudied, <note>underlined in original</note> of a profound sadness and a deep disdain for men!

Nothing is more unjust than to call him, as has been done, "the painter of the smile." The enigmatic smile of the <term>Mona Lisa</term> <note>original: "Gioconda"</note>, of <term>Saint John</term>, of Saint—

<summary>The author challenges the idealized view of Leonardo as a serene "demigod," suggesting instead that his calm exterior masked a profound, disciplined contempt for humanity and a deeply guarded private life.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Gabriele D'Annunzio, psychology, Prometheus, Mona Lisa, emotional restraint, artistic interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci's multifaceted genius and the common misconceptions regarding his "serene" temperament versus his darker, more complex reality.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

Saint Anne, often transformed into a stereotypical smirk by disciples and admirers, became almost the trademark of his school—a trademark, inherited from Verrocchio, of sweetness and tenderness that exists in some of the Master’s works. However, due to that love of schematic classification which is the passion of the human race, it was extended to all of Da Vinci’s art, willingly forgetting that He created the monstrous, the comic, and the tragic; He portrayed flayed viscera on the anatomy table and the petals of the violet, bestial profiles and the entanglements of monsters and knights, infuriated hags and the fragile little heads of infants, the catastrophe of the Deluge and the soft hands of the so-called <term>Mona Lisa</term> <note>original: "Gioconda"</note>.

At a distance of so many centuries, after we have laboriously rediscovered his findings and inventions, He appears to us as a ruler; his solitude amidst his contemporaries seems a heroic calm, the impassive objectivity of his writings a steady reflection of his soul, free from passions and murky sadness. The <insert>eurhythmy</insert> <note>A harmonious rhythm or proportion in art or movement.</note> that dominates even in the tragic moment of the <term>Last Supper</term> <note>original: "Cenacolo"</note> is an index of supreme serenity—not only of the artist who knew how to join expressive power and harmonious grace, but of the man who, in the ideal of truth and beauty, forgot the weight of existence.

I repeat: we are probably mistaken. How many times, in the rapid decline of his life lived too intensely, did He confess to himself the vanity of existence; how many times was his thought halted, suspended over the abyss of time, feeling—

<note>Vocabulary: Leonardo da Vinci, Andrea del Verrocchio, Mona Lisa, Last Supper, eurhythmy, anatomy, Deluge</note>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci's inner turmoil and his philosophical reflections on the vanity of existence, following the previous page's exploration of his "serene" versus "darker" genius.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

—feeling the wind of the frost of nothingness blow beneath him, and he lyrically expressed his dismay!

He is a painter in love with the fleeting beauty of forms, but he is also a poet of a dark and grandiose imagination who sings: "O time, consumer of things, and, oh! envious antiquity, you destroy all things! and you consume all things with the hard teeth of old age, little by little with a slow death! Helen, when she looked in the mirror, seeing the withered wrinkles of her face made by old age, weeps, and wonders to herself why she was abducted twice." <note>Leonardo is referencing Helen of Troy, the mythological symbol of supreme beauty, now ravaged by time.</note>

Time flees: "the water you touch in the rivers is the last of that which has passed and the first of that which is coming; so it is with the present time"; it flees, and leads to death. What is the end of life? Nothingness. Clearly, on several occasions, Leonardo states his disbelief in an existence beyond the grave; the only possible survival after death is that which glory bestows.

<margin> [A vertical pencil mark spans the following two paragraphs] </margin>
A common destiny: all lives of the vegetable and animal kingdoms plunge into nothingness; not the things, but the essence of things remains. A bitter philosophy: the perennial renewal of matter, the perennial transformation of energy, have never consoled anyone for a brief and painful life. And yet man, although he knows that every moment brings him closer to his destruction, always desires the future, hoping in it for that good which he has not enjoyed until now, and "he does not realize that he is desiring his own undoing."

Worse still: a restless discontent, man—due to the deceptive mirage of the future—toils, makes sacrifices, frets, miserably consumes the present, ruins the

Leonardo is making a crude pun: the laurel (Laura) is better used as a kitchen herb for seasoning meat than as an object of spiritual devotion.</note>

the dark sadness of satisfied flesh when the
soul is unsatisfied—He who, certainly, never
encountered a true soulmate.

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s philosophy, specifically his transition from a critique of sensual love to a broader, almost ascetic disgust for the physical biological process of life.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

to the fascination of disgust, regarding that which is repugnant in physical life, with words that recall those of certain ascetics—He who nevertheless knew how to overcome horror and nausea in the presence of fearsome, quartered corpses; He who at other times raises a hymn to the marvelous structure of man, and proclaims the respect for the lives of others to be holy.

> "Man and animals are merely a passage and conduit for food, a tomb for animals, a hostel for the dead (making life for themselves from the death of others), a sheath of corruption."
> <note>original: "L'omo e li animali sono propio transito e condotto di cibo, sepoltura d'animali, albergo de' morti (facendo a sè vita dell'altrui morte), guaina di corruzione." This passage from Leonardo's notebooks reflects a deeply cynical biological view of existence.</note>

A sheath of corruption! A phrase of Dante-esque vigor.

Man is inferior to many animals due to the imperfection of his sensory organs, but even more so due to the vanity of his presumptuous intelligence and the cruelty of his coarse heart. Between man and beasts, <term>Leonardo</term> <note>Leonardo da Vinci (1452–1519), the quintessential "Renaissance Man" whose notebooks reveal a complex, often dark philosophy.</note>, all things considered, prefers the latter.

It is said of him that he so recoiled from common ferocity that he no longer ate meat (is it true? who knows! it does not appear in the accounts of his manuscripts, but it must be noted that he kept family and servants with him who perhaps did not care for his vegetarian system); it is said that as soon as he saw imprisoned birds, he would buy them to set them free: the kindnesses of a philosopher-poet rebelling against brutal customs. But of Him we also know that he assisted in the killing of pigs, studying with the most vivid attention the contractions of the heart pierced by a long, sharp stiletto—experiments on the living that lost, before his high intellect, any character of atrocity because their purpose was not to satisfy a need or a base material taste, but a desire for scientific knowledge.

<summary>This page explores Leonardo da Vinci's paradoxical relationship with life, contrasting his ascetic disgust for the human body as a "sheath of corruption" with his scientific drive to study living anatomy.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, vegetarianism, anatomy, human cruelty, scientific observation, asceticism, animal rights</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci's preference for animals over humans, moving from his personal habits (vegetarianism and freeing birds) to his philosophical critique of human vanity and environmental destruction.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

Therefore, Leonardo's love and pity toward beasts is certainly not the sentimentalism of a spirit sick with romantic languors, and his words condemning man in the face of animals are the voices of a very grave pessimism.

"Man has great power of speech, of which the greater part is vain and false; animals have little, but it is useful and true; and better is a small certainty than a great lie." <note>original: "L'uomo ha gran discorso..." This quote highlights Leonardo's empirical preference for observable truth over abstract rhetoric.</note> Do you understand, deputies, lawyers, doctors, professors, journalists, etcetera? Your house cat, the quiet philosopher of the not-always-quiet domestic hearth, is wiser and more truthful than you... because he speaks less.

To man, instead, it is enough to know how to chatter; it does not matter to him if his knowledge is well-founded or not; he stuffs himself with a confused, wordy science, in a mad rush, only to resell it as quickly as possible.

King of animals, man, you proclaim yourself? But I would better say king of the beasts, you being the greatest of them! None commit the atrocities that you commit. You overturn nature to satisfy your greedy needs:

> "all the animals languish, filling the air with lamentations; the forests are ruined, the mountains opened to snatch away the generated metals." <note>original: "tutti li animali languiscono..." A remarkably modern-sounding critique of environmental exploitation and mining.</note>

What would Leonardo say today, when for the greed of profit the most marvelous beauties of nature are defaced?

Yet this is nothing:

> "But can I say," — He continues — "anything more wicked than of those who raise praises <gloss>original: lalde (archaic for lodi/praises)</gloss> to heaven for those who with the most fervor have harmed their country and the human species?"

Worshipers of warriors, of conquerors, of politicians, step forward! This is for you.

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, pessimism, animal rights, environmentalism, critique of rhetoric, human cruelty, greed, anti-war</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s misanthropy and his critique of human nature, moving from his environmental concerns to his disdain for political servility and intellectual vanity.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

Leonardo, having lived alongside the likes of a Ludovico il Moro <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), Duke of Milan and Leonardo’s long-time patron.</note>, a Cesare Borgia <note>The ruthless Italian cardinal and condottiero who employed Leonardo as a military engineer.</note>, never speaks, one might say, of politics; but if he does speak of it, do you see? He does not speak to bow down with the sheep-like multitude, always prone before those who strike them, always idolizing those who slit their throats, but to throw their own ferocity and cowardice back in the face of the human race.

Man is a prey to his passions precisely when he most believes he is governing them; man loves his vices, indeed he manages to persuade himself that they are lovable and precious qualities: it is Leonardo who says this, not I... And he says it with wit.

One certainly cannot say that Da Vinci flatters his fellow men! From the height of his proud solitude, the humanists, the philosophers, and the pseudo-scientists of his time seemed to him like bothersome parrots; the warriors and statesmen seemed like wild beasts, and against them he armed his flashing prose; the rest of men... sheep and moles and most stubborn mules, when not worse. From men dedicated only to material things he turned his gaze away, as if from a repugnant object, and speaking of them he suffocated them (allow me the baroque comparison) under a heap of foul insults, the only ones worthy of them:

> "I know that many will say this is a useless work, and these will be those of whom Demetrius <note>Likely referring to Demetrius the Cynic, known for his contempt for the masses.</note> said he made no more account of the wind which caused the words in their mouths than of the wind that came out of their lower parts; men who have desire only for riches and pleasures... And often when I see one of these take this work in hand, I doubt <insert>that he will not, like the monkey, put it to his nose, or ask me if it is something to eat.</insert>" <note>original: "So che molti diranno questa essere opra inutile..." Leonardo here defends his scientific and anatomical studies against the "coarse" men who only value immediate physical gratification.</note>

<summary>This page explores Leonardo da Vinci's profound disdain for the social and intellectual hierarchies of his time, contrasting his "proud solitude" with the "sheep-like" behavior of the masses and the vanity of contemporary scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Cesare Borgia, misanthropy, humanism, social critique, Demetrius, intellectual vanity</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s misanthropy, specifically his disdain for the common man and his cynical view of family life compared to Michelangelo's more traditional melancholy.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

not like the monkey who puts it to his nose, or who asks me if it is something to eat."

<margin>Who says this?</margin>
None, however, of the cited passages by Da Vinci reaches the cold bitterness mixed with sarcasm found in a draft of a letter to his brother, written on the occasion of the birth of a nephew. Never, I dare say, has a man—however ruthlessly skeptical, however painfully discouraged—had the courage to write what Leonardo writes. Michelangelo, in his old age and feeling dejected, also complained to Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Renaissance artists.</note> that his nephew Lionardo had celebrated the birth of another son with great pomp; it did not seem to him a time for joy, believing that celebrations should be reserved not for those who are born, but for those who die after a life well spent. These are melancholy reflections, but they possess nothing of the acrid Leonardesque pessimism.

> "...I understand that you have made extreme celebrations; since I consider you to be prudent, I am now entirely convinced that I am as far from having good judgment as you are from prudence; seeing as you have rejoiced at having created for yourself a diligent enemy, who with all his efforts will desire liberty, which shall not be yours without your death."

From where did all this bitterness come, this acrimony against humanity? Certainly from a coldly acute observation, similar to that which he exercised over physical facts—the same kind that was the glory of his friend, Machiavelli <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the political philosopher known for his realistic and often cynical view of human nature.</note>—but certainly, also in part from his own special conditions of life.

Absorbed in art and science, he shunned the bonds of affection; he lacked the sweetest illusions; that

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuing the psychological profile of Leonardo da Vinci, moving from his cynical views on family to the isolation caused by his superior intellect and his protective secrecy regarding his inventions.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

That which was the cause of his most superb joy—the height and immense vastness of his intellect—was also the cause of his deepest bitterness: the obtuse indifference, the malignant distrust, and the blind superstition of the crowd offended him as soon as he emerged from his proud isolation.

> "The highest happiness will be the highest cause of unhappiness, and the perfection of wisdom the cause of folly": the cry of an offended soul, a confession escaped from the great, proud man! <note>original: "La somma felicità sarà somma cagione della infelicità, e la perfezion della sapienza cagion della stoltizia." This quote reflects Leonardo's belief that extreme sensitivity and intelligence lead to greater suffering.</note>

Let us consider the fruit of his life as he himself must have seen it at times, in a flash: few works brought to completion that aroused wonder and admiration; several failed or unfinished; many scientific inventions left halfway; many discoveries grasped or glimpsed and kept jealously in his secret; all this must have been a heavy burden of discontent, bitterness, and, at the same time, pride.

<margin> [A hand-drawn vertical bracket marks this paragraph in the left margin] </margin>
Foolish is he who puts the fruit of his study in the public square! His generosity will be paid with lashes of the whip or the axe: this is what the fables of the walnut tree and the fig tree say, which were violently shattered for having made a display of their sweet children. <note>Leonardo wrote several fables where trees are punished by men or nature for being too productive or visible; here, the author interprets them as metaphors for the danger of sharing knowledge with the ungrateful public.</note> The harsh experience of life taught him to distrust and to despise; therefore, He hides his marvelous secrets from the <term>profane vulgar</term> <note>The "profano vulgo" refers to the common, uneducated masses who cannot appreciate higher truths.</note>.

"Do not teach, and you alone will be excellent!" — he admonishes with proud hardness, making of his knowledge a fortress and an Olympus; but then, immediately after (curiously!) he notes with the modesty of a housewife, good-naturedly:

<vocab>intellect, wisdom, scientific inventions, secret, profane vulgar, fables of the walnut and the fig</vocab>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s psychological state, specifically focusing on his self-imposed isolation, his distrust of the public, and the emotional toll of his genius.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

"Take a simple boy and have your clothes sewn at home." <note>original: "To' garzone semplice e fatti cuscire la vesta in casa." A quote from Leonardo's notebooks suggesting a preference for privacy and avoiding the gossip or judgment of outsiders.</note>
Strange, elusive soul!

He wanders from observation to observation, from meditation to meditation with an inexhaustible ardor for truth, never satisfied, and declares: "One must not desire the impossible." Is this a rule of his wisdom or a cry of anguish? And likewise, is it a cold observation of a general law, or the agitated accent of pain overflowing from the fullness of the soul: "where there is more feeling, there is more martyrdom, great martyrdom"? <note>original: "dov'è più sentimento lì è più martiri, gran martiri." Leonardo often linked heightened perception with heightened suffering.</note> He certainly did not draw this from ancient or medieval moralists... Nor this: "The vow is born when hope dies."

Enough; even after this too-rapid glance, it seems to me that one can no longer see in Vinci the demigod who, free from every care and every gloom, serenely lives his solitary intellectual life as a source of the highest joys: *Vinci was a balanced spirit, certainly, who dominated his passions with the restraint of the mind* (indeed, he probably intended to keep them silent from us), *but he was also a spirit steeped in acrid bitterness, a ruthless judge of human life, who looked upon the world with supreme contempt, from the height of his pride and his boundless wisdom.*

The contrast between the idealism and the reality of life, once again, turned a great intellect into a great solitary.

# THE WRITER <gloss>1</gloss>

We are standing before the most unique and rare instance of a mind that at one moment contemplates the world as an artist, and at another analyzes it as a scholar <note>original: "sapiente" — refers to a person of deep scientific and philosophical knowledge</note>; we must here capture those moments in which the scholar's analysis is colored and brightened by images, or is brought to life by a sudden, eloquent emotion.

At the beginning of this prose of a scientific character—

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo's prose style, specifically focusing on a famous passage where he describes his curiosity and fear before a cave.</meta>

...fic, but magnificently alive and restless in its anxiety and adoration of knowledge, I have placed—like the statue of a god at the threshold of a temple—the marvelous fragment in which he describes himself, leaning toward the menacing darkness of a terrifying cave (like <term>Petrarch</term><note>Francesco Petrarca (1304–1374), a foundational Italian poet and scholar</note> at the springs of the <term>Sorgue</term><note>The Fontaine de Vaucluse in France, a site of poetic inspiration for Petrarch</note>), torn between instinctive fear and the desire to enter and seize the wonderful secrets of nature: a representation captured (as the meticulous details suggest) from life, and which gives us a <term>Vinci</term> of much more human proportions, far more dramatic and poetic than the impassive demigod, master of things, events, and himself, whom many scholars have idolized.

<margin>Ideas = faith</margin>
The ideas, the great guiding ideas of his entire intellectual life, do not remain in him as mere cold concepts, but become a faith—his faith—which he enunciates in short, detached sentences, grand in their conciseness and simplicity, as if carved by a seer upon solitary glacial boulders to mark his passage through immense, still-unknown regions; appeals launched into the desert.

For these ideas, he fights with passion, with disdain, with bitterness, and with irony against the false, wordy doctrine of his time; then, his prose no longer proceeds by detached propositions but unfolds in agile and robust periods, acquiring a polemical character and thus a restless vividness.

Do not expect from him, however, a fixed model of prose, not even in grave scientific demonstration. Whatever subject he treats, it always results from the fusion of apparently opposite elements that are, in fact, found separately in our...

<summary>The author describes Leonardo da Vinci's prose as a reflection of his human struggle between fear and curiosity, contrasting his passionate, "faith-like" ideas with the dry academic doctrines of his era. Leonardo is presented not as an impassive genius, but as a dramatic and deeply human seeker of natural secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Petrarch, Sorgue, scientific prose, intellectual faith, nature, humanism, polemics</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's literary style, moving from his philosophical "faith" to the specific structural qualities of his prose—alternating between rigid scientific observation and passionate, unbridled oratory.</meta>

writers. In him, the power of <term>reasoning</term><note>original: "raziocinio"; the logical faculty of the mind</note> often provides a powerful skeleton to the complex sentence, which is nevertheless engineered with limpid order; yet in him, <term>scientific or artistic imagination</term><note>original: "fantasia scientifica o artistica"</note> also frequently surmounts and frees itself with a stroke of the wing from grammatical or syntactical constraints, and takes flight. Then, his sentence structure appears disconnected to those who are slaves to centuries-old literary habits; it is, as it stands, a torrent of words in the impetus of a mind hurrying fast toward the conquest of truth—a true reflection, with its rapid flow, its abrupt movements, and its unexpected concatenations, of the intense heartbeat that accompanies intense mental labor.

<margin>fragments / that outline / a grand / superb vision / of things</margin>
Here is the rigid formula, the concise statement, the naked list of observed phenomena—a raw list made of short propositions, but one that continues for pages and pages with admirable simplicity, order, connection, and progression, sketching in broad strokes a grand and superb vision of things. And here is the <term>invective</term><note>original: "invettiva"; a forceful, critical attack in speech or writing</note> thrown down all in one breath, in a single cast without a single erasure, against the wretched <term>alchemists</term><note>early practitioners of chemistry who sought to turn base metals into gold; Leonardo often criticized them for their lack of empirical method</note>, the painters driven by hunger for profit, and the despisers of science—an invective that swells long sentences powerfully, just as indignation swells the writer's chest.

It is not the <term>formal oration</term><note>original: "togata concione"; literally a "toga-clad speech," referring to the stiff, classical style modeled on ancient Roman rhetoric</note> modeled on ancient examples, but the heat and force of an orator who improvises under the lash of passion. It is the defense of his most cherished possessions, letting bitterness and irony flow from an offended soul like living blood from a deep wound: read, for example, the invective against the students who interrupt the <term>dissections</term><note>original: "sezioni"; refers to anatomical dissections of cadavers for scientific study</note> ca—

<summary>This page analyzes Leonardo da Vinci's writing style, contrasting his disciplined scientific lists with his passionate, unedited outbursts against alchemists and critics. It argues that his prose is a direct, "living" reflection of his mental intensity rather than a copy of classical rhetorical models.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, prose style, scientific imagination, rhetoric, alchemists, anatomical dissection, literary criticism</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci’s prose style, specifically focusing on his passionate invectives and the structural integrity of his more emotional writings compared to his scientific fragments.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

...truthful, and tell me if we are not standing before a passage of modern eloquence, characterized by a nervous and terrible spontaneity. It seems as though the writer, swept away by the impetus of his indignation, deviates from his initial movement; for at first, he turns against the insolent despisers of such profitable study—fools to whom life does not seem long enough to occupy themselves with the human body, yet long enough to analyze the universal mind of God as if they had anatomized it <note>Leonardo is critiquing theologians and scholastics who speculate on the divine while ignoring the physical reality of nature</note>; fools worthy only of brutal exercises like hunting. Then, with a brusque transition, he brands the "mad bestiality" of all men who, for the love of their bellies, become more ferocious than the beasts themselves... Suddenly, he takes up the first thread of his discourse again, making a moving <term>peroration</term><note>the concluding part of a speech, intended to inspire enthusiasm in the audience</note> in favor of high geniuses—those he already energetically defended at the start against the barbarism of coarse intellects—who should be regarded as "Earthly Gods" (the "Heroes" of Carlyle! <note>A reference to Thomas Carlyle’s 1841 work "On Heroes, Hero-Worship, and the Heroic in History," which argues that history is shaped by "Great Men"</note>) and honored with statues and images... It seems the orator pauses for a moment; he smiles bitterly, and concludes skeptically: provided that people do not then act like the savages of India, who cut miraculous images into pieces, scrape them, and eat them!

Rarely has Da Vinci abandoned his soul while writing as he does in these agitated, bitter, and sarcastic pages. These are not, fortunately—as is too often the case in his prose—interrupted fragments, resumed and then interrupted again with that supreme dissatisfaction that was a hallmark not only of the painter, but also of the writer and perhaps the man. Instead, they form a single organism, bursting with flavor...

<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s literary style, moving from his passionate outbursts to his scientific prose and linguistic innovations.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>

...of a sharp citrus quality, or rather more: the fierce bitterness of one who feels himself highly placed, yet misunderstood and alone, and sneers.

Alongside passages of such wild impetuosity, you will find the attempts at solemn, formal prefaces for the numerous treatises he conceived with grand designs and never completed; you will find—how shall I put it?—a sonorous cascade of words (concerning water, for example), which the artist delights in releasing, confident and bold in his fresh and vigorous linguistic material, granted to him by Florentine usage, and which He is the first to tame into scientific precision <note>The author highlights Leonardo's role in transforming the common Italian tongue into a language capable of technical and scientific description</note>; you will find plain anatomical descriptions, colored here and there by some energetic, raw brushstroke: it seems the writer speaks simply and almost coldly, and then, suddenly, from a single metaphor or adjective, the horrific reality leaps out.

Seek out the terrible description of the alterations—

The Italian grammar begun in the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> <note>The largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings, covering subjects from weaponry to botany</note> follows living usage, and in Leonardo’s lists of words, there are many that remained unrecorded for centuries by the <term>Crusca</term> <note>The Accademia della Crusca, the premier institution for the study of the Italian language, founded in 1583</note>. <meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s scientific prose, specifically focusing on his anatomical observations of aging and his transition into lyrical, poetic descriptions of nature.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

senile: "The major veins grow in length and twist themselves in the manner of snakes; the liver, deprived of blood, dries up and becomes like frozen bran, so that when rubbed it falls apart like sawdust; the veins of the gallbladder and the umbilical cord remain entirely stripped of the liver's matter, like stalks of maize or sorghum when the grains have been plucked from them." <term>Aneurysms</term> <note>A permanent swelling of an artery</note> and <term>phleboliths</term> <note>Small blood clots that have calcified into "stones" within a vein</note>: "stones as large as chestnuts, with the color and shape of truffles, or like the dross or slag of iron, and they had sacks attached to the said veins in the shape of goiters." He proceeds by way of comparisons—a thing unusual for Him—because he must describe unusual things; comparisons of a powerful rawness.

But Leonardo's scientific prose does not culminate in the enunciation or demonstration of laws, nor even in the description of observed phenomena, but rather where his spirit, ecstatic before the nature revealing itself to him, abandons itself to a true lyrical rapture.

<margin>lyricism even in scientific writings</margin>
Leonardo the lyricist! Yes, for by now everyone knows that poetry and prose are merely formal distinctions, and that one can have pure poetry in prose, and pure prose in verse.

I point to one of the passages marvelous for its grandiose imagination and for a feeling of infinite, almost religious wonder—a passage that in a certain way foreshadows one of the highest Italian lyrics, the ode "To a Fossil Shell" by Zanella <note>Giacomo Zanella (1820–1888), an Italian poet known for reconciling science and religion in his work</note>. Before a colossal fossil, discovered by Him within the bowels of a mountain, the vision of prehistoric ages lives in his moved imagination: he sees the great monster churning the sea and striking like a thunderbolt with the fury of—

<summary>This page examines Leonardo da Vinci's use of vivid, rustic metaphors to describe anatomical decay in the elderly and highlights the poetic, lyrical quality that emerges in his scientific observations of the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, scientific prose, metaphors, aging, fossils, Giacomo Zanella, lyricism</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci’s lyrical scientific prose, specifically his reaction to finding a fossilized whale or sea monster in the mountains.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>

the wings and the forked tail of schools of dolphins and tunas. He pauses, and with a rapid, fiery movement:

> "Oh time! — he exclaims — swift predator of human things, how many kings, how many peoples have you undone, since the marvelous form of this fish died here.... And now, patient you lie, and with your stripped bones you have made armor and support for the mountain placed above you."

I have followed this prose-ode with the three drafts (from the Atlantic Codex) of the vision of the monster that proudly furrows and surmounts the waves of the swollen ocean, so that they may be studied especially for the variations of imitative harmony attempted by the artist (see if it is possible not to speak of a full and secure artistic consciousness when dealing with Da Vinci as a writer!).

What a boundless horizon of poetry was opened to the scientist by his reliable observations of the humble <term>nicchi</term> <note>original: "nicchi" — an archaic Italian term for seashells or fossilized shells</note> of the mountains, and the subtle investigations with which, one by one, patiently and with total rigor of method, he refuted the erroneous explanations given for this curious fact. He did not hesitate even to demolish—using the weapon of ridicule—beliefs drawn from the sacred scriptures, such as certain ideas regarding the Universal Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah; Leonardo argued that fossils in mountains were not deposited by a single flood but by shifting seas over vast time</note>. From minute and weighted observation, and from concise and sharp deduction, his intellect rose to the marvelous intuition of unknown epochs, to the reconstruction of worlds collapsed forever. Read, read the grandiose vision of the Mediterranean before the great plains of Africa and Europe had emerged: is it a scientist or a poet who speaks?

But geology is not his only source of lyrical inspiration.

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s literary style, moving from his geological observations of fossils to his poetic-scientific insights on physics and the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

The history of physics will tell you that Leonardo, in studying the flame, intuited the nature of a "stationary system," but literary criticism will tell you that this fragment is pure poetry:

> "Look at the light and consider its beauty. Blink your eye and look at it again: that which you see of it now was not there before, and that which was there before is no more. Who is it that remakes it, if the maker <note>The "maker" here refers to the fuel or the process of combustion that is constantly consumed</note> continually dies?"

The scientist will tell you that among the great theories of physics, Leonardo intuited wave theory; but who can fail to recognize the scientist in this moving lyric, written in prose—but a pulsing, rhythmic prose that unconsciously approaches verse:

> "The movement of earth against earth, treading upon it, moves the struck part but little;
> Water struck by water forms circles around the place where it was struck;
> A longer distance is traveled by the voice through the air;
> Longer still through fire;
> Longest of all the mind through the universe, but because it is finite, it does not extend into the infinite."

The thought expands, scaling a fearsome image of vastness to climb toward another even more vast and fearsome, marking its gigantic stages with brief moments of pause; but having reached the summit, where with a cry of pride he proclaims that nothing spreads as far as the mind in the universe, as if seized by sudden vertigo, he crashes back (a human, dramatic transition) into the full consciousness of the narrowness and fragility of our intellect in the face of the enormous mystery of life; he feels, in the very moment he was exalting the greatest of forces...

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, physics, wave theory, stationary system, rhythmic prose, intellect, flame</vocab>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from page 36 regarding Leonardo da Vinci's intellectual tension between his scientific observations and his poetic intuition, specifically focusing on the limits of the human mind versus the infinite.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

natural forces, the power of the spirit, and the smallness of human grandeur when faced with the Infinite.

It was not this poetry, full of tragic shadows, that his brush gave us—nor could it have... yet poetry, which he sought to mistreat in his dispute over the preeminence of Painting versus Poetry <note>original: "Pittura e della Poesia". This refers to the *Paragone*, Leonardo's famous debate arguing that painting is superior to literature because it appeals to the eye, the "window of the soul."</note>, took its revenge without him even knowing it.

Vinci’s attitude toward poetry is curious; in his hostility, one clearly sees a premeditated bias. It is the echo of the scientist’s resentment against the "nonsense" <note>original: "fanfaluche"</note> of the Humanists <note>The 15th-century scholars who focused on Greek and Latin classics, often looking down on those without a formal classical education.</note>. It is the resentment of the painter against the verse-monger—that puffed-up, chest-thumping rhetorician who rattled off hexameters <term>hexameters: a traditional form of poetic meter used in Greek and Latin epic poetry</term> or sonnets and, in return, received money, praise, and honors. This rhetorician looked down his nose at him—the artist, the engineer, the organizer of ducal festivals—who dared to enter the world of wisdom <note>original: "sapienza"</note> without the proper childhood preparation under a pedant, or the required youthful apprenticeship in the learned universities. Instead—proud of the knowledge he had acquired by himself—Leonardo despised their doctrine based on the subservience to authority; he loudly proclaimed the value of experience and did not hesitate to assert that "good letters are born from a good nature" <note>original: "le buone lettere so’ nate da un bono naturale." Leonardo argued that true writing comes from innate talent and observation of nature rather than rote imitation of old books.</note>.

<margin>?</margin>
Otherwise, one could not explain how He—after having affirmed that "painting is a mute poetry, and poetry is a blind painting," thereby essentially recognizing that they share the same animating essence and the same power of imaginative evocation—then strips poetry of every peculiar merit. He compares the poet to a shopkeeper <note>original: "merciaio"</note> who gathers together goods made by different craftsmen. By a process of reduction, he relegates poetry to Philosophy, to Eloquence, and to the most disparate branches of knowledge <note>original: "scibile"</note>...

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 37, which discussed Leonardo’s resentment toward the Humanist scholars and his defense of experience over academic authority in the debate between painting and poetry.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

logical reasoning, but of no aesthetic value, everything in the art of words that is not description. It is clear to see how, in leading him into error, he had in mind those hybrid Humanistic patchworks <note>original: "centoni" – a literary work made up of various passages from other authors, common in the Renaissance.</note>.

As for poetic description (and, most curiously, he describes with evident pleasure at the very moment he condemns it, as well as many other times, willingly and in service... of painters!), he denies it that perfect, simultaneous, and minute representation of things that only painting can achieve. And he is right... if poetic description had, as he seems to believe here, the sole purpose of presenting the described object as clearly as possible in all its smallest details of form and color.

And this error comes to him, I believe, above all from his own self-observation. Being a painter before a writer, and gifted with a marvelous visual power (so much so that, as soon as he can, he releases hymns to the eye as the greatest instrument of the intellect), when describing—whether he takes from life or composes fantastic creations from distant, discordant elements of reality—he sees with great clarity, and expresses exactly as he sees.

A Florentine and a student of new, broad views regarding language, the word is as ready for him as the color on the palette,<gloss>1</gloss> and often—indeed—he jots it down boldly (perhaps with the intention of finishing more

Look for the descriptions of the effects of fog, of smoke, of the wind, of the sun at sunset, of clouds beneath the moon, of the beginning of the rain, of the shadows and lights in a forest, of the woman dressed in white in the middle of the open countryside—all pages fresh with sharp, crystal-clear vision; look for the notes for the *Last Supper* <note>original: "Cenacolo"</note>, the first sketch of that grandiose composition, the portrait of the angry man, of the desperate man, of the orator and his listeners, of the postures characteristic of the old, the young, women, and children, and you will always find the same merit of rapid, clear, and lively representation, but one might say it is totally devoid of sentimental effect. He has seen with a dominating eye, but without sentimental emotion; he portrays exactly as he has seen, and if it were not for that eagle eye of his which caught details entirely his own—even in representations that were by then conventional in the art of painting—perhaps we would find his description very impersonal, and therefore very unpoetic.

The broad, indeterminate line, so suggestive to the imagination, is almost entirely lacking, as it was, for that matter, in the greatest poet of the time, Ariosto <note>Ludovico Ariosto (1474–1533), author of Orlando Furioso, known for his clarity and precision.</note>: even in the descriptions of a somewhat turbid heat, in which image chases image like wave chases wave in a rough sea, and the propositions follow one another tumultuously, sometimes crowding one on top of the other (examples, and most successful ones, are those famous descriptions of the *Deluge* and the *Battle*), Leonardo conceives the grandiose not through summary strokes or great contrasts of shadows and lights, but —

<meta>Continuing from page 39, which analyzed Leonardo’s lack of sentimental emotion in his descriptions and compared his precise style to that of the poet Ariosto.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

the fruit of the pictorial <term>pictorial: relating to the art of painting or visual representation</term> environment in which he had grown up, and the natural result of a talent tending toward analysis rather than synthesis—like the result of an infinity of details, all precise and all alive.

An art that has the almost tormenting rapidity and mobility of images that is characteristic of cinematographic art... a very modern art, in short.

He never describes indirectly, that is, by means of the impression or the emotion aroused, letting the effect make us imagine the cause to be more gigantic and terrible (remember the spear of long shadow in Homer? <note>A reference to Homeric epic style where the size of a weapon is suggested by the length of its shadow.</note>), but the thing stands before him with an almost hallucinatory lucidity, until He has grasped it as one picks a fruit that, once squeezed, is thrown away to pluck another and another and another. Marvelous greed!

In his descriptions and in his sketches He achieves the same artistic effect: he proceeds with rapidity and accuracy together, with a sure and very sharp drawing, trusting in his infallible eye, in his extraordinary representative power to surprise things and men in the act that reveals the secret of their life.

More varied and powerful—let us say it without fear of injustice or irreverence—with the pencil than with the word, He creates not for our joy, but for his instinctive pleasure, an entire world of beauty, which men, stingy toward themselves, keep tightly locked away in many museums and libraries scattered throughout the world. The majority of these precious sheets are there, in gray England, and await to be revealed to the general public, since science per-

<page-num>41</page-num>

That suave *reverie* <note>original: "réverie" - a state of being pleasantly lost in one's thoughts; a daydream</note> which is missing from his prose but was not missing from his oceanic soul, is found not only in the mysterious smile of the *Mona Lisa* or the *Saint John*, but in those sketches of young girls' faces lost in a dreamlike penumbra, of an ineffable melancholy sweetness; in those sketches of Virgins bowing with infinite adoration, and of heads of veiled women, absorbed in their own grave thoughts. The graceful little pictures that one seeks in vain from him as a writer are there: in those profiles of plump children, in those naked little rascals playing with a kitten, in those cats sleeping in a thousand voluptuous poses.

The schematicism of certain descriptions—which was held against him as a blatant contradiction to his own precept: "always study nature"—is a schematicism especially noted in his descriptions of the poses appropriate to the various ages of man. In my opinion, these were practical tips <note>The author argues these were "how-to" guides for less talented students rather than Leonardo's personal artistic philosophy.</note> that He gave to ignorant artists who, accustomed to inveterate artistic habits, repeated movements that were graceless or contrary to all naturalness, and who required a guide to *discover* the truth¹; this schematicism disappeared in the

sketches of old men, so varied in their common old age. From the profile of the entirely bald old man, whose hard will is expressed by the strength of the skull, the clenched jaws, the predatory nose, and the eyes—small but extremely sharp under the eaves of a brow bare of eyebrows—to the profile of the old man with a neck still like a bull's, with wild hair in the wind, a type of irascible and indomitable pride; to the old men dazed by age, with a dead gaze and wrinkled skin that allows the skeleton to show through.

<margin>*</margin> Caricature—which, when taken to the study of the bestial degenerations of man, plays such a large part in the sketches—has no reflection in Leonardo's writings.

Barely a hint of the monstrous grotesque is found in the description of the fantastic Giant; the <term>octave: a poetic stanza of eight lines</term> beginning "He was blacker than a hornet" is a variation of an octave by Pulci <note>Luigi Pulci (1432–1484), an Italian poet known for his mock-epic style.</note>; a sketch of a portrait-caricature exists only in the *Codex Atlanticus*, but it is unfinished and barely intelligible. The author himself confesses to not having succeeded in it, and abandons it as an attempt unworthy of further care.

<margin>all less violent than the sketches</margin> Irony in the writings very often wears the form of allegory, in the fables and the prophecies, and for that very reason it loses the character of violence that it otherwise possesses in the immediacy of expression, remaining bitter, yes, but calm.
