—although some appear dictated by a particular feeling of his own (such as those about the walnut and the fig tree which, by showing men the richness of their fruits, were plundered), or by his considerations on specific events (such as that of the thrushes and the owl, which seems referable to the expulsion of Ludovico il Moro <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), Duke of Milan and Leonardo's patron, known as "the Moor."</note>), it is probable that He drew many subjects from originals unknown to us or lost, or from oral tradition.

<margin>
reveal and reflect
</margin>

There are some very brief ones, almost like notes taken for further development, and there are some that are more developed, which bear evident signs of careful artistic elaboration. In these cases the writer, while entering *into the heart of the matter* <note>original: "in medias res"</note>, expands the narrative and intersperses—to color the feelings of the plants and animals—<insert>dialogues and soliloquies</insert> almost always of a calm and well-turned eloquence. I do not say that they are entirely devoid of any spontaneous or easy movement, but certainly they too often suffer from that certain "I-know-not-what" <note>original: "non so che"</note> of coldness and stiffness that is found in too many fables, and which comes from the fact, I believe, that the <term>fabulist: a creator or writer of fables</term> does not have vivid human characters in his imagination, but seeks to animate beasts and plants artificially with generic sentiments, for the purpose of drawing a generic moral lesson.

<margin>
Is this true
Do I agree
</margin>

Many are the fabulists, but very few are the poets of the fable, because we do not ask of it to be the polite allegory of a very common moral truth, but <insert>to be the inspired allegory of a truth</insert> (luckily this word is very elastic!) discovered by a sharp spirit who observes life ironically, and who, having too much good taste and being too skeptical to sa—

<summary>The author analyzes Leonardo da Vinci's fables, noting that while some reflect personal feelings or political events like the fall of Ludovico il Moro, many suffer from the artificiality common to the genre. He argues that a true poetic fable requires a sharp, ironic observation of life rather than just a generic moral lesson.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, fables, Ludovico il Moro, allegory, moral instruction, literary criticism, storytelling</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 43, which discussed Leonardo's tendency to use fables as artificial moral allegories rather than spontaneous poetic expressions.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

->to leave the pulpit or the lecturer's chair, softening the tone into the pleasantness of a fantastical yet truthful tale.<-

Leonardo, in many of his fables—as well as in his allegories (transcribed almost word-for-word from the *Flower of Virtue* <note>A popular medieval bestiary and moral treatise</note>, yet with a form much more agile and lively!) and in many maxims copied from Seneca or others—is far more a literary moralist than a poet; whereas, as we have seen, he was truly great in soul in certain sketches written under the influence of genuine emotion.

See, for example, the speech of the snow which, as a symbol of humility, rolls downward and is rewarded by being the last to be melted by the sun:

> "Now, should I not be judged haughty and proud, I, a tiny drop of snow, for having placed myself in such a high position and for allowing such a great quantity of snow as can be seen from here to remain lower than myself?"

There is no one who does not sense the literary preoccupation of the snow... that is, of Leonardo, in crafting such an ornate little speech.

The fables, even the more elaborate ones, are also harmed by the very frequent placement of the <term>adjective: a word describing a quality</term> before the <term>noun: a word identifying a person, place, or thing</term>, a feature found in many other descriptive passages by Leonardo, but which perhaps never recurs so tediously as here.

For in those other passages, besides a greater merit of freshness, the use of adjectives almost always has the merit of being varied in its distribution; whereas in these fables, it becomes monotonous due to its excessive continuity and regularity: every <term>noun</term> has its fine <term>adjective</term>, or even two, and marches along stiffly like a

<meta>The text ends abruptly as the page breaks mid-sentence.</meta>

gentleman preceded and announced by one or two valets.

I give one example among many: "the blackberry bush <note>original: "il rovestrice"</note>, being stimulated in its slender branches, filled with new fruits, by the piercing claws and beak of the meddlesome blackbirds, etc."

In any case, when a comparison is made with previous moralist storytellers, what secure mastery of language and agility of syntactical movement is found, especially in the shorter fables and allegories! Compare "The Oyster and the Crab" with the version of the same little story (derived from the *Treasure* <note>original: "Tesoro"</note> by Brunetto Latini <note>A 13th-century Italian philosopher and scholar, mentor to Dante</note>), given by Cecco d'Ascoli in that *Acerba* <note>An encyclopedic poem from the 14th century</note> whose verses truly set one's teeth on edge.

<meta>Continuing from page 45, which compared Leonardo da Vinci’s fables with earlier versions by Cecco d’Ascoli, highlighting Leonardo's superior linguistic mastery and narrative agility.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

A small fifteenth-century <note>original: "quattrocentesca"</note> collection of one hundred fables <note><term>apologhi</term>: short moral fables or allegories, often featuring animals or inanimate objects</note> is that of Leon Battista Alberti <note>A renowned Renaissance humanist, architect, and polymath (1404–1472)</note>, preceded by a letter from the author to Aesop and Aesop's reply. These fables, translated (I believe) by Cosimo Bartoli, were printed together with one hundred others composed by Bernardino Baldi in 1582. Both Alberti’s and Baldi’s works are more varied in their moral intentions than Da Vinci’s little stories, and they are written with great simplicity. It is almost unnecessary to say that none of them share a common subject with Leonardo’s, because one can hardly speak even of a resemblance between Alberti’s fable "The Lily and the Waves" (already pointed out by Solmi <note>Edmondo Solmi, a prominent scholar of Leonardo da Vinci's writings</note>) and Da Vinci’s "The Lily."

In Leonardo’s extremely brief note, there is a far greater imaginative world than in Alberti’s short prose, which provides details and aims for a clear and obvious moral lesson! It seems that in that brief line, Da Vinci intuited that even in the art of the word, what is suggested sometimes has more value than what is actually presented—just as he had understood to be the case in Painting.

Alberti writes:

> "The Lily flower, dismayed and pale as the river approached it, had placed all its care and thought into maintaining its ancient and accustomed dignity of greeting all the largest and most swollen Waves as they arrived; finally it fell, by means of their arrival; and it would truly have been saved, if it had not wished to stand upon its pride <note>original: "stare in su il grande," an idiom meaning to act with haughty or grand manners</note>."

<summary>This page compares Leonardo da Vinci’s fables with those of Leon Battista Alberti, arguing that Leonardo’s brief, suggestive style possesses a deeper imaginative power than Alberti’s more explicit moralizing. It specifically contrasts their different treatments of the "Lily" motif to highlight Leonardo's artistic intuition.</summary>

<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Leonardo da Vinci, Bernardino Baldi, fables, literary style, Renaissance literature, The Lily and the Waves, moral allegory</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 46, which contrasted Leon Battista Alberti’s moralizing fable of the lily with Leonardo da Vinci’s more evocative and atmospheric version.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

## Da Vinci:

> "The lily placed itself upon the bank of the Ticino <note>original: "Tesino," the river flowing through Pavia</note>, and the current pulled the bank away along with the lily."

The fury of life overwhelms the delicate flower: a victim of its own imprudence, of pride, of curiosity, of love—of which, in short, among a thousand passions?

Perhaps none of Da Vinci's fables is as poetic as this one, which we like to imagine—based on the hint He himself gives us—was conceived along the beautiful wooded banks of the Ticino, near Pavia, during one of his meditative solitary walks. Perhaps, while studying the movements of the rushing wave, turbid from recent rains, he interrupted his scientific observation to follow—with a sigh of melancholy? with an ironic smile?—the flower swept away in the swift whirlpools.

<margin>JESTS</margin>
Those which Solmi <note>Edmondo Solmi (1874–1912), a pioneer in the historical study of Leonardo’s manuscripts</note> titled *Jests* <note>original: "Facezie"; witty anecdotes or humorous tales</note> (short stories of spirited sayings in the style of certain ones by Sacchetti <note>Franco Sacchetti (c. 1332–1400), a Florentine writer famous for his "Three Hundred Novellas"</note>), are certainly a small collection of things heard, some of which are very common, others quite coarse. ¹

Where the narrative expands a bit more, as in the one about the friar and the petty merchant, or the painter and the priest, we have the opportunity to catch Da Vinci's prose in an entirely familiar, simple, and humble tone, very different from the others observed so far. There is no artificial care for the adjectives that—

---

¹ One of these, titled by **SOLMI** (*Fragments*, p. 394), *The Jest of a Priest*, was found exactly in the *Jests, Witticisms, and Pranks of Various People* by L. Domenichi, Venice, Giorgio de' Cavalli, 1565, page 24. Da Vinci's account, however, possesses far greater artistic refinement.

<summary>The author analyzes Leonardo's poetic "Lily" fable, likely inspired by the Ticino river, and transitions into discussing Leonardo's "Facezie" (jests), noting their simpler, more colloquial prose style compared to his other writings.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 47, which discussed Leonardo’s "Jests" and his simpler, more familiar prose style compared to his scientific or poetic writings.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

—are indeed very few and scattered irregularly; there is no sustained pace of the sentence, no attempt at polished eloquence, but rather snatches of very natural dialogue, lively apostrophes, and all that juicy and rapid speech, full of back-and-forth, which He had learned in his youth in the workshop of Verrocchio, or among the sharp-tongued workers <note>original: "morditori," literally "biters," referring to the witty, sarcastic stonecutters</note> at the Marbles of Santa Maria del Fiore <note>The Cathedral of Florence</note>. But He has left us too little of this tone to risk a judgment that goes beyond stating that He possessed that most happy disposition for storytelling and dialogue which many Florentines have had and still have.

<margin>|</margin>
"The only true office of the poet is to feign the words of people speaking together," He said.

<margin>|</margin>
It has been said, it is true, that Leonardo’s prose is a continuous dialogue that He conducts with himself or with his imaginary opponents; but perhaps that is a bit much, even though I do not care much for the distinction of literary genres, and I recognize in much of the Leonardesque prose a vividness of movement—an agitation that drags it toward the dialogic, or rather, dramatic form.

Beautiful, besides the apostrophes and the invectives to which I have already alluded, are the few Vincian letters remaining to us in this respect.

From that famous one with which he offered his secrets to Duke Ludovico <note>Ludovico Sforza, known as "Il Moro," the Duke of Milan</note>, which begins with the gravity of a long, complex sentence—the bow of the artist to the munificent lord—and continues succinctly, fiercely bold like an ancient Amazon, through detached, rapid propositions, "things noted with brevity" <note>original: "cose notate sub brevità"</note>, not a parade of empty words, but a unique document

<summary>This page analyzes Leonardo da Vinci's prose style, noting its roots in the witty, rapid-fire speech of Florentine workshops and its inherent dramatic, dialogic quality, particularly in his letters to patrons.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, prose style, Verrocchio, Florence, Ludovico il Moro, rhetoric, dialogue, Sforza letter</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 48, which discussed Leonardo’s natural storytelling ability and his famous letter to Ludovico Sforza.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>

of Leonardo's soul; to the other very interesting one addressed to the Overseers <term>Fabbriceri: officials in charge of the construction and maintenance of a cathedral</term> of the Piacenza Cathedral—lively, with a smoothly discursive pace, persuasive at first, and then flashing with indignation; to that one addressed to the Magnificent Giuliano de' Medici to inform him of the terrible conduct of Giovanni degli Specchi <note>Giovanni "of the Mirrors," a German craftsman who worked for Leonardo and caused him much grief</note>, drafted in several versions that show Da Vinci was preoccupied even with the form of a letter which, after a brief preamble, proceeds swiftly, almost abruptly, stripped of ornaments, rapidly mentioning one by one the deeds of the rogue <note>original: "mariuolo"</note>; to the letter "to the lord deputies," rich in sentences with long introductory clauses <term>protasi: the first part of a complex sentence structure</term> spent in honor of observing historical logic in the continuation of a building, and which then ends (was the writer tired? or was another thought drawing him away?) with two short sentences thrown down in a fury like notes taken for a reminder; to the agitated notes to his brothers or those playful ones to Francesco Melzi <note>Leonardo’s favorite pupil and heir</note>—all the few remaining letters of Leonardo reveal to us his temperament as a born writer: spontaneous, alive, accepting every syntactical form from the most literary to the most popular, from the simplest to the most complex. Guided by his sure instinct, he uses them as they correspond to the ever-varying attitudes of his thought.

For one cannot speak of classical artifice when, from a mind superior in its power of analysis and synthesis like Da Vinci's, there flow complex sentences of the broadest breath, rich in incidental and subordinate clauses, in which inversion is the keystone that supports the well-constructed edifice; and one cannot speak of artifice

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 49, which analyzed Leonardo’s letters and his instinctive, non-artificial command of various syntactical forms to express his complex thoughts.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>

popular style when, in a simple tale or a rapid description where image chases image, or in a summary list of observed phenomena, he makes use of detached, coordinated clauses. He does this because such a construction responds to the natural mental process; or when, in the excitement of his imagination or feeling, he leaves out verbs, nouns, or other elements, forcing the sentence into abrupt movements. In doing so, he breaks faith with traditional literary syntax, yet preserves all the beautiful warmth of the sudden scientific or artistic creation that captivates and enthuses him.

We thus witness, with profound and reverent emotion, the <insert>intimate drama of Leonardo, a drama entirely intellectual in nature</insert>, but one which no other writer has presented to us as he has.

If Leonardo’s prose had no other merit, it would still possess this one: a truly great one.

# NOTICE

## TO THE PRESENT COLLECTION.

Scholars who wish to learn something of the varied and multifaceted prose of Leonardo <note>original: "vinciana"</note>, without consulting—I do not say the original autographs—but even the transcriptions published in high-priced editions (which are therefore not easy to find even in libraries), have until now relied confidently on the very successful selection compiled by Solmi for Barbera's "Diamond" collection. This work had the honor of being "plagiarized" by four translations and simultaneous Leonardo selections: one French, one German, and two English ^1; or they have turned to the recent small volume edited by Luca Beltrami, published by the Italian Editorial Institute in the "Immortals" collection. The idea of choosing from among Leonardo's <note>original: "del Vinci"</note> so varied notes, arranging them in such a way that the reader does not find themselves continually tossed from one subject to another, is excellent; and this explains the favor with which the *Frag-*

---

^1 See the bibliographical note placed at the end of the lecture: "The Resurrection of Leonardo's Work" by SOLMI, in the volume *Leonardo da Vinci, Florentine Lectures*, Milan, Treves, 1910, pp. 47-48.

<summary>This preface introduces a collection of Leonardo da Vinci's writings, discussing previous popular editions by scholars like Solmi and Beltrami that aimed to make his disorganized notes accessible to a general audience.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, prose, Edmondo Solmi, Luca Beltrami, bibliography, Italian literature, manuscript transcriptions</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Notice to the Reader" regarding the editorial history and philological shortcomings of previous collections of Leonardo da Vinci's writings, specifically critiquing the editions by Solmi and Beltrami mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>

-ments by Solmi were received, and the lively interest that the *Writings* collected by Beltrami arouse in every scholar of Vincian matters.

But alas! An unjust fate weighs upon the work of the Great Man.

It is impossible to explain how the many absurdities—often ridiculous—that embellish the *Fragments* (leaving aside the useless and often capricious modernization of the language, the omission of entire propositions, the arbitrary grammatical corrections, and the often incorrect punctuation), could have passed unnoticed under the eyes of so many severe readers, who were ecstatic before the miracle, revealed by Solmi, of Leonardo's <note>original: "vinciana"</note> prose. A mystery! Or perhaps, instead, a very explainable thing. We are dealing with an ancient writer, not a professional man of letters, who speaks—often for the first time—of difficult subjects, and who, moreover, is a bit whimsical <note>original: "ghiribizzoso"</note>: therefore! therefore (so most people reason), it is no wonder if he is sometimes obscure. Is it not understood? The fault lies with Da Vinci: it is natural!—and one skips to the following passage, admiring its crystalline clarity. The moral: the reader, even if reading for the purpose of culture and not mere pastime, is more hurried and indolent than one might believe.

Taking due account of the difficulties of the many and disparate subjects, the truth is that Da Vinci's prose is limpid, with a logical connection that is stringent and compelling, even where syntax is not respected. The obscure passages are such only because of our weak intellect and our scarce scientific preparation, or otherwise, they are spoiled by an unhappy transcription.

Certainly, Leonardo's prose requires in the reader, and even more in the editor, a continuous and acute reflection; this was too often lacking in Solmi, who is otherwise so deserving in other areas of Vincian studies. As for Beltrami, he trusted his predecessor too much, following him step by step in the text, and only varying the arrangement of the material. The reading <term>lezione: the specific version of a text resulting from an editor's choices</term> that he provides in the *Writings* does not result from a new application of the rules adopted by Giovanni Piumati for the publication of the *Codex Atlanticus* <note>The "Codice Atlantico" is the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings</note> and the other codices edited by him,

<summary>The author critiques previous editors of Leonardo da Vinci's notebooks, arguing that Leonardo's prose is actually logical and clear, and that perceived obscurities are usually the result of poor transcription or editorial laziness.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Edmondo Solmi, Luca Beltrami, Giovanni Piumati, philology, Codex Atlanticus, transcription errors, Italian prose</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of previous editors of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically focusing on how Beltrami's edition inherited the philological errors of Solmi's earlier work, despite some minor corrections.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>

to the other transcriptions of Vincian notes, nor from a new attempt to conform, as far as was possible, the transcriptions deriving from different standards (Preface, p. 29-30), but it is the precise confused reading, uncertain due to a lack of guiding principles, given by Solmi in the *Fragments* <note>original: "Frammenti"</note>.

Beltrami noticed some massive inaccuracies and errors, such as, for example, in the famous passage on anatomy, in which Da Vinci enumerates the obstacles that easily oppose such a study, correcting: "squared dead" <note>original: "morti squadrati"</note> to: "quartered dead" <note>original: "morti squartati"</note>; but—unfortunately—the new collector overlooked too many passages spoiled by Solmi, failing to notice the blunders <note>original: "strafalcioni"</note> and making them his own. I content myself with a few examples, but such as to prove my assertion to excess. They require no comment, or, to put it better, sufficient comment will be the exact Vincian text that I place opposite them.

|---|---|
| ..... there were found underground and beneath the deep excavations of the stone slabs, the worked wood of beams, already turned black, which were found in my time in the area of Castel Fiorentino; and these, in such a deep place, were there before the silt <note>original: "litta"</note>, thrown by the Arno into the sea, which covered that place, was abandoned at such a height, and before the plains of Casentino were so lowered by the earth that they have continually cleared from there. | There are found underground and beneath the deep excavations of the stone slabs, the worked wood of beams, already turned black, which were found in my time in the area of Castel Fiorentino; and these in such a place sank <note>original: "profondorono"</note> before the silt <note>original: "lita"</note>, thrown by the Arno into the sea which covered that place, had flooded <note>original: "abondata"</note> to such a height, and before the plains of Casentino were so lowered by the earth that the Arno continually clears from there. |

In the letter regarding the fantastical giant who with kicks "threw men through the air, who fell upon the other men no differently than if it had been a thick hail":

<note>The author is highlighting how Beltrami's edition repeated the transcription errors of Solmi, such as "interi" (whole) instead of "in terra" (to the ground), which significantly alters Leonardo's visceral descriptions of the Deluge and other scenes.</note>

<meta>Continuing the critique of editorial errors in Leonardo da Vinci's transcriptions, moving from textual omissions to the disorganized fragmentation of his longer prose passages by editors Solmi and Beltrami.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>

He did not avoid, however, thrusting into the very middle of the description of the Deluge a passage on the motion of dust stirred by a galloping horse (!), a passage that Richter had indeed transcribed in sequence, but while noting that it formed a separate section. <note>original: "moto della polvere mossa dal corso del cavallo"; the author is criticizing the lack of thematic consistency in the edited versions of Leonardo's notes.</note> (Solmi, p. 318; Beltrami, p. 152-53; Richter par. 327).

It is curious, then, to see the even further fracturing of Leonardo's already fragmentary prose, upsetting its order from beginning to end: to what profit? to what utility? Already so rare is the pleasure of having a long passage by Da Vinci! I say pleasure because when writing at length, He—something He does not usually do—grows heated and becomes eloquent.

I speak of the pages against the interrupters and abbreviators of anatomical sections, pages among the most beautiful by Leonardo. The Leonardesque text <note>original: "testo vinciano"</note> (Anatomical Notebooks <note>original: "Quad. Anat."</note> edited by Vangensten, Christiania, II, fol. 14 r) had already been broken up by Solmi, in order to follow a rather capricious division of the subject matter, into three fragments (the 1st on page 101, the 2nd on page 219, the 3rd on page 228, but preceded by and merged with another fragment—Anatomical Notebooks I, 42 r—which has absolutely nothing to do with it). Beltrami, not content with this slaughter, divides it into five incomplete fragments arranged in his own way: in fact, the first paragraph of page 38 is the third in the exact order, so that it begins with a "but," which seems to refer to the preceding passage which is, conversely, entirely foreign in subject matter; the last should be the first, the second would remain the second... if only the order of the passages between which it is placed were overturned. Then an entire section is skipped, and on page 96 another incomplete "stew" <note>original: "spezzatino," referring to a chopped-up or mangled text.</note> is calmly presented, also unfinished, and capped with two other passages of different origin from the manuscripts, of which the second has not the slightest relation to either what precedes or follows it.

It stands there only because in Solmi’s collection (on the aforementioned page 228) it exists... and God only knows why! Beltrami, in copying, has—naturally—also copied a blunder, and a very large one at that. Here it is: "And in this case I know that I will acquire NOT <note>The author emphasizes the error in the transcription where a negative was likely misplaced or added.</note> few enemies, since no one will believe that I can speak of him...."

<meta>Continuing the critique of editorial fragmentation in Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically addressing how editors like Solmi and Beltrami have separated connected passages across different pages of their editions.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

Furthermore:

There is a passage on page 114 of Solmi’s edition that begins: "Example of the lightning among the clouds" and continues by speaking... of a colossal fossil! <note>Leonardo often juxtaposed atmospheric observations with geological ones.</note> Except that this beautiful passage is interrupted: you must search for its continuation on page 148! And this single mutilated fragment was reported by Beltrami (p. 86). Having piously reconstructed it for the admiration of readers, I have followed it with the three versions from the <term>Codice Atlantico</term> <note>The "Atlantic Codex," the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings.</note> which evidently reconnect with it.

Here I stop speaking, not because the subject matter is lacking, but because it seems to me I have said enough.

I will add only something regarding the guiding criteria of this new selection of Leonardesque passages.

While the previous small volumes and even the costly <term>zibaldone</term> <note>A "miscellany" or "commonplace book" containing a chaotic collection of thoughts.</note> by Richter <gloss>1</gloss> attempt to organize fragments to give the public a pale idea of Leonardo's immense knowledge, this collection has a different purpose: that of providing the essential lines of Leonardesque prose, distinguished not only for its strength of thought, but for a meditated artistic consciousness and a native power of eloquence.

This collection, which may be improved in successive editions following the lead of the much-invoked <term>Edizione Nazionale</term> <note>The "National Edition," a projected definitive scholarly publication of all Leonardo's works.</note>, aims not only to remove from Italy the shame of circulating, under Leonardo's name, sentences in which only common sense is lacking (a small thing!), but to point out for the reader's admiration and study the most beautiful and original passages of Leonardesque prose. This prose will remain, certainly, as a monument to his scientific knowledge—marvelous for those times—and as interesting testimony of his life as a painter, but also and more so as one of the most beautiful examples of prose, alongside the prose of <term>The Prince</term> <note>Machiavelli’s famous political treatise, cited here as a standard of Italian literary excellence.</note>.

To the historians of science belongs the task of studying in Da Vinci's writings to what extent his thought preceded modern scientific thought; to the historians of the rules of figurative arts, the evaluation of what place

<meta>Continuing the discussion on the editorial criteria for this collection of Leonardo da Vinci's writings, specifically focusing on the literary and linguistic choices made by the editor to preserve the author's voice while ensuring readability.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

which the *Treatise on Painting* <note>original: "Trattato della Pittura"; Leonardo's influential collection of notes on art theory and practice.</note> occupies within it; here it is not important to provide the most significant passages from those points of view, but rather those in which scientific observation or pictorial precepts are artistically expressed.

---

*Note.* — While seeking to connect different fragments, I have not dared, as my predecessors did, to merge them; instead, I have separated each one with a small dash. I do not include the citation of the codex from which each passage was taken at every step, so as not to burden the printing and, even more, the reading with a heavy encumbrance of citations.

I have maintained Leonardo’s orthography <note>The specific spelling and writing style used by Leonardo.</note> only insofar as it resonates in the pronunciation, so as not to spoil the fifteenth-century and Florentine "patina," but not, for example, in the capricious separation or joining of words, or in the Latinate use of the letter *h*, etc. Unfortunately, this spelling is not uniform: first, because Leonardo himself varied it frequently; second, because the passages derive from different transcriptions, both ancient and modern, conducted according to various criteria. In any case, I believe I have prudently avoided jarring discrepancies: thus, for example, in the passages taken from Ludwig <note>Heinrich Ludwig, a 19th-century editor of the Treatise on Painting.</note>, I have removed *et* <note>Latin for "and."</note>, replacing it with *e*, as found in other transcriptions.

I have taken great care with the punctuation, sometimes changing it entirely from that of my predecessors, persuaded that removing or placing a single comma can often illuminate the text better than long notes.

The few square brackets indicate necessary emendations because the text was corrupted at that point.

The various ellipses mark the location of words or phrases omitted because they were erroneous in the transcriptions or indecipherable in the manuscripts, or out of a duty of honesty, as this book is intended for use in schools.

<summary>The editor explains her methodology for selecting Leonardo's writings based on their artistic eloquence and details her linguistic choices, such as modernizing punctuation while preserving the 15th-century Florentine flavor.</summary>

# LEONARDO AND NATURE

The tempestuous sea does not make so great a roar when the northern wind <note>original: "aquilone"; a classical term for the North Wind.</note> strikes it back with foaming waves between Scylla and Charybdis <note>The famous whirlpool and rock monster from Greek mythology, located in the Strait of Messina.</note>; nor do Stromboli or Mount Etna <note>original: "Mongibello"; the local Sicilian name for Mount Etna, derived from the Arabic 'Jabal' and Latin 'Mons', both meaning mountain.</note> when the sulfurous flames, breaking and opening the great mountain by force, hurl stones and earth through the air together with the exited and vomited flame; nor when the fiery caverns of Mount Etna, vomiting back the ill-contained element, pushing it toward its own region <note>Alludes to the "region of fire," placed by the ancients between the sphere of the air and the moon.</note>, furiously drive before them whatever obstacle stands in the way of its impetuous fury. And drawn by my eager desire, wandering to see the great mixture of the various and strange forms made by artful nature, having roamed for a while among the shadowy rocks, I arrived at the en—

---

<margin>Leonardo da Vinci, Nature, Scylla and Charybdis, Stromboli, Mount Etna, region of fire</margin>

<summary>Leonardo describes the terrifying power of natural forces like the sea and volcanoes, comparing them to his own driven curiosity to explore the mysteries of the natural world. This passage serves as a vivid example of his literary style and his empirical fascination with "artful nature."</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, nature, volcanoes, Mount Etna, Scylla and Charybdis, elements, scientific observation, curiosity</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo describes his journey through a rocky landscape, arriving at the entrance of a dark cave.</meta>

—trance of a great cavern. Standing before it for some time, stunned and ignorant of such a place, I arched my back and rested my tired hand upon my knee, and with my right hand I shaded my lowered and narrowed eyes. And frequently bending this way and that to see if I could discern anything within, I was prevented by the great darkness that was inside. After staying there for a while, two things suddenly awoke within me: fear and desire; fear of the threatening, dark cave, and desire to see if there were any miraculous thing within.

## Against the principle of authority
## and against the Humanists.

I know well that, because I am not a man of letters, some presumptuous person may think they can reasonably blame me by alleging that I am an <term>unlettered man</term> <note>original: "omo sanza lettere"; Leonardo’s famous self-description, meaning he lacked a formal classical education in Latin and Greek.</note>. Foolish folk! They do not know that I could respond as Marius did against the Roman patricians, saying: those who deck themselves out in the labors of others will not allow me my own. They will say that, because I lack book-learning, I cannot properly express what I wish to treat.

Now, do they not know that my subjects are to be drawn from <term>experience</term> <note>original: "sperienzia"; for Leonardo, direct observation and physical testing were the only true sources of knowledge.</note> rather than from the words of others? Experience was the mistress of those who wrote well, and so I take her as my mistress, and I shall cite her in all cases.

<margin>Leonardo da Vinci, unlettered man, experience, authority, Humanists, Marius, Roman patricians</margin>

<summary>Leonardo describes his internal conflict of fear and curiosity at the mouth of a dark cave before launching a defense of his empirical methods against critics who value classical book-learning over direct experience.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, cave, curiosity, empiricism, experience, Humanism, authority, scientific method</keywords>

Even if, like them, I did not know how to cite au-

These men go about puffed up and pompous, dressed and

and they will not grant me my own. And if they 
despise me as an inventor, how much more should they—
who are not inventors, but merely trumpeters and 
reciters of the works of others—be blamed.

---

Those men who are inventors and interpreters between 
nature and humanity—in comparison to the reciters 
and trumpeters of the works of others—are to be 
judged and esteemed no differently than the 
relationship between an object outside of a mirror 
and the image of that object appearing within the 
mirror; for the one is something in itself, and 
the other is nothing. These are people who owe little 
to nature, for they are only dressed in accidental 
trappings <gloss>1</gloss>, and without these, I could 
place them among the herds of beasts.

### In Praise of Experience.

Experience does not err; it is only our 
judgments that err by promising themselves things 
from her that are not in her power.

Wrongly do men complain of experience, 
accusing her with great reproaches of being 
fallacious. But let them leave experience alone, and turn

Wrongly do men complain of innocent experience, often accusing her of fallacy and of lying demonstrations.

->O seeker of things<- <note>original: "speculatore delle cose." Leonardo uses this to describe a scientific observer or philosopher of nature.</note>, do not pride yourself on knowing the things that nature, by her own ordinary processes, brings about. But rejoice in knowing the purpose of those things which are designed by your own mind.

### True Science.

No human investigation can be called true science if it does not pass through mathematical demonstrations; and if you should say that the sciences which begin and end in the mind have truth, this is not granted, but rather denied for many reasons—firstly, because in such mental discourses, experience does not occur, and without experience, nothing provides certainty of itself ¹.

---

<note>¹ This appears to be written specifically to oppose the opinion of Pico della Mirandola, the spokesperson for the Florentine Platonists, who claimed: "Mathematics are not true sciences." Leonardo argues the opposite: that without the grounding of physical experience and mathematical proof, thought remains speculative and uncertain.</note>

<vocab>True science, experience, mathematical demonstrations, Pico della Mirandola, seeker/observer, science</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo defends "experience" against those who call it deceptive, arguing that human error—not nature—is to blame for false expectations. He asserts that true science must be grounded in both mathematical proof and physical experience to achieve certainty.</summary>
<keywords>experience, mathematical demonstration, scientific method, Pico della Mirandola, certainty, nature vs. judgment</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of empirical knowledge and painting as a science, contrasting certain mathematical truths with the endless disputes of purely speculative philosophy.</meta>

to those senses, such as the essence <note>original: "assenzia," a variant of "essenza"</note> of God and of the soul and similar matters, over which there is always dispute and contention. And truly it happens that wherever reason is lacking, shouting takes its place; this does not happen with certainties.

    For this reason, where there is shouting, there is no true science, because truth has a single conclusion. Once this is made public, litigation is destroyed forever, and if litigation arises again, it is a lying and confused science, and not a reborn certainty.

    But true sciences are those which experience <note>term: "sperienzia" (experience/experiment) — the foundation of the scientific method involving sensory observation</note> has caused to penetrate through the senses, imposing silence on the tongues of litigants. These do not feed their investigators on dreams, but always proceed from the first true and known principles, moving successively and with true consequences until the end. This is seen in the primary mathematics—that is, number and measure, called arithmetic and geometry—which treat with supreme truth both discontinuous and continuous quantity. Here, one will not argue <note>original: "arguirà," meaning to dispute or debate</note> that two times three makes more or less than six, nor that a triangle has angles smaller than two right angles; rather, with eternal silence, every argument is destroyed, and these truths are enjoyed in peace by their devotees. This cannot be done by the lying mental sciences. 

    And if you should say that such true and known sciences are a species of the "mechanical" <note>term: "meccaniche" (mechanical arts) — in the Renaissance, these were often looked down upon as manual labor compared to the "liberal arts" of the mind</note> because they cannot be completed except manually, I will say the same of all the arts that pass through the hands of writers, which are a species of drawing <note>term: "disegno" (drawing/design) — the fundamental skill of both writing and painting</note>,

<summary>The author argues that true science must be based on sensory experience and mathematical certainty to avoid the endless, noisy disputes found in speculative philosophy. He defends "mechanical" or manual sciences, noting that even writing is a form of manual drawing.</summary>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that painting is a science. The previous page established that true sciences are based on sensory experience and mathematical certainty, contrasting them with speculative "mental" sciences that lead only to shouting and dispute.</meta>

part of painting, and astrology and the others pass through manual operations, but first they are mental, just as painting is, which exists first in the mind of its observer <note>original: "speculatore" — one who contemplates or investigates</note> and cannot reach its perfection without manual operation. Regarding this painting, its scientific and true principles first establish what a shaded body is, and what primary shadow and derivative shadow are <note>term: "ombra primitiva e ombra derivativa" — primary shadow is the shadow on the object itself; derivative shadow is the shadow cast by the object onto another surface</note>, and what light is (that is: darkness, light, color), body, figure, location, distance, proximity, motion, and rest. These are understood by the mind alone without manual work. And this shall be the science of painting, which remains in the mind of those who contemplate it, from which the operation is then born, which is far more worthy than the aforementioned contemplation or science.

## **Against alchemy and necromancy.**

Consider well how, by means of the motion of the tongue, with the help of the lips and teeth, the pronunciation of all the names of things known to us, and the simple and compound words of a language, reach our ears through such an instrument. If all the effects of nature had names, they would extend toward infinity, together with the infinite things that are in act <note>term: "in atto" — actually existing</note> and that are in the potentiality of nature. And these would not be expressed in a single language, but rather in many, which also extend toward infinity, because they continually vary from century to century and from country to country, through the mixing of peoples who, by wars

<summary>Leonardo defines the "science of painting" as a mental discipline based on optical principles like light and shadow, while beginning a critique of how language and pseudo-sciences like alchemy fail to capture nature's infinity.</summary>
<keywords>science of painting, primary shadow, derivative shadow, optics, linguistics, alchemy, necromancy, potentiality and act</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s argument regarding the limits of human creation versus nature. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how languages vary infinitely across time and geography.</meta>

...or other accidents are continually mixed in; and these same languages are subject to oblivion, and are mortal like all other created things; and if we were to concede that our world is eternal, we would say that such languages have been, and must yet be, of infinite variety, by means of the infinite centuries contained within infinite time <note>original: "infinito tempo" — Leonardo reflects on the vastness of geological and human time</note>.

And this is not in any other sense, because it only extends to the things that nature continually produces, which does not vary the ordinary species of the things created by her, in the way that things created by man vary from time to time. Man is the greatest instrument of nature, because nature only extends to the production of simple things <note>term: "semplici" — basic elements or primary substances not yet combined</note>. But man, with such simples, produces infinite compounds, yet he has no power to create any simple thing, except for another like himself, that is: his children. 

And of this, the old alchemists <note>original: "archimisti"</note> shall be my witnesses, who never—either by chance or by deliberate experiment—succeeded in creating the smallest thing that can be created by nature itself. And this generation <note>i.e., the alchemists</note> deserves infinite praise <note>original: "lalde"</note> because of the utility of the things they have found for the use of men; and they would deserve even more, if they had not been inventors of harmful things, like poisons and other similar ruins of life or of the mind. From which they themselves are not exempt, seeing as, with great...

<margin>Admire the confidence and the exactness of the linguistic intuition, and at the same time the correspondence of the complex but singular sentence to the complex but singular thought.</margin>

<margin>Leonardo here sees chemistry being born from alchemy.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo argues that while human language and inventions are mortal and ever-changing, nature's fundamental elements (simples) remain constant. He praises alchemists for their practical discoveries (the birth of chemistry) but critiques their inability to create life or primary substances.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of language, nature vs. art, alchemy, chemistry, creation of life, human invention, mortality of language</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s critique of alchemy, transitioning from the previous page’s discussion on the limitations of human invention compared to the primary creations of nature.</meta>

...study and practice, they wish to create not the least noble production of nature, but the most excellent: that is, gold, the true son of the sun, because it resembles him more than any other creature, and no created thing is more eternal than this gold. It is exempt from the destruction of fire, which extends to all other created things, reducing them to ash, glass, or smoke. And if foolish avarice leads you into such error, why do you not go to the mines where nature generates such gold? There, make yourself her disciple, and she will faithfully cure you of your foolishness by showing you how nothing you do with fire will be among those things that nature employs in generating that gold. There is no quicksilver <note>original: "argento vivo" — mercury</note>, no sulfur of any kind, no fire, nor any other heat than that of the nature which gives life to our world. She will show you the branchings of gold spread through the stone <note>original: "lapis"</note> or ultramarine blue, which is a color exempt from the power of fire. Consider well such branchings of gold, and you will see that their extremities, with slow motion, continually grow and convert into gold whatever those extremities touch; and note that there is a vegetative soul <note>term: "anima vigitativa" — the biological force of growth and reproduction</note> there, which is not in your power to generate.

---

<margin>1. He implies: lazuli, or lapis lazuli, a blue precious stone, mostly scattered with veins of gold. From it is made ultramarine blue, used for painting in oil, fresco, and tempera.</margin>
<margin>2. Leonardo gives gold here a fantastic power of development similar to that of plants, poetically imagining that nature can create simple substances through a mysterious generative force that escapes human research.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo argues that alchemists cannot replicate the "vegetative soul" of nature, using the natural growth of gold in lapis lazuli as evidence of a divine generative process that fire and chemistry cannot mimic.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s critique of pseudo-sciences, moving from the previous page's discussion on the impossibility of creating gold through alchemy to a scathing dismissal of necromancy and magic.</meta>

# Page 72

But of all human discourses, that which extends to the belief in **necromancy** <note>term: "negromanzia" — here referring generally to black magic or the conjuring of spirits</note> must be judged the most foolish. It is the sister of **alchemy** <note>original: "archimia"</note>, which gives birth to simple, natural things <insert>1</insert>. Yet necromancy is so much more worthy of condemnation than alchemy, insofar as it produces nothing but things like itself—namely, lies. This does not happen in alchemy, which serves as the administrator of the simple products of nature. This office cannot be performed by nature herself, because she lacks the organic instruments with which she might operate as man does by means of his hands, who in such work has created vessels and glassware <insert>2</insert>, etc.

But this necromancy is a standard or a flying banner moved by the wind, a guide for the foolish multitude, which continually bears witness with its barking to the infinite effects of such an art. They have filled books with it, asserting that incantations and spirits can act and speak without a tongue, and speak without organic instruments—without which speech is impossible—and that they can carry very heavy weights, cause storms and rain, and that men are converted into cats, wolves, and other beasts, though those who affirm such things enter into the state of a beast first.

And certainly, if such necromancy truly existed <insert>3</insert>, as is believed by low intelligences, there is nothing else on earth that would be of such great potency for both the harm and service of man. For if it were true that in such an art there resided the power to disturb the tranquil serenity of the air, converting it into the aspect of night, and to create lightning <note>original: "corruscazioni"</note> and winds with terrifying thunders and—

---

<insert>1. Which would like to produce simple substances.</insert>
<insert>2. Glassware.</insert>
<insert>3. If it truly existed.</insert>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci delivers a sharp critique of necromancy, labeling it a fraudulent "sister" to alchemy that relies on impossible claims like spirits speaking without organs or humans transforming into animals. He argues that while alchemy at least works with natural materials, necromancy is merely a collection of lies that appeals to the "foolish multitude."</summary>
<keywords>necromancy, alchemy, nature, organic instruments, magic, superstition, atmospheric phenomena, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

## On Physiognomy and Palmistry

Regarding the fallacious arts of Physiognomy <note>term: "Fisionomia" — the practice of judging character from facial features</note> and Palmistry <note>term: "Chiromanzia" — the art of reading palms</note>, I shall not restrain myself [from speaking], because there is no truth in them; and this is manifest because such chimeras have no scientific foundations.

It is true, however, that the marks of the face show in part the nature of men, their vices and temperaments <note>original: "complessioni"</note>. In the face:

a) The lines that separate the cheeks from the lips of the mouth, and the nostrils of the nose, and the sockets of the eyes, are prominent in men who are cheerful and often laughing; and those who have them little marked are men given to deep thought <note>original: "cogitazione"</note>.

b) And those who have the parts of the face with great relief and depth are bestial and wrathful men, possessing little reason.

c) And those who have the lines placed between the eyebrows very prominent are wrathful.

d) And those who have the transverse lines of the forehead strongly marked are men full of lamentations, whether hidden or overt.

---

<note>1 "Nature abhors a vacuum," said the ancients and Leonardo with them, but such a belief, as everyone knows, collapsed after the Galilean studies. Leonardo uses this repeatedly to demonstrate that spirits cannot have a dwelling place among the elements of our world.</note>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his argument against spirits by citing the impossibility of a vacuum, then shifts to debunking physiognomy and palmistry while acknowledging that facial lines can reflect habitual emotions.</summary>
<keywords>vacuum, incorporeal spirits, physiognomy, palmistry, facial expressions, temperament, anatomy of the face</keywords>

And so it can be said of many parts [of the body].
But what of the hand? You will find that vast armies have died by the sword in the same hour, yet no sign upon their hands was similar to another; and the same is seen in a shipwreck.

### Science and Practice

Science is the captain, and practice are the soldiers.

### Principle of the Indestructibility of Matter

->(*Anaxagoras*)<- <note>Anaxagoras was a Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who argued that nothing is born or dies, but is instead composed of existing things.</note>

Every thing comes from every thing, and from every thing every thing is made, and every thing returns into every thing, because that which is in the elements is made from those very elements.

### On Necessity

O admirable and stupendous Necessity, you constrain by your law all effects to participate in their causes by the shortest path; these are the miracles!

---

Necessity is the teacher and guardian of nature.
Necessity is the theme and inventor of nature, its curb and its eternal rule.

<note>original: "Anassagora, scienza, pratica, materia, elementi, necessità, natura, miracoli"</note>

## Nature does not break her law.

---

Nature is constrained by the reason of her law, which lives infused within her.

## **The mind in the universe.**

When earth strikes against earth, compressing it, the part that is struck moves very little. <note>Leonardo is comparing the "conductivity" or speed of impact across different substances.</note>
Water struck by water forms circles around the point of impact;
The voice travels a long distance through the air; longer still through fire;
But the mind travels further through the universe; yet, because the mind is finite, it does not extend into the infinite. <note>Leonardo suggests that while thought is the fastest and most expansive force, it remains bound by the limits of human perception and cannot fully grasp the concept of infinity.</note>

---

# NATURE

## Gravity - Force - Motion.

Gravity, force, and accidental motion <note>In Aristotelian physics, "accidental" refers to a property that is not essential to a thing's nature, such as an object being moved by an external force.</note>, together with percussion <note>The impact or striking of one body against another.</note>, are the four accidental powers with which all the evident works of mortals have their being and their death.

Motion is the cause of all life.

I find force to be infinite together with time <gloss>¹</gloss>, and weight to be finite together with the weight of the entire globe of the terrestrial machine <note>Leonardo often refers to the Earth or the universe as a "machine" to describe its complex, interconnected physical systems.</note>.

---

Force is nothing other than a spiritual virtue, an invisible power, which is created and infused by

---

<note>¹ Thus Leonardo intuited one of the great laws of theoretical physics: the principle of the conservation of energy.</note>

<note>original: "Leonardo da Vinci, fisica teoretica, principio della conservazione dell'energia, gravità, forza, moto, percussione, potenzie accidentali, terrestre macchina"</note>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci defines the four fundamental "accidental powers"—gravity, force, motion, and percussion—that govern the physical world and the life cycle of all human works. He reflects on the infinite nature of force and time compared to the finite weight of the Earth.</summary>

accidental violence, from sensible bodies into the insensible, giving to these bodies a semblance of life; which life is of marvelous operation, constraining and changing the position and form of all created things, running with fury toward its own destruction, and diversifying itself according to its causes.

Slowness makes it great and it dies through liberty.
It lives by violence and dies through liberty.
It transmutes and constrains every body to a change of position and form.
Great power gives it a desire for death.
It drives away with fury whatever opposes its ruin.
Transmuter of various forms.
It always lives with the discomfort of that which holds it.
It always opposes natural desires.
From a small beginning with slowness it amplifies itself, and becomes a thing of horrible and marvelous power.
And by constraining itself, it constrains everything else <gloss>¹</gloss>.

## Flame.

Look at the light and consider its beauty. Blink your eye and look at it again: that which you see of it now was not there before, and that which was there of it no longer exists.

## What water is.

Water is, among the four elements, the second least heavy and of the second greatest volatility; it never finds rest until it joins with its maritime element <note>The ocean, considered the natural home of water in the Aristotelian system.</note>, where, if not disturbed by winds, it establishes itself and rests with its surface equidistant from the center of the World. This is the increase and the humor <note>The vital fluid or moisture.</note> of all living bodies. No sublunary thing <note>Anything existing in the realm below the moon, i.e., the Earth.</note> retains its primary figure and form without it.

It connects and increases bodies, and gives them growth. Nothing lighter than itself can penetrate it without violence. It willingly rises through heat into thin vapor through the air. Cold congeals it; stagnation corrupts it; it takes on every odor, color, and flavor, and of itself has no flavor, nor color, nor odor.

It penetrates all porous bodies. Against its fury, no human defense is of use, and if it is of use, it shall not be permanent. In its swift course, it becomes a supporter of things heavier than itself. It can, with motion and leaping, raise itself on high. When it falls, it submerges with it in its ruins things lighter than itself.

It has the mastery of its course <margin>The maximum force.</margin> sometimes in the middle, sometimes at the bottom, sometimes at the top.

---

<vocab>water, elements, volatility, sublunary, surface, mastery, vapor</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his list of hydrological terms and provides a scientific definition of water as a vital, transformative element that sustains life but possesses destructive power.</summary>
<keywords>water, four elements, hydrology, physics of fluids, sublunary realm, erosion, vital moisture</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical properties and destructive power of water to a philosophical comparison between the circulation of water in the Earth and the circulation of blood in living beings.</meta>

One quantity of water surmounts the cross-current of another.... Every small obstacle, whether in the embankment or on the bottom, will cause ruin to the opposite embankment or bottom. Low water causes more damage to the bank in its course than it does when it runs full. Its parts weigh nothing upon the parts beneath them, and its upper parts do not give weight <note>original: "granella" - likely a transcription error for "gravella" or "gravità" (gravity/weight)</note> to the lower ones.

## The office of water in terrestrial life.

That same cause which moves the humors <note>The vital fluids, such as blood or sap.</note> in all species of animated bodies against the natural course of their gravity, is precisely that which moves the water through the terrestrial veins, enclosed within them, and distributes it through subtle passages. And just as the blood rises from below and pours out through the broken veins of the forehead, and as water rises from the lowest part of the vine to its severed branches, so from the lowest depth of the sea, water rises to the summits of the mountains. There, finding its veins broken, it falls through them and returns to the low sea. Thus, it goes varying within and without, sometimes rising with accidental motion <note>Motion caused by an external force, like heat or pressure.</note>, and sometimes descending with natural, free motion. Thus, joined together in a continuous revolution, it goes circling around. Thus, flowing here, there, up, and down, no quiet ever rests it—not only in its course, but in its very nature ¹; it has nothing of itself, but takes on everything.

---

¹ These words echo the famous verses of Dante:

> Here, there, down, up, it leads them;
> no hope ever comforts them,
> not of rest, but of lesser pain.
<note>original: "Di qua, di là, di giù, di su li mena; / nulla speranza li conforta mai, / non che di posa, ma di minor pena."</note>

->*Inferno V, 43-45.*<-

<term>humors (omori)</term>: In Renaissance science, these were the fluids that maintained life; Leonardo here draws a direct parallel between the "microcosm" of the human body and the "macrocosm" of the Earth.

<summary>Leonardo explains the hydrological cycle by comparing the Earth to a living organism, where water circulates like blood through veins. He concludes with a poetic reflection on water's restless nature, referencing Dante's Inferno.</summary>

<keywords>hydrology, macrocosm and microcosm, blood circulation, Dante Alighieri, mountain springs, humors, restless motion</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of water’s transformative nature, comparing its adaptability to a mirror, and transitions into a discourse on the destructive power of floods compared to fire.</meta>

and it transmutes into as many various natures as there are various places through which it passes; acting exactly like a **mirror**, which takes into itself as many images as there are things passing before it. So this [water] is always changing—sometimes in position, and sometimes in color; sometimes it takes in new odors and flavors, sometimes it retains new substances or qualities, and sometimes it proves itself to be either deadly or life-giving. At times it mixes with the air, or allows itself to be drawn upward by heat, and when <gloss>sometimes</gloss> it reaches the cold region, the heat that was its guide is there constricted. And just as a hand squeezes a sponge underwater, so that the water escaping from it creates a wave among the other water, so does the air that was mixed with the water; when that air is squeezed by the cold, it flees with fury and drives away the other air: thus, this is the cause of the **wind**.

## The ruins caused by water.

Among the harmful causes <gloss>the causes of damage</gloss> to human property, it seems to me that **rivers**, with their excessive and impetuous floods, hold the principal place. 
And if anyone should wish to place **fire** before the fury of ruinous rivers, it would seem to me that such people lack good judgment; for fire remains confined and dies where it lacks nutri- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "nutrimento" (nourishment/fuel).</note>

<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 84, finishing the comparison between fire and water before launching into a vivid description of the catastrophic power of floods and the atmospheric effects of wind over the Arno river.</meta>

-ment; but against the irreparable inundation of swollen and proud rivers, no human defense or human counsel is of any avail. On the contrary, in company with the turbulent and tumultuous waves, gnawing at and ruining the high banks and becoming turbid with the soil of cultivated estates, ruining houses there, uprooting tall trees, it carries them as prey to the sea, its place of rest, bearing with it men, plants, beasts, villages, and possessions, ruining every embankment and all other defenses. It carries light things with it, and the heavy ones it ruins and destroys, making great chasms out of small fissures, filling the low valleys with deluges, and plunging down with troublesome and rigid waters.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How much one must flee such a neighbor! Oh, how many cities, how many lands, castles, villages, and houses has it consumed! Oh, how many labors of troubled husbandmen <note>cultivators/farmers</note> have been made vain and without fruit! Oh, how many families has it undone and submerged! What shall I say of the herds drowned and lost?

### The wind and the sandbank of the Arno.

¹ It is implied: "I will say" or "I will describe": <note>original: "Sottintende: dirò"</note>

## Clouds over Lake Maggiore.

... already above Milan, toward Lake Maggiore, I saw a cloud in the shape of a vast mountain, full of glowing crags, because the rays of the sun—which was already at the reddening horizon—tinted it with its color; and this great cloud attracted to itself all the small clouds that were around it; and the large cloud did not move from its place, but rather retained the light of the sun upon its summit until an hour and a half into the night, so immense was its greatness; and within two hours of the night <margin>¹</margin> it generated such a great wind that it was a stupendous, unheard-of thing; and this happened during its closing-in, for the air that was enclosed within it, being squeezed by the condensation of the cloud, broke out and fled through the weakest parts, rushing through the air with frequent tumult, acting in the likeness of a sponge squeezed by a hand under water, from which the water it was soaked with flees between the fingers of the hand that presses it, escaping with impetus into the surrounding water. Thus did the cloud, driven back and restricted by the cold that clothed it, driving out the air from itself with impetus...

---

<margin>¹ The day was counted as twelve hours and the night as twelve hours; the sixth hour of the day was midday, the sixth hour of night was midnight. Therefore, one and a half hours into the night corresponds to our 7:30 PM, and two hours to 8:00 PM.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo describes a massive, stationary cloud formation over Lake Maggiore that glowed with sunset light and eventually collapsed, triggering a violent and sudden windstorm. He uses the scientific analogy of a squeezed sponge to explain how the condensation of the cloud forced the air out at high speeds.</summary>

<keywords>atmospheric pressure, meteorology, Lake Maggiore, Milan, cloud formation, optics, sunset, wind dynamics</keywords>

## Journey to Monte Rosa.

I say that the blue in which the air reveals itself is not its own proper color, but is caused by warm moisture, evaporated into most minute and imperceptible atoms, which catches the percussion of the solar rays and becomes luminous against the obscurity of the immense darkness of the region of fire that serves as a lid above it <gloss>1</gloss>.

And this will be seen, as I saw it, by whoever goes atop Monte Rosa <note>original: "Momboso"; likely the Monte Rosa massif</note>; a ridge of the Alps that divides France from Italy. The base of this mountain gives birth to the four rivers that flow in four contrary directions through all of Europe: and no mountain has its base at such a height.

This rises to such an altitude that it almost passes all the clouds, and rarely does snow fall there, but only hail in summer, when the clouds are at their greatest height; and this hail is preserved there in such a way that, if it were not for the rarity of clouds falling or rising there—which does not happen twice in an age—there would be an enormous quantity of ice, built up from the layers of hail. Which I found to be very thick in the middle of July; and I saw the air above me to be dark; and the sun, which was striking the mountain,

---

<gloss>1</gloss> This refers to the erroneous ancient belief in the "sphere of fire," a layer of elemental fire thought to exist between the Earth's atmosphere and the moon.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci explains the blue color of the sky as a result of light hitting moisture against a dark background, citing his climb of Monte Rosa as evidence. He describes the mountain's unique climate, noting that its height reaches above the clouds where hail accumulates even in summer.</summary>

### The terrestrial organism.

Nothing is born in a place where there is no sentient, vegetable, and rational life: feathers grow upon birds and are changed every year; hairs grow upon animals and every year they are changed, except for some parts, such as the hair of the manes of lions and cats and the like; grass grows in the meadows and leaves upon the trees, and every year they are largely renewed; therefore we might say that the earth has a vegetative soul <term>vegetative soul: the lowest level of life in Aristotelian philosophy, responsible for growth and reproduction</term>, and that its flesh is the soil, its bones are the arrangements of the connections of the rocks of which the mountains are composed, its tendons are the tufa stone, its blood is the veins of the waters, the lake of blood that exists around the heart <note>Reminiscent of the "lake of the heart" in Dante: Inferno I, 20; Ballata: *Ladies, I know not*.</note> is the ocean sea, its breathing and the increase and decrease of the blood through the pulses is, in the earth, the flow and ebb of the tide, and the heat of the soul of the world is the fire which is infused throughout the earth, and the re-

### III.

I find the site of the earth to have been, from ancient times <note>original: "ab antico"</note>, entirely occupied and covered by salt waters in its plains; and the mountains, the bones of the earth, with their wide bases, penetrate and rise into the air, covered and clothed with much deep soil.

And the soil from the shores and the high peaks of the mountains has already descended to their bases, and has raised the floors of the seas which surrounded those bases, and created a visible plain, and from there, in some places, has driven away the seas for a great distance.

And the peaks of the Apennines stood in that sea in the form of islands, surrounded by salt waters; and even Africa, within its Mount At-

# On the Flood and Marine Shells.

If you should say that the shells <note>original: "nichi"</note>, which are seen in our times throughout the borders of Italy, far from the seas and at such great heights, were there because of the Flood which left them there, I answer you that—believing as you do that such a Flood rose above the highest mountain by 7 cubits, as he who measured it wrote <note>2</note>—these shells, which always stay near the shores of the sea, ought to have remained atop such mountains, and not so slightly above the roots of the mountains, nor all at the same height, layer upon layer.

---

> <note>1</note> In this passage, Da Vinci speaks of the geographical conditions of the Mediterranean region in the era that modern geology calls the <term>Tertiary: the geological period from about 66 million to 2.6 million years ago</term> or <term>Cenozoic: the current geological era, beginning 66 million years ago</term>; one cannot, however, specify which period he refers to, because the divisions and subdivisions of geological time were clarified only much later, and the facts cited here are perhaps not perfectly contemporary. It is certain that in Tertiary times the Apennines were represented only by islands, and that, also in Tertiary times, the sea extended into the current plains of Africa, though this does not mean Memphis stood on the beach, as neither Memphis nor man himself yet existed.
> <note>2</note> The ironic tone is clear! <note>Leonardo is likely mocking the literal biblical measurements of the Flood's depth.</note>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the presence of marine fossils in the mountains of Italy and the former sea-coverage of Memphis prove long-term geological shifts rather than a single, brief biblical flood. He points out that fossils appear in organized layers at the base of mountains, which contradicts the chaotic deposition expected from a sudden deluge.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Great Flood, shells, fossils, geology, Tertiary era, Memphis, Apennines, marine layers</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s systematic refutation of the Diluvial theory (the Great Flood). He is specifically addressing the physical impossibility of slow-moving shellfish traveling hundreds of miles in the short duration of the biblical flood.</meta>

And if you should say that, since such shells <note>original: "nichi"</note> are fond of staying near the seashores, and that as the water increased in height, the shells left their original site and followed the rising of the waters up to their highest point; here the answer is that the shellfish is an animal with a motion no faster than that of a snail out of water—and perhaps even slower, because it does not swim, but rather makes a furrow in the sand, supporting itself by the sides of said furrow—it will travel from 3 to 4 cubits <note>original: "braccia," a traditional unit of length roughly equal to the length of an arm</note> a day. Therefore, with such a motion, it could not have traveled from the Adriatic Sea as far as Monferrato in Lombardy, which is a distance of 250 miles, in forty days, as was said by him who kept track of that time <note>Leonardo is ironically referencing the biblical account of the 40 days of rain</note>. <margin>Again ironic!</margin> <insert>The repetition of the word "that" <note>original: "che"</note> is unnecessary here, as often happens in speech.</insert>

And if you say that the waves carried them there, they—by their own weight—cannot be supported except upon the sea floor; and if you do not grant me this, at least confess to me that they would have had to remain on the peaks of the highest mountains and in the lakes that are enclosed within the mountains: such as Lake Lario <note>modern Lake Como</note>, Lake Maggiore... and the Lake of Perugia <note>modern Lake Trasimeno</note> and similar ones.

And if you should say that the shells were carried by the waves because they were empty and dead, I say that where the dead ones went, they were moved very little from the living ones; and in these mountains are found all the living ones, which are recognized because they have their shells in pairs <note>meaning the two halves of the bivalve are still joined, indicating they died in situ and were not tossed about</note>, and they are in a layer where none of the dead ones are; and a little higher up is found where all the dead ones were thrown by the waves with their shells separated, near where the rivers used to fall.

<summary>Leonardo argues that the slow movement of shellfish proves they could not have migrated hundreds of miles during the forty days of the Flood, and notes that the presence of paired shells indicates they lived and died where they are now found.</summary>
<keywords>paleontology, fossils, shellfish, Great Flood, snail movement, Adriatic Sea, Monferrato, Lombardy, bivalves, geological layers</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s geological observations, moving from the theoretical movement of shells to a specific case study of the Arno River valley to prove that the Earth's surface was formed over vast periods of time by sedimentation.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

into the sea at great depth, just as the Arno did, which fell from the Gonfolina <note>original: "Golfolina"; a narrow gorge where the river breaks through the mountains</note> near Monte Lupo, and there it left the gravel, which can still be seen today, which has frozen together <note>meaning "cemented" or "conglomerated" into rock</note> into a single mass made of stones from various countries, natures, colors, and hardness; and a little further on, the freezing together of the sand has turned into tufa <note>a porous rock formed from compacted sand or volcanic ash</note>, where the river turned toward Castel Fiorentino; further still, the mud was discharged, in which the shells <note>original: "nichi"</note> lived, which rose in layers according to the floods that the turbid Arno poured into that sea, and from time to time, the bottom of the sea was raised; which, in stages, produced these shells, as is shown in the cutting of Colle Gonzoli, eroded by the river Arno, which wears away at its base. In this cutting, one can clearly see the aforementioned layers of shells in bluish mud, and various marine things are found there... <margin>Leonardo describes the natural sorting of river sediment: heavy gravel first, then sand, then fine mud.</margin>

---

<note>1. Modernly Gonfolina: a gorge in the final branches of the Pistoiese Sub-Apennines.</note>
<note>2. In this passage on the Arno, Da Vinci refers to a much more recent geological epoch than the one alluded to in the previous passage—namely, the end of the Pliocene <note>a period from about 5.3 to 2.5 million years ago</note>, when the Italian peninsula had almost entirely emerged. He describes the floods of the Arno very well: in fact, in the first part of their course, rivers deposit gravel (which, when cemented, produces conglomerates), further along they deposit finer materials, namely sands (which, when cemented, produce sandstones), and finally they leave only slime, silt, and mud, which is to say clays and clay-like materials. In an even more recent era, namely the Quaternary or Neozoic era (of which the present is a continuation), the Arno, by cutting into its own ancient deposits, has brought back into view the fossil-bearing clay layers it had previously sedimented.</note>

<summary>Leonardo provides a detailed geological analysis of the Arno River valley, explaining how gravel, sand, and fossil-bearing mud layers prove that the land was once a seabed shaped by gradual sedimentation rather than a single biblical flood.</summary>
