## On a colossal fossil.

### I.

   Oh, how many times were the terrified schools of dolphins and great tunas seen fleeing from your cruel fury! O you, who with the swift beating of your fins and your forked tail, lightning-like, generated a sudden tempest in the sea with great clamor and the sinking of ships with massive waves; filling the exposed shores with frightened and bewildered fish, [which], in escaping from you—remaining high and dry as the sea receded—became a superfluous and abundant prey for the neighboring peoples!
   O Time, swift predator of created things, how many kings, how many peoples have you undone, and how many mutations of states and various events have followed since the marvelous form of this fish died here within the cavernous and winding recesses <note>Leonardo likely refers to the winding valleys or the interior of the mountain where the fossil was found</note>. Now, undone by time, you lie patient in this enclosed place; with your stripped and naked bones, you have provided an armor and support for the mountain placed above you!

### II.

   Oh, how many times were you seen among the waves of the swollen and great ocean, with your bristly and black back, like a mountain, and with a heavy and proud gait!

## The end of the world’s life.

The element of water remains enclosed within the swollen banks of the rivers, and the sea is seen within the risen earth; and the surrounding air, having to wrap and circumscribe the softened machine of the earth <note>Leonardo views the Earth as a complex mechanical body or organism</note>, its thickness—which used to stand between the water and the element of fire <note>original: "elemento del foco". The sphere of fire which—as already noted—the ancients placed between the air and the moon.</note>—will remain much restricted and deprived of the necessary <note>original: "bisogniosa"</note> water. The rivers will remain without their...

<page-num>96</page-num>

...their waters; the fertile earth will no longer send forth light foliage, nor will the fields be adorned any longer by drooping plants. All the animals, finding no fresh grass to graze upon, will die; and food will fail the predatory lions and wolves and other animals that live by prey. And to men, after many attempts at survival, life must eventually fail, and the human race will perish.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In this manner the fertile and fruitful earth, abandoned, will remain arid and sterile; and because of the moisture of the water being locked away (enclosed within its womb) and because of its lively nature, it will maintain something of its growth for a time, until—once the cold and thin air has passed—it is forced to end with the element of fire. Then its surface will remain as parched ash, and this shall be the end of terrestrial nature. <note>Leonardo envisions a cosmic cycle where the Earth eventually burns up after losing its protective moisture and atmosphere.</note>

---

<margin>1. The concepts expressed here by Da Vinci are still largely acceptable and accepted. It is a well-known concept that compares the earth to a metallic sphere, hydrated and oxidized on the surface. The further hydration and oxidation proceed, the less water there will be in the seas, on the continents, and in the air, and the less oxygen there will be in the atmosphere; the extent of the continents will continue to increase. In other words, our planet will move toward a "Martian phase." The erroneous belief in the "sphere of fire" <note>A classical and medieval belief that a layer of fire existed above the atmosphere</note> then leads him to imagine that, once the air is consumed, the earth will be scorched and reduced to ash.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo describes the total collapse of the biosphere as water disappears, leading to the extinction of animals and humanity, and the ultimate destruction of the Earth by fire.</summary>

The further hydration and oxidation proceed, the less water there will be in the seas, on the surface, and in the atmosphere. Eventually, the oxygen will be entirely consumed by the oxidation of the crust, and the hydrogen will escape into space. Without an atmosphere, the Earth will be exposed to the full heat of the sun and internal fires, leading to the final "combustion" Leonardo describes.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his grim prophecy of the Earth's end, describing a world where the loss of water leads to the extinction of all life and a final destruction by fire. A marginal note connects this Renaissance intuition to modern geological theories of planetary oxidation and atmospheric loss.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Leonardo’s macrocosmic vision of the Earth’s destruction to his microcosmic study of the human body, specifically addressing the biological purpose of pain and the nature of medicine.</meta>

# THE HUMAN BODY

---

## **The function of pain in animal life.**

If nature has ordained pain in vegetative souls <note>original: "anime vigitative"; Leonardo refers to living beings capable of growth and movement</note> endowed with motion, it is for the preservation of the instruments <note>original: "strumenti"; meaning the limbs or organs</note> which might be diminished or damaged by that motion. Vegetative souls without motion <note>i.e., plants</note> do not have to travel or encounter opposing objects; therefore, pain is not necessary in plants, and thus when they are broken, they do not feel pain as animals do.

## **Medicine and illness.**

Medicine is the restoration of imbalanced elements. Illness is the discordance of elements fused within the living body. <note>Leonardo applies the classical theory of the four humors here, viewing health as a state of equilibrium.</note>

<vocab><term>pain</term> (doglia), <term>vegetative souls</term> (anime vigitative), <term>instruments</term> (strumenti), <term>restoration</term> (riparegiamento), <term>imbalanced elements</term> (disequalati elementi), <term>discordance</term> (discordanza), <term>living body</term> (vitale corpo)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that pain is a biological necessity for mobile creatures to prevent self-injury, while defining health as a balance of bodily elements and illness as their discord.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, biology, pain, medicine, elements, vegetative soul, physiology</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s philosophical defense of rigorous scientific study, moving from the definition of health to a sharp critique of those who seek shortcuts in the study of anatomy.</meta>

## Against those who interrupt cadaveric dissections.

Those who abbreviate <note>original: "abbreviatori"; those who summarize or condense complex works</note> works do an injury to both knowledge and love; for the love of anything is the daughter of that very knowledge, and love is more fervent as knowledge becomes more certain. This certainty is born from the integral knowledge of all those parts which, when united together, compose the whole of those things which ought to be loved.

What value is there for the man who, in order to abbreviate the parts of those things of which he professes to give complete information, leaves behind the greater part of the things of which the whole is composed?

It is true that impatience, the mother of foolishness, is what praises brevity; as if such people did not have enough of a lifespan to allow them to have a complete knowledge of a single particular, such as the human body! And then they wish to embrace the mind of God, in which the universe is included, weighing and mincing that mind into infinite parts, as if they had to <term>anatomize</term> <note>original: "notomizzare"; to dissect or perform an autopsy</note> it.

O human foolishness! Do you not perceive that you have been with yourself all your life, and you still do not have knowledge of that thing which you possess most, namely your own madness? And then you wish, with a multitude of sophists, to deceive yourself and others, despising the mathematical sciences, in which is contained the true knowledge of the things they encompass; and then you wish to wander into miracles and write and give information of those...

<summary>Leonardo criticizes those who use summaries to avoid the hard work of detailed study, arguing that true love for a subject requires a complete understanding of all its parts, especially regarding human anatomy.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, dissection, knowledge, love, mathematical sciences, philosophy, human body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s polemic against "abbreviators" who simplify complex scientific and historical truths, transitioning from a critique of intellectual laziness to a moral condemnation of those who lack empathy for nature.</meta>

things of which the human mind is not capable, and which cannot be demonstrated by any natural example; and it seems to you that you have performed miracles when you have spoiled a work of some speculative genius; and you do not perceive that you fall into the same error as one who strips a plant of the ornament of its branches, full of leaves mingled with fragrant flowers and fruits, and demonstrates that the plant is merely fit for making bare planks.

As did Justin <note>Marcus Junianus Justinus, a 2nd-century Roman historian</note>, the abbreviator of the Histories written by Trogus Pompeius <note>original: "Troco Pompeo"; a Roman historian of the Augustan age</note> (who wrote ornately of all the excellent deeds of his ancestors, which were full of most wonderful ornaments), and thus he composed a bare thing, worthy only of impatient minds, who feel they are losing as much time as that which is usefully employed—that is, in the study of the works of nature and of human affairs.

But let such people stay in the company of beasts, and let their courtiers be dogs and other animals full of rapine, and let them keep company with them; always running after those who flee, they follow the innocent animals who, driven by hunger in times of great snows, come to your houses asking for alms as if from their protector.

And if you are, as you have written, the king of animals (though you would do better to say king of the beasts, be-

This work has been lost. Justin made a summary of each book and accompanied it with a selection of the most beautiful passages taken from the original.
</margin>

And I would say even more, if telling the whole truth were entirely permitted to me. Let us not depart from human affairs without mentioning a supreme wickedness, which does not occur among terrestrial animals; for among them, one does not find animals that eat their own species, except through a lack of brain <note>original: "celabro"; Leonardo implies a physiological defect or madness</note> (for among them there are madmen, as among men, though they are not so numerous). And if anyone is found among them who is virtuous and good, do not drive them away from you, but do them honor, so that they do not have

These are the ones who deserve statues, images <note>original: "simulacri"</note>, and honors from us. But I well remind you that their images should not be eaten by you, as happens in some regions of India; for when their images perform some miracle (according to them), the priests cut them into pieces—since they are made of wood—and give them to all the people of the country, and not without a fee <note>original: "premio"; Leonardo notes this is a paid transaction</note>. Each person finely scrapes their portion and puts it upon the first food they eat; and thus they hold by faith that they have eaten their saint, and believe that he will then guard them from all dangers. What do you think, O man, of your species here? Are you as wise as you hold yourself to be? Are these things to be done by men?

### **He who does not value life does not deserve it.**

And you, O man, who considers in this labor of mine the marvelous works of nature, if you judge it a nefarious thing to destroy her, then consider it a most nefarious thing to take the life of a man. If this composition of his seems to you a work of marvelous craftsmanship <note>term: "artifizio" (craftsmanship); the intricate mechanical and biological design of the body</note>, consider that this is nothing compared to the soul that dwells within such architecture; and truly, whatever that soul may be, it is a divine thing. So let it dwell—

<summary>Leonardo condemns the superstitious practice of consuming sacred wooden relics in India and transitions into a profound philosophical defense of the sanctity of human life and the soul.</summary>

to dwell in its work at its own pleasure, and do not let your anger or malice destroy such a life (for, truly, he who does not value it does not deserve it), since it departs from the body so unwillingly, and I well believe that its weeping and pain are not without cause.

And strive to preserve your health, which you will succeed in doing all the more as you guard yourself from physicians <note>original: "fisici"; in Leonardo's time, physicians often relied on superstition rather than observation</note>, because their compositions are of a kind of alchemy <note>original: "archimia"; Leonardo uses this term pejoratively to mean pseudo-science or false chemistry</note>, of which there are no fewer books than there are of medicine. <margin>That is, they are not based on scientific principles, but on chimeras: how sharply Leonardo judges the medicine of his time!</margin>

## Correspondence between the body and the soul.

He who wishes to see how the soul dwells in its body, let him observe how that body uses its daily habitation <note>original: "cotidiana abitazione"</note>; <margin>How the body benefits from the soul dwelling within it.</margin> that is, if the habitation is without order and confused, the body will be kept in a disordered and confused state by its soul.

It does not seem to me that coarse men <note>term: "omini grossi" (coarse men); those lacking in intellect or refinement</note>, of wretched habits and little reasoning, deserve so beautiful an instrument, nor such a variety of mechanisms <note>original: "macchinamenti"; referring to the complex anatomical structures of the body</note>, as do speculative men <note>term: "omini speculativi" (speculative men); deep thinkers or philosophers who observe and reflect</note> and those of great reasoning; but rather only a sack where food is received and from whence it leaves; for

### Preface to his anatomy.

And you, who say it is better to watch an <term>anatomy</term> <note>original: "notomia"; the historical term for a dissection</note> being performed than to see such drawings, you would be right, if it were possible to see all these things, which are demonstrated in such drawings, in a single figure; in which, with all your talent <note>original: "ingegnio"</note>, you will not see and will not have knowledge except of a few veins; of which I, in order to have true and full knowledge, have dismantled more than ten human bodies, destroying all other members, consuming with the smallest particles all the flesh that was found around these veins, without making them bloody, except for the imperceptible bleeding of the capillary veins. And a single body was not enough for so much time, for it is necessary to proceed step by step with as many bodies as required to finish the entire knowledge; which I repeated twice to see the differences.

And if you should have a love for such a thing, you will perhaps be prevented by the stomach; and if this does not prevent you, you will perhaps be prevented by the fear of living in the night hours in the company of such dead bodies, quartered and flayed and terrifying to behold; and if this does not prevent you, perhaps you will lack the good drawing skill which belongs to such representation.

And if you should have the drawing skill, it will not be accompanied

<summary>Leonardo defends his anatomical drawings over live dissections, detailing the grueling and gruesome process of dissecting over ten bodies to map the vascular system. He highlights the physical, psychological, and artistic challenges required to master anatomical science.</summary>

Whether or not all these qualities have been present in me, the one hundred and twenty books composed by me will give the verdict of "yes" or "no," in which I have been hindered neither by avarice nor negligence, but only by time. Farewell.

### **The advantages of drawing in anatomical studies.**

... I remind you not to trouble yourself with words unless you are speaking to the blind; or if you indeed wish to demonstrate with words to the ears and not to the eyes of men, speak of matters of substance or of nature <gloss>of the essence of things, that is, of Metaphysics</gloss>, and do not involve yourself in things belonging to the eyes by trying to make them pass through the ears, because you will be far surpassed by the work of the painter.
With what words will you describe this heart without filling a book? And the more lengthily you write in detail, the more you will confuse the mind of the listener, and you will always have need of commentators <note>original: "sponitori"; those who explain or interpret a text</note> or of returning to experience <note>original: "sperienzia"; here meaning direct observation or experiment</note>, which for you is very brief and gives knowledge of only a few things compared to the whole of the subject of which you desire integral knowledge <gloss>One or two corpses a year were dissected in the most famous Universities!</gloss>.

<summary>Leonardo concludes the defense of his lifelong anatomical work, citing his 120 manuscripts as evidence, and argues that visual illustration is far superior to text for explaining the complexities of the human body.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Leonardo’s defense of visual illustration over text to his specific plan for organizing his anatomical treatise, comparing the human body to a map of the world.</meta>

## Outlines for the Treatise on Anatomy.

Begin your anatomy with the perfect man, and then make him old and less muscular; then proceed by stripping him away in stages right down to the bones.

And you shall then depict the child, along with a demonstration of the womb <note>original: "matrice"; the uterus or matrix where life begins</note>.

After the demonstration of all the parts of the limbs of man and of other animals, you will represent the manner of the proper functioning of such limbs, that is: of rising from a lying position, of walking, running, jumping from various perspectives, lifting and carrying great weights, throwing things far from oneself, and of swimming; and likewise, in every action, demonstrate which limbs and muscles are the cause of the aforementioned operations, and especially of the play of the arms <note>referring to the mechanics of arm movements and gestures</note>.

---

Therefore, here with 15 entire figures, the cosmography of the minor world <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The "minor world" or microcosm is a Renaissance concept viewing the human body as a reflection of the universe or "macrocosm"</note> will be shown to you, in the same order that was established before me by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century geographer and astronomer</note> in his cosmography; and so I will then divide those limbs, just as he divided the whole into provinces; and then I will describe the function of the parts from every direction, placing before your eyes the knowledge of the entire figure and the physical capacity <note>original: "valitudine"; health or functional strength</note> of man insofar as he possesses local motion through his parts.

And would that it might please our Author <note>original: "Altore"; a reference to God as the Creator or Author of nature</note> that I could demonstrate the nature of men and their customs in the same way that I describe their figure.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Man.

<summary>Leonardo outlines his plan to illustrate the human body from infancy to old age, comparing his anatomical method to Ptolemy’s mapping of the world. He expresses a desire to illustrate human character and behavior as clearly as he does the physical form.</summary>
<keywords>Treatise on Anatomy, microcosm, Ptolemy, biomechanics, muscle function, human growth, cosmography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s anatomical treatise, moving from the general plan of the "microcosm" to a specific investigation of the heart and the vascular system.</meta>

## The heart.

The heart in itself is not the principle of life; rather, it is a vessel made of dense muscle, quickened and nourished by the artery and vein, just as other muscles are. It is true that the blood and the artery, which is purified within it, are the life and nourishment of the other muscles. It is of such density that fire can scarcely harm it; this is seen in the case of men who have been burned, whose hearts remain bloody inside even after their bones have been turned to ash. Nature has created this great resistance to heat so that it may withstand the immense warmth generated in the left side of the heart by means of the arterial blood, which becomes refined <note>original: "s’ assottiglia"; literally "thinned" or "subtilized," referring to the ancient belief that blood was refined into "vital spirits" in the heart</note> in that ventricle ¹.

---

The heart is the seed <note>original: "nocciolo"; literally the pit or stone of a fruit</note> that generates the tree of the veins. These veins have their roots in the manure—that is, the mesenteric veins <note>original: "vene miseraice"; the veins of the digestive system</note>, which go to deposit the acquired blood into the liver, from which the upper veins of the liver are then nourished.

---

<note>
¹ It is now a proven fact that the muscles of the heart, especially those of the wall of the left ventricle, have a particular consistency and thickness. As for the resistance that the heart would offer to combustion, according to Leonardo, it is to be considered a myth.
</note>

<term>Heart (core)</term>: The central organ of the circulatory system.
<term>Ventricle (ventriculo)</term>: One of the chambers of the heart.
<term>Mesenteric veins (vene miseraice)</term>: The veins that carry nutrients from the intestines to the liver.
<term>Liver (fegato)</term>: The organ Leonardo identifies as a secondary processing center for blood.
<term>Muscle (muscolo)</term>: Fibrous tissue capable of contraction.
<term>Artery (arteria)</term>: Vessels carrying "vital" blood from the heart.
<term>Blood (sangue)</term>: The fluid of life and nourishment.

<summary>Leonardo describes the heart as a powerful muscle designed to withstand heat and compares the vascular system to a tree, with the mesenteric veins acting as roots in the "manure" of the intestines.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, heart, blood vessels, mesenteric veins, liver, heat resistance, microcosm</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s anatomical observations, moving from the general structure of the heart to a specific experimental observation regarding its movement and position at the moment of death.</meta>

### Whether the heart changes position at its death or not.

The change of the heart at its death is equal to the change it makes during the expulsion of its blood <gloss>1</gloss>, and perhaps a little less.

This is demonstrated when one observes the pigs in Tuscany, where they pierce the hearts of the pigs with an instrument called a <term>spillo</term> <note>A "spillo" here refers to a sharp awl or a small tap used to draw wine from barrels.</note>, with which wine is drawn from casks. Thus, having turned the pig on its back and secured it well, they pierce its right side along with the heart with such a spike, thrusting it straight inward. And if such a spike passes through the heart when it is elongated, the heart, in its expulsion of the blood, shortens itself and pulls the wound upward along with the point of the spike; and as much as the point of the spike is raised on the inside, so much is the handle of the spike lowered on the outside. Then, when the heart expands and pushes that wound downward, the part of the spike on the outside makes a motion contrary to the part on the inside, which moves together with the motion of the heart. And it does this many times, so that at the end of life, the exterior spike remains in the middle of the extremes where the last contrary motions of the heart were when it was alive. And when the heart is entirely cold, it withdraws a tiny amount and shortens by as much as the space occupied by the heat, because heat causes expansion and contraction.

---

<note>1 In fact, the heart ceases to pulse in <term>systole</term> <note>The phase of the heartbeat when the heart muscle contracts and pumps blood from the chambers into the arteries.</note>, and sudden death, furthermore, results in complete systole.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes an experimental observation of a pig's heartbeat using a metal spike to track the organ's mechanical contraction and expansion during death. He concludes that the heart's final position is determined by the cessation of movement and the cooling of the muscle.</summary>

<keywords>heart, Tuscany, anatomy, systole, thermal expansion, animal dissection, mechanics of life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s anatomical observations regarding the heart and blood vessels, moving from the experimental observation of the heart's motion in a dying animal to the physical properties and pathologies of the veins and arteries.</meta>

that body where it enters or exits; and this I have seen many times and have observed such measurements and left such an instrument in the heart until such an animal is opened up.

## The veins.

The veins are extensible and dilatable; and of this I shall provide testimony by having seen a man accidentally wound his common vein, and having immediately closed it with a tight ligature, within the space of a few days a blood-filled <term>aposteme</term> <note>A swelling or abscess, in this case likely a hematoma or aneurysm resulting from the injury.</note> grew, as large as a goose egg, full of blood, and remained so for several years; and furthermore, I found in a decrepit man that the <term>mesenteric veins</term> <note>Leonardo uses "miseraice," referring to the veins of the mesentery which carry blood from the intestines.</note> had the passage of blood closed off <gloss>1</gloss>, and were doubled in length.

## Senile alterations.

The artery and the vein <gloss>2</gloss> which, in the elderly, extend between the spleen and the liver, become of such thickness of skin that it closes the passage of the blood that comes from the mesenteric veins, through which this blood flows to the liver and the heart and to the two major veins.

---

<gloss>1 This phenomenon is known in modern times by the name of <term>phlebosclerosis</term> <note>Hardening of the walls of the veins.</note>. Further below, da Vinci also speaks of <term>arteriosclerosis</term> <note>Hardening and thickening of the walls of the arteries, typically occurring in old age.</note>.</gloss>

<gloss>2 Usually da Vinci uses the two words without much distinction. Here he seems to allude to the abdominal aorta and the vena cava. Structure: in the elderly, the artery and the vein that extend, etc.</gloss>

<summary>Leonardo describes the elasticity of veins through a case study of a traumatic hematoma and observes the thickening and lengthening of vessels in the elderly, providing an early description of arteriosclerosis.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, veins, artery, mesenteric, phlebosclerosis, arteriosclerosis, aging, spleen, liver</keywords>

and, consequently, throughout the whole body; and such veins, besides the thickening of their skin, grow in length and twist themselves like a snake, and the liver loses the moisture of the blood that was brought to it by these; whereby this liver becomes parched and turns into something like frozen bran, both in color and in substance, so that with a little rubbing applied to it, this substance falls away in tiny particles, like sawdust, and leaves behind the veins and arteries; and the veins of the gallbladder and of the navel which, through the <term>portal vein</term> <note>original: "porta del fegato"</note>, entered into said liver, remain entirely stripped of the substance of that liver, like <term>maize or sorghum</term> <note>original: "meliga o saggina"</note> when the grains have been plucked from them <insert>1</insert>.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s account of the famous "Centenarian" dissection at the Hospital of Santa Maria Nuova. he felt no failure of his person, other than weakness; and so, while sitting upon a bed in the Hospital of Santa Maria Nuova in Florence, without any other movement or sign of any mishap, he passed from this life.

And I performed an **anatomy** <note>original: "notomia"; Leonardo uses this term to mean a systematic dissection or autopsy.</note> to see the cause of so sweet a death, which I found to come about <insert>1</insert> <note>original: "venire meno"; Leonardo writes "failing," thinking of the life force rather than the death itself.</note> through the failure of blood and the artery that nourished the heart and the other lower members, which I found very parched, wasted, and dry; which anatomy I described quite diligently and with great ease, because of it being deprived of fat and moisture, which greatly hinders the recognition of the parts.

The other anatomy was of a child of two years, in which I found everything to be the contrary to that of the old man.

The elderly who live in health die from a lack of nourishment, and this happens because the passage of the <term>mesenteric veins</term> <note>original: "vene miseraice"; these are the veins that carry nutrients from the intestines to the liver.</note> is continually constricted by the thickening of the skin of these veins, progressing even to the capillary veins, which are the first to close up entirely; and from this it arises that the elderly fear the cold more than the young, and that those who have died old have skin the color of wood or of a dry chestnut, because such skin is almost entirely deprived of nourishment.

And this tunic of the veins acts in man as it does in **oranges**, in which the peel thickens the more

And if you should say that the thickening of the blood prevents it from flowing through the veins, this is not true, because the blood does not thicken within the veins, for it is continually dying and being reborn. <margin>In fact, the popular belief in so-called "thick blood" is erroneous; the blood always maintains itself the same.</margin>

### **Difficulty of the anatomical examination of the nerves.**

And I remind you that the <term>anatomy</term> <note>original: "notomia"; the process of dissection.</note> of the nerves will not show you the position of their branching, nor into which muscles they branch, by means of bodies dissolved in running water or in lime water; because although the origin of their starting points remains the same without such water as with it, their branchings—due to the flow of the water—tend to bunch together, not otherwise than does flax or hemp combed for spinning, all in one bundle, so that it is impossible to find again into which muscles or with what or with how many branchings the nerves spread into the aforesaid muscles.

### **Mechanism of respiration.**

Those muscles <margin>The intercostals.</margin> have voluntary and involuntary motion, seeing that they are the ones that open and shut the lung; they open when they let go

the spine of the neck upright; and, by means of the cords that
rise to that spine, these cords perform a double function,
that is, they support the spine by means of the ribs,
and support the ribs by means of the spine; and such
duality of powers, situated at the opposite ends of
such a cord, operate with that cord no differently
than a string is used with the ends of a bow. But this
concurrence of muscles at the spine supports it
upright, just as the ropes of ships support their
mast; and the same ropes, tied to the mast,
also support in part the sides of the ships to
which they are joined.

<summary>Leonardo describes the bellows-like mechanism of the lungs and explains how the muscles and tendons of the neck act as tension cables to stabilize the spine and ribs.</summary>
<keywords>respiration, lungs, vacuum, cervical spine, biomechanics, anatomical analogy, ribs</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the mechanical anatomy of the neck and lungs to the protective mechanisms and optical functions of the eye, specifically the pupil's reaction to light.</meta>

## Defense of the eye.

Because the eye is the window of the soul, the soul is always in fear of losing it, so much so that when something is moved before it that causes sudden fright to a man, he does not use his hands to protect the heart, the source of life, nor the head, the receptacle of the lord of the senses, nor hearing, nor smell, nor taste; instead, the frightened sense immediately acts: not content with closing the eyes with their lids clamped with the greatest force, he suddenly turns his face in the opposite direction; and not yet feeling secure <margin>Not being yet secure.</margin>, he places one hand there and extends the other, forming a vanguard against his suspicion.

Furthermore, nature has ordained that the eye of man should close itself by means of the eyelid, so that, while he is sleeping and it is not being watched over by him, it may not be harmed by anything.

### Dilation and contraction of the pupil.

The pupil <note>original: "pupilla"</note> of the eye changes into as many different sizes as there are varieties of brightness and darkness in the objects that present themselves before it.

In this case, nature has provided for the visual faculty <term>virtù visiva: the power or ability to see, thought in the Renaissance to be an active force</term> when it is offended by excessive light, by narrowing the pupil <note>original: "popilla"</note> of the eye; and when it is offended by various darknesses, by enlarging that opening, in the likeness of the mouth of a purse. And here nature acts—

<summary>Leonardo explains the instinctive physical reflexes used to protect the eyes and describes how the pupil dilates and contracts like a drawstring purse to regulate light.</summary>
<keywords>eye, soul, reflexes, eyelids, pupil, light and shadow, visual faculty, anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the pupil's adaptation to light, comparing it to a window, and transitions into a biological theory on the constant renewal of the living body through nutrition.</meta>

nature acts like someone who has too much light in their dwelling and closes half a window, or more or less, according to the necessity; and when night comes, they open the whole window to see better inside said dwelling. And here nature uses a continuous equation, with constant tempering and balancing, by increasing and decreasing the pupil <note>original: "pupilla"</note> in proportion to the aforementioned darkness or brightness that continually presents itself.

You will see the experiment in nocturnal animals, such as cats, owls <note>original: "grifi, allocchi"</note>, and the like, which at midday have a small pupil and at night a very large one; and all terrestrial, aerial, and aquatic animals do the same, but the nocturnal animals beyond any comparison. And if you wish to experiment with this in man, look fixedly at the pupil of his eye while holding a lit candle somewhat far away, and have him look at that light; bring it closer little by little, and you will see that the closer the light approaches, the more that pupil constricts.

## **How the body of the animal continually dies and is reborn.**

The body of anything that nourishes itself continually dies and is continually reborn, because nourishment cannot enter except in those places where the past nourishment has expired <note>original: "spirato"; here meaning used up or evaporated</note>; and if it has expired, it no longer has life; and if you do not return nourishment equal to the nourishment that has departed, then life fails in its health <term>valetudine: a state of physical health or vigor</term>; and if you take away that nourishment, life is entirely destroyed; but if you

<column-break/>

<gloss>pupil, nocturnal animals, nourishment, health/vigor, nature, experience/experiment</gloss>

<summary>Leonardo compares the pupil's adjustment to light to a window shutter and observes this phenomenon in nocturnal animals before proposing that living bodies exist in a constant state of decay and renewal through nutrition.</summary>
<keywords>pupil dilation, nocturnal animals, cats, owls, biology, nutrition, cellular renewal, health</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy between the biological renewal of the body and the mechanical or physical processes of nature, specifically transitioning from the previous page's discussion on the eye and the necessity of constant nourishment to maintain life.</meta>

if you return as much as is destroyed day by day, then as much life is reborn as is consumed; similar to the light produced by a candle through the nourishment given to it by the moisture <note>original: "omore"; referring here to the liquid wax or tallow</note> of said candle. This light also, continuously and with swift assistance, restores from below what is consumed above in dying; and in dying, it is converted from splendid light into dark smoke. This death is continuous, just as the smoke is continuous, and the continuity of such smoke is equal to the continuous nourishment; and in an instant $^1$ the entire light is dead and entirely regenerated together with the movement of its nourishment $^2$.

And its life also receives a flow and ebb, as shown to us by the flickering <note>original: "ventilazione"; literally ventilation or fanning</note> of its tip; and the same happens in the bodies of animals, by means of the beating of the heart, which generates the wave of blood through all the veins, which continuously dilate and contract. The dilation occurs in receiving the excess blood, and the contraction <note>original: "diminuzione"</note> occurs in releasing the overabundance of the received blood; and this is taught to us by the beating of the pulse, when one touches the aforementioned veins with the fingers in any part of the living body. But, to return to our purpose, I say that the flesh of animals is remade by the blood, which is continuously generated from their nourishment, and that this flesh undoes itself and returns through the <term>mesenteric arteries: vessels believed by early anatomists to carry waste or nutrients between the intestines and the liver</term>, and is rendered to the intestines, where it putrefies in a foul and stinking death, as shown to us by their expulsions and vapors <note>original: "caligine"</note>, just as the smoke and fire used for comparison.

---

$^1$ In an instant.
$^2$ See the note on page 81.

<summary>Leonardo compares the continuous renewal of the living body to a candle flame, arguing that life is a constant cycle of destruction and regeneration fueled by nourishment and blood circulation.</summary>

to the intestines, where it putrefies in a foul death, as it is expelled through the bowels and the pores, as is seen with the sweat and the skin's effluvia.

### On the Movement of the Blood

The heart is the center of life, and just as the candle is the source of the light, so the heart is the source of the heat that maintains the body. This heat is distributed through the blood, which acts as the vehicle for the nourishment of all the members. <margin>The blood is the soul's instrument.</margin> You must understand that the blood is in constant motion, driven by the pulse which we have already compared to the flickering of a flame. 

->**The Process of Nutrition**<-

The food, once received into the stomach, is converted into <term>chyle: a milky fluid formed during digestion</term>, which then passes to the liver to be transformed into blood. From there, it enters the heart to be purified and heated. 

| Part of the Body | Function in Renewal |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Stomach** | Receives the raw nourishment |
| **Liver** | Converts nourishment into blood |
| **Heart** | Gives life and heat to the blood |
| **Veins** | Distribute the blood to the limbs |

<column-break/>

As the blood flows, it leaves behind the substance necessary to rebuild the flesh that has been worn away by the heat of life. <note>The author views life as a "consuming fire" that must be constantly fed to prevent the body's destruction.</note> This process is so subtle that it cannot be seen by the eye, yet it is proven by the fact that an animal which does not eat will soon waste away and perish. 

<insert>Note that the lungs also play a part by cooling the heart.</insert>

The waste products of this continuous "burning" of life are then carried away. Just as the candle produces smoke, the body produces waste. If the nourishment is equal to the waste, the body remains in health; if the nourishment is less, the body declines; if it is more, the body grows. <gloss>This is the balance of nature.</gloss>

<summary>This page concludes the analogy of the candle by explaining how blood nourishes the body and replaces decaying flesh, while waste is expelled through the intestines and pores. It outlines the roles of the stomach, liver, and heart in maintaining the body's vital heat and substance.</summary>

<keywords>blood circulation, digestion, heart, liver, nutrition, metabolic renewal, vital heat</keywords>

### The balance of man in the air.

The bird is an instrument operating according to mathematical law, which instrument it is within the power of man to be able to replicate with all its motions, but not with as much power (as it only extends to the power of balancing) <gloss>But this lesser power is only in regard to the capacity for equilibrium.</gloss>; therefore we shall say that such an instrument composed by man lacks nothing except the soul of the bird, which soul must be counterfeited by the soul of man.
   The soul within the limbs of birds will without doubt obey their needs better than the soul of man would do, being separated from them, and especially in the motions of almost imperceptible balancing; but since we see the bird provide for many sensible varieties of motions, we can judge by such experience that those strongly <gloss>Strongly.</gloss> sensi-

---

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci argues that a bird is a mechanical instrument governed by mathematics that man can replicate, though the human pilot must manually provide the "soul" or conscious balance that the bird possesses naturally.</summary>

...ble motions will be able to be known by the cognition of man, and that he will be able to provide extensively against the ruin <note>In this context, "ruin" refers to the mechanical failure or the falling of the machine.</note> of that instrument of which he has made himself the soul and the guide.

## **Why the bird is supported upon the air ¹.**

The air that is struck with a greater velocity by a moving body condenses itself with a greater sum of its own substance. This is proven because a less dense fluid body will never support a denser one above itself; as is seen by experience with the anvil floating upon molten bronze, and liquefied gold and silver remaining beneath the smelting of lead. And for this reason, since air is a body capable of condensing within itself, when it is struck by a motion of greater velocity than that of its own escape, it presses into it-

---

<margin>¹ The principle of "heavier-than-air," studied by Leonardo in the flight of birds to reach an application for human flight, is the foundational principle of modern aviation. Leonardo's studies on the flight of birds are of such perfection that they were only equaled, never surpassed. The principle of the "heavier-than-air" was only arrived at recently; at first, the other principle of "lighter-than-air" <note>Referring to balloons and dirigibles.</note> triumphed. Leonardo's studies are divided into two phases: in the 1st, he attempts to apply a winged device to man, which with the man himself becomes a single instrument; in the 2nd, he conceives and designs a device entirely similar to the airplane, a device detached from the man, which the man was only to govern. He lacked, for practical application, the means to lift the conceived apparatus—namely, the motor.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo explains the aerodynamic principle that air condenses and provides resistance when struck rapidly, allowing a heavier-than-air body to be supported. The editorial note highlights Leonardo's foresight in conceptualizing modern aviation principles centuries before the invention of the engine.</summary>
<keywords>aerodynamics, air density, heavier-than-air, fluid dynamics, Leonardo da Vinci, aviation history, compression</keywords>

# OBSERVATIONS ON THE FLIGHT OF BIRDS OF PREY.

## The Short-toed Eagle <note>original: "Il cortone." Likely the *Circaetus gallicus*, a bird of prey common in Tuscany.</note>.

When the bird has a great breadth of wings and a small tail, and it wishes to rise, it will then raise its wings strongly, and by turning, it will receive the wind under its wings. This wind, swirling around it, will push it with great speed, as I saw the *cortone*, a bird of prey, while going to Fiesole above the place of Barbiga <note>A locality near Fiesole, north of Florence.</note> in 1505 on the 14th day of March.

## The Kite.

The kite and other birds that beat their wings very little go searching for the current of the wind; and when the wind reigns high up, they will then be seen at a great height, and if it reigns low, they stay low.

When the wind does not reign in the air, the kite then beats its wings several times in its flight, in such a way that it raises itself on high and acquires momentum; then, declining somewhat, it travels a long distance without beat-

<summary>Leonardo explains the principle of relative motion in aerodynamics—that moving air provides the same lift to a stationary wing as a moving wing does in still air—and records specific observations of eagles and kites near Fiesole.</summary>
<keywords>aerodynamics, lift, wind currents, Short-toed Eagle, Kite, Fiesole, observation of nature, 1505</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the kite's flight patterns and the mechanics of momentum, Leonardo concludes his observation of the bird's rest during flight before transitioning into a famous personal reflection and technical specifications for his flying machine.</meta>

ing its wings; and when it has descended, it does the same again, and so it follows successively; and this descending without beating its wings serves as a way of resting in the air after the fatigue of the aforementioned wing-beating.

This writing so distinctly of the <term>kite</term><note>The kite (*Milvus milvus*) is a bird of prey known for its graceful soaring and forked tail.</note> seems to be my destiny, because in the first memory of my infancy it seemed to me that, while I was in my cradle, a <term>kite</term> came to me, and opened my mouth with its tail, and many times struck me with such a tail inside my lips.

### « The Great Bird »

The aforementioned bird <note>Referring here to the flying machine Leonardo was designing, often called "The Great Bird."</note> must, with the help of the wind, rise to a great height, and this shall be its safety; because, even if all the aforementioned revolutions <note>Sudden shifts or turbulence.</note> should occur, it has time to return to a position of equilibrium <gloss>to regain balance</gloss>, provided that its members are of great resistance, so that they can safely resist the fury and impetus of the descent, with the aforementioned defenses, and its joints made of strong tanned leather, and its tendons of cords of very strong raw silk; and let no one meddle with iron fittings, because they quickly snap under the strain of their twists, or wear out, for which reason they are not to be used.

Remember how your bird must imitate no other than the bat, because the <gloss>the</gloss> membranes <note>The "paniculi" or skin-like membranes that form the bat's wing.</note>

<summary>Leonardo recounts a prophetic childhood dream involving a kite and outlines the structural requirements for his flying machine, emphasizing the use of organic materials over iron.</summary>

form the armor, or rather the connection to the armor, that is, the primary feathers of the wings <note>original: "maestre delle alie"; Leonardo refers to the primary flight feathers which provide the main thrust.</note>.

And if you were to imitate the wings of feathered birds, these are made of more powerful bone and sinew because they are perforated—that is, their feathers are separate and the air passes through them.

But the bat is aided by the membrane <note>original: "panniculo"; a thin skin or tissue.</note>, which binds the whole together and is not perforated.

## Persuasion for the enterprise which removes objections.

If you should say that the sinews and muscles of a bird are beyond comparison of greater power than those of a man—considering that the entire fleshiness of so many muscles and the pulp of the breast are made for the benefit and increase of the movement of the wings, with that bone made of one piece in the breast <note>The sternum or breastbone.</note> which provides the bird with very great power, with wings entirely woven of thick sinews and other very strong ligaments of cartilage and very tough skin with various muscles—here the answer is that such great strength is provided so that it may have power beyond <note>To have force greater than the ordinary amount required to sustain it on its wings.</note> its ordinary support. It must, at its will, double and triple its motion to flee from its predator or to follow its prey; therefore, in such an instance, it must double or triple its force, and, besides this, carry as much weight in its feet through the air as its own weight; as one sees the falcon carrying a duck, and

<summary>Leonardo argues that while birds have superior breast muscles for bursts of speed and carrying prey, a human flying machine is still feasible because birds possess far more power than is strictly necessary for basic flight.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, aerodynamics, bat wings, feathers, muscle power, sternum, falcon, flight mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo argues that birds possess a surplus of strength far beyond what is needed for simple flight, allowing them to carry prey or escape predators.</meta>

the eagle carrying a hare, which clearly demonstrates where such excessive force is distributed. However, very little force is needed for the bird to sustain itself, to balance upon its wings, to fan them above the current of the winds, and to direct its rudder <note>original: "temone"; Leonardo uses the term for a ship's rudder to describe the bird's tail.</note> along its path. Only a small movement of the wings is sufficient, and the larger the bird, the slower that movement becomes.

Man, likewise, possesses a greater sum of strength in his legs than is required for his own weight. To prove this is true, have a man stand upon sand <note>original: "lita"; likely referring to the shore or soft earth.</note> and observe how deep the print of his foot goes. Then, place another man upon his back, and you will see how much deeper it sinks. Then, remove the man from his back and have him jump straight up as high as he can; you will find that the print of his feet is deeper from the jump than it was when he carried the other man. Therefore, it is proven here in two ways that a man has twice the strength required to support himself.

### Bags — Parachutes.

Bags <note>original: "Baghe"; inflated skins or bladders used as cushions.</note>, whereby a man falling from a height of 6 braccia <note>A "braccio" (arm's length) is roughly 23 inches; 6 braccia is about 11.5 feet.</note> will not hurt himself, whether falling into water or onto the ground. These bags, tied together like the beads of a rosary <note>original: "pater nostri"; literally "Our Fathers," referring to the large beads on a rosary.</note>, are to be wrapped around a person. <margin>They should be wrapped around.</margin>

The great bird will take its first flight from the back of its great Swan <note>original: "magnio cecero"; a pun on Monte Ceceri (Swan Mountain) near Fiesole.</note>, filling the u- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely intended to be "universo" (universe).</note>

<margin>Mount Ceceri near Florence, probably.</margin>

# DISCUSSIONS ON THE PRE-
# EMINENCE OF PAINTING

---

->**He who despises Painting loves neither Philosophy,**<-
->**nor Nature.**<-

If you despise Painting, which is the sole imitator of all the visible works of nature, you certainly despise a subtle invention which, with philosophical and ingenious speculation, considers all the qualities of forms: seas, landscapes, plants, animals, grasses, and flowers, all of which are enveloped in shadow and light. Truly, this is a <term>science</term> <note>Leonardo uses "scienzia" to mean a systematic body of knowledge based on principles, rather than just a craft.</note> and the legitimate daughter of nature, because Painting is born of nature itself. But to be more accurate, we shall call it the grandchild of nature, because all visible things were born of nature, and from these things Painting was born. Therefore, we may rightly call it the grandchild of nature and a relative of God ¹.

---

¹ See Dante:
... your Art follows her [*Nature*], as much as it can, <note>original: "... l' Arte vostra quella [la Natura], quanto puote," from Inferno, Canto XI. Dante argues that human industry/art follows nature, and thus is like a grandchild to God.</note>

<summary>This page presents an argument for the intellectual dignity of painting, defining it as a "science" and a "grandchild of nature" that requires philosophical observation of the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Painting, Philosophy, Nature, science, Dante, God, Art, Paragone, light and shadow</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's argument that painting is the "grandchild of nature," Leonardo now expands on the artist's god-like power to create and manipulate any reality within their mind and work.</meta>

## The painter is master of every kind of people and of all things.

If the painter wishes to see beauties that would make him fall in love, he is the master of their generation; and if he wishes to see monstrous things that terrify, or those that are buffoonish and laughable, or truly compassionate, he is their master and god. And if he wishes to produce landscapes and deserts, shady or dark woods in hot weather, he depicts them; and likewise, warm places in cold weather. If he wants valleys, or to reveal a vast countryside from the high peaks of mountains, and if he wants to see the horizon of the sea beyond them, he is the master; and if from the low valleys he wishes to see the high mountains, or from the high mountains the low valleys and beaches. And in effect, whatever exists in the universe through essence, presence, or imagination, he has it first in his mind, and then in his hands; and these are of such excellence that in the same amount of time they generate a proportioned harmony in a single glance, just as real things do.

---

The divinity <gloss>1</gloss> possessed by the painter's science causes the painter's mind to be transformed into a likeness <note>Leonardo suggests the artist's mind becomes a mirror of God's own creative power.</note>

---

> It follows, as the teacher does the student,
> That your art is almost the grandchild of God.
> ->Inferno XI, 103-105.<-

Mixing it with its turbid foam, it vents its enraged wrath; sometimes overcome by the winds, it flees from the sea, <margin>The great swells of the sea surpass the shores.</margin> ¹ running along the high banks of the neighboring promontories, where having crested the mountain peaks, it descends into the opposite valleys; and part <insert>[remains]</insert> preyed upon by the fury of the winds; and part flees from the winds, falling back as rain upon the sea; and part descends ruinously from the high promontories, driving before it whatever opposes its ruin; and often it encounters an oncoming wave, and clashing with it, rises to the sky, filling the air with a confused and <unclear>turbulent mist</unclear>.

### The Difference between Painting and Poetry.

The same proportion exists between the imagination and the effect as exists between a shadow and the shaded body; and the same proportion exists between Poetry and Painting. This is because Poetry places its subjects within the imagination of the reader, while Painting presents them truly outside the eye, from which the eye receives the <term>similitudes</term> <note>In Leonardo's time, "similitudes" or "species" were thought to be the physical forms or images that traveled from an object to the eye.</note> no differently than if they were natural. Poetry presents them without this similitude, and they do not pass to the <term>impressive faculty</term> <note>original: "impressiva." This refers to the part of the brain Leonardo believed received and processed sensory images.</note> by way of the visual power as Painting does.

Painting represents the works of nature to the senses with more truth and certainty than words or letters do; but letters represent words with more truth than Painting does. However, we shall say that the science which represents the works of nature is more admirable than that which represents the works of the "operator"—that is, the works of men, which are words, such as Poetry and the like, which pass through the human tongue.

### Dispute between the poet and the painter.

The poet says that his science <term>science: Leonardo uses "scienzia" to mean a rigorous, rule-based discipline rather than just a creative whim</term> is invention and measure, and this is the simple body of Poetry: invention of subject matter and measure in the verses; and that he then clothes himself in all the other sciences. To which the painter responds that he has the same obligations in the science of Painting, namely invention and measure; invention

---

¹ <margin>Following similar ideas, Lessing also, in his *Laocoön*, censures Ariosto's description of the beauties of Alcina, and Homer's description of the shield of Achilles.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo argues that poetry cannot achieve the "harmonic concord" of painting because words are sequential, causing the viewer to forget the whole, whereas painting is perceived all at once. He begins a formal debate on whether "invention and measure" belong more to the poet or the painter.</summary>

<keywords>Paragone, aesthetics, harmony, simultaneity, Lessing, Laocoön, poetic measure, visual perception</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>Continuing the "Dispute between the poet and the painter," where Leonardo argues that painting is superior to poetry because it presents a unified harmony to the eye all at once, whereas poetry must describe parts sequentially, leading to the loss of the whole.</meta>

in the subject matter that he must devise, and measure in the things painted, so that they are not disproportionate; but he does not clothe himself in other sciences; rather, the others for the large part clothe themselves in Painting—such as astrology <note>original: "astrologia." In Leonardo's time, this term encompassed both what we now call astronomy and astrology.</note>, which can do nothing without perspective, which is a principal member of Painting itself; I mean mathematical astrology, I do not speak of the fallacious judicial kind <margin>Mathematical astrology: which measures the movements of the stars; judicial astrology: which derives judgments on the destiny of men from the course of the stars. From the first, which is the only one he values, Da Vinci sees the rise of astronomy.</margin> (may those who live by it through the means of fools forgive me).

The poet says that he describes a thing that represents another full of beautiful maxims. The painter says he has the authority to do the same, and in this part he too is a poet; and if the poet says he can kindle men to love, which is a principal thing among all species of animals, the painter has the power to do the same, even more so because he places before the lover the actual likeness of the beloved thing. The lover often acts with this likeness, kissing it and speaking to it, in a way he would not do with the same beauties placed before him by the writer; and [Painting] overcomes the wits of men even further, in that it induces them to love and fall in love with a painting that does not represent any living woman. It once happened to me that I made a painting representing a divine thing, which, being bought by one who loved it, he wished to remove the representation of such a deity so that he could kiss it without suspicion. But in the end, his conscience overcame his de-

If you say: "I will describe hell or paradise and other delights or terrors to you," the painter surpasses you, because he will place before you things that, while remaining silent, will express such delights, or will terrify you, and move your soul to flee.

Painting moves the senses more readily than Poetry. And if you say that with words you can move a crowd to tears or to laughter; I tell you that it is not you who moves them, it is the orator, and that is a science <note>Leonardo uses "scienzia" here to mean a structured body of knowledge or a formal discipline.</note> that is not Poetry.

But the painter will move people to laughter, though not to tears, because weeping is a more profound physical occurrence <note>original: "accidente." In Renaissance philosophy, an accident is a non-essential or external physical manifestation of an internal state.</note> than laughter.

A painter once made a picture that caused whoever saw it to immediately yawn, and they repeated this action as long as they kept their eyes on the painting, which itself was depicted as yawning...

And if you write of the form of some gods, such writing will not be held in the same veneration as the painted deity, because to such a painting, vows and diverse prayers will be continually offered, and various generations from different provinces and across the eastern seas will flock to it. And from such people, help will be asked of that painting, and not of the writing.

## Music should be called the sister and the junior of Painting.

Music is not to be called anything other than the sister of Painting, given that it is the subject of hearing—the second sense <note>Leonardo ranks the senses hierarchically, with sight as the primary and most noble sense, followed by hearing.</note> after the eye—and it composes harmony through the conjunction of its proportional parts operated at the same time, forced to be born and to die in one or more harmonic intervals. These intervals surround the proportionality of the members of which such harmony is composed, no differently than the circumferential line <note>The outline or contour.</note> surrounds the limbs from which human beauty is generated.

But Painting excels and reigns over Music, because it does not die immediately after its creation, as does unfortunate Music; rather, it remains in existence and shows you as being alive what is, in fact, a single surface. <margin>And it gives relief and life to a canvas or a panel.</margin> Oh, marvelous science! <note>original: "scienzia." Leonardo views painting as a rigorous intellectual discipline based on optical laws and mathematical proportions.</note> You preserve in life the fleeting beauties of mortals, which have more permanence than the works of nature, which are continually changed by time, leading them to their inevitable old age. And such a science has the same proportion to divine nature as its works have to the works of that nature; and for this reason, it is adored.

---

<margin>¹ In relation to the eye.</margin>

<term>Music</term> <term>Painting</term> <term>Hearing</term> <term>Eye</term> <term>Harmony</term> <term>Human Beauty</term> <term>Science</term> <term>Divine Nature</term>

<summary>Leonardo argues that while music and painting both rely on proportional harmony, painting is superior because it is permanent, whereas music vanishes the moment it is performed. He elevates painting to a "science" that preserves beauty more effectively than nature itself.</summary>

### The Difference between Painting and Sculpture.

Between Painting and Sculpture, I find no other difference than this: the sculptor performs his work with greater physical fatigue than the painter, while the painter performs his work with greater mental fatigue. This is proven to be true in this manner: since the sculptor, in creating his work, uses the strength of his arms and the impact of the hammer to consume the marble or other surplus stone that exceeds the figure enclosed within it <note>Leonardo refers to the Platonic idea that the statue already exists inside the stone, and the artist merely reveals it by removing the "superfluous" material.</note> <insert>1</insert>, through a most mechanical exercise. This is often accompanied by great sweat, which mixes with the dust and turns into mud; his face is pasted and all floured with marble dust, making him look like a baker; he is covered in tiny stone chips, as if it had snowed on him, and his dwelling is filthy and full of chips and stone dust.

The exact opposite happens to the painter (speaking here of excellent painters and sculptors), for the painter sits before his work with great ease, well-dressed, and moves the lightest brush with lovely co- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "colori" (colors), matching the catchword "co-" noted in the metadata.</note>

---

<margin>1 Michelangelo expresses the same concept: to whom belongs the priority?</margin>

That a single marble block does not contain
Within its excess, and only to that arrives
The hand that obeys the intellect.<-

---

Painting involves more mental discourse and greater artifice and wonder than Sculpture, because necessity compels the mind of the painter to transform itself into the very mind of nature, so that he may be the interpreter between nature and art <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The gloss implies: "he demonstrates this."</note>. He explains with that mind the causes of nature's manifestations as dictated by its laws <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Explaining with the mind the causes and laws of natural phenomena.</note>, and in what way the likenesses of objects <gloss>3</gloss> <note>The images.</note> surrounding the eye converge with their true images upon the pupil of the eye. He determines, among objects equal in size, which will appear more or less dark or more or less light; and among things of equal depth, which will appear more or less low; and of those placed at an equal height, which will appear more or less high; and of equal objects placed at various distances, why one will appear less distinct than the other. And such an art embraces and contains within itself all visible things—which the poverty of Sculpture cannot do—namely: the colors of all things and their diminutions; this

<meta>Continuing the argument from page 143 regarding the superiority of painting over sculpture. The text transitions from the painter's ability to represent the atmosphere to a personal testimony of the author's experience in both arts.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>

depicts transparent things, while the sculptor will show you the natural ones without his own artifice; the painter will show you various distances with the variation of the color of the air interposed between the objects and the eye; he shows the mists, through which the appearances of objects penetrate with difficulty; he shows the rains, which reveal behind them the clouds, with mountains and valleys; he shows the dust, which reveals within and behind itself the combatants who stir it up; he shows the rivers, more or less dense; he will show you the fish playing between the surface of the waters and their bottom; he shows the polished pebbles of various colors resting upon the washed sands of the riverbeds, surrounded by greening plants within the surface of the water; he shows the stars at different heights above us, and thus other innumerable effects to which <term>Sculpture</term> <note>original: "Scoltura"</note> cannot reach.

Since I have practiced no less in <term>Sculpture</term> <note>The art of carving or modeling three-dimensional forms.</note> than in <term>Painting</term> <note>The art of representing images on a flat surface through color and light.</note>, and performing both to the same degree, it seems to me that, with little reproach, I can give a judgment as to which is of greater genius, difficulty, and perfection than the other. First, <term>Sculpture</term> is subject to certain lights—namely, from above—whereas <term>Painting</term> carries light and shadow with it everywhere; and light and shadow are therefore the very essence of <term>Sculpture</term>. The sculptor, in this case, is aided by the nature of the <term>relief</term> <note>The physical three-dimensionality of the work.</note>, which generates it by itself, while the painter, through accidental art, creates it in the places where nature would reasonably do so. The sculptor cannot diversify his work with the various natures of the colors of things;

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci argues that painting is superior to sculpture because it can represent complex natural phenomena like transparency, atmosphere, and weather. He asserts his authority to judge based on his mastery of both arts, noting that sculpture relies on physical relief while painting must intellectually recreate light and shadow.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Paragone, Painting vs Sculpture, perspective, atmospheric effects, light and shadow, relief, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>Continuing the "Paragone" or comparison of the arts, specifically detailing the technical limitations of sculpture versus the infinite capabilities of painting, and addressing the argument of durability.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>

Painting is not lacking in any part. The perspectives of sculptors <margin>In low reliefs.</margin> do not appear true at all; those of the painter appear hundreds of miles beyond the work itself. <term>Aerial perspective</term> <note>The technique of representing distance by modifying the clarity and color of objects to simulate the effect of the atmosphere.</note> is far from their work; they cannot represent transparent bodies, they cannot represent luminous ones, nor reflected lines, nor bodies as shiny as mirrors and similar lustrous things, nor mists, nor dark weather, and infinite other things that are not mentioned so as not to be tedious.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What sculpture does have is that it is more resistant to time, although painting has similar resistance when done upon thick copper covered with white enamel, and painted upon that with enamel colors, and put back into the fire, and baked. This, for eternity, surpasses sculpture. They <margin>The sculptors are implied.</margin> might say that where they make an error, it is not easy to repair. This is a poor argument: to want to prove that an irremediable lapse of memory makes the work more worthy! But I will say indeed that the mind of the master who makes such errors <margin>Construct: the mind of the master who makes such errors will be more difficult to repair than...</margin> will be more difficult to repair than it is to repair the work ruined by him. We know well that he who is experienced and good will not make such errors; rather, with good rules, he will go about removing so little at a time that he will lead his work to a good conclusion. Furthermore: the sculptor, if he works in clay or wax, can remove and add, and when it is finished, it is easily cast in bronze; and this is the final operation and the most permanent.

<sig>10</sig>

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting can depict atmospheric effects and light that sculpture cannot, and refutes the idea that sculpture's permanence or difficulty of correction makes it a superior art.</summary>
<keywords>painting, sculpture, aerial perspective, enamel, bronze casting, Paragone, artistic errors, durability</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>Continuing the comparison (Paragone) between Painting and Sculpture, specifically refuting the argument that sculpture is superior due to its permanence, and highlighting the intellectual depth of painting.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>

that Sculpture has, because that which is made only of marble is subject to more ruin <note>original: "sottoposta alle ruine." The author implies marble is more fragile than bronze.</note> than that made of bronze.

Therefore, that painting made on copper which can, through the methods of Painting, be removed and added to, is equal to bronze; for when you first made the work in wax, it could also be removed and added to <note>The author argues that painting on copper/enamel allows for corrections during the process, just as a sculptor corrects a wax model before casting it in bronze.</note>. If this sculpture of bronze is eternal, then this painting on copper or glass is most eternal; if the bronze remains black and ugly, this painting is full of various and charming colors and infinite variety. Regarding which, as stated above, if you wished to speak only of painting done on wood panels, in this case I am content to give the judgment alongside Sculpture, saying this: just as Painting is more beautiful and possesses more imagination and is more copious, so Sculpture is more durable, and it has nothing else.

Sculpture shows what it is with little effort; Painting appears to be a miraculous thing, making impalpable things appear palpable, making flat things appear in relief, and making near things appear distant! In effect, Painting is adorned with infinite <term>speculations</term> <note>In this context, "speculazione" refers to deep intellectual study, scientific observation, and the mental labor required to simulate reality.</note> that Sculpture does not employ.

## II. — THE LIFE OF THE PAINTER.

-> The painter must be solitary
and consider what he sees
and speak with himself, selecting
the most excellent parts of what-
ever thing he sees, acting
in the likeness of a mirror,
which transforms itself into as many
colors as there are in the

*Treatise on Painting, Ludwig edition, 58.* <note>original: "Tratt. d. Pittura, Ludwig, 58." This refers to the standard scholarly edition of Leonardo da Vinci's writings on art compiled by Heinrich Ludwig.</note>

<vocab>painter, Nature, mirror, Treatise on Painting, Ludwig</vocab>

<page-num>146</page-num>

that Sculpture has, because that which is made only of marble is subject to more ruin <note>original: "sottoposta alle ruine." The author implies marble is more fragile than bronze.</note> than that made of bronze.

Therefore, that painting made on copper which can, through the methods of Painting, be removed and added to, is equal to bronze; for when you first made the work in wax, it could also be removed and added to <note>The author argues that painting on copper/enamel allows for corrections during the process, just as a sculptor corrects a wax model before casting it in bronze.</note>. If this sculpture of bronze is eternal, then this painting on copper or glass is most eternal; if the bronze remains black and ugly, this painting is full of various and charming colors and infinite variety. Regarding which, as stated above, if you wished to speak only of painting done on wood panels, in this case I am content to give the judgment alongside Sculpture, saying this: just as Painting is more beautiful and possesses more imagination and is more copious, so Sculpture is more durable, and it has nothing else.

Sculpture shows what it is with little effort; Painting appears to be a miraculous thing, making impalpable things appear palpable, making flat things appear in relief, and making near things appear distant! In effect, Painting is adorned with infinite <term>speculations</term> <note>In this context, "speculazione" refers to deep intellectual study, scientific observation, and the mental labor required to simulate reality.</note> that Sculpture does not employ.
