# THE LIFE OF THE PAINTER

---

## The painter in his studio.

In order that the prosperity of the body does not spoil that of the mind, the painter or draftsman <note>original: "disegniatore"</note> must be solitary, and especially when he is intent on those speculations and considerations ¹ which, continually appearing before the eyes, give material to the memory to be well preserved. And if you are alone, you will be entirely your own master, and if you are accompanied by even a single companion, you will be only half your own, and even less so the greater the indiscretion of your associate; and if you are with more, you will fall further into similar inconvenience. And if you should say: "I will do it my own way, I will withdraw to one side so as to better contemplate the forms of natural things," I say this can be done only with difficulty, because you could not prevent yourself from often

---

¹ Meditative observation of things. <note>The original term "ispeculazione" refers to a deep, scientific looking combined with philosophical inquiry.</note>

<summary>Leonardo advises that the artist must embrace solitude to maintain mental clarity and focus, arguing that the presence of others distracts the mind from the deep observation of nature.</summary>
<keywords>solitude, artistic discipline, observation of nature, memory, studio practice, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the social habits of the artist, contrasting the necessity of solitude for deep thought with the benefits of group practice for technical skill.</meta>

listening to their chatter; and, since one cannot serve two masters, you would perform the duties of a companion poorly and the results of your artistic speculation <note>The term "speculazione" implies a deep, investigative looking into the laws of nature.</note> even worse. And if you should say: "I will withdraw so far to one side that their words will not reach me and will not hinder me," I tell you that in this regard you will be considered a madman; but do you not see that by doing so, you would still be alone? And if you truly desire company, take it from your own studio: this can be helpful to you for the sharing of various speculations that may occur; any other company could be very harmful to you.

### Whether it is better to draw in company or not.

I say and confirm that drawing in company is much better than alone for many reasons. The first is that you will be ashamed to be seen among the number of draftsmen if you are insufficient, and this shame will be the cause of good study; secondly, a healthy envy <margin>Emulation.</margin> <note>original: "invidia bona." Leonardo distinguishes between malicious jealousy and the productive desire to match the skill of a master.</note> will stimulate you to be among those more praised than yourself, for the praise of others will spur you on; another reason is that you will learn from the techniques of those who do better than you. And if you are better than the others, you will profit by avoiding their faults, and the praise of others will increase your virtue.

<summary>Leonardo concludes that while philosophical meditation requires solitude, the physical act of drawing is best done in a group to foster healthy competition and mutual learning. He warns that non-artists are a distraction, whereas fellow students provide productive company.</summary>
<keywords>artistic education, emulation, drawing, studio practice, social habits, competition, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the social environment of the studio to the internal mental habits of the artist, specifically focusing on techniques for memory and creative inspiration.</meta>

## On studying when you wake or before you fall asleep in bed, in the dark.

I have experienced in myself that it is of no small utility, when you find yourself in the dark in bed, to go about repeating with the imaginative faculty <note>original: "imaginativa." This refers to the mental ability to visualize forms and concepts without physical sight.</note> the superficial outlines of the forms previously studied, or other notable things understood through subtle speculation <note>Deep, investigative study of nature's laws.</note>; and this is truly a laudable act and useful for confirming things within the memory.

### On dwellings.

Small rooms or dwellings concentrate the mind <note>original: "'ngegnio." This encompasses wit, genius, and intellect.</note>, while large ones distract it.

## A way to increase and awaken the mind to various inventions.

I will not, however, omit from these precepts a new method of speculation, which, although it may appear small and almost worthy of laughter, is nonetheless of great utility in awakening the mind to various inventions.

And it is this: if you look at certain walls fouled with various stains or stones of various mixtures <gloss>Of varied composition.</gloss>, and you have to invent some scene, you will be able to see there

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to practice mental visualization in the dark to strengthen memory and suggests that small spaces aid focus. He introduces a famous technique for finding inspiration by looking for patterns in stained walls or variegated stones.</summary>
<keywords>memory, visualization, architecture, creativity, imagination, stains on walls, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of finding inspiration in random patterns (stains on walls) and transitions into a historical argument for why artists must study nature directly rather than imitating other painters.</meta>

similarities to diverse landscapes, adorned with mountains, rivers, rocks, trees, plains, great valleys, and hills in various ways; there you will also be able to see various battles and figures in swift action, strange expressions of faces and costumes, and infinite things, which you will be able to reduce into complete and good forms. And it happens with such walls and mixtures <gloss>Stained and variegated.</gloss> as it does with the sound of bells, in whose tolling you will find every name and word that you can imagine.

### The painter must have Nature as his teacher.

The painter will produce paintings of little excellence if he takes the paintings of others as his authority, but if he learns from natural things, he will bear good fruit; as we see in the painters after the Romans, who always imitated one another, and from age to age that art always went into decline. After these came Giotto the Florentine, who—born in solitary mountains inhabited only by goats and similar beasts—being turned by nature toward such art, began to draw upon the rocks the actions of the goats of which he was a keeper; and so he began to depict all the animals found in the countryside, in such a way that after much study, he surpassed not only the masters of his own age, but all those of many centuries past. After him, art fell back again, because everyone imitated paintings already made, and so from century to century it went on declining, until Tommaso the Florentine, nicknamed Masaccio, showed with perfect work how those who

<summary>Leonardo explains how random stains can inspire complex compositions and argues that art declines when painters imitate each other instead of Nature, citing Giotto and Masaccio as examples of those who broke this cycle.</summary>

[they] took as their authority anyone other than nature, the mistress of masters, they labored in vain.

I wish to say the same regarding these mathematical matters <note>original: "cose matematiche" — Leonardo refers here to things with a scientific or demonstrable foundation.</note>, that those who study only the authorities and not the works of nature are, by their art, the grandchildren and not the children of nature herself, who is the mistress of all good authors. Oh, what supreme folly it is of those who blame those who learn from nature, while setting aside the authorities who were themselves but disciples of that same nature! <margin>Compare Leon Battista Alberti, *Treatise on Painting*, Book III, chapters IV and VI, on the necessity of not copying other painters nor creating solely from one's own mind, but taking Nature as a model.</margin>

### Imitation.

The imitation of ancient things is more praiseworthy than that of modern ones.

### Composition of a fantastic animal using naturalistic details.

You know that it is impossible to create any animal that does not have its limbs, each in itself, similar to those of some other animal. Therefore, if you wish to make an animal imagined by you appear natural (let us say it is a serpent), take for the head that of a mastiff or a hound, for the eyes those of a cat, and

<summary>Leonardo argues that artists who copy other painters are merely "grandchildren" of nature and explains how to create believable monsters by combining real anatomical parts from different animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for creating a believable imaginary creature (a dragon or serpent) by combining anatomical parts of various animals, then transitions into a philosophical discussion on the necessity of universal observation and the dangers of mindless imitation.</meta>

...by the ears of a porcupine, the nose of a greyhound, the eyebrows of a lion, the temples of an old rooster, and the neck of a water turtle.

## The painter is not praiseworthy unless he is universal.

The mind of the painter must be like a mirror, which always transforms itself into the color of whatever thing it has as its object, and fills itself with as many likenesses as there are things placed before it. Therefore, knowing that you, painter, cannot be good if you are not a universal master of imitating <note>original: "contraffare" — in this context, it means to skillfully replicate or represent nature through art.</note> with your art all the qualities of the forms that Nature produces—which you will not know how to do unless you see them and depict them in your mind—whenever you go through the countryside, ensure that your judgment is turned toward various objects. Look at one thing after another, making a bundle of various things selected and chosen from among the less good ones. Do not do as some painters who, tired in their imagination, set aside their work and take exercise by going for a walk, retaining a weariness in their mind; not only do they not wish to pay attention to various things, but often, when meeting friends or relatives and being greeted by them, they neither see nor hear them, being no more aware of them than if they had encountered so much thin air.

The painter who draws by practice and the judgment of the eye, without reason <note>original: "sanza ragione" — meaning without an understanding of the underlying principles or scientific logic of what is being observed.</note>, is like a mirror, which imitates all things placed before it without any knowledge of them.

---

<summary>Leonardo concludes his recipe for a fantastic creature and advises painters to maintain a mind like a mirror, constantly observing and selecting the best forms from nature rather than working from rote habit.</summary>
<keywords>universal painter, observation, mirror metaphor, imitation of nature, artistic judgment, reason vs practice, Leonardo da Vinci, Treatise on Painting</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the painter's need for universality, moving from the general requirement of observation to a specific critique of portrait specialists who fail to master the broader art of composition.</meta>

I have observed universally among all those who make a profession of portraying faces from life, that the one who creates the most striking likeness is a poorer composer of narrative scenes <note>original: "componitore di storie" — a painter of complex historical, biblical, or mythological scenes involving multiple figures.</note> than any other painter. <margin>The portraitist is generally a poor composer of scenes.</margin> And this arises because it is evident that nature has disposed the person who does one thing best more toward that specific thing than another; for this reason, he has felt a greater love for it, and this greater love has made him more diligent. However, all the love that is placed upon a single part is taken away from the whole, because his entire delight has been united in that one thing alone, abandoning the universal for the particular. 

Since the power of such a mind is reduced to a small space, it lacks power in expansion. This mind acts in the likeness of a concave mirror, which, capturing the rays of the sun, reflects that quantity of rays with a milder warmth when it reflects them across a greater area of expansion; but when it reflects them all into a smaller place, then such rays are of immense heat, yet they only act <margin>Acts.</margin> in that small place. 

Such is the way of these painters: they love no other part of painting but the face of man alone, and what is worse is that they recognize no other part of the art which they value or upon which they have judgment. Their works, being without movement—because they themselves are lazy and of little motion—lead them to criticize those things which possess greater and more prompt movements.

<summary>Leonardo argues that painters who specialize only in portraits lose the ability to compose complex scenes, comparing their narrow focus to the intense but limited heat of a concave mirror. He critiques these artists for their lack of "universality" and their inability to depict dynamic motion.</summary>

<keywords>portraiture, composition, universality, concave mirror, artistic focus, movement, nature, specialization</keywords>

It is true that one must observe <term>decorum</term> <note>The principle of appropriateness in art, ensuring that actions suit the character and the setting.</note>, that is, that the movements should be the announcers of the <term>motions of the soul</term> <note>original: "moto de l’animo" — Leonardo’s famous concept that physical gestures must reveal internal thoughts and emotions.</note> of the mover <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The attitudes should express the feelings of the character represented.</note>. So, keep it in mind not to fall into such defects.

That man will not be universal who does not equally love all the things contained in Painting; as if one does not like <term>landscapes</term> <note>original: "paesi" — referring to the background scenery and natural environments.</note>, he deems them to be a matter of brief and simple investigation. ## Against Mercenary Art.

I remind you, painter, that when—either through your own judgment or by the advice of others—you discover any error in your works, you should correct them, so that in making such a work public, you do not publish your own shortcomings along with it. And do not excuse yourself by persuading yourself that you will redeem your poor reputation in your next work; because a painting does not die immediately after its creation as music does, but for a long time it will bear witness to your ignorance.

And if you say that correcting it takes time—time which, if spent on another work, would earn you much more—you must understand that the money earned in excess of what we need for living is not worth much. If you desire it in abundance, you will never finish using it, and thus it is not truly yours; all treasure that is not used belongs to us all in the same way, and whatever you earn that does not serve your life is in the hands of others without your benefit. But if you study and refine your works well with the discourse of the two <term>perspectives</term> <note>Leonardo likely refers to linear perspective (the geometry of lines) and aerial perspective (the effect of atmosphere on color and clarity).</note>, you will leave behind works that will give you more honor than money, because money is only valued for itself

### On the painter judging his own works
### and those of others.

When the work is equal to the judgment <note>The artist's critical standard or mental conception.</note>, that is
a poor sign for such judgment; and when the work sur-
passes the judgment, that is the worst case, as happens to
one who marvels at having performed so well; but when
the judgment surpasses the work, this is a perfect sign.
And if a young man is in such a disposition, without doubt
he will be an excellent practitioner; he will be a creator
of few works, but they will be of such quality that they will
stop men in admiration to contemplate their
perfections.

There is nothing that deceives us more than our own
judgment when it is used to pass sentence on our own
works; yet it is good at judging the things of our
enemies, but not of our friends; because hate and friendship are
two of the most powerful accidents <note>In the philosophical sense: a non-essential attribute or emotional state that affects the soul.</note> found among
living beings. And for this reason, you, O painter, should be eager
to hear no less willingly what your ad—

<summary>Leonardo warns that the pursuit of wealth stifles the mind and argues that a true artist's critical judgment must always exceed their technical execution to achieve excellence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the artist's critical faculty, specifically the value of listening to enemies versus friends and the importance of public opinion in identifying anatomical errors.</meta>

—versaries say of your works, than to hear what your friends say; because hate is more powerful than love, for hate ruins and destroys love; because if he is a true friend, he is another yourself, whereas you find the opposite in an enemy, and the friend might deceive himself <note>Out of a desire to please or through shared bias.</note>. There is also a third kind of judgment, which, moved by envy, gives birth to flattery, praising the beginning of good works so that the lie may blind the practitioner ¹.

---

Certainly, while a man is painting, he should not refuse the judgment of anyone; because we clearly know that a man, even if he is not a painter, will have knowledge of the form of another man, and will judge well if he is hunchbacked, or has one shoulder high or low, or if he has a large mouth or nose or other defects. And if we know that men can truthfully judge the works of nature, how much more must we confess that they can judge our errors? For you know how much a man deceives himself in his own works, and if you do not recognize it in yourself, consider it in others, and you will profit from the errors of others.

So be eager to hear the opinions of others with patience; and consider well and think well if the critic has cause or not to blame you: and if you find that he does,

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to value the criticism of enemies over friends and to respect the common person's ability to spot anatomical errors, as self-delusion is a constant threat to the artist.</summary>

correct it; and if you find he does not, pretend you did not hear him, or, if he is a man you respect, show him the reason why he is mistaken.

### How one must recognize a good painting,
### and what qualities it must have to be good.

The first thing one must judge in order to recognize a good painting is whether the motion is appropriate to the mind of the mover <note>Leonardo means that the physical movement of a figure must accurately reflect its internal thoughts or emotions.</note>; second, that the greater or lesser relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality or "popping out" from the surface</term> of shaded objects is adjusted according to their distances; third, that the proportions of the limbs correspond to the proportionality of the whole; fourth, that the decorum <term>decorum: the suitability or appropriateness of a style to its subject</term> of the setting corresponds to the decorum of the figures' actions; fifth, that the limb-structures are suited to the condition of those depicted—that is, to the refined, refined limbs; to the coarse, coarse limbs; and to the fat, fat limbs likewise.

### How the mirror is the master of painters.

When you wish to see if your painting as a whole conforms with the thing portrayed from nature, take a mirror and have the living thing reflected within it; then compare the reflected thing with your painting, and consider well if the subject of both likenesses conforms together.

And above all, the mirror should be taken as one's master—specifically the flat mirror—because upon its surface, things have a likeness to painting in many respects.

<sig>11</sig> <note>This is a signature mark used by printers to keep pages in the correct order.</note>

<vocab>painting, mirror, painters, proportions, decorum, limb-structures, likeness</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo outlines five criteria for a successful painting—including emotional expression and anatomical consistency—and champions the flat mirror as the ultimate tool for checking the accuracy of one's work against nature.</summary>
<keywords>artistic judgment, motion and emotion, perspective, decorum, anatomy, mirror as tool, naturalism</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between painting and the mirror, establishing the mirror as the ultimate standard for realism before transitioning into the necessity of theoretical study over mere practice.</meta>

That is to say, you see a painting made upon a flat surface demonstrating things that appear in relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality</term>, and the mirror upon a flat surface does the same; the painting is a single surface, and the mirror is the same; the painting is impalpable <note>meaning it cannot be felt as a 3D object</note> in that what appears round and detached cannot be surrounded by the hands, and the mirror does the same; the mirror and the painting show the likeness of things surrounded by shadow and light; both appear to be far beyond their own surfaces.

And if you recognize that the mirror, by means of outlines and shadows and lights, makes things appear detached to you, and since you have among your colors shadows and lights more powerful than those of the mirror, certainly if you know how to compose them well together, your painting will also appear as a natural thing, seen in a large mirror.

# On the method of study.

Study science first, and then follow the practice born from that science.

Those who fall in love with practice without science are like the pilot who enters a ship without a rudder or a compass, who never has certainty of where he is going.

Practice must always be built upon good theory; of which <term>Perspective</term> <note>original: "Prespettiva"</note> is the guide and the gateway, and without this, nothing is done well in the matters of Painting.

---

<summary>Leonardo explains why the mirror is the perfect model for painters due to its ability to create depth on a flat surface, and warns that artistic practice is useless without a foundation in scientific theory and perspective.</summary>

## III. — WHAT THE PAINTER MUST KNOW.

### A = PERSPECTIVE.

-> Perspective is the bridle and the <-
-> rudder of Painting. <-

*Treatise on Painting, Ludwig, 509.* <note>Referring to the Heinrich Ludwig edition of Leonardo's "Trattato della Pittura."</note>

<vocab>Perspective, Painting, Painter, Ludwig, Treatise on Painting</vocab>

That is to say, you see a painting made upon a flat surface demonstrating things that appear in relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality</term>, and the mirror upon a flat surface does the same; the painting is a single surface, and the mirror is the same; the painting is impalpable <note>meaning it cannot be felt as a 3D object</note> in that what appears round and detached cannot be surrounded by the hands, and the mirror does the same; the mirror and the painting show the likeness of things surrounded by shadow and light; both appear to be far beyond their own surfaces.

And if you recognize that the mirror, by means of outlines and shadows and lights, makes things appear detached to you, and since you have among your colors shadows and lights more powerful than those of the mirror, certainly if you know how to compose them well together, your painting will also appear as a natural thing, seen in a large mirror.

# On the method of study.

Study science first, and then follow the practice born from that science.

Those who fall in love with practice without science are like the pilot who enters a ship without a rudder or a compass, who never has certainty of where he is going.

Practice must always be built upon good theory; of which <term>Perspective</term> <note>original: "Prespettiva"</note> is the guide and the gateway, and without this, nothing is done well in the matters of Painting.

<summary>Leonardo argues that the mirror is the ultimate standard for realism and asserts that artistic practice is aimless without a foundation in scientific theory and perspective.</summary>

# PERSPECTIVE

---

## On Perspective.

->Perspective is the best bridle of Painting.<-

Perspective is a demonstrative system <note>original: "ragione dimostrativa"</note> through which experience confirms that all things transmit their likeness to the eye by means of <term>pyramidal lines</term> <note>Leonardo uses this term to describe how light rays travel from an object to a single point in the eye, forming a pyramid shape</note>. By pyramidal lines, I mean those which start from the edges of the surfaces of bodies and, by converging from a distance, are led to a single point; which point, in this case, I will show to be located in the eye, the universal judge of all bodies...

---

<margin>The <term>pyramid of sight</term> is also treated by Alberti <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), a foundational Renaissance art theorist</note>, but he differs from Leonardo in the specifics. Both, in the broad strokes of their theory, may have drawn from ideas commonly accepted by painters of the time.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo defines perspective as a mathematical and experiential science, explaining how visual information travels in a pyramidal shape from objects to the eye. He establishes the eye as the central "judge" of physical reality through these geometric principles.</summary>

<keywords>perspective, painting, Leonardo da Vinci, Leon Battista Alberti, pyramidal lines, pyramid of sight, optics, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of perspective, moving from the geometric definition of the "pyramid of sight" to the specific classification of the three types of perspective and the introduction of atmospheric effects.</meta>

## On the Three Natures of Perspective.

There are three natures of perspective <gloss>original: "I say that there are three sorts of perspectives"</gloss>. The first is concerned with the principle of diminution, and is called **diminutive perspective**, dealing with objects as they recede from the eye. The second contains within it the method of varying colors as they recede from the eye. The third and final one extends to the explanation of how objects must be less finished <note>rendered with less detail and softer edges</note> the further they recede. And these shall be their names: **linear perspective**, **perspective of color**, and **perspective of disappearance** <gloss>original: "of sharpness or the finished quality of the drawing"</gloss> <note>Leonardo uses "spedizione" to refer to the loss of definition or "vanishing" of detail in the distance</note>.

## On Aerial Perspective.

There is another perspective which I call **aerial**, because through the variations of the atmosphere one can recognize the different distances of various buildings that appear to start from a single line at their base. For example, if one were to see many buildings beyond a wall, so that they all appeared above the edge of said wall to be of the same size, and you wished in a painting to make one appear further away than the other, you must represent a somewhat thick atmosphere. You know that in such atmospheres, the furthest things seen within it—such as mountains—because of the great quantity of air that exists between the eye...

<summary>Leonardo categorizes perspective into three distinct branches—linear, color, and loss of detail—and introduces "aerial perspective" as a method for showing depth through atmospheric density.</summary>

<keywords>linear perspective, perspective of color, perspective of disappearance, aerial perspective, atmospheric effects, Trattato della pittura, Leonardo da Vinci, artistic depth</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of aerial perspective, specifically how the color blue and the position of the sun affect the perception of distance in a landscape.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>

between your eye and the mountain, the mountain appears blue, almost the color of the air when the sun is in the east <note>original: "levante"</note>. Therefore, you shall make the first building above the aforementioned wall in its own color; make the one further away less defined <note>original: "profilato," referring to the sharpness of the outlines</note> and more blue. That which you wish to be as much further away again, make it that much more blue; that which you want to be five times further away, make it five times more blue. This rule will ensure that buildings which appear to be the same size along a single line will be clearly recognized for which is more distant and which is larger than the others.

---

<term>Aerial Perspective: the technique of representing distance by modifying colors and clarity to mimic the effect of the atmosphere.</term>
<term>Levante: the East, or the direction of the rising sun.</term>

<summary>Leonardo provides a practical rule for aerial perspective, instructing the painter to increase the blue tint and soften the outlines of objects in proportion to their distance from the viewer.</summary>

<keywords>aerial perspective, color of the air, blue, distance, atmospheric effects, painting technique, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# ANATOMY

---

### **How it is necessary for the painter to know anatomy ¹.**

It is a necessary thing for the painter, in order to be a good composer of limbs <note>original: "membrificatore"</note> in the attitudes and gestures that can be made for nude figures, to know the anatomy of nerves, bones, muscles, and tendons. This is so that he may know, in different movements and exertions, which nerve or muscle is the cause of such movement, and to make only those evident and enlarged, and not the others throughout. Many do this who, in order to appear like great draftsmen, make their nudes woody and without grace, so that they appear to the sight like a sack

---

¹ L. ALBERTI, (*Treatise on Painting and Statuary*, Milan, Classics, 1804) at pages 55-56: "...just as in dressing a figure one must first draw the nude beneath, which we then wish to wrap around with garments, so in painting a nude, one must first arrange and place the bones and muscles in their proper places, which you must then cover with flesh and skin in such a way that it is not difficult to recognize in which place those muscles are situated." <note>This citation refers to Leon Battista Alberti, a foundational Renaissance humanist and architect who argued that art should be grounded in scientific principles.</note>

<vocab>Anatomy, Painter, Anatomy <note>original: "notomia," an archaic spelling</note>, Leon Battista Alberti, Composer of limbs, Muscles, Nerves, Bones, The Nude</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that a painter must understand internal anatomy—bones, muscles, and nerves—to accurately depict the human body in motion without making it look unnaturally stiff or "woody." It includes a supporting citation from the Renaissance theorist Leon Battista Alberti regarding the structural layering of a figure.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy for artists, Leon Battista Alberti, figure drawing, musculature, Renaissance art theory, artistic proportion</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dangers of over-emphasizing anatomical detail, following the previous page's warning against making figures look like a "sack of nuts."</meta>

of nuts rather than a human surface, or a bundle of radishes rather than muscular nudes.

---

O <term>anatomical painter</term> <note>original: "pittore natomista"; an artist who studies dissection to improve their work</note>, take care that too much knowledge of the bones, tendons, and muscles does not cause you to become a "woody" painter, by wanting your <term>nudes</term> <note>original: "ignudi"; the standard artistic term for undraped figures</note> to display all their internal feelings. Therefore, wishing to remedy this, observe in what manner the muscles in the elderly or the thin cover or clothe their bones; and beyond this, note the rule of how these same muscles fill the superficial spaces that are interposed between them. Note which are the muscles that are never lost to sight at any degree of fatness, and which are the muscles whose points of contact are lost at the slightest <term>corpulence</term> <note>original: "pinguedine"; fatness or fatty tissue</note>. Many times, several muscles become a single muscle as one grows fat, and many times, in thinning or aging, a single muscle becomes several muscles. Regarding this discourse, all their particularities will be demonstrated in their proper place, and especially in the spaces located between the joints of each limb.

<vocab>anatomical painter, muscles, bones, tendons, nudes, corpulence, anatomy</vocab>

<keywords>anatomy for artists, musculature, artistic technique, aging, corpulence, anatomical painter</keywords>

# BEAUTY, GRACE,

## On the selection of bodies.

The long winter evenings should be used by young students to study the materials prepared during the summer.

That is to say, you should gather all the nude studies you have made during the summer, and make a selection of the best limbs and bodies among them, and put them into practice and commit them well to memory.

---

Then, in the following summer, you shall make a selection of someone who is well-built in the torso and who has not been raised in tight doublets¹, so that the person's form is not distorted.

---

¹ <note>original: "giubboni"</note> Coarsely, so that he is not found to be displaced from his natural posture. <note>The author warns that tight-fitting Renaissance clothing like doublets could permanently alter a model's natural posture or muscle definition.</note>

<term>Propriety</term> <note>original: "convenienza"</note> — The principle of artistic fitness or decorum, ensuring every part of a painting suits the subject and setting.

<term>Selection of bodies</term> <note>original: "elezione de' corpi"</note> — The practice of choosing the most beautiful parts from various models to create an idealized "perfect" figure, a common technique in Renaissance art.

<summary>This page advises art students to spend their winter reviewing summer sketches to memorize ideal proportions and warns them to choose models whose bodies have not been deformed by tight clothing.</summary>
<keywords>artistic training, anatomy, nude studies, Renaissance fashion, ideal beauty, proportion</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical advice for art students on how to synthesize their anatomical studies into graceful compositions and how to select ideal facial features for their figures.</meta>

from his nature, and you shall have him perform graceful and gallant actions. If this model does not show his muscles clearly within the outlines of his limbs, it does not matter at all; it is enough for you to obtain only the good poses from him, and then you can correct the limbs with those studies you practiced during the winter.

### On the selection of beautiful faces.

It seems to me no small grace in a painter who gives good expressions to his figures. He who does not possess this grace by nature may acquire it through accidental study in this manner: look to take the good parts of many beautiful faces, whose beauty is confirmed <gloss>confirmed as beautiful</gloss> more by public reputation than by your own judgment, because you could deceive yourself by choosing faces that have a similarity to your own. For it often seems that such similarities please us, and if you were ugly, you would choose faces that are not beautiful and would create ugly faces, as many painters do, for often the figures resemble the master. Therefore, take beauties, as I tell you, and commit them to memory.

---

<term>Grace</term> <note>original: "grazia"</note> — An aesthetic quality beyond mere technical correctness, often described as a "je ne sais quoi" that makes a figure appear effortless and charming.

<term>Accidental study</term> <note>original: "accidentale studio"</note> — Here meaning supplementary or deliberate practice intended to acquire a skill one was not born with.

<term>Resemble the master</term> <note>original: "somigliano il maestro"</note> — A common Renaissance observation (often attributed to Leonardo da Vinci) that artists unconsciously paint their own physical likeness into their subjects.

### **On the selection of the atmosphere that gives grace to faces.**

If you have a courtyard that you can cover at your pleasure with a linen awning <gloss>original: "tendalina," likely a small linen cloth</gloss>, this light will be good; or when you wish to portray someone, portray them in bad weather toward evening, having the subject <gloss>original: "ritratto," meaning the person being portrayed</gloss> stand with their back against one of the walls of said courtyard. Pay attention in the streets, toward evening, to the faces of men and women when the weather is bad: how much grace and sweetness is seen in them! Therefore, you, the painter, should have a courtyard arranged with the walls painted black, with a slight overhang of the roof above the wall; let it be ten cubits <term>braccia</term> <note>A "braccio" or "arm" was a common Italian measure of length, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> wide, twenty long, and ten high. When it is sunny, [be sure to] cover it with a tent, or else portray your subject for an hour toward evening, when it is cloudy or misty; and this is the perfect atmosphere.

### **On the grace of the limbs.**

The limbs, together with the body, must be arranged with grace according to the purpose of the effect you wish the figure to produce. If you wish to make a figure that demonstrates elegance in itself, you must make the limbs delicate and extended, without showing too many muscles; and those few that you do show for the purpose, make them soft—that is, of little prominence—and with shadows that are not harsh. The limbs, especially the arms, should be "unknotted" <note>original: "disnodate," meaning relaxed or fluidly posed rather than stiffly jointed</note>; that is, no limb should stay in a straight line with the...

---

<note>1 A linen tent or awning?</note>
<note>2 The person being portrayed.</note>

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to use soft, diffused evening light or shaded courtyards to capture "grace" in portraits and emphasizes that elegant figures should have relaxed, subtly defined musculature.</summary>

If the hip, which is the pole of the man, is found—due to the stance taken—to have the right side higher than the left, you shall make the joint of the upper shoulder fall in a perpendicular line over the most prominent part of the hip <gloss>On the most protruding point</gloss>, and let this right shoulder be lower than the left. The "little fountain" <note>The "fontanella" refers to the pit of the throat or the sternal notch.</note> should always remain above the center of the joint of the supporting foot <gloss>The foot that rests on the ground and sustains the weight of the body</gloss>. The leg that does not support the weight should have its knee lower than the other and close to the other leg <gloss>How many figures, especially in the Umbrian school, are posed in this way!</gloss>. The attitudes of the head and arms are infinite; therefore, I will not dwell on giving any specific rule for them, provided that they are easy and pleasing, with various twists and turns and with the joints "unknotted," so that they do not look like pieces of wood <gloss>L. B. **ALBERTI** [*Treatise on Painting* Book II, chapter XXIII] teaches that attitudes should not be forced, but should have grace and sweetness.</gloss>.

## Beauty is the enemy of affectation.

And you, painter, strive to make your works such that they draw viewers to them, and hold them with great admiration and delight; do not draw them in only to then drive them away, as the air does to one who, in the night-time, leaps naked from bed to contemplate the quality of that air—whether cloudy or serene—who, being immediately driven away by the cold of it, re-

</note>

<meta>This page continues the metaphor of the viewer as a person rising from bed, transitioning from the warning against repelling the audience to advice on prioritizing natural beauty over excessive ornamentation and stiff hairstyles.</meta>

turns to the bed from which he first rose; but make your works like that air which, in warm weather, draws men from their beds and holds them with delight to possess the summer coolness. And do not wish to be a practitioner before you are learned, nor let avarice overcome the glory that is deservedly acquired from such an art.

Do you not see that among human beauties, it is the most beautiful face that stops the passers-by, and not their rich ornaments? And this I say to you, who adorn your figures with gold or other rich trimmings. Do you not see the shining beauties of youth diminish in their excellence because of excessive and overly cultivated ornaments? Have you not seen mountain women, wrapped in uncultivated and poor clothes, acquire greater beauty than those who are adorned?

Do not use affected hairstyles or arrangements of heads, where—near to those with dull wits—if a single hair is placed more to one side than the other, the one who wears it promises himself <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "se ne promette"</note> great infamy, believing that those around him will abandon every previous thought and only speak of that hair and only criticize that; and such people always have the mirror and the comb for their counselor, and the wind is their capital enemy, the disarranger of their polished hair.

Therefore, for your heads, make the hair play together with an imagined wind around youthful faces, and adorn them gracefully with diverse turnings; do not do as those who plaster them with glues and make the faces <gloss>2</gloss> appear as if they were glazed.

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to prioritize natural grace and intellectual mastery over gaudy decorations and rigid, over-styled hair, which he mocks as the vanity of the dull-witted. ### **On the Suitability of the Limbs.**

And I remind you again to take great care in giving limbs to your figures, so that they appear consistent with the size of the body and likewise with the age; that is, the young should have few muscles in their limbs and veins, with a delicate surface, and rounded limbs of a pleasing color; for men, let them be sinewy and full of muscles; for the elderly, let them have wrinkled surfaces, rough and veiny, with very prominent tendons.

### **The Different Ages.**

Just as the elderly must be depicted with sluggish and slow movements, with legs bent at the knees when they stand still, and feet parallel and distant from one another, backs sloping downwards, the head forward and bowed, and the arms not too extended.

---

<margin>1 <term>Leon Battista Alberti</term> <note>A foundational Renaissance humanist and architect (1404–1472) whose theories on perspective and proportion deeply influenced Leonardo.</note> writes: ... they (the limbs) are well-proportioned when they correspond both in size and in function, and in species and in colors, and in other similar things—if there are any more—to beauty and majesty. *Treatise on Painting and Sculpture, cited p. 55.*</margin>

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to match the anatomy and movement of figures to their specific age, contrasting the smooth limbs of youth with the sinewy muscles of men and the stooped, sluggish posture of the elderly.</summary>
<keywords>artistic anatomy, suitability of limbs, proportions, Leon Battista Alberti, aging in art, muscle definition, posture</keywords>

### How small children have joints that are the opposite of men in their thickness.

Small children have all their joints thin, while the spaces located between one joint and another are thick. And this happens because the skin over the joints is alone, without any other flesh than that of a sinewy nature, which girds and binds the bones together; and the moist fleshiness is found between one joint and another, enclosed between the skin and the bone. But because the bones are thicker at the joints than between the joints, as a man grows, the flesh comes to lose that superfluity that stayed between the skin and the bone, whereby the skin draws closer to the bone and the limbs become thinner. And over

A man's arm span is equal to
his height.

shall be one-sixth of a man; from the top of the chest to the hairline

<note>
1. Implied: measure.
2. Let it be measured by taking one of the limbs as a unit. Vitruvius takes the foot as the unit of measurement; ALBERTI considers it more worthy to take the head. Furthermore, he found that the measurement of the foot is the same as the distance from the chin to the top of the head. *Treatise on Painting and Sculpture*, cited, pp. 55-56.
</note>

# On the Proportions of the Human Body

The distance from the sole of the foot to the bottom of the knee shall be the fourth part of the man; from the said knee to the start of the genitals shall be the fourth part of the man. <note>Leonardo is dividing the body into four equal segments: from the feet to the knee, knee to genitals, genitals to the chest, and chest to the top of the head.</note>

The parts that are found between the joints of the body:

| Body Part | Proportion of Total Height |
| :--- | :--- |
| From the sole of the foot to the knee | One quarter (1/4) |
| From the knee to the hip joint | One quarter (1/4) |
| The length of the foot | One seventh (1/7) |
| The length of the ear | One third of the face (1/3) |

-> **Of the Arm and Hand** <-

The distance from the interior of the elbow to the tip of the longest finger is the fifth part of the man; <margin>The cubit measurement</margin> and from the elbow to the pit of the arm shall be the eighth part of the man. The whole hand shall be the tenth part of the man. <note>original: "palmo"</note>

The <term>genitals</term> <note>The anatomical center of the human figure when standing upright</note> mark the exact center of the man. If a man were to lie on his back with his arms and legs extended, and a pair of compasses were centered on his navel, the fingers and toes of both hands and feet would touch the circumference of a circle described therefrom. <note>This is a direct reference to the "Vitruvian Man" concept, where the human form fits perfectly within a circle and a square.</note>

# MOVEMENT AND EXPRESSION

---

## On expressive movements ^1.

That figure is most praiseworthy which, through its actions, best expresses the passion of its soul. <note>The author emphasizes that physical gesture must serve as a window into the subject's internal emotional state.</note>

The figures of men should possess actions appropriate to their activities, in such a way that, by looking at them, you understand what they are thinking or saying. These actions will be well-learned by those who imitate the motions of the mute, who speak with the movements of their hands and eyes. <note>The "mute" or "silent ones" were often cited in Renaissance art theory as the ultimate models for gesture, as they rely entirely on physical rhetoric to communicate.</note>

---

<note>Footnote at the bottom of the page</note>
^1 Also Leon Battista Alberti, *Treatise on Painting*, Book II, Chapter XVIII: "How much the painted history will hold the attention of the viewer if the figures used therein represent the movements of the soul very well!" <note>original: "moti dell'animo"</note> and, on the same subject, chapters XIX, XX, XXI, XXII.

<note>Vocabulary: Leon Battista Alberti, Treatise on Painting, expressive movements, movements of the soul, painting, expression</note>

<summary>This page outlines the principle that a painted figure's excellence is judged by how effectively its physical gestures convey internal emotions, suggesting that artists study the non-verbal communication of the mute.</summary>

<keywords>art theory, expression, gesture, Leon Battista Alberti, movements of the soul, painting techniques, Renaissance aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional treatise on art theory, specifically focusing on the observation of human gesture and the practical methods for sketching spontaneous emotional expressions.</meta>

...and eyebrows and the whole person, in their desire to express the concepts of their minds.

And do not laugh at me because I propose to you a teacher without a tongue, who must teach you an art that he himself cannot perform; for he will teach you better through deeds than all others can with words. Do not despise such advice, for they are the masters of movements, and they understand from a distance what a person is saying when he coordinates the motions of his hands with his words.

### On learning the movements of man.

The movements of man must be learned—after gaining knowledge of the limbs and the body as a whole—through all the motions of the limbs and joints. Then, with a brief notation of a few signs, observe the actions of men in their various <term>accidents</term> <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to sudden emotional states or spontaneous physical events rather than mishaps.</note>, without them noticing that you are watching them. For if they become aware of such observation, their minds will be focused on you, and they will have abandoned the intensity of their action, upon which the mind was previously entirely intent. 

This intensity is seen when two angry people contend together, each believing they are in the right; they move their eyebrows, arms, and other limbs with great ferocity, using actions appropriate to their intentions and their words. You could not achieve this if you asked them to feign such anger or any other state, such as laughter, weeping, pain, admiration, fear, and the like. Therefore, be eager to carry with you a small notebook of paper... and with a <term>silverpoint</term> <note>original: "stile d'argento." A drawing instrument made of a silver wire or rod used on specially prepared paper, common in the Renaissance before the widespread use of graphite pencils.</note> briefly note such movements, and similar—

<summary>The author advises artists to study the gestures of the mute and to secretly sketch people in the midst of genuine emotional outbursts, as authentic expression cannot be faked upon request.</summary>

<keywords>artistic observation, human movement, silverpoint, emotional expression, sketching from life, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s advice on the importance of sketching from life and transitions into the necessity of using physical gestures to express the internal "intentions of the mind."</meta>

mind, note the actions of those standing around and their distribution <note>original: "compartizione." This refers to the spatial arrangement or grouping of figures in a scene.</note>, and this will soon teach you how to compose narrative scenes <term>istories</term> <note>In Renaissance art theory, a "history" or "istoria" was the highest form of painting, depicting a complex narrative with multiple figures.</note>. And when you have filled your book, set it aside and keep it for your future purposes; then take up another and do the same. This will be an extremely useful practice for your method of composing, regarding which I shall write a specific book that will follow the knowledge of the figures and limbs in particular, and the variety of their joints <term>gionture</term>.

### On the movements of the hands and arms.

The hands and arms in all their operations must demonstrate the intention of their mover <note>The "mover" refers to the soul or the mind directing the body.</note> as much as possible, because those who possess a refined judgment accompany their mental intentions with these movements in all their actions <gloss>¹</gloss>. Good orators, when they wish to persuade their audience of something, always accompany their words with their hands and arms; although some foolish men do not care for such ornament, and appear on their rostrums like wooden statues, through whose mouths the voice of some hidden man passes as if through a tube. This habit is a great defect in living men and much more so in painted figures <term>figure finte</term>; if these are not aided by their creator with prompt actions suited to the intention you imagine to be in such a figure, then that figure will be judged twice dead: dead because it is not alive, and dead in its action. But to return to our purpose, below we shall illustrate and discuss various <term>accidents</term> <note>Spontaneous physical expressions of emotion.</note>, namely: of the—

---

<gloss>¹</gloss> Because those who speak with a passionate soul always accompany their reasoning with them.

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to keep sketchbooks of real-life observations to master narrative composition and emphasizes that figures must use expressive hand and arm gestures to convey their internal thoughts, lest they appear "twice dead."</summary>

the movement of the angry man, of sorrow, of fear, of sudden fright, of weeping, of flight, of desire, of commanding, of laziness, and of diligence and the like <gloss>¹</gloss> <note>original: "sollecitudine." In this context, it refers to being industrious or attentive.</note>.

### On laughing and weeping.

You shall not make the face of one who weeps with the same movements as one who laughs, even though they often resemble one another; rather, the true method is to vary them, just as the event of weeping is varied from the event of laughter. For in weeping, the brows and the mouth vary according to the causes of the weeping: for some weep with anger, some with fear, some for tenderness and joy, some out of suspicion, some for pain and torment, and some for pity and grief for lost relatives or friends. Of these types of weeping, some appear desperate, some moderate, some merely tearful, and some involve screaming; some with the face turned toward the sky and with the hands held low, having their fingers interwoven; others fearful, with the shoulders raised to the ears; and thus they are positioned according to the aforementioned causes. He who sheds tears raises his eyebrows at their joints <term>gionture</term> <note>The points where the eyebrows meet the bridge of the nose.</note> and presses them together, creating wrinkles above and in the middle <gloss>²</gloss>; the corners of the mouth are turned downward <gloss>³</gloss>; whereas he who laughs has them high, and the eyebrows are open and wide.

---

<gloss>¹</gloss> The movements of the soul—said L. B. Alberti—are known through the movements of the body. (*Treatise on Painting and Statuary*, cited p. 64). <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472) was a foundational Renaissance humanist and architect whose theories deeply influenced Leonardo.</note>
<gloss>²</gloss> It is implied: of the eyebrows.
<gloss>³</gloss> It is implied: he has.

<summary>Leonardo distinguishes between the physical expressions of various emotions, specifically detailing the anatomical differences between the facial mechanics of weeping and laughing.</summary>

### How an angry figure should be made.

For the angry figure, you shall have him holding someone by the hair, with the head twisted toward the ground, and with one of his knees on the ribs, and with the right arm raising the fist high: this man should have his hair standing up <gloss>¹</gloss> <note>original: "ritti" (upright/bristling)</note>, the eyebrows low and narrow, the teeth clenched, and the two corners of the mouth arched, the neck thick, and in front—due to the leaning toward the enemy—it should be full of wrinkles.

### How a desperate man is depicted.

For the desperate man, you shall have him striking himself with a knife, and with his hands having torn his garments, and let one of those hands be in the act of tearing at the wound; and you shall make him with his feet apart and the legs somewhat bent and the body similarly toward the ground, with hair torn and scattered.

---

¹ Upright.

---

# COMPOSITION

---

## The Sketch.

The sketching of narrative scenes <gloss>1</gloss> should be done quickly, and the defining of limbs <term>membrificare: the process of articulating the parts of a body</term> should not be too finished. Be content merely with the positions of these limbs, which you can then finish at your leisure as it pleases you.

### **Why placing narrative scenes one above the other is a practice to be avoided.**

This universal custom practiced by painters on the walls of chapels is strongly and reasonably to be blamed, because they place one narrative scene on one level with its landscape and buildings, and then they rise up one

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Regarding the composition of narrative scenes, the following fragments should be compared with chapters 16 and 17 of Book II of *On Painting* by Leon Battista Alberti <note>Alberti was a foundational Renaissance humanist and architect whose 1435 treatise codified the laws of linear perspective.</note>.

<margin>Composition, sketch, painting, L. B. Alberti, narrative scenes, chapels, articulation of limbs</margin>

<summary>This page provides advice on the importance of loose initial sketching and criticizes the common Renaissance practice of stacking multiple narrative scenes vertically on chapel walls, which violates spatial logic.</summary>
<keywords>composition, sketching technique, Leon Battista Alberti, chapel murals, narrative painting, spatial perspective</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>Continuing the critique of vertical narrative stacking on chapel walls, where the author argues that multiple horizons on a single wall create a visual impossibility for the viewer.</meta>

another level and create a story, varying the point of view <note>original: "punto"</note> from the first; and then they add a third and a fourth, so that a single wall is seen with four points of view, which is the height of foolishness for such masters. We know that the point of view is placed at the eye of the spectator of the narrative; and if you were to ask: "What method should I use to represent the life of a person divided into many stories on a single wall?" to this I answer that you must place the first foreground with the point of view at the eye level of the spectators of that story. On said plane, depict the first story on a large scale, and then, by gradually diminishing the figures and buildings <term>casamenti: large urban structures or apartment blocks</term> across various hills and plains, you will create the entire setting of that story. For the rest of the wall's height, you shall paint large trees in comparison to the figures, or angels if they suit the subject of the story, or birds or clouds or similar things; otherwise, do not trouble yourself with it, for all your work will be false.

### Perspective effects in narrative scenes.

That object, or the figure of that object, which is closest to the eye will be shown with the most distinct and clear outlines. And for this reason, you, painter—who under the name of a "practical man" <note>original: "pratico"; one who relies on habit and experience while neglecting the underlying theory of art</note> feign the sight of a head viewed from a close distance with finished brushstrokes and harsh, crude hatching—know that you are mistaken.

<margin>perspective, point of view, divided, buildings, trees, practical man, painting, narrative scene</margin>

<summary>The author argues against using multiple vanishing points on a single wall, proposing instead a single unified perspective where depth is suggested by diminishing scale and atmospheric elements. He also warns "practical" painters against using overly harsh outlines for close-up subjects.</summary>
<keywords>linear perspective, vanishing point, narrative composition, atmospheric perspective, artistic theory, Renaissance painting techniques</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's critique of "practical" painters who use harsh outlines, Leonardo now explains the necessity of soft edges (sfumato) and the importance of sketching from real life to master narrative composition.</meta>

You are mistaken, because at whatever distance you imagine your figure to be, it is always finished to the degree appropriate for that position, even if at a long distance the knowledge of its boundaries is lost; and despite this, one still sees a "smoky" <note>original: "finito fumoso"; a reference to Leonardo's famous *sfumato* technique where edges are softened like smoke</note> finish rather than clear and harsh outlines and profiles. Therefore, one must conclude that in a work where the eye of the viewer can come close, all parts of that painting must be finished in their respective degrees with the utmost diligence. Furthermore, the foreground elements should be defined with clear and distinct boundaries against their background, while those further away should be well-finished but with "smokier" boundaries—that is, more blurred, or you might say, less distinct. For those even further away, successively observe what was said above: namely, less distinct boundaries, then less distinct limbs, and finally the whole figure and color becoming less distinct.

### **On the method of learning well how to compose**
### **figures together in narrative scenes.**

When you have thoroughly learned Perspective <term>Prospettiva: the mathematical science of representing three-dimensional space on a flat surface</term>, and have committed to memory all the limbs and bodies of things, you should take pleasure, quite often, as you go about for your amusement, in seeing and considering the positions and actions of men while talking, arguing, laughing, or brawling together; what actions are in them, and what actions are performed by the bystanders, the mediators, or the onlookers of these things. Note these down with brief marks, in this manner, in a small notebook of yours. This you should always carry with you, and let it be of tinted paper, so that you do not have to <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to the use of a silverpoint stylus which requires prepared tinted paper.</note>

<vocab>Perspective, painting, smoky boundaries, limbs, small notebook, men, narrative scenes, diligence</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that distance should be represented by softening edges (sfumato) rather than losing detail, and advises students to carry a notebook to sketch real-life human interactions for better compositions.</summary>
<keywords>sfumato, atmospheric perspective, sketching from life, artistic observation, narrative composition, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo advises the artist to observe and sketch the spontaneous actions of people in the streets to master narrative composition.</meta>

...erase, but rather change from the old into something new; for these are not things to be erased, but rather preserved with great diligence. This is because the infinite forms and actions of things are so numerous that the memory is not capable of retaining them; therefore, you shall preserve these sketches as your authors and masters.

### **On depicting one who speaks among several people.**

When you wish to represent someone speaking among many people, you should consider the subject matter of which he has to treat, and adapt to him the actions belonging to that matter. That is: if it is a persuasive matter, let the actions be appropriate to the purpose; if it is an explanatory matter involving various reasons, let the speaker take one finger of the left hand with the first two fingers of the right hand, having the two smaller fingers closed <note>Leonardo describes a specific rhetorical gesture used to "enumerate" points of an argument</note>. Have his face turned toward the people, with the mouth somewhat open so that he appears to be speaking; and, if he was sitting, let him appear to raise himself somewhat upright with his head forward; and if you depict him standing, make him lean somewhat with his chest and head toward the people.

You shall depict the audience there as silent and attentive, all looking at the orator's face with admiring gestures. You should make the mouths of some old men, out of wonder at the opinions they have heard, with the corners of the mouth pulled down, drawing back many folds in the cheeks, and with the eyebrows raised at the joints, which create many wrinkles across the forehead. Some who are sitting should have the fingers of their hands woven together, holding their left knee <note>original: "stanco ginocchio"; literally "tired knee," but used here to mean the left side</note> within them; others with one knee over the other, upon the...

<meta>Text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, describing the varied seated postures of the audience.</meta>

## Notes for the Last Supper <note>original: "Cenacolo"; refers to the dining hall or the painting of the Last Supper</note>.

One who was drinking leaves the cup <note>original: "zaina"; a large drinking vessel or mug</note> in its place, and turns his head toward the speaker <note>original: "proponitore"; the one proposing the topic, in this case, Christ</note>.
Another weaves the fingers of his hands together, and with stiffened eyebrows turns to his companion; the other, with open hands, shows their palms, and raises his shoulders toward his ears, making a mouth of wonder.
Another speaks into the ear of another, and he who listens twists toward him and offers him his ears, holding a knife in one hand and in the other the bread half-divided by that knife. Another, in turning, holding a knife in his hand, knocks over a cup upon the table with that hand.
Another places his hands upon the table and watches; another blows upon his morsel; another leans down to see the speaker, and shades his eyes with his hand; another draws back behind the one who leans down, and sees the speaker between the wall and the leaning man.

## Way to represent a battle.

You shall first make the smoke of the artillery, mixed into the air together with the dust stirred up by the movement of the horses and the combatants. Which...

<summary>Leonardo provides vivid, cinematic descriptions of the gestures and reactions for the figures in his Last Supper, followed by the beginning of a guide on painting a battle scene.</summary>
<keywords>Last Supper, gestures, expression, composition, battle painting, artillery, movement, anatomy of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo began describing the visual elements of a battle scene, specifically the initial mixing of artillery smoke and dust.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>

you shall use this mixture in this way: the dust, because it is an earthly and heavy thing, and although because of its fineness it easily rises and mixes into the air, nonetheless it willingly returns downward; its highest ascent is made by its finest part, therefore there it will be least seen and will appear almost the color of the air. The smoke that mixes within the dusty air, the more it rises to a certain height, the more it will appear like a dark cloud, and at the summits, the smoke will be seen more clearly than the dust.

The smoke will lean toward a somewhat bluish color, and the dust will keep its own color. From the side where the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear much more luminous than from the opposite side. The combatants, the more they are within said turbulence, the less they will be seen, and the less difference there will be between their lights and their shadows.

You shall make the faces and the people and the air and the musketeers <note>original: "scoppettieri"; soldiers armed with an early type of firearm called a 'scoppietto'</note> together with those nearby turn red; and this redness, the further it moves from its cause, the more it is lost. And the figures that are between you and the light, being distant, will appear dark against a light field; and their legs, the closer they approach the ground, the less they will be seen, because the dust is thicker and denser there.

And if you make horses running outside the crowd, make little clouds of dust distant from one another, as far as the interval of the jumps made by the horse might be; and that cloud which is furthest from said horse should be least seen—rather, let it be high, scattered, and thin—and the one nearest should be more evident, smaller, and denser.

<gloss>mixture, dust, smoke, air, turbulence, lights, shadows, musketeers, horses</gloss>

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical effects of a battlefield, detailing how dust and smoke interact with light and how distance and density affect the visibility of soldiers and horses.</summary>
<keywords>painting technique, atmospheric perspective, battle scene, optics, light and shadow, dust, smoke, musketeers, horses in motion</keywords>

The air should be full of arrows <note>original: "saettume"; a collective term for projectiles like arrows or bolts</note> of various kinds:
some ascending, some descending, and some traveling in a straight line;
and the balls from the muskets <note>original: "scoppietti"; early hand-held firearms</note> should be accompanied

And for the figures in the foreground, you shall make the hair and
eyebrows dusty, as well as other flat areas capable of holding dust.
You shall make the victors running with their hair and other
lightweight things scattered to the wind: with their brows lowered, and driving
opposite limbs forward; that is, if a man moves
his right foot forward, let his left arm also come
forward. And if you depict someone who has fallen, you shall show the mark
of his sliding through the dust, which has been turned into
bloody mud; and around the half-liquid
earth, you shall show the imprinted footsteps of the
men and horses who have passed there.

You shall show some horse dragging its dead
master, and behind it, leaving in the dust and
mud the mark of the dragged body.

You shall make the conquered and beaten pale, with their eyebrows raised
where they meet, and the flesh above
them should be full of painful wrinkles. The sides of the

nostrils <note>original: "anarise"; an archaic term for nostrils</note> and ending at the corner of the eye; the
nostrils should be high, causing said folds; the lips
arched to reveal the upper teeth, the teeth parted as if
shouting in lamentation; let one of the hands
form a shield for the fearful eyes, turning the palm <note>original: "il dentro"</note>
toward the enemy, while the other stays on the ground to support the
raised torso.

Others you shall make screaming with their mouths wide open and fleeing; you shall place many kinds of weapons among the feet of the combatants, such as broken shields, lances, shattered swords, and other similar things; you shall make dead men, some half-covered by the dust, others entirely; the dust that mixes with the spilled blood turns into red mud, and you shall see the blood of its true color¹ running in a winding course from the body into the dust; others, while dying, shall grind their teeth, roll their eyes, clench their fists against their bodies, and have their legs contorted.

One might see someone disarmed and beaten down by the enemy turning toward that enemy to take cruel and bitter revenge with bites and scratches; you might see some light horse running with its mane scattered to the wind, charging among the enemies and doing great damage with its hooves; you would see some crippled man, fallen to the ground, making a cover for himself with his shield, and the enemy, leaning down low, struggling to deal him death.

One might see many men fallen in a heap over a dead horse. You will see some victors leaving the fight and exiting the crowd, wiping their eyes and cheeks with both hands, which are covered in mud formed by the watering of their eyes because of² the dust.

You would see the rescue squads standing full of hope and suspicion, with their brows knit, shading them with their hands and peering through the thick and confused haze <term>caligine: a dense, dark mist or smoky atmosphere</term> to be attentive to the command.

# THE MANNER OF DRESSING
## THE FIGURES

---

### **Concerning the cloths that clothe the figures and their folds <note>original: "De li panni che vestono le figure e pieghe loro"</note>.**

The cloths that clothe the figures must have their folds arranged to encircle the limbs they cover, in such a way that in the illuminated parts no folds of dark shadow are placed, and in the shaded parts no folds of too much brightness are made. The outlines of these folds should go in some parts surrounding the limbs covered by them, and not with outlines that cut across the limbs, nor with shadows that sink deeper than the surface of the clothed body. And in effect, the cloth should be adapted in such a way that it does not appear uninhabited—that is, that it does not appear like a bundle of cloth stripped from a man, as one sees many do, who be- <note>The original Italian "s'inna-" is the start of "s'innamorano" (fall in love with) or a similar construction, continuing on the next page.</note>

---

<margin>1. Leon Battista Alberti <note>A foundational Renaissance humanist and art theorist (1404–1472).</note> treats this subject briefly: *Treatise on Painting*, Book II, chapter XXIV.</margin>

<note>Technical vocabulary used in this section:
- **Cloths/Drapery** <term>panni</term>: The fabric or garments worn by subjects in a painting.
- **Folds** <term>pieghe</term>: The rhythmic creases in fabric that reveal the form beneath.
- **Illuminated** <term>alluminate</term>: Areas of the painting where light strikes directly.
- **Outlines/Contours** <term>lineamenti</term>: The descriptive lines that define the shape of the fabric and body.</note>

<summary>This page provides instructions on the realistic depiction of drapery, emphasizing that fabric should follow the anatomy of the body rather than appearing hollow or disconnected from the form. It references the classical theories of Leon Battista Alberti regarding the harmony between clothing and the human figure.</summary>

<keywords>drapery, folds, anatomy, light and shadow, Leon Battista Alberti, Renaissance painting technique, clothing figures</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on drapery from page 207, completing the sentence regarding artists who become overly enamored with complex folds at the expense of anatomical logic.</meta>

-fall in love so much with the various groupings of diverse folds that fill an entire figure, that they forget the purpose for which such cloth is made: namely, to dress and surround with grace the limbs where they rest, and not to fill everything with "bellies" or deflated bladders over the illuminated highlights of the limbs. I do not deny that one should make some beautiful folds, but let them be made in parts of the figure where the limbs, between themselves and the body, collect and gather such cloth <insert>1</insert> <note>original: "tal panno"</note>.

And above all, vary the cloths in your narrative scenes <note>original: "storie"; referring to complex multi-figure compositions</note>, as in making in some the folds with facing breaks <insert>2</insert>, and this is for dense cloths; let some cloth have soft folds, and their turns not lateral <insert>3</insert> but curved, and this happens in serges and satins <note>original: "saie e rasse"; types of fine wool or silk fabrics</note> and other thin cloths, such as linens, veils, and the like; and you shall also make cloth of few and large folds, as in thick cloths, like those seen in felts and coarse blankets <note>original: "schiavine"; a heavy, rough wool blanket often used by pilgrims</note> and other bed covers.

And these reminders I do not give to the masters, but to those who do not wish to teach; for certainly these are not masters, because he who does not teach is afraid that his profit will be taken from him, and he who values profit abandons study, which is contained in the works of nature—the mistress of painters—of which the things learned are forgotten, and those that have not been learned are never learned at all.

---

<insert>1 For example, between the arms and the torso.</insert>
<insert>2 Broken folds with high relief.</insert>
<insert>3 In a straight line.</insert>

<note>Technical vocabulary used in this section:
- **Cloths/Drapery** <term>panni</term>: The fabric or garments worn by subjects in a painting.
- **Folds** <term>pieghe</term>: The rhythmic creases in fabric that reveal the form beneath.
- **Nature** <term>natura</term>: The ultimate teacher for the artist, according to the author.
- **Masters** <term>maestri</term>: Established artists; the author distinguishes between true masters and those who hoard knowledge for profit.
- **Study** <term>studio</term>: The diligent observation and practice of art.
- **Serges/Satins** <term>saie, rasse</term>: Specific historical textiles requiring different painterly techniques.
- **Coarse blankets** <term>schiavine</term>: Heavy, utilitarian fabrics used to demonstrate large, simple folds.</note>

<summary>The author warns against excessive, "bladder-like" drapery that obscures the body, advising instead that folds should vary based on the weight and type of fabric. He concludes with a philosophical defense of teaching, arguing that true artists must prioritize the study of nature over financial gain.</summary>
