--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical execution of drapery folds discussed on page 208 to the historical and aesthetic decorum of costume design in painting. It continues the artist's advice on how to represent figures with dignity.</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>
<sig>14</sig>

The clothing of the figures should be suited to their age and to decorum; that is, the old man should be draped in a toga, and the young man adorned with a garment that occupies less of the neck from the tops of the shoulders upward, except for those who belong to a religious order. One should avoid as much as possible the fashions of one's own time <note>The author argues that contemporary fashion quickly becomes dated and looks ridiculous to future generations.</note>, unless the figures are meant to represent those who are buried in churches <note>Effigies or portraits of specific deceased individuals.</note>, so that we do not leave our successors to laugh at the foolish inventions of men, but rather leave them in admiration of the figures' dignity and beauty.

And I remember in my time, during my childhood, seeing men both small and large having all the edges of their garments jagged <note>original: "frappati"; referring to the 'dagging' or decorative slashing of fabric edges popular in the 15th century.</note> in every part, from head to foot and at the sides; and this invention seemed so beautiful to that age that they even jagged the jags themselves, and they wore their hoods and shoes in a similar way, with jagged crests of various colors emerging from the main seams of the garments.

Afterward, I saw shoes, caps, purses <note>original: "scarselle"</note>, weapons carried for offense, the collars of garments, the bottom edges of doublets <note>original: "giupponi"</note>, the tails of robes, and indeed even the mouths of those who wished to appear beautiful, were all finished with long and sharp points.

In the next age, the sleeves began to grow—

---

<margin>The bottom edges of the doublets.</margin>
<margin>From the tips of the feet to the mouth, the elegant ones were all a mass of pointed cut-work!</margin>

He emphasizes that painting should inspire admiration in future generations rather than mockery.</summary>

<page-num>210</page-num>

—and they were so large that each one by itself was greater than the rest of the garment; then they began to raise the clothing around the neck so high that at last they covered the entire head; then they began to strip the body in such a way <note>Likely referring to the 'off-the-shoulder' styles or deep necklines that followed the high collars.</note> that the clothes could not be supported by the shoulders, because they no longer rested upon them.

Then they began to lengthen the garments so much that men constantly had their arms burdened with fabric to keep from trampling it under their feet; then they went to such an extreme <note>original: "stremità"</note> that they wore clothes only down to the hips and to the elbows, and they were so tight that they suffered great torment <note>original: "supplicio"</note> from them, and many of the garments burst open from underneath; and the feet were so squeezed that the toes were forced one on top of the other and became burdened with calluses.

# LIGHT, SHADOW
# AND COLORS

---

### Proof of how all things placed in a location are all in the whole and all in the part ¹.

I say that if the facade of a building, or another square, or a landscape that is illuminated by the sun has a dwelling opposite to it, and in that wall which does not face the sun a small round hole <note>original: "spiracolo"</note> is made, then all the illuminated objects will send their likeness <note>original: "similitudine"; referring to the projected image</note> through the said hole and will appear inside the dwelling on the opposite wall, which should be white. There they will be exactly as they are, but upside down; and if you were to make similar holes in many places on the said wall, a similar effect would be produced in each one.

---

<note>¹ In this passage, the "<term>camera obscura</term>" <note>Latin for "dark room"; an optical device that led to the invention of photography</note> is already described.</note>

<note>Vocabulary: camera obscura, small opening, likeness/image, building, proof, places, countryside/landscape</note>

<summary>This page describes the fundamental principle of the camera obscura, explaining how light passing through a small aperture projects an inverted image of an illuminated exterior onto an interior wall.</summary>
<keywords>optics, camera obscura, light projection, Leonardo da Vinci, perspective, physics of light</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on light, moving from the physical principles of the camera obscura to practical advice for painters on how to illuminate figures in various environments.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>

## **How light should be given to figures.**

Light must be used according to the natural setting where you imagine your figure to be. That is, if you imagine it in the sun, make the shadows dark and the areas of light large <note>original: "gran piazze di lumi"</note>, and imprint the shadows of all surrounding bodies upon the ground. And if you depict it in gloomy weather, make little difference between the lights and the shadows, and without creating any other shadow at the feet. And if you depict it inside a house, make a great difference between the lights and the shadows, with shadow upon the ground; but if you depict a window covered with cloth <note>original: "finestra impannata"; a common practice before glass was affordable, using oiled cloth to diffuse light</note> and a white dwelling, make little difference between the lights and the shadows. And if it is illuminated by fire, you shall make the lights reddish and powerful, and the shadows dark; and the projection of the shadows <note>original: "battimento dell’ombre"</note> against the walls or the ground should be sharp ¹, and the further it moves away ² from the body, the broader and larger it should become. And if the figure were illuminated ³ partly by fire and partly by the air <note>meaning natural daylight</note>, make the light from the air more powerful, and that of the fire almost red, in the likeness of fire. And above all, ensure that your painted figures have a large light from above—that is, the living person whom you are portraying ⁴—because the people you see in the streets all have the light from above; and know that there is no acquaintance of yours so great that, if given light from below, you would not find it difficult to recognize them.

---

¹ Sharp <note>original: "Nitido"</note>.
² The further the shadow moves away.
³ If the figure were illuminated.
⁴ Portray that which is living.

<summary>Leonardo provides specific instructions for painters on how to render light and shadow based on the environment, emphasizing that light from above is most natural for human recognition.</summary>
<keywords>painting technique, chiaroscuro, lighting conditions, firelight, shadows, portraiture, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical advice for painters regarding lighting, specifically focusing on the transition between light and shadow and the effects of atmospheric conditions on the clarity of forms.</meta>

<page-num>215</page-num>

## On the quality of light for portraying reliefs, whether natural or artificial.

Light that is separated from shadows with too much sharpness is highly criticized among painters; therefore, to avoid such an inconvenience, if you paint bodies in the open countryside, you should not make the figures illuminated by the sun. Instead, imagine some quality of mist or transparent clouds being interposed between the object and the sun; thus, since the shape of the sun is not distinct, the boundaries of the shadows will not be distinct from the boundaries of the lights.

The painter ought to apply, in figures and things remote from the eye, only patches of color <term>macchie: literally "spots" or "stains," referring to the broad application of color without fine detail</term>, but not finished—rather, with confused boundaries. And let the choice of such figures be made when it is cloudy or towards evening. Above all, take care, as I have said, to avoid finished lights or shadows, because they appear like tinted patches when you see them from a distance, and the resulting works are difficult <note>original: "difficili"; the author's note clarifies this as "harsh" or "stiff"</note> and without grace. And remember that shadows should never be of such a quality that, through their darkness, you lose the color where they are produced, unless the place where the bodies are situated is already pitch black. And do not make sharp profiles, do not delineate individual hairs, and do not use white highlights except on white objects; let those highlights demonstrate the primary beauty of the color upon which they rest.

---

1 Harsh.

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to avoid harsh, sharp edges between light and shadow, suggesting that figures be depicted under diffused light like mist or dusk to achieve "grace." He emphasizes that shadows should not be so dark as to obscure local color and warns against overly detailed outlines in distant figures.</summary>
<keywords>sfumato, atmospheric perspective, lighting techniques, soft shadows, color theory, landscape painting, artistic grace</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for painters, moving from the general quality of light to specific studio setups, the rendering of flesh tones, and the treatment of outlines.</meta>

<page-num>216</page-num>

A light filtered through flesh-colored glass, and a room for the man painted in that same flesh-tone, along with similarly colored garments, will make the face appear with the true lights and shadows of its own flesh. This method is extremely useful for making the flesh appear most beautiful, but such a precept <note>A rule or instruction</note> is contrary to the rules for figures placed in the open countryside surrounded by diverse colors.

---

The light for portraying from nature should be from the north <note>original: "tramontana"; painters prefer north light because it remains consistent and cool throughout the day</note>, so that it does not change; and if you do it at midday, keep the window covered with a cloth <note>original: "impannata"; likely a linen or paper screen to diffuse the light</note>, so that as the sun shines all day, the light does not shift. The height of the light: it should be situated in such a way that every body casts a shadow on the ground as long as the body is tall.

---

Avoid profiles, that is, the finished <gloss>1</gloss> boundaries of things. Do not make the boundaries of your figures a different color than the very background that meets them; that is, do not make dark outlines between the background and your figure.

---

That which is beautiful is not always good. And I say this for those painters who love the beauty of colors so much that, not without a great burden on their conscience, they give

---

<gloss>1</gloss> The boundaries.
<gloss>2</gloss> The fine speakers <note>original: "I belli parlatori"; likely a metaphorical reference to those who prioritize style over substance, or a fragment of a note on rhetoric</note>.

<term>incarnato: a flesh-colored or rosy tint</term>
<term>tramontana: the north wind or north star, here referring to northern light</term>
<term>termini: the edges or boundaries where one form ends and another begins</term>
<term>pittura: the art of painting</term>
<term>precetti: formal rules or principles of art</term>
<term>lume: the source or quality of light</term>

<summary>Leonardo provides practical studio advice on using diffused light to enhance flesh tones and emphasizes the importance of consistent northern light. He also warns against the use of harsh outlines and prioritizing "beautiful" colors over realistic depth.</summary>
<keywords>lighting, flesh tones, north light, outlines, sfumato, studio practice, color theory</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the use of light and shadow (chiaroscuro) to create depth, specifically warning against over-reliance on bright colors at the expense of form, and describing the ideal lighting conditions for portraits.</meta>

<page-num>217</page-num>

their very weak and almost imperceptible shadows, not valuing their relief <note>The illusion of three-dimensionality or "popping out" from the surface</note>. And in this error are those who are fine speakers without any substance <note>original: "belli parlatori sanza alcuna sentenzia"; a metaphor comparing painters who use bright colors without shadow to orators who use fancy words without meaning</note>.

---

The greatest grace of shadows and lights is added to the faces of those who sit at the doorways of those dwellings which are dark, where the eyes of the observer see the shaded part of such a face darkened by the shadows of the aforementioned dwelling, and see the illuminated part of the same face joined with the brightness given to it by the radiance of the air; by this increase of shadows and lights, the face has great relief, with the shadows being almost imperceptible in the illuminated part, and the lights almost imperceptible in the shaded part. And from such a representation and increase of shadows and lights, the face acquires beauty <note>original: "bellezza"</note>. <note>Leonardo is describing a "doorway effect" similar to what would later be called tenebrism, where a dark background makes the subject appear more vivid.</note>

---

### On the shadows of faces which, passing through wet streets, do not seem to match their flesh tones.

Regarding the question asked <note>The small question proposed here is explained as follows.</note>, it happens that often a face will be colorful or white and the shadows will appear yellowish. And this happens because wet streets appear more yellow than dry ones, and because the parts of the face—

## Relief of light figures.

That thing which is painted with white and black will appear with better relief <note>The illusion of three-dimensionality or depth on a flat surface</note> than any other; and therefore I remind you, painter, to dress your figures in colors as light as you can, for if you make them of a dark color they will have little relief and little <unclear>e</unclear>vidence <note>visibility or clarity</note> from a distance. This is because of the shadows of all things, which are dark; and if you make a dark garment, there will be little difference between the lights and the shadows, but in light colors there will be a great variation.

## A woman dressed in white in the open countryside.

If you represent a white body surrounded by much air (because white has no color of its own, but tints and transforms itself in part into the color of the object facing it), and if you see a woman dressed in white in the midst of a countryside, that part of her seen by the sun will be so bright in color that it will, in part, like the sun, be painful to the sight. And in that part seen by the luminous air—due to the rays of the sun woven into and penetrating through it—because the air itself is blue <note>original: "azzurra"</note>, the part of the woman seen by said air will appear to lean toward blue. If on the upper—

And thus, the color goes on transforming itself into the colors of the nearby luminous and non-luminous objects.

### How every color that does not shine is more beautiful in its luminous parts than in its shaded parts.

Every color is more beautiful in its illuminated part than in its shaded part, and this arises because light vivifies and gives true knowledge of the quality of colors, while shadow dampens and obscures that same beauty and prevents the knowledge of that color; and if, on the contrary, black is more beautiful in the shadows than in the lights, the answer is that black is not a color, nor indeed is white. <note>Leonardo often argued that white and black were the "extremes" of light and darkness rather than true colors like red or blue.</note>

### On the nature of the colors of backgrounds, against which white stands out.

A white object will appear whiter when it is against a darker background, and it will appear darker when it is against a whiter background. And this is taught to us by the falling of snow, which, when we see it against the background of the air, appears dark to us, and when we see it against the background of an open window,

# THE LANDSCAPE

### **On the reflection and color of seawater seen from different perspectives.**

The undulating sea has no universal color; rather, he who views it from the mainland sees it as a dark color, and it appears darker the closer it is to the horizon. There, one sees certain brightnesses or glimmers that move slowly, like white sheep within a flock. However, he who views the sea while out on the open ocean sees it as blue. This occurs (regarding why the sea appears dark from land) because you see within it the waves which reflect the darkness of the earth; from the high seas, they appear blue because you see the blue air reflected in those waves.

### **Effects of morning mist.**

In the morning, the mist is thicker toward the heights than at its base, because the sun draws it upward. Thus, with large buildings—even if the top is as far from you as the foundation—the top will be <note>The sentence breaks off here, likely continuing on the next page to explain that the top of a building appears more obscured by mist than the bottom due to the rising vapors.</note>

Air infused with mists is entirely deprived of blue, but instead appears to be the color of the clouds <note>original: "nugoli"</note> which show white when the weather is clear; and the further you look toward the west, the darker you will find it, and it will be more lustrous and clear toward the east. And the greenery of the countryside in a moderate mist takes on a somewhat blue tint, but it turns blackish in the thickest mist...

When the sun rises and chases away the mists, and the hills begin to clear from the side where the mists depart, they become blue and seem to smoke toward the fleeing mists; and the buildings show highlights <note>original: "lumi"</note> and shadows, and in the less dense mists they show only the highlights, and in the thickest mists, nothing at all. This occurs when the motion of the mist moves transversely, and then the edges of said mist will be barely visible against the blue of the air, and toward the earth, it will appear almost like rising dust. The thicker the air is, the more sparse the buildings of the cities and the trees of the countryside will seem; because only the most prominent and largest will show themselves.

---

No opaque body is without shadow or highlights, unless it is in the mist, or upon ground covered in snow; and the same happens when it snows in the countryside: it will be without light and will be surrounded by darkness.

## On Dust.

The dust that is raised by the movement of any animal appears lighter the higher it rises, and darker the less it is elevated, provided that it stands between the sun and the eye. <note>Leonardo is observing how backlighting affects particles; higher, thinner dust scatters more light toward the viewer, while lower, denser dust remains in shadow.</note>

## On Smoke.

Smoke is more transparent and dark toward the edges of its <term>globules</term> <note>original: "globulenzie"; Leonardo's term for the rounded, billowing clusters or "puffs" of smoke.</note> than toward their centers.

Smoke moves with a greater slant the more powerful the wind is that drives it. Smokes are of as many various colors as there are varieties of things that generate them.

Smokes do not cast defined shadows, and their boundaries are less distinct the further they are from their source; and objects placed behind them are less visible the denser the clusters of smoke are. They appear whiter the closer they are to their beginning, and more blue toward their end. <note>This refers to the scattering of light; near the fire, the smoke is thick and reflects white light, but as it disperses, it takes on the blue characteristic of the atmosphere.</note>

Fire will appear darker the greater the amount of smoke that is interposed between the eye and said fire. Where the smoke is more remote, objects are less obscured by it.

Depict a landscape with smoke in the manner of a thick fog, in which smoke can be seen in various places with its flames at the source, illuminating the densest <term>globules</term>.

<column-break/>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the optical properties of dust and smoke, noting how their transparency, color, and density change based on light source, wind, and distance from the origin. He provides specific instructions for painters on how to render these atmospheric effects to create depth and realism.</summary>
<keywords>dust, smoke, globules, defined shadows, fire, fog, painting techniques, aerial perspective</keywords>

### **Sunsets.**

When the sun is in the west, the mists that fall back down thicken the air, and the things that are not seen by the sun remain dark and confused, while those illuminated by the sun turn reddish and yellowish, according to how the sun shows itself on the horizon. Furthermore, the houses that are illuminated by this are very evident, especially the buildings and houses of the city and villas, because their shadows are dark, and it seems that such clear definition of theirs arises from confused and uncertain foundations <note>The "uncertain foundations" refer to the lower parts of the buildings being lost in the dense, unlit ground-level mist.</note> <margin>Because only the upper part is illuminated.</margin>; for everything is of a single color if it is not seen by the sun itself.

The sun makes a beautiful spectacle when it is in the west, for it illuminates all the high buildings of cities and castles, and the tall trees of the countryside, and tints them with its color, and all the rest from there downward remains with little relief <note>original: "poco rilevo"; in painting terms, this means a lack of three-dimensional depth or modeling.</note>, because being only illuminated by the air, they...

Therefore, you must take some of the color with which you make the sun, and place it into whatever light color you use to illuminate those bodies.

When the sun makes the clouds on the horizon turn red, the things which due to distance were clothed in blue will participate in this redness; thus a mixture will be created between blue and red, which will make the countryside very cheerful and pleasant; and all things that are dense, being illuminated by such redness, will be very evident and will glow red <insert>and all dense things, illuminated by such redness, will be etc.</insert> <note>This insertion reflects the grammatical correction noted in the original manuscript's footnote.</note>. ### Wind.

In the representation <term>representation: "figurazione," the act of depicting a physical phenomenon in art</term> of the wind, besides the bending of branches and the overturning of their leaves toward the direction the wind is coming from, one must depict the clouds of fine dust mixed with the turbid air.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the reddish light of a sunset blends with atmospheric blue to create vibrant landscapes and describes the visual markers necessary to depict wind in painting.</summary>
<keywords>sunset, atmospheric perspective, color theory, wind, landscape painting, light and shadow</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical observations on the visual effects of wind on nature, moving from the general depiction of dust and air to the specific behavior of foliage, branches, and fields.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>

Trees and meadows appear much brighter when looking at them in the direction of the wind's flight <note>original: "fuga del vento"; meaning looking downwind, in the direction the wind is blowing</note> than when looking toward its arrival <note>original: "avvenimento"; the direction from which the wind originates</note>. And this arises because every leaf is paler on its underside than on its right side <note>original: "diritto"; the top or front surface of the leaf</note>; and whoever looks at them following the flight of the wind sees them from the underside; and whoever looks at them against the arrival of the wind sees them in shadow, because their tips <note>original: "stremi"</note> bend and cast shadow toward their center, and besides this, they are seen from the direction of their right side.

The top of the tree will be bent more by the percussion <term>percussion: "percussione," the physical impact or force of the wind hitting an object</term> of the wind if it has thinner and longer branches, such as willows and the like.

If the eye is positioned between the arrival and the flight of the wind, the trees will show their branches to be thicker toward the arrival of said wind than toward its flight; and this arises because the wind that strikes the tops of those trees turned toward it leans them against the other more powerful branches, whereby they become dense there and of little transparency; but the opposite branches, struck by the wind that penetrates through the openings <note>original: "trasforazione"; the gaps or perforations in the canopy</note> of the tree, move away from the center of the plant and become thinned out <note>original: "rarificano"</note>.

Those trees are bent more by the course of the wind which are taller.

Plants that are thicker with leaves will be bent more by the percussion of the wind.

In great forests and in grain fields and meadows, waves made by the wind will be seen no differently than those seen in the sea or open waters <note>original: "pelaghi"</note>.

<margin>
**Vocabulary**
*Arrival of the wind:* the source direction.
*Flight of the wind:* the direction it travels.
*Underside:* the back of the leaf.
*Right side:* the front of the leaf.
*Tips:* the extremities.
*Shall be:* the future state.
*Percussion:* the strike of the wind.
*Perforation:* the gaps in the tree.
*Thin out:* to become less dense.
*Open waters:* the deep sea.
</margin>

## On the beginning of a rainstorm.

The rain falls through the air, darkening it with a leaden <note>original: "livida"; literally a bluish-gray or bruised color</note> tint, catching the light of the sun on one side and shadows on the opposite side, just as one sees happening with mists. The earth grows dark because the splendor of the sun is taken from it by such rain; and things seen beyond the rain have confused and unintelligible outlines, while those things that are closer to the eye will be more distinct. Furthermore, things seen within the shaded rain will be more distinct than those in the illuminated rain; this happens because things seen in the shaded rains only lose their primary lights, but things seen in the luminous rains lose both light and shadow, because their luminous parts blend with the brightness of the illuminated air, and their shaded parts are brightened by that same clarity of the said illuminated air.

## Of clouds beneath the moon.

The cloud that is found beneath the moon is darker than any of the others, and those more remote are lighter. The part of the cloud that is transparent, within and between the edges <note>original: "stremi"</note> of that cloud, appears lighter than any other similar part found in the transparencies of more remote clouds; this is because at every degree of distance, the middle of the clouds becomes lighter, and their light parts become more opaque, turning a reddish hue of mortifi- <note>The text breaks mid-word; likely "mortificazione" or "mortificata," referring to a dulling or "killing" of the color intensity.</note>

<meta>The text ends with the hyphenated "mortifi-", indicating a continuation onto page 230 regarding the shifting colors of clouds at a distance.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo describes the optical effects of rain on visibility and contrast, followed by an analysis of how clouds appear at varying distances when illuminated by the moon.</summary>
<keywords>rain, atmospheric perspective, light and shadow, optics, clouds, moonlight, transparency, distance</keywords>

-fied redness <note>original: "mortificato rossore"; completing the thought from the previous page regarding the dulling of color intensity</note>; and the edges of their dark areas, seen within their transparent clarity, have smoky and confused boundaries. The same occurs at the edges of their bright areas where they meet the air.

And clouds of little thickness are entirely transparent, more so toward the center than at the edges, which is a dull reddish color fading into a coarse and confused hue. The further the clouds are from the moon, the whiter their light becomes, which increases around the dark areas of the cloud, especially on the side facing the moon <margin>The light bordering the dark part of the cloud is brighter the further it is from the moon, and especially on the side of that moon.</margin>. That which is thin has no blackness and little whiteness, because the darkness of the night, which shows through the air, penetrates it.

## A storm of winds and rain.

### I.

One sees the air tinted with dark cloudiness during the preparation for tempests or sea storms <term>fortuna: a historical term for a violent storm or gale at sea</term> (which are mixtures of rain and winds), with the snaking of the winding courses of the threatening <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, describing the visual movement of storm clouds.</note>

---

<margin>Dark clouds beneath the moon are the darkest; the further they move from it, the more darkness they lose. Conversely, the transparent part of them appears clearer the closer it is to the moon, because as it moves away, it becomes more opaque and acquires a reddish color.</margin>

heavenly lightnings, and the plants bent to the earth with their leaves turned upside down upon the drooping branches, which seem to want to flee from their places, as if terrified by the strikes of the horrible and frightening flights of the winds; among which are infused the swirling courses of the turbulent dust and sand from the sea shores. The darkness of the sky becomes a field of smoky clouds, which, struck by the solar rays penetrating through the opposite breaks in the clouds, strike the earth, illuminating it beneath their impact. The winds, persecutors of the dust, lift it in leaping, clustered masses <note>original: "grupolenti globosità"</note> into the air, with an ash-blue color <note>original: "cineruleo"</note>, mixed with the reddish solar rays that penetrate them. The animals, without guidance, run terrified in circles through various places. The thunders, created within the globular clouds, drive out from themselves the infuriated lightning bolts, the light of which illuminates the shadowy countryside in various locations.

## II.

If you wish to represent a <term>tempest</term> <note>original: "fortuna"; here used in the sense of a violent storm at sea or great gale</note> well, consider and arrange its effects well: when the wind, blowing over the surface of the sea and the land, removes and carries with it those things that are not firm with the universal mass.
And to represent this <term>tempest</term> well, you shall first make the clouds, shattered and broken, rushing along the course of the wind, accompanied by the sandy dust lifted from the sea shores, and branches and leaves lifted by the power

by the fury of the wind, scattered through the air, and, in company with those, many other light things. The trees and the grasses bent to the ground almost seem to want to follow the course of the winds, with their branches twisted out of their natural path and with their leaves disheveled and turned upside down. And the men who find themselves there, some fallen and rolled over, covered by their clothes and by the dust, so that they are almost unrecognizable; and those who remain standing should be behind some tree, embracing it so that the wind does not drag them away; others, with their hands over their eyes because of the dust, stooped to the ground, with their clothes and hair streaming in the direction of the wind. Let the sea be troubled and tempestuous, full of eddies and foam among the towering waves, and the wind lifting, within the embattled air, the finest foam like a thick and enveloping mist. As for the ships that are therein, make some of them with torn sails and fragments of them flapping in the air along with some broken ropes; some with broken masts, fallen, with the ship turned sideways and shattered among the tempestuous waves; certain men crying out as they embrace what remains of the vessel. You shall make the clouds, driven by the impetuous winds, beaten against the high peaks of the mountains, creating against them tangled eddies <note>original: "retrosi"; referring to the swirling back-currents of air or water</note> in the likeness of waves striking against the rocks. The air should be terrifying because of the dark shadows created in the atmosphere by the dust, mist, and thick clouds.

<vocab>painting, tempest, wind, sea, ships, men, mountains, foam, mist</vocab>

### First notes for the "Deluge". <note>The "Deluge" refers to the biblical Great Flood, a subject Leonardo explored extensively in both his writings and his late, apocalyptic drawings.</note>

Darkness, wind, tempests at sea, a deluge of water, forests on fire, rain, bolts of lightning from the heavens, earthquakes and the ruin of mountains, the leveling of cities.

Whirling winds that carry water, branches of plants, and men through the air.

Branches torn away by the winds, mingled with the course of the winds, with people on top of them.

Broken plants, laden with people.

Ships broken into pieces, dashed against the rocks.

Of the herds: hail, lightning, whirling winds.

People who are atop plants that cannot support them; trees and rocks, towers, hills full of people; boats, planks, kneading-troughs <note>original: "madie"; large wooden chests used for kneading bread, here repurposed as makeshift life-rafts</note> and other instruments for swimming; hills covered with men and women and animals, and lightning from the clouds that illuminates all things.

### Representation of the Deluge.

->I.<-

The air was dark because of the thick rain, which, falling in an oblique descent bent by the transverse course of the winds, made waves of itself through the air, not otherwise than what is seen of dust, but differing only because such an inundation was traversed by the lines—

—formed by the drops of the descending water.
But its color was tinged by the fire generated by the lightning bolts, which split and tore through the clouds; the flashes <margin>Flashes = the blazes of light.</margin> of these struck and opened the great depths of the flooded valleys, and these openings revealed within their bellies <margin>In the midst of the whirlpool.</margin> the bent tops of the trees.
And <term>Neptune</term> <note>The Roman god of the sea, often depicted by Leonardo as a personification of the power of water.</note> was seen in the midst of the waters with his trident, and <term>Aeolus</term> <note>The ruler of the winds in Greek and Roman mythology.</note> was seen with his winds entangling the floating, uprooted trees, mixed with the immense waves.

One could see men and birds filling the great trees that were left exposed by the expanding waves—waves which formed hills surrounding the great abysses <margin>The trees left exposed by the waves in their retreat; by the waves which, in swelling, resembled hills rising around the depths of the whirlpools.</margin>.

## II.

The dark and cloudy air was seen to be buffeted by the rush of various winds, enveloped by the continuous rain and mixed with hail, which carried here and there infinite branches of torn plants, mixed with countless leaves. All around were seen ancient trees uprooted and dragged away by the—

How many terrifying noises were heard through the dark air, struck by the fury of the thunders and the lightning bolts <note>original: "fulgori"</note> cast out from them, which rushed ruinously through the atmosphere, striking whatever opposed their course! Oh, how many you would have seen closing their ears with their own hands to shun the immense noises made through the gloomy air by the fury of the winds mixed with rain, heavenly thunders, and the fury of bolts!

Others, for whom closing their eyes was not enough, but placing one hand over the other, covered them even more so as not to see the cruel slaughter <note>original: "strazio"</note> made of the human species by the wrath of God.

Oh, how many laments, and how many terrified people threw themselves from the cliffs! One could see the great branches of the massive oaks <note>original: "quercie"</note>, loaded with men, being carried through the air by the fury of the impetuous winds.

How many were the boats turned upside down—both those still whole and those in pieces—with people upon them, struggling for their escape with sorrowful acts and movements, prognosticating a frightful death. Others with desperate movements took their own lives, despairing of being able to endure such suffering; of whom some threw themselves from the high cliffs, others strangled their own throats with their own hands, some took their own children and with great violence <note>original: "ràpito"</note> dashed them against the ground, some wounded themselves with their own weapons and killed themselves, while others, throwing themselves on their knees, commended themselves to God. Oh! How many mothers wept for their drowned sons, holding them upon their knees, raising their open arms toward heaven, and with voices composed of diverse wailings, rebuked the wrath of the Gods <note>original: "Dei"; Leonardo often shifts between singular "God" and the classical "Gods" in these descriptions.</note>;

## III.

One could see, driven by the swirling <note>original: "revertiginosi"</note> currents of the winds, great quantities of flocks of birds coming from distant lands; these appeared almost imperceptible in their movements, because, in their wheeling about, one moment a flock would show all its birds edgewise—that is, showing their thinnest profile—and at another moment showing their greatest breadth—that is, facing forward. At the beginning of their appearance, they took the form of an indiscernible cloud, and then the second and third squadrons became more distinct as they drew nearer to the eye of the observer.

And the nearest of the aforementioned flocks descended low with an oblique motion, and they settled upon the dead bodies carried by the waves of this deluge, and they fed upon them; and this they did until the buoyancy of the bloated <note>original: "infiati"</note> dead bodies failed, at which point, with a slow descent, they went sinking to the bottom of the waters.

## On representing a night scene.

That thing which is entirely deprived of light is all darkness. Since night is in such a condition, if you wish to represent a historical scene therein, you shall arrange it so that, there being a great fire, the object which is nearest to said fire is most tinted by its color, because

that thing which is closer to the object participates more in its nature; and since fire tends toward a red color, you shall make all things illuminated by it likewise reddish. Those things which are further from said fire should be more tinted by the black color of the night. The figures that are between you and the fire will appear dark against the brightness of that fire, because that part of the object which you see is tinted by the darkness of the night and not by the brightness of the fire. Those who are situated at the sides should be half dark and half reddish; and those who can be seen beyond the edges <margin>The outlines.</margin> of the flames will be entirely illuminated by a reddish light against a black background. As for their actions, you shall make those who are close to it shield themselves with their hands and cloaks as a protection against the excessive heat, turning their faces away as if showing they are fleeing. For those further away, you shall make a great many of them <insert>shielding</insert> their eyes with their hands, as they are pained <insert>by</insert> the excessive brightness.

### On representing autumn.

In autumn, you shall depict things according to the age of that season; that is, at the beginning, the trees start to turn pale in their leaves on the older branches, more or less according to whether the plant is depicted in a sterile or fertile place. Make them even more pale and reddish in those species of trees which were the first to produce their fruit. And do not do as many do, making all sorts

Nature is so delightful and abundant in variation that among trees of the same kind, one would not find a plant that closely resembles another; and not just the plants, but the branches, or leaves, or fruits of those—you will not find one that precisely resembles another. Therefore, pay attention and vary as much as you can.

### **On depicting winter.**

Landscapes made in the representation of winter should not show their mountains as blue, as is seen with mountains in the summer. This is proven by the fourth [rule] of this <gloss>He implies: the rule of the subject I am treating.</gloss> which says: among mountains seen at a long distance, that one will appear more blue which is in itself darker. Therefore, since plants are stripped of their leaves, they appear a <term>berrettino</term> <note>original: "berrettino" - a dull, brownish-gray or ash color, similar to the color of a common cap or "berretto"</note> color, given that the leaves are green. And just as green is darker than this grayish-brown, the green will appear more blue than the grayish-brown; and for

<summary>Leonardo emphasizes the infinite variety of nature, noting that no two leaves or plants are identical, and provides a scientific rule for why winter mountains appear less blue than summer ones due to the loss of dark green foliage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the optical properties of trees and landscapes, moving from the color of winter mountains to the density of shadows in foliage and the effects of direct sunlight on forests.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>

the fifth [rule] of this: the shadows of plants clothed in leaves are as much darker than the shadows of those plants stripped of leaves, as the clothed plants are less sparse than those without leaves; and thus we have proven our intent.

### On the sun illuminating the forest.

When the sun illuminates the forest, the trees of the woods will show themselves with distinct shadows and lights, and for this reason, they will seem to have moved closer to you, because they take on a more recognizable shape. Whatever part of them is not seen by the sun appears equally dark, except for their thin parts that are positioned between the sun and you, which will become bright due to their transparency. This happens <gloss>1</gloss> because a smaller quantity of lights is created in trees illuminated by the sun than by the sky, because the sky is larger than the sun, and a larger cause produces larger effects in this case. <note>Leonardo is explaining that the broad light of the sky softens shadows, while the concentrated light of the sun creates sharp, high-contrast "lights" and "darks."</note>

As the shadows of the plants become smaller, the trees will appear to be more sparse, especially where they have a uniform color and are by their nature of sparse branches and thin leaves—such as the peach tree, plum tree, and the like. This is because, as the shadow retreats toward the center of the plant, the plant itself appears to have diminished, and the branches that remain entirely outside of the shadow appear to be of the same color and background.

---

<note>1 Here it is appropriate [to say].</note>

<sig>16</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the density of foliage affects the darkness of shadows and how direct sunlight creates high-contrast shapes that make trees appear closer and more distinct. He also notes how specific species like peach and plum trees appear thinner when their shadows contract toward the center.</summary>

<page-num>242</page-num>

## Leaves transparent against the sun.

Never represent leaves as transparent against the sun, because they are confusing; this happens because upon the transparency of one leaf, the shadow of another leaf situated above it will be imprinted. This shadow has sharp edges and a defined darkness, and sometimes it covers half or a third of the leaf it overshadows. In this way, such a branching structure becomes muddled, and its imitation <note>meaning the artistic reproduction of this effect</note> should be avoided.

## Diverse foliages.

Trees in the countryside are of various natures of green among themselves; for some tend toward black, such as firs, pines, cypresses, laurels, boxwoods, and the like; some tend toward yellow, such as walnuts and pears, vines and greenery; some tend toward yellow with darkness, such as chestnuts and oaks; some turn reddish toward autumn, such as service trees, pomegranates, vines, and cherries; some tend toward white, such as willows, olives, reeds, and the like.

## Arrangement of leaves on the branch. ¹

The leaf always turns its right side <note>the upper surface or "adaxial" side</note> toward the sky, so that it can better receive, with all its—

surface, the dew which descends with slow motion from the air; and such leaves are distributed upon their plants in such a way that one overlaps the other as little as possible, by alternating one above the other <note>Leonardo is describing the staggered arrangement of leaves</note>, as is seen with the ivy that covers walls. And such alternating serves two purposes: first, to leave intervals so that the air and the sun can penetrate among them; the second reason is so that the drops that fall from the first leaf can fall onto the fourth or the sixth leaf of the other trees <note>Leonardo observes that the staggered pattern ensures water is distributed to lower branches rather than just being blocked by the top layer</note>.

# SKETCHES OF CIVIL AND MILITARY ARCHITECTURE

### Project for Milan.

Give me the authority¹ so that, without any expense to you, all the lands shall be made to obey their leaders.

The fame of the first founder becomes eternal, together with the inhabitants of the city built or enlarged by him.

The beds of the waterways, which are behind the gardens, should be as high as the level of the gardens themselves; and by means of channels <term>spine; literally "thorns," referring here to small distribution channels</term> they may provide water to the gardens every evening. Whenever the water is dammed up, by raising the sluice gates <term>incastri; the grooves or the gates themselves used to regulate water flow</term> by half a cubit <term>braccio; an Italian unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches</term>, the water will flow; and the Elders <note>The "Anziani" were municipal officials or magistrates responsible for local governance</note> shall be held responsible for this.

And let nothing be thrown into the canals, and let every boat be required to carry away a certain amount of silt from the Canal <note>original: "Navilio," referring to the Naviglio Grande or the system of navigable canals in Milan</note>, which shall then be dumped upon the embankment.

---

¹ He addresses Ludovico il Moro <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), the Duke of Milan and Leonardo's patron</note>.

<margin>Architecture, Milan, Ludovico il Moro, Canal, Elders, authority, sluice gates, cubit</margin>

<summary>Leonardo proposes a series of urban improvements for Milan to Ludovico il Moro, focusing on the eternal fame of builders and a practical hydraulic system for irrigating gardens and desilting the canals.</summary>
<keywords>Milan, Ludovico il Moro, urban planning, hydraulics, irrigation, Naviglio, canal maintenance, civil engineering</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s urban planning proposals for Milan, moving from hydraulic maintenance to a radical social and architectural restructuring of the city to prevent plague and ensure political loyalty.</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>

Cause the Canal <note>original: "Navilio"</note> to be dried and the channels cleaned. <insert>Of.</insert>

All peoples obey and are moved by their magnates <note>The nobility or powerful landowners</note>, and these magnates connect themselves and bind themselves to their lords in two ways: either through blood-kinship, or through "bloodied" property. Blood-kinship is when their children are—in the likeness of hostages <term>statichi; an archaic term for hostages used as political guarantees</term>—the security and pledge of their doubted faith. Property is when you cause each of them to build a house or two inside your city, from which he may derive some income. And you will draw from ten cities five thousand houses with thirty thousand dwellings, and you will disperse such a great congregation of people, who, like a herd of goats, stand one on top of the other, filling every part with stench, and becoming the seeds of pestilential death. <note>This reference makes it reasonable to hypothesize that this proposal to Ludovico il Moro was written by Leonardo during the plague that desolated Milan from 1484 to 1485.</note>

And the city becomes a companion in beauty to its own name <note>The city becomes as illustrious in beauty as it is already famous for power</note>, and to you it brings useful revenues <term>dazi; customs duties or state tax revenues</term>, and eternal fame for its growth...

That foreigner who owns a house in Milan will often find that, to live in a more magnificent place, he becomes an inhabitant of his own house; and whoever builds possesses at least some wealth, and in this way the poor rabble will be separated from such inhabitants. And thus the duties <note>The taxes or sources of income for the State</note> will increase, as will the fame of the city's magnitude; and even if he does not wish to reside in Milan, he will remain faithful so as not to lose the profit of his house along with the capital.

<summary>Leonardo advises Ludovico Sforza to decentralize Milan's overcrowded population to prevent the plague and to secure the loyalty of the nobility by requiring them to invest in urban real estate.</summary>
<keywords>Milan, Ludovico il Moro, urban planning, plague prevention, public health, social engineering, taxation, real estate, political loyalty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s urban planning vision, moving from the social and political motivations for restructuring the city to the specific architectural and engineering designs for a multi-level urban center.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>

## The Ideal City. <note>original: "La città ideale." This description accompanies a drawing in the original manuscript.</note>

The high roads *N* are 6 cubits <term>braccia; a Florentine unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters</term> higher than the lower roads *P S*, and each road must be 20 cubits wide and have a slope of half a cubit from the edges to the center. In this center, at every cubit’s length, there should be a slit one finger wide, where rainwater may drain into the channels built at the same level as the lower roads *P S*. Along each edge of the width of said road, there shall be a portico 6 cubits wide supported on columns.

And know that whoever wishes to travel through the entire land via the high roads may use them at their convenience, and whoever wishes to travel by the lower ones may do the same. No carts or other similar things should travel on the high roads; rather, they are solely for the use of gentlemen <note>original: "gentili omini"; the nobility and upper classes</note>. On the lower roads, the carts and other burdens for the use and convenience of the common people should travel. One house must turn its back to the other, leaving the lower road in the middle; through these doors, provisions such as wood, wine, and similar things are to be brought in. Through the subterranean ways, the privies <term>destri; latrines or cesspools</term>, stables, and similar foul-smelling things must be emptied. From one arch to the next should be 300 cubits—that is, for each passage that receives light from the slits in the roads above—and at every arch there should be a round spiral staircase, because people urinate in the corners of square ones, and

---

<note>The text ends abruptly, a common feature of Leonardo's notebooks where he moves between ideas or runs out of space.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes a revolutionary two-tier city design that separates pedestrian traffic for the elite from commercial and waste traffic for the working class. This vertical separation aims to improve hygiene and social order through advanced drainage and architectural planning.</summary>
<keywords>urban planning, ideal city, sanitation, social hierarchy, drainage systems, spiral staircases, Milan, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s architectural specifications for a two-tiered city, focusing on the dimensions of the streets, the disposal of waste, and a comparison with other Renaissance urban theories.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>

wide, and at the first turn let there be a door that leads into the latrines <term>destri; private toilets</term> and public urinals <term>pisciatoi</term>, and by the staircase one descends from the high street to the low one. The high streets should begin outside the gates, and by the time they reach said gates, they should have reached a height of 6 cubits <term>braccia; a Florentine unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches</term>.

Let said city be built near the sea or another large river, so that the filth of the city, carried away by the water, may be washed away.

¹ As a matter of curiosity, compare this dream of an artist and scientist—which anticipates modern hygienic requirements—with the "ideal city" <term>città ideale</term> that A. F. Doni describes in the *Dialogue between the Madman and the Wise Man*. [*The Worlds*, Venice, Cavalli 1568] — I provide a brief summary of it here:

The structure of the city is regular and symmetrical. In the exact center of it rises a great temple, four or six times as large as the dome of Florence, which has one hundred doors that open onto one hundred straight streets running from the temple to the walls. To each door of the temple corresponds a gate in the walls, so that the city comes to have one hundred gates and one hundred streets, all equal and converging at the center.

On one side are all the tailors, on the other all the cloth shops, covering about two hundred trades. Two or three streets are for the inns, one for the hospices for the elderly unable to work, and one for the hospitals.

<summary>Leonardo specifies the height and drainage requirements for his two-tiered city, emphasizing hygiene through river access, while a footnote compares his vision to A.F. Doni's symmetrical "Ideal City."</summary>
<keywords>urban planning, hygiene, drainage, Leonardo da Vinci, A.F. Doni, Ideal City, Renaissance architecture, public health</keywords>

## Portable houses.

Let the houses be moved and arranged in order, and this will be done with ease because such houses are first made of pieces upon the squares, and then they are joined together with their timbers in the site where they are to be established. <note>Leonardo is describing a concept for prefabricated or modular housing that can be assembled and disassembled.</note>

## Project for a garden of delights.

With the mill, I will cause wind to be generated at all times; in the summer, I will cause the rising and cool spring water to be elevated, which will pass through the middle of the divided tables...

The channel shall be half a cubit <term>braccio; approximately 23 inches</term> wide, with the wine-coolers <term>zaine; vessels used for cooling drinks</term> kept constantly very fresh with their wines; and other water shall run through the garden, watering the orange trees <term>pomeranci; an archaic term for bitter oranges</term> and citrons according to their needs. These citrons shall be permanent, because the site will be adapted in such a way that they can be covered with ease; and the heat, which the spring water continually breathes out in the wintertime, shall be the cause of maintaining them much better than fire, for two reasons: the first is that this heat from the springs <term>fontanili; natural rising springs</term> is natural, and is the same that warms the roots of all plants; the second is that fire is an accidental heat to these plants, being deprived of moisture, and it is not uniform, and it is not continuous, because it is hotter in the beginning than at the end, and is often forgotten through the negligence <term>indiligenza; lack of diligence</term> of those who tend to them.

<summary>Leonardo describes a system for prefabricated modular housing and a sophisticated "garden of delights" featuring automated cooling, wine-chilling channels, and a geothermal heating system for citrus trees.</summary>
<keywords>prefabricated housing, modular architecture, garden design, hydraulic engineering, citrus cultivation, geothermal heating, Renaissance technology</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s design for a "garden of delights," moving from the irrigation of citrus trees to the maintenance of springs, the inclusion of mechanical water jokes, an aviary, and musical instruments powered by water mills, concluding with a military engineering note on fortresses.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

At the springs <term>fontanili; natural rising springs</term>, the weeds must be mown frequently so that the water may be seen clearly against its gravelly bottom; only those plants suitable for the nourishment of fish should be left, such as watercress <note>original: "crescioni"</note> and other similar herbs.

The fish must be of the variety that do not cloud the water; that is, do not put in eels, nor tench <note>a freshwater fish of the carp family</note>, nor even pike, because they destroy the other fish.

By means of the mill, many water conduits shall be made throughout the house, with fountains in various places, and a certain walkway where, whoever passes over it, water will jump upward from all parts below; and this shall be done at the whim <insert>at the request</insert> of whoever wishes to soak the women from below, or others who pass by there. <note>Leonardo describes "giochi d'acqua" or water jokes, a popular feature in Renaissance gardens designed to playfully drench unsuspecting guests.</note>

Above, we shall make a very fine copper net which will cover the garden and enclose beneath it many various sorts of birds; thus, you will have continuous music together with the scents of the blossoms of citrons and lemons.

With the mill, I will create continuous sounds from various instruments, which will play for as long as the motion of said mill shall last.

## Fortress <note>This project for a fortress—which has the usual mysterious tone dear to Da Vinci—continues, and indeed increases, the proportions of the previous designs.</note>.

Let a bored well be made which, being situated in the middle of the fortress <term>rocca; a fortified stronghold</term>, can provide water to all the moats at the request of the castellan <term>the governor or warden of a castle</term>.

<summary>Leonardo describes the maintenance of clear garden springs, a mechanical aviary with copper netting, and water-powered musical instruments and pranks. He also proposes a central well system for a fortress to control the water level of its moats.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, garden design, watercress, fish ponds, water jokes, aviary, mechanical music, fortress, bored well, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s military engineering proposals, transitioning from the internal mechanics of a fortress (wells and water systems) to defensive ballistics, subterranean passages, and strategic control of the garrison.</meta>

<page-num>253</page-num>

Many shots shall be fired, for the balls <term>ballotte; stone or metal projectiles</term> will bounce back upon the enemies with great detriment to them <note>original: "detrimento". Leonardo suggests a defensive geometry where projectiles ricochet off surfaces to strike the attackers again.</note>.

Subterranean passages <margin>Underground ways will put the various parts of the fortress in communication, but not with the citadel, to prevent the Governor from being betrayed by his own soldiers.</margin> beneath the waters, through which one defensive circuit <term>procinto; a fortified perimeter or enclosure</term> may give aid to another; but let no one enter the citadel <term>rocca; the strongest, innermost part of the fortress</term>, and let the passage of the citadel go above all the other circuits.

No timber or mantlet <term>mantelletto; a movable wooden shield used by besiegers</term> shall be set on fire.

I can flood every passage individually, and all of them together, and then empty them with ease.

The dead bridges <note>Fixed bridges that do not move, as opposed to drawbridges.</note>, which, even if they remain permanent, can be entirely undone with a small action; they cannot be bombarded with damage to the walls.

A single shot fired circles the entire citadel <margin>A shot of artillery makes the circuit of the citadel; that is, it defends the whole of it.</margin>. If anyone <note>original: "nessun" used here in the archaic sense of "someone" or "anyone".</note> climbs upon the walls, he makes himself a target for the defenders of those walls. The relief force always remains a prisoner of the Governor <margin>There is no need to fear the defection of the reserve squads, because the Governor can keep them in a secure place like a prison.</margin>.

->astounding harvests of military engineering which are found in the famous letter to Ludovico il Moro <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), the Duke of Milan and Leonardo's patron.</note>. It may well be that He wished with this to make a way for himself among the ducal engineers in the war against Venice of 1483-84, that is, when he had recently arrived at Court and it was important to him to be held in high esteem.<-

<summary>Leonardo describes advanced defensive features for a fortress, including ricocheting projectiles, floodable subterranean passages, and a strategic layout that ensures the Governor maintains absolute control over his own troops.</summary>
<keywords>military engineering, ballistics, Ludovico il Moro, fortress defense, subterranean passages, Venice, Renaissance warfare</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s discourse on the strategic design of a fortress, focusing on the absolute control the Governor (Castellan) maintains over both external enemies and his own garrison.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>

No enemy can see the defender of these walls. If bridges or wooden scaling ladders are constructed, they are burned at the convenience of the defender.

The Governor <term>castellano; the commander of the castle</term> cannot be assaulted by his own hired soldiers <term>provigionati; mercenary troops or garrisoned soldiers paid a provision</term>. A single cannonball <term>ballotta di bombarda; a stone or metal projectile fired from a large-caliber gun</term> can reach all the rooms of the hired soldiers. The Governor can set fire to all the lodgings of the hired soldiers. The Governor can, at any moment, see and hear the discussions and councils of his hired soldiers without being seen himself. No one can hide from him in the outworks <term>rivellini; triangular fortifications located outside the main walls</term>, even after those outworks have been captured by the enemies <margin>No soldier can hide there out of cowardice.</margin>.

A single guard, without moving, watches the entire castle inside and out; this guard should be changed every three hours. Even if the enemies were to capture all the fortified perimeters <term>procinti; defensive enclosures</term> of the walls, none of them can descend inside those perimeters without the ruin of themselves and their lives. The mantlets <term>mantelletti; movable wooden shields or protective covers</term> will never be damaged or removed, unless <note>original: "e" used here as "che" (unless/except that).</note> they are replaced by themselves <note>Leonardo likely refers to a self-renewing or mechanical system of shutters that reset automatically.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo describes a fortress designed for total surveillance and internal security, ensuring the Governor can suppress a mutiny as easily as an external siege. The architecture allows for the monitoring of soldiers' conversations and the use of fire and artillery against internal quarters if necessary.</summary>

<keywords>fortification, military engineering, surveillance, mercenaries, artillery, outworks, defensive strategy</keywords>

# LETTERS
## AND AUTOBIOGRAPHICAL NOTES

### To Ludovico "the Moor" <gloss>1</gloss>.

Having, my most Illustrious Lord, seen and sufficiently considered the proofs of all those who count themselves masters and inventors of <term>instruments of war</term> <note>original: "instrumenti bellici"; refers to military engines and siege machinery</note>, and finding that the invention and operation of said instruments do not differ at all from common use, I shall endeavor, without disparaging anyone else, to make myself understood by Your Excellency, opening my secrets to you, and afterward offering them at your every pleasure at the appropriate times, to work effectively regarding

---

<note>1</note> In this letter (dated approximately 1483), Leonardo da Vinci offers his manifold skills to Ludovico Sforza (known as "the Moor"), placing in the foreground—take good note—his brilliant inventions in military and civil engineering, rather than his artistic knowledge.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci writes a formal letter of application to Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, asserting that his military inventions surpass those of contemporary engineers.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, military engineering, Renaissance warfare, Milan, Sforza court</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered list of military services and inventions Leonardo da Vinci is offering to Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan.</meta>

all those things which, for the sake of brevity, shall be partly noted below:

1. I have plans for bridges that are very light and strong, and suitable to be carried with great ease, with which to pursue and, at times, flee from enemies; and others that are secure and indestructible by fire or battle, easy and convenient to lift and place. Also, I have ways to burn and destroy those of the enemy.

2. In the <term>siege</term> <note>original: "obsidione"</note> of a place, I know how to remove the water from the moats, and how to make infinite bridges, <term>cats</term> <note>original: "gatte"; a "cat" was a mobile covered gallery or shed used to protect soldiers as they approached a fortification's walls</note>, scaling ladders, and other instruments pertinent to such an expedition.

3. Likewise, if by reason of the height of the banks, or the strength of the place and its site, it is not possible in the siege of a place to use the service of <term>bombards</term> <note>original: "bombarde"; early forms of cannons or mortars used to throw large stones</note>, I have methods for ruining every rock or other fortress, unless it be founded upon the very stone.

4. I have also types of bombards that are most convenient and easy to carry, and with these, I can fling a storm of small stones; and with the smoke thereof, strike great terror into the enemy, to his heavy damage and confusion.

5. And should it happen to be at sea, I have plans for many instruments most efficient for offense and defense, and vessels which will resist the fire of the largest bombards, and powder and smoke.

6. Likewise, I have ways, through mines and secret and winding paths, made without any noise, to reach a certain <insert>place</insert> <note>original: "luogo"; the word was omitted in the original draft but is necessary for the sense</note> and designated point, even if it were necessary to pass under moats or any river.

---

7. Likewise, I will make covered chariots, secure and indestructible, which, entering among the enemy with their artillery, there is no multitude of men-at-arms so great that they would not break through. and behind these, infantry can follow in great numbers, unharmed and without any hindrance. <note>This is Leonardo's famous concept for an early armored tank.</note>

8. Likewise, should the need arise, I will make <term>bombards</term> <note>large-caliber cannons for throwing stone balls</note>, mortars, and <term>passavolanti</term> <note>original: "passavolanti"; a type of long, small-caliber light cannon used for rapid fire</note> of most beautiful and useful forms, out of the common usage.

9. Where the operation of bombards might fail, I will design <term>briccole</term> <note>catapults using a counterweight</note>, <term>mangonels</term> <note>original: "mangani"; engines for throwing heavy stones</note>, <term>trebuchets</term> <note>original: "trabucchi"</note>, and other instruments of marvelous efficacy and out of the ordinary; and, in short, according to the variety of cases, I will design various and infinite things for offense and defense.

10. In time of peace, I believe I can give perfect satisfaction, in comparison with any other, in <term>architecture</term>, in the composition of buildings both public and private, and in conducting water from one place to another. Likewise, I will carry out <term>sculpture</term> in marble, bronze, and clay; similarly in painting, I can do whatever may be done, in comparison with any other, be he who he may. Furthermore, work could be undertaken on the <term>bronze horse</term> <note>original: "cavallo di bronzo"; a massive equestrian monument Leonardo intended to build for Ludovico's father</note>, which will be immortal glory and eternal honor to the happy memory of the Lord your father and of the illustrious <term>House of Sforza</term> <note>original: "casa Sforzesca"</note>.

And if any of the above-mentioned things should seem to anyone impossible or unfeasible, I offer myself as most ready <note>The letter traditionally concludes on the next page with an offer to demonstrate these skills in the Duke's park.</note>

### Note of April 23, 1490.

On the 23rd day of April 1490, I began this book and restarted the horse <note>original: "cavallo"; referring to the massive equestrian monument intended to honor Francesco Sforza, the father of Ludovico.</note>.

Jacomo <note>Gian Giacomo Caprotti, nicknamed "Salai" (Little Devil), who became Leonardo's lifelong companion and pupil.</note> came to stay with me on St. Mary Magdalene’s day <note>July 22nd</note> in 1490, at the age of 10 years.

On the second day, I had two shirts, a pair of hose, and a doublet cut out for him; and, when I put the money aside to pay for these things, he stole said money from my purse, and it was never possible to make him confess to it, although I had certain proof of it. — 4 Lire.

The following day I went to supper with Giacomo Andrea, <note>Giacomo Andrea da Ferrara was an architect and "most accurate follower of the works of Vitruvius." A scientist rather than a writer, he joined the Milanese court around 1480 and was a very close friend of Leonardo. When Gian Giacomo Trivulzio entered Milan at the head of the French army on April 15, 1500, he had Giacomo Andrea imprisoned, beheaded, and quartered for remaining loyal to Ludovico Sforza.</note> and the aforementioned Jacomo ate for two and did mischief for four, for he broke three carafes, spilled the wine, and after this, came to supper where I was.

## To Ludovico il Moro.

It grieves me greatly that having to earn my living has forced me to interrupt the progress of the work which Your Lordship previously commissioned me <gloss>2</gloss> <note>original: "l'opera"; referring to the massive bronze equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza, which Leonardo worked on for years but never finished in bronze.</note>; but I hope in a short time to have earned enough that I will be able to satisfy Your Excellency with a restful mind, to whom I commend myself. And if Your Lordship should believe that I have money, you would be mistaken, for I have kept six mouths <note>meaning six dependents or household members</note> for thirty-six months and have only had fifty ducats!

<note>2. The equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza. This fragment proves that Ludovico did not always treat the artist with great generosity.</note>

## To the Overseers <term>Fabbriceri: members of a committee responsible for the construction and maintenance of a cathedral</term> of the Cathedral of Piacenza.

The most important feature sought after in cities are their cathedrals; when approaching them, the first things that appear to the eye are the doors through which one passes into these churches.

Take heed, Lords Overseers, that the excessive haste of your desire to so quickly settle the contract for such a great work—which I hear you have ordered—does not become the reason that what is done for the honor of God and men instead turns into a great dishonor to your judgment and to your city. For, as it is a worthy land and a place of passage, it is frequented by countless foreigners. And this dishonor would occur if, through your lack of diligence, you were to place your trust in some boaster who, through his chatter or through favors granted to him here, might obtain such a work from you, which would bring long and immense infamy upon himself and upon you. 

I cannot help but feel vexed when I think of what kind of men they are who have conferred with me, wishing to enter into such an undertaking without considering their own competence <note>original: "soffizienzia"; referring to their technical skill and artistic capacity</note>, to say nothing more: one is a maker of jugs, another of armor, another a bell-founder, someone else a maker of harness-bells, and even a master of heavy artillery <term>bombardiere: a specialist in casting and firing large cannons</term>. Among these, one of the Lord's men has boasted that, because he is a close friend <note>original: "compare"; literally a godfather or close companion</note> of Master Ambrogio Ferrere <note>Ambrogio Ferrere was a high-ranking official in the Sforza administration</note> who has some authority, he has received good promises from him; and if that is not enough, that...

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci writes a draft letter to the authorities in Piacenza, warning them against hiring unqualified craftsmen—such as potters or cannon-makers—to cast the cathedral's bronze doors.</summary>
<keywords>Piacenza Cathedral, bronze casting, artistic competence, Ambrogio Ferrere, architectural commissions, Renaissance craftsmanship</keywords>

...he will mount his horse, go to the Lord, and obtain such letters of recommendation that you will never be able to deny him the work. Now, look at what wretched state scholars capable of such works are reduced to, when they must compete with such men! With what hope can they expect a reward for their virtue? Open your eyes and try to see clearly so that your money is not spent on buying your own shame. I can announce to you that from this land you will draw nothing but works of a common sort, made with vile and crude craftsmanship; there is no man here of any worth, believe me, except for <term>Leonardo the Florentine</term> <note>original: "Lonar Fiorentino"; Leonardo refers to himself in the third person here</note> who is making the bronze horse for Duke Francesco. But one should not count on him, because he has enough work to last his whole life, and I doubt that, because it is such a great undertaking, he will ever finish it.

---

There is one here whom the Lord has brought from Florence to perform this work of his; he is a worthy master, but he has so much, so very much to do, that he will never finish it.

What difference do you think there is between seeing a beautiful thing and an ugly one? Cite <term>Pliny</term> <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of "Natural History," often cited by Renaissance artists as the supreme authority on ancient art and aesthetics</note> ¹.

## To Cardinal Ippolito d’Este.

It has been only a few days since I arrived from Milan, and I have found that one of my elder brothers is unwilling to honor a will made three years ago upon the death of our father. Even though the law is on my side, nevertheless, so as not to fail myself in a matter I value greatly, I did not wish to neglect requesting from **Your Most Reverend Lordship** a letter of recommendation and favor addressed here to Lord **Raffaello Girolami**, who is currently one of our High Lords <note>The "Eccelsi Signori" or Priors, the executive body of the Florentine Republic</note>, before whom my case is being argued. Specifically, the case has been referred by His Excellency the <term>Gonfaloniere</term> <note>Piero Soderini, the chief magistrate of Florence</note> to the aforementioned Lord Raffaello, and his Lordship must decide and conclude it before the Feast of All Saints arrives. And therefore, my Lord, I pray as much as I know how and am able that **Your Reverend Lordship** writes a letter here to the said Lord Raffaello, in that tactful and affectionate manner that you possess, recommending to him **Leonardo da Vinci**, your most devoted servant, as I call myself and always wish to be; requesting him and urging him that he might grant me not only justice, but a favorable and swift conclusion. I have no doubt, based on many reports given to me, that since Lord Raffaello is most devoted to **Your Lordship**, the matter will succeed according to my wish <note>original: "ad votū"</note>. This I shall attribute to the letter of **Your Reverend Lordship**, to whom I recommend myself *again* <note>original: "iterum"</note>. And may you be well.

Florence, September 18, 1507
Your Reverend Lordship's humble servant,
**Leonardo da Vinci, painter** ¹.

---

¹ This letter is addressed to Ippolito d'Este and requests support with Raffaello Girolami, who was a prior of the Si- <note>The note cuts off, referring to the Signoria, the governing council of Florence</note>

<summary>Leonardo writes to Cardinal Ippolito d’Este seeking political influence in a legal dispute with his brothers over their father's inheritance. He asks the Cardinal to write to Raffaello Girolami, a Florentine official, to ensure a swift and favorable ruling.</summary>
