# Magnificent Lord ¹...

My Magnificent Lord, the love that Your Excellency has always shown me, and the benefits that I have continually received from you, are ever present before my mind.

I suspect that the small repayment I have made for the great benefits I have received from Your Excellency may have caused you to be somewhat upset with me; and this is because, despite the many letters I have written to Your Excellency, I have never received a response. Now I am sending <term>Salai</term> <note>Gian Giacomo Caprotti, Leonardo’s pupil and companion</note> there to inform Your Lordship how I am nearly at the end of my litigation with my brothers, and how I believe I shall be there with you this Easter, and

---

Lordship, to quickly settle the case against the brothers which was taking place in Florence. Toward the end of the spring of 1507, three years after the death of his father—from whom he had not inherited a cent due to his illegitimate birth—<term>Vinci</term> <note>Leonardo da Vinci</note> learned that his seven brothers did not want to honor the will made by his uncle Francesco in his favor. The inheritance was a trivial matter, but Vinci pursued the case with passion, making it a matter of self-respect. For this reason, he traveled from Milan—where he was in the service of <term>Louis XII</term> <note>King of France</note>—in July 1507 to Florence, where he stayed until Easter of 1508, eventually obtaining a plot of land near <term>Fiesole</term>.

¹ It is not known precisely to whom this was addressed. <term>Solmi</term> <note>Edmondo Solmi, a noted Leonardo scholar</note> believes it was intended for <term>Charles d’Amboise</term>, Lord of Chaumont and governor of Milan for Louis XII; however, after the words **Magnificent Lord**, in the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> <note>The largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings</note> there is another name—crossed out: <term>Anton Maria</term>. It was certainly written before his return from Florence to Milan, that is, before Easter 1508.

I would be very glad to know, upon my return there, where I am to reside, because I would not wish to cause any further annoyance to Your Lordship; and furthermore, having worked for the Most Christian King, I wish to know if my <term>provision</term> <note>A regular salary or stipend</note> is to continue or not. I am writing to the President regarding that water which the King granted to me, of which I was not put in possession because of a shortage in the <term>Naviglio</term> <note>The system of navigable canals in Milan</note> due to the great droughts, and because its <term>outlets</term> <note>original: "bocchegli"; the physical apertures or sluices used to measure and divert water flow</note> had not been regulated; but he certainly promised me that, once such regulation was completed, I would be put in possession of it$^1$; so I pray you that, if you should encounter the said President, you would not find it burdensome—now that such outlets are regulated—to remind the said President to grant me possession of this water, as I seemed to understand that it rested largely with him. Nothing else occurs to me. I am always at your command.

Magnificent President, having many times remembered the offers made to me by Your Excellency,

Several times I have taken the liberty of writing to remind you of the promise made to me at our last parting: namely, the possession of those twelve <term>ounces of water</term> <note>A traditional unit of measurement for water flow in Lombardy, roughly equivalent to the amount of water passing through a specific sized aperture</note> granted to me by the Most Christian King <note>Louis XII of France</note>. Your Lordship knows that I did not enter into possession of it, because at the time it was granted to me there was a water shortage in the <term>Naviglio</term> <note>The canal system of Milan</note>, both due to the great drought and because its <term>outlets</term> <note>original: "bocchelli"; the sluice gates or openings used to divert water to private property</note> had not yet been regulated. However, I was promised by Your Excellency that once such regulation was completed, I would have my wish fulfilled. Since then, understanding the Canal to be in good order, I have written several times to Your Lordship and to Master Girolamo da Cusano—who has the deed of said grant in his keeping—and I also wrote to the <term>Corigero</term> <note>Likely an official involved in the administration of the canals or a specific courier</note>, yet I never received a reply. Now I am sending Salai <note>Gian Giacomo Caprotti, Leonardo’s long-time pupil and servant</note>, my pupil and the bearer of this letter, to whom Your Lordship may say in person all that has happened; for which I pray Your Excellency.

# To Master Francesco Melzi.

Good day, Master Francesco! Can it be, by God, that of the many letters I have written you, you have never once replied? Now, just wait until I arrive there; by God, I shall make you write so much that perhaps you will grow weary of it!

My dear Master Francesco, I am sending Salai there to learn from the Magnificence of the President what end has come of that regulation of the water, which at my departure was ordered for the outlets of the Canal.

## **Drafts of letters to Giuliano de' Medici.** <note>1</note>

I have someone who, having promised himself things from me that were far less than his due, and having been disappointed in his presumptuous desire, has attempted to take all my friends away from me. And because he found them wise and not easily swayed to his will, he has threatened me that he will find such a person . . . . . who will take away my benefactors. Therefore, I have informed Your Lordship of this, so that, should this man wish to sow his usual scandals, he may not find soil fit for sowing, nor for receiving the thoughts and acts of his evil nature; so that, in his attempt to make Your Lordship an instrument of his iniquitous and wicked nature, he may remain disappointed in his desire <note>2</note>.

And if any of my people entered the workshop, he would scold them; and if anyone reproached him, he said that he was working for the <term>wardrobe</term> <note>The administrative office in charge of the Pope's furnishings and armor</note>, cleaning armor and firearms.

the malice of that German deceiver; for whom I left nothing undone that I believed might please him. First, I invited him to live and dwell with me, so that I might continually see the work he was doing and easily correct any errors; furthermore, he would have learned the Italian language, through which he could easily have spoken without an interpreter. And first of all, his money was always given to him entirely in advance of the time. Afterward, his request was to have the finished wooden models, just as they were to be made in iron, which he wanted to take back to his own country; this I denied him, telling him that I would give him a drawing showing the width, length, thickness, and shape of what he had to make; and so we remained on bad terms.

The second thing was that he set up another workshop for himself, with new vices and tools in the room where he slept, and there he worked for others; afterward, he went to dine with the Swiss Guard, where idle people congregate, and in this, he surpassed them all. From there he would go out, and most of the time two or three of them would go together with their <term>scoppietti</term> <note>Early firearms or hand-cannons, often used for hunting small game</note>, killing birds among the <term>anticaglie</term> <note>The "ancient things"—referring to the ruins of ancient Rome or the classical statues in the Vatican gardens</note>, and this lasted until evening.

## To the Lords Father Deputies¹.

Lords Father Deputies, just as it is necessary for doctors, guardians, and caretakers of the sick to understand what thing...

---

<margin>¹ It is not known precisely to whom this letter is addressed nor at what time.</margin>

--- Page 277 ---
<meta>This page continues the draft addressed to the "Lords Father Deputies," likely the officials overseeing the construction of the Milan Cathedral (the Duomo). Leonardo is using a medical analogy to explain why an architect must understand the fundamental principles of nature and physics to "heal" or complete a building.</meta>

<page-num>273</page-num>
<sig>18</sig>

...is man, what life is, what health is, and in what way a parity and a harmony of elements maintains it, and likewise how a discordance of those elements ruins and undoes it; and having well understood the aforementioned natures, he will be better able to provide a remedy than one who is deprived of such knowledge.

You know that medicines, when properly applied, restore health to the sick; and he who understands them well will apply them well, provided he also understands what man is, what life and <term>complexion</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "complexion" refers to the balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined a person's health and temperament</note> are, and what health is. Knowing these, he will well understand their opposites; and this being so, he will be closer to the remedy than any other. This same thing is needed for the "sick" Cathedral—that is, a <term>medical architect</term> <note>original: "medico architetto." Leonardo views the architect as a doctor who heals structural flaws</note> who understands well what <term>construction</term> <note>original: "edificazio"</note> is, and from what rules correct building derives, and from where those rules are drawn, and into how many parts they are divided, and what the causes are that hold the building together and make it permanent, and what the nature of <term>weight</term> <note>original: "peso"</note> is, and what the desire of <term>force</term> <note>original: "forza"</note> is, and in what manner they ought to be woven and bound together, and what effect they produce once joined. Whoever has true knowledge of these aforementioned things will leave you satisfied by his reasoning and his work.

Therefore, with this in mind, I will strive—without detracting from or defaming anyone—to satisfy you partly with reasons and partly with works; sometimes demonstrating effects through their causes, and sometimes confirming reasons through experiences, fitting to these the authority of the <term>ancient architects</term> and the evidence of buildings already made, and what the reasons are for their ruin or their permanence, etc.

<note>The list below appears to be a vocabulary or index of key terms used in the draft.</note>
<gloss>man, health, medical architect, construction, weight, force, the ancient architects, buildings</gloss>

<summary>Leonardo uses an extended medical metaphor to argue that an architect must understand the "anatomy" of a building—specifically the physics of weight and force—just as a doctor must understand the human body. He offers his services to the Cathedral deputies, promising to prove his methods through both ancient authority and scientific experience.</summary>
<keywords>architecture as medicine, structural engineering, weight and force, Milan Cathedral (Duomo), scientific method, ancient architects, harmony of elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s address to the Cathedral deputies regarding his model for the "tiburio" (crossing tower) of the Milan Cathedral. He transitions from the theoretical analogy of the "medical architect" to a specific promise of demonstration, followed by a starkly contrasting personal letter to his brother.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>

And with those <note>referring to the principles of physics and medicine mentioned on the previous page</note> I will demonstrate what the primary nature of weight is, and what and how many are the causes that bring ruin to buildings, and what the method is for their stability and permanence.

But so as not to be tedious to Your Excellencies, I will first speak of the invention of the first architect of the Cathedral <note>likely referring to the original designers of the Duomo di Milano</note>, and I will clearly demonstrate to you what his intention was, confirming it by the building already begun; and by making you understand this, you will be able to clearly recognize that the model made by me possesses in itself that <term>symmetry</term> <note>original: "simetria"; in the Renaissance sense of proportional harmony</note>, that correspondence, and that conformity which belongs to the building already commenced.

What a building is, and from where the rules of correct building derive, and how many and what are the parts belonging to them.

Whether it be I, or someone else who demonstrates it better than I, accept it; put aside all passion <note>Leonardo urges the committee to judge the architectural merits objectively rather than through personal bias or politics</note>.

### To a brother.

My most beloved brother. This is only to advise you that in the past days I received a letter of yours, by which I understood that you have had an heir <note>original: "erete," likely a variant of "erede" (heir)</note>, a thing which I understand has caused you extreme joy. Now, considering you to be prudent, I am entirely convinced that I am as far from having good judgment as you are from prudence; for you have rejoiced at having created for yourself a diligent enemy, who with all his efforts will desire liberty, which will not come without your death. <note>A cynical reflection on the inheritance laws and family dynamics of the time, where a son only comes into his estate upon the father's passing</note>

### To a brother.

My most beloved brother. This is only to advise you that in the past days I received a letter of yours, by which I understood that you have had an heir <note>original: "erete," likely a variant of "erede" (heir)</note>, a thing which I understand has caused you extreme joy. Now, considering you to be prudent, I am entirely convinced how far removed your judgment is from being happy about such a thing. For you have been congratulating yourself on having created a watchful enemy, who will use all his efforts for liberty, which he cannot have without your death.

# FABLES, SHORT STORIES

# AND WHIMSIES

<page-num>274</page-num>

And with those <note>referring to the principles of physics and medicine mentioned on the previous page</note> I will demonstrate what the primary nature of weight is, and what and how many are the causes that bring ruin to buildings, and what the method is for their stability and permanence.

But so as not to be tedious to Your Excellencies, I will first speak of the invention of the first architect of the Cathedral <note>likely referring to the original designers of the Milan Cathedral</note>, and I will clearly demonstrate to you what his intention was, confirming it by the building already begun; and by making you understand this, you will be able to clearly recognize that the model made by me possesses in itself that <term>symmetry</term> <note>original: "simetria"; in the Renaissance sense of proportional harmony</note>, that correspondence, and that conformity which belongs to the building already commenced.

What a building is, and from where the rules of correct building derive, and how many and what are the parts belonging to them.

Whether it be I, or someone else who demonstrates it better than I, accept it; put aside all passion <note>Leonardo urges the committee to judge the architectural merits objectively rather than through personal bias or politics</note>.

### To a brother.

My most beloved brother. This is only to advise you that in the past days I received a letter of yours, by which I understood that you have had an heir <note>original: "erete," likely a variant of "erede" (heir)</note>, a thing which I understand has caused you extreme joy. Now...

# FABLES

---

**The Torrent.** — The torrent carried so much earth and stones into its own bed that it was eventually forced to change its location <note>Leonardo uses this as a metaphor for how excessive ambition or greed can destroy the very foundation one stands upon</note>.

**The Paper and the Ink.** — Seeing itself all stained by the dark blackness of the ink, the paper complained; but the ink showed the paper that it was only because of the words composed upon it that the paper would be preserved <note>A reflection on how the written word grants immortality to fragile materials</note>.

**The Water.** — Finding itself in the proud sea, its own element, the water was seized by a desire to rise above the air. Encouraged by the element of fire <note>referring to the heat of the sun</note> and rising as a thin vapor, it seemed almost as subtle as the air itself. Having mounted high, it reached the thinnest and coldest air, where it was abandoned by the fire; and the tiny particles, being restricted, joined together and became heavy. Whereupon, as it fell, its pride was converted into flight. It fell from the sky and was then drunk up by the dry earth, where,

...imprisoned for a long time, she did penance for her sin.

**The Snow.** — A small amount of snow, finding itself clinging to the top of a rock which was situated at the very highest peak of a towering mountain, gathered its thoughts and began to consider within itself, saying:
— Now, should I not be judged haughty and proud for having placed myself, a tiny grain of snow, in such a high place, and for allowing such great quantities of snow as can be seen from here to remain lower than me? Certainly, my small quantity does not deserve this height; for I can well know, by the testimony of my small form, what the sun did yesterday to my companions, who in a few hours were destroyed by the sun. This happened to them for having placed themselves higher than was required of them. I wish to flee the wrath of the sun, and humble myself, and find a place suitable for my small size. — And having thrown itself down, it began to descend, rolling from the high slopes over the other snow. The more it sought a low place, the more its size increased, so that when it finished its course upon a hill, it found itself to be of no less size than the hill that supported it. And it was the last of the snow to be melted by the sun that summer. This is said for those who, by humbling themselves, are exalted.

**The Razor.** — One day, a razor emerged from the handle which serves as its sheath,

<summary>Leonardo recounts the fable of a grain of snow that finds greatness through humility and begins the tale of a vain razor. These stories serve as moral allegories on the virtues of modesty and the dangers of pride.</summary>
<keywords>fables, allegory, humility, snow, pride, Leonardo da Vinci, moral lessons</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the fable of the Razor, which began on the previous page as an allegory for the dangers of vanity and the necessity of constant practice.</meta>

and having placed itself in the sun, it saw the sun reflected in its body; at which it took supreme pride <note>original: "groria" (glory/pride)</note>, and turning its thoughts backward, it began to say to itself:
— Shall I ever return again to that shop from which I have newly emerged? Certainly not; may it not please the Gods that such splendid beauty should fall into such baseness of spirit! What madness would it be that would lead me to shave the soapy beards of rustic peasants and perform such mechanical operations! Is this body made for such exercises? Certainly not. I wish to hide myself in some secret place, and there pass my life in tranquil repose. — And so, having remained hidden for several months, one day it returned to the open air, and emerging from its sheath, it saw that it had been transformed into the likeness of a rusty saw, and its surface no longer reflected the shining sun. With vain repentance, it wept in fruitlessly over the irreparable damage, saying to itself: — Oh! how much better it was to practice with the barber my lost edge of such sharpness! Where is the lustrous surface? Certainly, the annoying and foul rust has consumed it! — This same thing happens to those wits <note>original: "ingegni" (intellects/talents)</note> who, instead of exercise, give themselves over to idleness; who, like the aforementioned razor, lose their keen sharpness, and the rust of ignorance spoils their form.

**The Lily.** — The lily placed itself upon the bank of the Ticino <margin>Ticino.</margin>, and the current pulled the bank away together with the lily.

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the conclusion of the fable of the Razor and the brief image of the Lily into a series of short fables or allegories involving trees and plants, likely written by Leonardo da Vinci as moral lessons on human behavior.</meta>

**The Walnut Tree. —** The walnut tree, displaying the richness of its fruit to travelers along a road, was stoned by every man <note>A common motif in fables where generosity or visibility leads to being mistreated.</note>.

**The Fig Tree. —** The fig tree, standing without fruit, was noticed by no one; wishing to be praised by men by producing such fruit, it was instead bent and broken by them.

**The Plant and the Stake. —** The plant complains of the dry and old stake that had been placed at its side, and of the dry poles that surround it: yet the one keeps it upright, and the others guard it from bad company <note>The "bad company" likely refers to trampling animals or encroaching weeds.</note>.

**The Cedar and the other plants. —** The cedar, grown proud of its beauty, became suspicious of the plants that were around it, and had them removed from its presence; afterwards the wind, no longer being interrupted by the other trees, threw it to the ground uprooted.

**The Clematis. —** The clematis <note>original: "vitalba" (Old Man's Beard/Traveler's Joy)</note>, not being content within its own hedge, began to spread its branches across the public road and attach itself to the opposite hedge; because of this, it was later broken by travelers.

**The Vine and the old tree. —** The vine, having grown old upon an old tree, falls together with the ruin of that tree: and so, because of its poor choice of company, it perished along with it.

**The Willow and the Vine. —** The willow, which by its long shoots wishes to grow so as to surpass every other plant, because it made company with the vine—which is pruned every year—was itself also always maimed <note>The willow is cut back just as the vine is, losing its desired height due to its proximity to the cultivated plant.</note>.

<summary>A collection of short fables by Leonardo da Vinci using botanical allegories to illustrate moral failings such as pride, poor company, and the dangers of overreaching.</summary>
<keywords>fables, Leonardo da Vinci, allegory, nature, pride, walnut, fig, cedar, willow, vine</keywords>

**The Cedar.** — The cedar, having a desire to produce beautiful and great fruit at its very summit, directed all the strength of its sap toward that end; once this fruit had grown, it became the cause of the bending and bowing of the tree's high and straight peak. <note>Leonardo often uses botanical growth as a metaphor for overreaching ambition.</note>

**The Peach Tree.** — The peach tree, feeling envy toward the great quantity of fruit it saw produced by its neighbor, the walnut tree, determined to do the same; it loaded itself with its own fruit in such a way that the weight of said fruit pulled it down, uprooted and broken, to the level ground.

**The Elm and the Fig Tree.** — The fig tree, standing near the elm and seeing that the elm's branches were without fruit, and yet had the audacity to keep the sun away from its own unripe figs, said to it with reproaches: — O elm, are you not ashamed to stand before me? Just wait until my children reach their mature age, and you will see where you find yourself. — When those children had later matured, a squad of soldiers happened upon the spot; in order to take its figs, the tree was entirely torn, stripped of its branches, and broken by them. As it stood there, thus maimed in its limbs, the elm questioned it, saying: — O fig tree, how much better it would have been to remain without children, than to come to such a miserable state because of them! <note>The "children" here are the figs; Leonardo uses this allegory to suggest that great wealth or offspring can bring unwanted and destructive attention.</note>

**The Net.** — The net, which was accustomed to catching the fish, was itself caught and carried away by the fury of the fish.

**The Walnut and the Bell Tower.** — A walnut, finding itself carried by a crow to the top of a high bell—

And having brought them to the **willow**, she was received with a joyful face; and having scraped the ground near the willow a little with her feet, she planted those grains with her beak in a circle around it. These, growing in a short time, began with the increase and opening of their branches to occupy all the branches of the willow, and with their large leaves to take away from it the beauty of the sun and the sky. And, as if such evil were not enough—the gourds following <note>original: "seguendo"; meaning the gourds grew larger afterward</note>—it began, by its awkward weight, to pull the tips of the tender branches toward the earth, with strange twisting and discomfort to them. Then, shaking itself and tossing in vain to make those gourds fall off, and raving in vain for several days in such a delusion, because the good and strong connection <note>original: "collegazione"; referring to the way the gourd stems wind tightly around the willow</note> denied such thoughts, seeing the wind pass by, it commended itself to it, and the wind blew hard. Then the old and hollow trunk of the willow split into two parts, down to its roots, and having fallen in two pieces, it wept for itself in vain, and realized it was born to never have any good.

**The Spider.** — The spider, wishing to catch the fly in its deceptive nets, was cruelly killed upon them by the hornet.

**The Donkey and the Ice.** — The donkey, having fallen asleep upon the ice of a deep lake, the heat of its body dissolved the ice, and the donkey woke up underwater to its own great misfortune, and immediately drowned.

**The Ant and the Grain of Wheat.** — The ant, having found a grain of millet, the grain, feeling itself seized by her, cried out: — If you do me the great favor of letting me fulfill my desire to be born, I will give you back a hundred of myself. — And so it was done. <note>This reflects the biological reality of germination, where one seed produces many more.</note>

**The Oyster and the Crab.** — This creature, when the moon is full, opens itself completely; and when the crab sees it, he throws a stone or a stalk inside it, so that the oyster cannot close itself again, and thus becomes the food of that crab. So it is for one who opens his mouth to tell his secret: he makes himself the prey of the indiscreet listener.

**The Thrushes and the Owl.** — The thrushes rejoiced greatly, seeing that the man caught the owl and took away its liberty, tying it with strong bonds by its feet. This same owl was later, by means of birdlime <term>vischio: a sticky substance spread on branches to catch birds</term>, the cause not of the thrushes losing their liberty, but their very lives.

This is said for those lands which rejoice to see their superiors lose their liberty, by means of...

**The Spider and the Grapes.** — A spider, having found a bunch of grapes which, because of its sweetness, was much visited by bees and various kinds of flies, thought he had found a place very convenient for his deception. Having lowered himself down by his thin thread and entered his new dwelling, he stayed there every day, positioning himself at the openings <term>spiraculi: small air holes or gaps</term> formed by the spaces between the grapes. From there, like a thief, he would assault the wretched animals who were not on guard against him. But after several days had passed, the grape-gatherer plucked that bunch of grapes and placed it with the others, and together with them, it was crushed. Thus, the grapes became a snare and a deception for the deceptive spider, just as they had been for the deceived flies.

**The Peasant and the Vine.** — The peasant, seeing the utility that resulted from the vine, gave it many supports <term>sostentaculi: props or poles used in viticulture</term> to hold it up high; but once he had taken the fruit, he removed the poles and let the vine fall, making a fire out of its supports. <note>This allegory reflects on the transactional nature of human gratitude, which often vanishes once a benefit is received.</note>

**The Legend of Wine and Muhammad.** — Wine, the divine liquor of the grape, finding itself in a golden and rich cup upon the table of Muhammad <note>original: "Maumetto"</note>, and being puffed up with the glory of such great honor, was suddenly assailed by a contrary thought, saying to itself: — What am I doing? Why do I rejoice? Do I not perceive that I am near my death, and that I must leave the golden dwelling of this cup to enter into the foul

And the vines were then left free with their fruits.

### **The Flames and the Cauldron** (Fragment).

— A little bit of fire, which had remained in a small piece of coal among the lukewarm ashes, was nourishing itself scantily and poorly on the little moisture that remained within it, when the kitchen maid appeared there to perform her usual culinary duties. Having placed the wood in the hearth <note>original: "focolare"</note> and, with a match <note>original: "solfanello": a sulfur-tipped stick used for lighting</note>, having already resuscitated that fire which was almost dead, a small flame broke out; and having caught among the arranged logs, and having placed the cauldron <note>original: "caldara"</note> on top, she confidently departed from there without any further suspicion.

Then, the fire, rejoicing at the dry wood placed above it, began to rise up: driving out the air

**The Flint and the Steel.** — The stone <note>The flint</note>, being struck by the steel <note>original: "acciarolo"</note> of the fire, was greatly astonished, and with a harsh voice said to it: "What presumption moves you to trouble me? Do not give me such toil, for you have mistaken me for another; I have never displeased anyone." To which the steel replied: "If you remain patient, you will see what marvelous fruit shall come forth from you."

At these words the stone, finding peace, stood firm with patience through the ordeal <note>original: "martire," literally martyrdom or suffering</note>, and saw the marvelous fire born from itself, which, by its own power, worked upon infinite things.

This is told for those who are frightened at the beginnings <note>original: "prencipi," meaning the start or principles</note> of their studies, and who then, once they dispose themselves to be able to master them and give continuous work to those studies with patience, see things of marvelous demonstration result from them.

**The Butterfly and the Light.** — As the painted butterfly <note>original: "parpaglione," an archaic term for butterfly</note> went wandering and flitting through the darkened air, it caught sight of a light, toward which it immediately directed itself. Circling it with various turns, it marveled greatly at such splendid beauty; and not being content only with seeing it, it set itself forward to treat the light as it was accustomed to do with fragrant flowers. Directing its flight, it passed with a bold spirit near the light, which consumed the tips of its wings and legs and other ornaments. Having fallen at the foot of it, the butterfly considered with wonder how this event had happened, for it could not grasp in its mind that any evil or harm could come from such a beautiful thing. Having somewhat restored its failing strength, it took flight once more, and passing through the body of that light, it fell immediately burnt into the oil that nourished the flame. Only so much life remained that it could consider the cause of its ruin, saying to the light: "Oh cursed light! I believed I had found my happiness in you; in vain I weep for my mad desire, and to my own cost

# SHORT STORIES AND CURIOSITIES

---

**A friar's trick played on a merchant.** — It is the custom of the <term>Friars Minor</term> <note>The Franciscans, a religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi, known for their vows of poverty.</note>, at certain times, to observe specific Lents of theirs, during which they do not eat meat in their convents; but while traveling, because they live on alms, they have permission to eat whatever is set before them. Thus, it happened on such a journey that a pair of these friars found themselves at an inn in the company of a certain petty merchant. They were at the same table, upon which was served—due to the poverty of the inn—nothing but a single roasted chicken. The merchant, seeing that this was hardly enough for himself, turned to the friars and said: "If I remember correctly, you do not eat any kind of meat in your convents on such days." At these words, the friars were forced by their rule, without any further quibbling, to admit that this was the truth. Therefore, the little merchant got his wish; and so he ate the chicken himself, and the friars did the best they could <note>Meaning they likely ate only bread or side dishes, leaving the meat to the merchant.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces a humorous anecdote about a clever merchant who uses the religious rules of two Franciscan friars against them to secure a whole roasted chicken for himself at an inn.</summary>
<keywords>Friars Minor, Franciscans, merchant, wit, fasting, Lent, social satire, Leonardo da Vinci, hospitality</keywords>

--- Page 296 ---
<meta>This page continues the humorous anecdotes involving friars and merchants, specifically resolving the story of the chicken from the previous page before starting a new jest about a painter and a priest.</meta>

Now, after such a dinner, these three companions all departed together; and after traveling some distance, they found a river of considerable width and depth. Since all three were on foot (the friars due to poverty and the other due to greed), it was necessary, for the sake of the company, that one of the friars—being barefoot—should carry this petty merchant across on his shoulders. Therefore, having given the merchant his wooden sandals to hold, the friar loaded himself with the man.

It so happened that, while this friar was in the middle of the river, he too remembered his rule; and stopping, in the manner of Saint Christopher,¹ he raised his head toward the one who was weighing him down and said: "Tell me a little, do you have any money on you?" "You know well," the other replied, "how do you think merchants like me would go about otherwise?" "Alas!" said the friar, "our rule forbids us from carrying any money on our person"; and he immediately threw him into the water. When the merchant realized this, he saw that the previous injury <note>Referring to the merchant tricking the friars out of their dinner on the previous page.</note> had been wittily avenged; with a pleasant laugh, and half-blushing with shame, he peacefully endured the vengeance.

### **The witty remark of an artisan to a lord.** — An

lord could only see men of less power
than himself: for this reason, lords lack that very pleasure.

### **A friend to a slanderer.** — A man stopped associating with one of his friends because that friend often spoke ill of his other friends. This friend who had been left behind, meeting him one day and complaining bitterly, begged him to say what the reason was that had made him forget such a long friendship. To which the other replied: — "I no longer wish to associate with you because I wish you well; I do not want you to speak ill of me, your friend, to others, and have them form a poor impression of you as I have, hearing you speak ill of me. Therefore, if we no longer spend time together, it will appear that we have become enemies <note>original: "nimici"</note>, and when you speak ill of me, as is your habit, you will not be as deserving of blame as you would be if we were still seen together."

**Of a sleepyhead.** — A man was told to get out of bed because the sun had already risen, and he replied: — "If I had as much traveling and business to do as he does, I too would already be up; however, having so little a journey to make, I do not wish to get up yet."

**Between biters.** <note>The term "morditori" (biters) refers to people who use sharp, biting wit or sarcasm against one another.</note> — One man sees a large sword at another's side and says: — "Oh, you poor fellow! It has been a long time since I saw you tied to this weapon; why do you not untie yourself, since your hands are free and you possess your liberty?" — To which the other replied: — "This is none of your business; rather, it is an old story." — The first man, feeling the "bite" of this retort, replied: — "I know you to be aware of so few things in this world that I believed every well-known thing would be news to you."

A man said to an acquaintance: — "Your eyes have all changed to a strange color." — The other replied that this happened to him often: — "But you have never paid it any mind." — "And when does this happen to you?" — The other responded: — "Every time my eyes see your strange face; because of the violence received from such a great displeasure, they turn pale and change into a strange color."

One man said that in his country, the strangest things in the world were born. The other replied: — "You, having been born there, confirm this to be true by the strangeness of your own ugly presence."

## Mock Epitaph.

Here is preserved the pit <note>original: "nocciolo," literally the stone of a fruit, used here metaphorically for the body</note> in which the virtuous soul of such-and-such a poet was clothed.

## Letters on the Giant.

->I.<-

Dear Benedetto, to give you news of the things from the <term>Levant</term><note>The lands of the Eastern Mediterranean.</note>, know that in the month of June there appeared a giant who comes from the desert of Libya.

This giant was born on Mount Atlas, and he was black, and he fought against Artaxerxes alongside the Egyptians and the Arabs, Medes and Persians; he lived in the sea, feeding on whales, great sperm whales, and ships.

His black face at first sight is very horrible and frightening to look upon, and especially the deep-set and red eyes, positioned under fearful and dark brows, enough to make the weather turn cloudy and the earth tremble.

And believe me, there is no man so fierce that, wherever the giant turned his fiery eyes, he would not have gladly put on wings to flee, for he seemed like the face of the infernal Lucifer.

<summary>This page features a humorous epitaph and the beginning of a fantastical letter describing a terrifying giant from the East, blending mythological references with vivid caricature.</summary>
<keywords>satire, giant, Libya, Mount Atlas, Artaxerxes, monsters, Levant, mock-heroic</keywords>

--- Page 302 ---
<meta>This page continues the fantastical letter to Benedetto describing the terrifying giant from the Levant, moving from a description of his face to his destructive rampage against a crowd of people.</meta>

...angelic in comparison to that one. The nose was wrinkled with wide <term>nostrils</term><note>original: "anari," an archaic form of "nari."</note>, from which sprouted many large bristles, beneath which was the curled mouth with thick lips, from the ends of which grew hair like that of a cat, and yellow teeth. He towers above the heads of <term>men</term><note>original: "omini," archaic spelling of "uomini."</note>, even when they are on horseback, from the tops of their feet upward. ..... Turning his anger into fury, he began with his feet—driven by the rage of his powerful legs—to enter among the crowd, and with kicks he threw <term>men</term> through the air, who fell upon the other <term>men</term> no differently than if it <term>had been</term><note>original: "fussi," archaic form of "fosse."</note> a thick fall of hail.

And many were those who, in dying, brought death to others; and this cruelty lasted until the dust stirred up by those great feet, rising into the air, forced this <term>infernal fury</term> to retreat <term>backward</term><note>original: "indrieto," archaic form of "indietro."</note>.

And we continued our flight.

Oh! how many various assaults were used against this <term>possessed creature</term><note>original: "indiavolata," literally "en-deviled" or one possessed by a demon.</note>, to whom every injury was nothing! Oh! miserable people, for you the impregnable fortresses are of no use, nor the high walls of the city, nor being in a great multitude, nor your houses or palaces! Nothing is left to you but small holes and underground caves, in the manner of crabs or crickets or similar animals: <insert>[there]</insert> you find safety and your escape!

Oh, how many unhappy mothers and fathers were deprived of their children! Oh, how many miserable women deprived of their companions!

Certainly, certainly, my dear <term>Benedetto</term><note>Likely Benedetto Dei, a Florentine merchant and traveler known for his correspondence with Leonardo da Vinci.</note>, I do not believe that, since the world was created, there <term>was</term> ever seen a lamentation, a public weeping performed with such terror!

<summary>The letter continues with a vivid description of the giant's physical grotesqueness and his violent rampage through a crowd, forcing the survivors to hide in holes like insects.</summary>
<keywords>giant, destruction, Benedetto Dei, caricature, terror, Levant, metaphorical violence</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the terrifying giant from the Levant, shifting from the description of the people's desperate flight to the giant's sudden fall and subsequent retaliation against the swarming crowd.</meta>

Certainly, in this case, the human race <note>original: "spezie umana"</note> has reason to envy every other generation of animals. For if the eagle surpasses other birds in power, the lesser ones are not defeated in speed of flight; thus the swallows, with their swiftness, escape the predation of the merlin <note>original: "smerlo," a small species of falcon</note>. Dolphins <note>original: "dalfini"</note>, with their quick flight through the water, escape the predation of whales and great sperm whales <note>original: "capidogli"</note>. But for us, miserable ones! No flight is of any use, because this creature, even with a slow pace, far outstrips the course of any fast steed <note>original: "corsiero"</note>. I know not what to say or what to do; it seems to me that I am swimming head-down through that great throat, remaining buried in confusion and death within that vast belly.

## II.

When the fierce giant fell upon the blood-stained and muddy earth, it seemed as if a mountain had collapsed, whereby the countryside <insert>[was]</insert> shaken by an earthquake, bringing terror even to the infernal Pluto <note>The Roman god of the Underworld</note>. Because of the great impact, he lay upon the flat ground for a while, stunned. Immediately the people, believing he had been killed by some lightning bolt, returned in a great throng—in the manner of ants that scurry in a fury, running over a fallen oak <note>original: "rogere," likely referring to a "rovere" or sessile oak tree</note>—so these people ran over his vast limbs, traversing them and inflicting frequent wounds.

Whereupon the giant, having regained his senses and feeling himself almost covered by the multitude, suddenly felt the sting of their punctures. He let out a bellow <note>original: "muglio"</note> that seemed like a terrifying peal of thunder; and, placing his hands upon the ground, he raised his fearsome face. Placing one of his hands upon his head,

Whereupon, as he shook his head, the men flew through the air no differently than hail does when it travels with the fury of the winds; and it was found that many of these men were killed by those who rained down upon them, then standing upright and trampling them with their feet.

And holding onto the hair and striving to hide among it, they acted in the likeness of sailors who, when they encounter a storm <note>original: "fortuna," a common Renaissance term for a sea-storm</note>, run up the ropes to lower the sail when there is little wind.

## Dialogue between the brain and the spirit
## that dwelt within it.

The spirit, finding the brain <note>original: "cerebro"</note> from which it had departed, spoke with a loud voice, moved by these words:

> O happy, O fortunate spirit, from whence you departed! I have, to my great regret, known this man well. This is a receptacle of villainy; this is a true storehouse <note>original: "ammunizione," here meaning a collection or magazine</note> of supreme ingratitude, in the company of all the vices.
> 
> But why do I go on wearying myself with words in vain? The sum of all sins is found in him alone. And if any among them is found who possesses any goodness, they are treated no differently than I am by other men; and in effect, I have reached this conclusion: that it is bad if they are enemies, and worse if they are friends.

<summary>The giant violently shakes the humans from his hair like lice or hail, followed by a cynical dialogue where a spirit laments the inherent vice and ingratitude found within the human mind.</summary>
<keywords>giant, lice metaphor, hail, brain and spirit, human vice, ingratitude, allegory</keywords>

# ALLEGORIES AND EMBLEMS

## Love of Virtue.

The Caladrius <note>original: "Calendrino," referring to the mythical bird known in medieval bestiaries as the Caladrius or Charadrius</note> <margin>The calandra lark.</margin> is a bird of which it is said that, when brought before a sick person, if the said sick person is destined to die, this bird turns its head away and never looks at them; but if the sick person is to recover, this bird never loses sight of them, and indeed is the cause of removing all illness from them.

In a similar way, the love of virtue never looks upon anything base or wicked, but rather always dwells upon honest and virtuous things, and always finds its home in the noble heart <note>original: "cor gentile," a central concept in 13th-century Italian poetry regarding innate nobility of soul</note>, just as birds do in the green forests upon the flowering branches <note>original: "Al cor gentil ripara sempre Amore, | come a la selva augello in la verdura." This is a direct reference to the famous poem by Guido Guinizelli, the father of the "Sweet New Style" of poetry.</note>; it demonstrates this love most in adver—

---

<summary>This page uses the myth of the Caladrius bird to illustrate how the love of virtue focuses only on the noble and the good, drawing a direct parallel to the poetic traditions of the 'noble heart'.</summary>
<keywords>Caladrius, virtue, noble heart, Guido Guinizelli, allegory, bestiary, love</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of moral allegories begun on the previous page, transitioning from the definition of the 'noble heart' into specific animal emblems representing human emotions and vices.</meta>

sity as in prosperity, acting like a light, which shines most brightly where it finds the darkest place.

### Joy.

Joy is attributed to the rooster, which rejoices at every little thing and sings with various and playful movements.

### Sadness.

Sadness is likened to the raven, which, when it sees its newly born children are white, departs out of great sorrow; with a sad lament, it abandons them and does not feed them until it sees at least a few black feathers on them. <note>A common medieval belief that ravens were horrified by their pale hatchlings and only recognized them as their own once they turned black.</note>

### Avarice.

The toad feeds upon the earth, and always remains thin because it is never satisfied; so great is its fear that the earth itself might fail it. <note>This reflects the classic definition of greed: fearing lack even amidst abundance.</note>

### Correction.

When the wolf goes stealthily around some livestock pen, and if, by chance, it should place its foot wrongly so that it makes a noise, it bites its own foot to correct itself for such an error.

<summary>This page presents a moral bestiary, using the rooster, raven, toad, and wolf as allegorical symbols for joy, sadness, greed, and self-discipline.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, bestiary, joy, sadness, avarice, correction, rooster, raven, toad, wolf, morality</keywords>

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral bestiary from page 304, moving from the themes of joy, sadness, and correction to the dangers of flattery and the virtues of prudence and truth.</meta>

### Flattery or Deceit.

The siren <note>original: "serena"; referring to the mythological creature, often depicted in medieval bestiaries as half-woman, half-bird or fish</note> sings so sweetly that she lulls the sailors to sleep; she then climbs aboard the ships and kills the sleeping mariners.

### Prudence.

The ant, by natural counsel, provides in the summer for the winter, killing the gathered seeds so that they do not sprout again; and upon these, they feed in due time. <note>A common ancient observation that ants bite the germ of a seed to prevent it from growing in their damp burrows.</note>

### Folly.

The wild ox <note>original: "bo salvatico"</note>, having a hatred for the color red, causes hunters to dress the trunk of a tree in red; the ox runs toward it and, with great fury, strikes it so hard that its horns become stuck, whereupon the hunters kill it.

### Truth.

Although partridges steal eggs from one another, nevertheless the chicks born from those eggs always return to their true mother. <note>This allegory suggests that truth and natural bonds cannot be permanently severed by theft or deception.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a series of moral emblems using the siren, ant, wild ox, and partridge to illustrate the dangers of flattery and folly, and the virtues of prudence and truth.</summary>
<keywords>flattery, prudence, folly, truth, siren, ant, wild ox, partridge, bestiary, moral allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moral bestiary, transitioning from the virtues of prudence and truth on page 305 to the vices of vainglory and intemperance, and the qualities of constancy and inconstancy.</meta>

## Vainglory.

In this vice, one reads that the peacock <note>original: "pagone"</note> is more subject to it than any other animal, because it is always contemplating the beauty of its tail, spreading it out in the shape of a wheel, and with its cry, it draws to itself the gaze of the surrounding animals. And this is the last vice that can be conquered.

## Constancy.

The phoenix <note>original: "finice"</note> resembles constancy; which, understanding by its nature its own renewal, is constant in enduring the scorching flames that consume it, and then it is born anew.

## Inconstancy.

The swift <note>original: "rondone"; a bird similar to a swallow, known for its continuous flight</note> is set as the symbol for inconstancy; for it is always in motion, because it cannot bear even the slightest discomfort.

## Intemperance.

The licorn, or rather the unicorn <note>original: "alicorno overo unicorno"</note>, because of its intemperance and not knowing how to conquer itself, and because of the delight it takes in young maidens, forgets its ferocity and wild-

<summary>This page uses the peacock, phoenix, swift, and unicorn as allegorical figures to illustrate the nature of vainglory, constancy, inconstancy, and intemperance.</summary>
<keywords>vainglory, constancy, inconstancy, intemperance, peacock, phoenix, swift, unicorn, bestiary, moral allegory</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral bestiary, completing the entry on the unicorn's intemperance before moving into descriptions of pride, moderation, magnanimity, and the vigilance of cranes.</meta>

ness; setting aside all suspicion, it goes to the seated maiden and falls asleep in her lap; and in this manner, the hunters capture it.

## Pride.

The falcon, because of its haughtiness <note>original: "alterigia"</note> and pride, wishes to lord over and overpower all the other birds of prey, and desires to be alone; and many times the falcon has been seen attacking the eagle, the queen of birds.

## Moderation.

The ermine, because of its moderation <note>original: "moderanzia"</note>, eats only once a day; and it would sooner let itself be captured by hunters than flee into a muddy den, so as not to stain its nobility <note>original: "gentilezza"; referring here to the purity of its white fur</note>.

## Magnanimity.

The falcon captures only large birds, and would sooner die than eat meat that does not have a good scent.

## Cranes.

The cranes, so that their king does not perish through poor guarding, stand around him at night with stones in their feet <note>The crane was believed to hold a stone while sleeping so that if it fell asleep, the stone would drop and wake the flock</note>.

Love, fear, and reverence: write these upon three stones of the crane.

### The Goldfinch.

The goldfinch <note>original: "calderugio"</note> gives spurge <note>original: "tortomalio"; a poisonous plant. Legend held that the goldfinch would poison its caged young to spare them a life of captivity</note> to its caged children. Death before losing liberty!

### For Doing Well.

By the branch of the walnut tree, which is only struck and beaten when it has brought its fruit to perfection, are denoted those who, upon the completion of their famous works, are struck by envy in various ways.

### On the Same Subject.

By the thorn bush, upon which good fruits are grafted <note>original: "insiditoli"; likely referring to the practice of grafting a productive branch onto a wilder, hardier rootstock</note>, is signified one who by nature was not disposed toward virtue, but through the help of a teacher produces most useful virtues from within himself.

### Of Flax.

Flax is dedicated to the death and corruption of mortals: to death by the snares <note>original: "lacciuoli"</note> used for birds, animals, and fish; to corruption by the linen cloths in which the dead are wrapped when they are buried, who then corrupt within such cloths. And furthermore, this flax is not separated from its

## Ostrich.

The gaze, combined with wisdom, gives birth to the children <gloss>1</gloss>.

## Shuttle.

I shall move so much that the web shall be finished. <note>The shuttle is a tool used in weaving to carry the thread back and forth; here it symbolizes persistence and the completion of a task.</note>

## Mottos for an emblem of generosity.

First deprived of motion than weary of being useful.
Motion will fail before the usefulness.
Death before weariness.
I do not tire of being useful: this is a motto for the carnival.
Without weariness <note>original: "Sine lassitudine"</note>.
I am never sated by serving.
All these works are not enough to tire me.

## Ingratitude.

The wood nourishes the fire that consumes it.
When the sun appears, which chases away the darkness for everyone in common, you extinguish the lamp that chased it away for you in particular for your own necessity and convenience.

<meta>This page begins the formal categorization and text of the "Prophecies," following the blank leaf noted previously. These are not supernatural predictions, but rather riddles or "social prophecies" intended for courtly entertainment.</meta>

# PROPHECIES

### Division of the prophecies.

First, concerning the affairs of rational animals <note>human beings</note>; second, of irrational animals; third, of plants; fourth, of ceremonies; fifth, of customs; sixth, of events, or edicts, or disputes; seventh, of cases that cannot exist in nature, such as saying: "the more you take from it, the more it grows" <note>a riddle referring to a hole or a ditch</note>; and reserve the great cases for the end and the weak ones for the beginning; and show first and then the punishments; eighth, of philosophical matters.

## I. — CONCERNING THE AFFAIRS OF RATIONAL ANIMALS.

### Of infants who are wrapped in swaddling clothes.

O seaside cities! I see in you your citizens, both females and males, being tightly bound with strong ties, their arms and legs being fettered <note>Leonardo uses the dramatic language of imprisonment to describe the common Renaissance practice of swaddling infants tightly in linen bands</note>.

<summary>Leonardo outlines the thematic organization of his "Prophecies"—which function as witty riddles—and provides the first entry regarding the swaddling of infants.</summary>
<keywords>prophecies, riddles, classification, rational animals, swaddling clothes, social customs</keywords>

--- Page 320 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Prophecies" or riddles concerning the human condition. The previous page introduced the concept of swaddling infants as a form of imprisonment; this page continues that theme before moving on to the social burden of dowries and the surreal nature of dreams.</meta>

...by people who will not understand your languages, and you will only be able to vent your sorrows and lost liberty through tearful weeping and sighs and lamentations among yourselves; for those who bind you will not understand you, nor will you understand them. <note>Leonardo continues the riddle of swaddling infants: the "captors" are the parents and nurses who speak to the baby in a language it cannot yet comprehend.</note>

### Of the dowries of young women.

And whereas formerly young women could not defend themselves from the lust and rapine of males, neither by the guardianship of parents nor by the strength of walls, the time will come when it will be necessary for the fathers and relatives of these girls to pay great prices to whoever is willing to sleep with them, even though they be rich, noble, and very beautiful. <note>A cynical riddle regarding the social custom of the dowry (dote), where a bride's family had to pay the groom to take her in marriage.</note>

Certainly, it seems here that Nature wishes to extinguish the human species, as a thing useless to the world and a destroyer of all created things.

### Of dreaming.

Men shall walk and yet not move; they shall speak with those who are not present; they shall hear those who do not speak. <note>Leonardo describes the sensory paradoxes of the dreaming state.</note>

It shall seem to men that they see new destructions in the sky; it shall seem that they rise up in flight therein, and flee in fear from the flames descending from it; they shall hear animals of every sort speaking human language; they shall instantly travel in their own person to different parts of the world without motion; they shall see the greatest splendors amidst the darkness.

<note>original: "fanciulle" (young girls), "lussuria" (lust), "natura" (nature), "spezie" (species), "sognare" (to dream), "omini" (men), "ruine" (ruins/destructions), "splendori" (splendors)</note>

<summary>Leonardo presents riddles regarding the economic burden of marriage dowries and the vivid, hallucinatory experiences of dreaming.</summary>
<keywords>dowries, marriage customs, nature, dreaming, flight, animals, human destruction</keywords>

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s "Prophecies" (Profezie), which are structured as riddles or paradoxes. The previous page concluded with the beginning of a description of dreams, which continues in the opening lines here.</meta>

Oh, wonder of the human species! What frenzy has led you thus? You shall speak with animals of every species, and they with you in human language. You shall see yourself falling from great heights without harm to yourself. Torrents shall accompany you. <note>Leonardo concludes his riddle on dreaming; in dreams, the impossible becomes mundane, such as surviving falls or talking to beasts.</note>

### Of the diggers.

Many shall be those who, flaying their mother, shall turn her skin inside out upon her — the laborers of the earth. <note>original: "zappatori" (hoers/diggers). The "mother" is the Earth; the "skin" is the soil or sod being turned over by the plow or spade.</note>

### Of the mouth of man which is a tomb.

Great noises shall issue from the tombs of those who have died a wretched and violent death. <note>A riddle referring to the eating of meat; the "tombs" are the stomachs of men, and the "noises" are the cries of the animals before they were slaughtered.</note>

## The numbered hours.

I shall say one word, or two, or ten, or more, as it pleases me, and I desire that in that time more than a thousand persons in that same moment say that same thing; that is, that they immediately say what I say, and they shall not see me, nor hear what I say.

These shall be the hours numbered by you; for when you say "one," all those who, like you, are counting the hours, say the same number as you at that same moment. <note>Leonardo describes the synchronized nature of time-keeping; as a clock strikes or a person counts the hour, everyone across a city or region acknowledges the same number simultaneously.</note>

<summary>This page features Leonardo da Vinci’s riddles regarding the paradoxes of dreaming, the labor of farming as a "flaying" of the earth, and the collective synchronization of time-keeping.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Prophecies, dreams, agriculture, meat-eating, time-keeping, riddles, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>
