<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s "Prophecies," a collection of riddles where common activities are described in apocalyptic or paradoxical terms. The previous page ended with riddles on dreaming, agriculture, and the measurement of time.</meta>

## II. — OF IRRATIONAL ANIMALS.

### Of donkeys.

->The many labors shall be rewarded with hunger, with<-
->thirst, with discomfort, and with beatings and stabbings.<-
<note>Leonardo highlights the cruel irony of the beast of burden's life: the more it works, the more it suffers.</note>

### Of cockerels.

->All the astrologers <gloss>¹</gloss> shall be castrated, that is, the cockerels.<-
<note>original: "strolagi" (astrologers). Leonardo refers to the practice of caponizing roosters to improve their meat.</note>

### Of kids.

->The time of Herod shall return, because the innocent<-
->children shall be taken from their nurses, and by cruel<-
->men, from great wounds, they shall die.<-
<note>A riddle for the slaughter of young goats (kids) for food. The "nurses" are the mother goats, and the "time of Herod" refers to the Biblical Massacre of the Innocents.</note>

## III. — THE PROPHECIES OF PLANTS

->The olives that fall from the olive trees give us the oil<-
->that makes light.<-

->There shall descend with fury toward the earth that which<-
->shall give us nourishment and light.<-
<note>Leonardo describes the harvesting of olives; they are beaten from the trees (falling with "fury") to be pressed into oil for cooking and lamps.</note>

---

¹ He calls the rooster an astrologer because it announces the coming day with its song.

<summary>Leonardo uses dark, metaphorical language to describe the mistreatment of domestic animals and the harvesting of olives for oil. He frames the slaughter of livestock as a return to the biblical cruelty of Herod.</summary>
<keywords>donkeys, roosters, capons, kids, Herod, olives, oil, animal cruelty, agriculture</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s "Prophecies," shifting from the natural world and agriculture to human customs and religious ceremonies. The riddles continue to use dramatic, apocalyptic language to describe everyday social and religious practices.</meta>

## Of the trees that nourish grafts.

The fathers and mothers will be seen to provide much more benefit to their step-children than to their own true children.
<note>Leonardo refers to the practice of grafting, where a "parent" tree trunk (the stock) provides nutrients to a branch from a different tree (the graft) rather than its own natural shoots.</note>

# IV. — THE PROPHECIES OF CEREMONIES.

### Of the dead who are taken to be buried.

The simple folk will carry a great quantity of lights to provide illumination on the journeys of all those who have entirely lost their visual faculty. O human follies! O living madnesses! (These two epithets belong at the beginning of the proposition).
<note>A riddle describing funeral processions. Leonardo mocks the irony of carrying candles and torches to "show the way" for a corpse that can no longer see.</note>

### Of the weeping done on Good Friday.

In all parts of Europe, there shall be weeping by great multitudes of people for the death of a single man.
<note>original: "venerdì sancto". This refers to the liturgical mourning for the crucifixion of Jesus Christ.</note>

### Of the Christians.

Many who hold the faith of the son only build temples in the name of the mother.
<note>Leonardo observes the prevalence of churches dedicated to the Virgin Mary (the Mother) rather than to Christ (the Son) directly.</note>

<vocab>prophecies, ceremonies, grafts, Good Friday, Christians, son, mother</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo presents riddles regarding the biological paradox of grafting and the perceived irrationality of religious ceremonies, specifically funerals and the veneration of the Virgin Mary.</summary>
<keywords>grafting, funerals, Good Friday, Christ, Virgin Mary, religious satire, ceremonies</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s "Prophecies," specifically the section on religious ceremonies. The previous page concluded with observations on Christian devotion to the Virgin Mary; this page continues to use the "prophecy" format to describe the rituals of the Catholic Church through a skeptical, observational lens.</meta>

### **Of the priests who say mass.**

Many shall there be who, in order to practice their craft, will dress themselves in the richest of garments; and this shall appear to be done according to the fashion of wearing aprons.
<note>Leonardo is describing the elaborate vestments worn by priests during the liturgy, comparing them to protective aprons used by tradesmen to perform their "work."</note>

### **Of the priests who carry the host within them.**

Then almost all the tabernacles where the Body of the Lord <note>original: "corpus domini"</note> resides, will be seen to move of their own accord through the various streets of the world.
<note>This riddle describes the priest (the "living tabernacle") carrying the consecrated bread (the Host) during processions or when visiting the sick.</note>

### **Of the friars who hear confession.**

The unfortunate women, of their own free will, shall go to reveal to men all of their lusts and their shameful and most secret acts.
<note>A description of the Sacrament of Penance (Confession), framed as a strange social phenomenon where private "shame" is voluntarily disclosed to a man.</note>

### **Of the paintings of the saints which are adored.**

Men shall speak to men who do not hear; they shall have their eyes open and yet not see; they shall speak to them and receive no answer; they shall ask favors of those who have ears but do not listen; and they shall carry lights for those who are blind.
<note>Leonardo highlights the irony of religious icons and statues: though they possess human features (eyes, ears, mouths), they are inanimate objects that cannot respond to the prayers or candles offered by the faithful.</note>

<summary>Leonardo continues his "Prophecies" by describing the rituals of the Church—vestments, processions, confession, and the veneration of icons—as if they were strange, paradoxical future events.</summary>
<keywords>priests, Mass, Eucharist, Tabernacle, Confession, friars, religious art, idolatry, icons</keywords>

--- Page 325 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s "Prophecies," shifting from the previous page's focus on the rituals of priests and the veneration of icons to a sharp critique of the commercialization of the Church, specifically regarding the sale of religious art, indulgences, and the wealth of monastic orders.</meta>

### Of sculptures.

->Alas! I see the Savior crucified once again.<-
<note>Leonardo refers to the act of carving or casting a crucifix as a "re-crucifixion" of Christ.</note>

### Of crucifixes which are sold.

I see Christ sold and crucified once again, and his saints martyred.
<note>A critique of the trade in religious art; by selling a representation of the suffering Christ for profit, the merchant "sells" Him just as Judas did.</note>

### Of the religion of the friars who live by their saints, who have been dead for a long time.

Those who are dead, after a thousand years, shall be the ones who provide the livelihood for many who are living.
<note>Leonardo observes that monastic orders (friars) sustain themselves financially through the reputation, relics, and shrines of saints who died centuries prior.</note>

### Of the selling of Paradise.

An infinite multitude shall publicly and peacefully sell things of the highest price, without the permission of the owner of those things, and which never belonged to them, nor were ever in their power; and to this, human justice shall provide no remedy.
<note>This refers to the sale of indulgences—the practice of paying the Church for the remission of temporal punishment for sins—effectively "selling" entry into Heaven (Paradise).</note>

### Of the friars who, by spending words, receive great riches and give away Paradise.

Invisible coins shall cause many who spend them to triumph.
<note>The "invisible coins" are prayers and promises of spiritual rewards; the friars "spend" these words to acquire physical wealth and influence.</note>

<sig>21</sig>

<summary>Leonardo continues his "Prophecies" by satirizing the commercial practices of the Church, specifically the sale of crucifixes and the trade in spiritual favors or indulgences. He highlights the irony of living friars gaining immense wealth through the names of long-dead saints.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Prophecies, religious satire, crucifixes, friars, indulgences, Paradise, monastic wealth, corruption</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Prophecies," moving from the critique of the sale of indulgences and religious art to the hypocrisy of monastic living and the strange behaviors of humanity.</meta>

## Of the churches and habitations of friars.

There shall be many who will leave behind their labors and their hardships, and their poverty of life and of goods, and will go to dwell within riches and triumphant edifices, demonstrating that this is the means of making oneself a friend to God.
<note>Leonardo satirizes how monks, who take vows of poverty, often lived in the most opulent buildings of the Renaissance.</note>

---

# V. — THE PROPHECIES OF CUSTOMS.

## Of the clearing out of All Saints.
<note>original: "Ognissanti." This likely refers to the Feast of All Saints or a specific location/church of that name in Florence.</note>

Many shall abandon their own habitations, and shall carry with them their valuables, and go to dwell in other lands.

## Of the men who sleep in the wood of trees.

Men shall sleep, and eat, and dwell among the trees, born in the forests and the fields.
<note>This "prophecy" likely describes the use of wooden beds or furniture, or perhaps the construction of timber-framed houses, framed as a return to a primitive state.</note>

## The physicians who live off the sick.

Men shall fall into such baseness that they will be grateful that others should triumph over their ills, or over the loss of their true wealth—which is to say, their health.
<note>A cynical observation that the medical profession only prospers when the rest of the population is suffering.</note>

<summary>Leonardo critiques the hypocrisy of wealthy monastic orders and offers "prophecies" regarding human migration, the use of timber in daily life, and the parasitic nature of the medical profession.</summary>
<keywords>friars, monastic wealth, All Saints, migration, furniture, physicians, health, social satire</keywords>

--- Page 327 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Prophecies," shifting from social and moral critiques to riddles concerning everyday objects and industrial processes, framed as apocalyptic or miraculous events.</meta>

# VI. — PROPHECIES OF NATURAL PHENOMENA,
# OR QUESTIONS.
<note>original: "Casi." The author implies events that can occur in nature, as opposed to those of a purely moral or social character.</note>

### **Of lanterns.**

The fierce horns of powerful bulls shall defend the light of the night from the impetuous fury of the winds.
<note>In the Renaissance, lantern panes were often made of "horn"—thin, translucent slices of ox horn—which protected the candle flame from being blown out.</note>

### **Of feathers in beds.**

Flying animals shall support men with their own feathers.
<note>A riddle describing feather-filled mattresses and pillows.</note>

### **Of the reed-comb in the loom.**

Many times a thing disunited shall be the cause of a great union — that is, the comb, made of disunited reeds, unites the threads in the silk.
<note>The "comb" or "reed" of a loom consists of many separate teeth that push the weft threads together to create a solid piece of fabric.</note>

### **The silk-spinning wheel.**

There shall be heard mournful cries, loud shrieks, and hoarse, fiery voices from those who are tormented and stripped, and finally left naked and motionless; and this shall be because of the motor, which turns everything.
<note>Leonardo personifies the silk cocoon. To harvest silk, the cocoons are boiled (the "fiery voices") and the silk is unwound or "stripped" by the spinning machine (the "motor"), leaving the pupa inside dead and "naked."</note>

<summary>Leonardo presents a series of riddles where common objects—lanterns, beds, and weaving tools—are described in dramatic, metaphorical language to disguise their mundane identities.</summary>
<keywords>lanterns, horn, feathers, weaving, loom, silk industry, technology, riddles, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Prophecies," maintaining the format of riddles where common industrial processes and everyday objects are described as if they were epic or tragic events.</meta>

### Of the flax that makes paper from rags.

He shall be revered and honored, and his precepts shall be listened to with reverence and love, who was first despised, torn, and martyred by many and diverse beatings.
<note>Leonardo describes the process of making paper from linen (flax) rags. The "beatings" refer to the pounding of the rags in a paper mill to create pulp, which eventually becomes the paper upon which wise "precepts" are written.</note>

### Of men who walk upon trees.

Walking in wooden clogs. — The mud shall be so great that men will walk upon the trees of their own countries.
<note>A clever play on words: "clogs" (zoccoli) were made of wood from trees. By wearing them, men literally stand "upon trees" to keep their feet out of the mud.</note>

### Of dice.

The bones of the dead shall be seen, with swift motion, deciding the fortune of their mover — the dice.
<note>Dice were traditionally carved from animal bone. The "mover" is the gambler whose fate depends on the roll.</note>

### Of navigation.

The trees of the great forests of Taurus and Sinai, the Apennines and Atlas, shall be seen rushing through the air from East to West, and from North to South, carrying through the air a great multitude of men.
<note>original: "aquilone a meridie" (North to South). Leonardo refers to the masts and hulls of ships made from timber. To an observer on shore, the masts of ships on the horizon appear to be "trees" moving through the air.</note>

Oh, how many vows! Oh, how many deaths! Oh, how many separations of friends and relatives! Oh, how many there shall be who will never again see their provinces or their homelands, and who will die without burial, with their bones scattered in diverse places of the world!
<note>Leonardo shifts from the riddle of the "moving trees" to a somber reflection on the perils of seafaring and the tragedy of shipwreck.</note>

<summary>Leonardo uses riddles to describe the manufacture of paper from rags, the use of wooden clogs, and the gambling of dice, concluding with a dramatic vision of wooden ships as "flying trees" that lead many to their deaths at sea.</summary>
<keywords>paper making, flax, linen, clogs, dice, gambling, navigation, shipbuilding, maritime tragedy, riddles</keywords>

--- Page 329 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Prophecies," a collection of riddles and philosophical paradoxes. The previous page concluded with a dramatic reflection on the dangers of navigation and shipwrecks.</meta>

# VII. — PROPHECIES OF CASES
# WHICH CANNOT EXIST IN NATURE

## Of the pit.

<margin>(Speak of this in the manner of a frenzy or delirium, or an insanity of the brain).</margin>
Many shall be occupied in the labor of taking away from that thing which grows larger the more one takes away from it.
<note>The answer to this riddle is a hole or a pit. The more earth one removes, the larger the hole becomes.</note>

## Of the head placed upon the pillow.

And it shall be seen that many bodies, upon having their heads removed, will manifestly grow; and when their removed heads are returned to them, their size will immediately diminish.
<note>Leonardo is describing the act of placing one's head onto a feather pillow. When the head is lifted (removed), the pillow regains its volume and "grows"; when the head is placed back down, the pillow is compressed and "diminishes."</note>

# VIII. — THE PROPHECIES
# OF PHILOSOPHICAL MATTERS.

## Of the desire for wealth.

Men shall pursue that thing which they most fear; that is, they shall be miserable, in order to avoid falling into misery.
<note>A philosophical observation on greed: people often live in self-imposed poverty and stress (misery) while hoarding wealth, all because they are terrified of becoming poor (misery).</note>

<summary>This page features Leonardo da Vinci’s riddles regarding physical paradoxes, such as the digging of a pit and the compression of a pillow, alongside a philosophical critique of the pursuit of wealth.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, riddles, paradoxes, pit, pillow, greed, wealth, misery, philosophical prophecies</keywords>

--- Page 330 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Prophecies," shifting from physical paradoxes to darker, philosophical reflections on the nature of life, consumption, and the inherent cruelty of mankind.</meta>

# On the food of animated beings.

A great part of animated bodies will pass through the bodies of other animals; that is, uninhabited houses will pass in pieces through inhabited houses, providing a benefit to the latter and bringing their own damages with them. This is to say: the life of man is made from the things he eats, which bring with them that part of man which is dead.
<note>Leonardo uses the metaphor of "houses" to describe bodies. An "uninhabited house" is the body of a dead animal or plant, which, when eaten, enters the "inhabited house" of the living person.</note>

## Of the life of men who every year change their flesh.

Men shall pass, dead, through their own bowels.
<note>A riddle describing the process of digestion and the constant regeneration of the human body through the consumption of meat.</note>

## Of the cruelty of man.

Animals shall be seen upon the earth who will always be fighting one another, with very great losses and, often, death on each side.

These shall have no limit to their malice; by their fierce limbs, a great part of the trees in the vast forests of the universe shall be brought to the ground. And after they are fed, the nourishment of their desires shall be to deal out death, affliction, labors, fears, and flight to every living thing. And because of their immeasurable pride, these will wish to raise themselves toward the heavens, but the excessive weight of their limbs...
<note>This "prophecy" describes the human race as the most predatory and destructive of all animals. Leonardo highlights how humans destroy nature and oppress all other living creatures to satisfy their endless desires.</note>

<column-break/>

<term>animated bodies</term> <note>original: "corpi animati" — living beings or creatures endowed with a soul/life force.</note>
<term>men</term> <note>original: "omini" — humans/mankind.</note>
<term>bowels</term> <note>original: "budelle" — intestines or guts.</note>
<term>cruelty</term> <note>original: "crudeltà" — inhumanity or ruthlessness.</note>
<term>malice</term> <note>original: "malignità" — wickedness or evil intent.</note>
<term>pride</term> <note>original: "superbia" — arrogance or hubris.</note>
<term>limbs</term> <note>original: "membra" — parts of the body or physical strength.</note>

Nothing shall remain upon the earth, or under the earth and the water, that shall not be pursued, displaced, or spoiled; and that of one country shall be moved to another; and the bodies of these <note>meaning humans</note> shall become the sepulcher and the passage for all the living bodies they have already killed.
O world! Why do you not open and hurl them into the deep fissures of your great chasms and caverns, and no longer show to the heavens so cruel and heartless a monster?

### On reading good works.

Happy shall be those who lend their ears to the words of the dead! — <gloss>to read good works and observe them.</gloss>

### On fame.

Feathers shall raise men, just as they do birds, toward the heavens — <gloss>that is, by the letters <note>original: "lettere" — meaning both the alphabet and literature/learning</note> written with those pens.</gloss>

### On counsel and misery.

There is a thing which, the more it is needed, the more it is refused; and this is counsel <note>advice</note>, listened to with ill-will by those who need it most, namely the ignorant. There is a thing which, the more you fear it and the more you flee from it, the closer you come to it; and this is misery, for the more you flee from it, the more miserable and without rest you make yourself.

### On the lie.

All things that in the winter shall be hidden under the snow, will remain uncovered and revealed in the summer — <gloss>said of the lie, which cannot remain hidden.</gloss> <note>Leonardo uses the cycle of seasons as a metaphor for how time eventually exposes falsehoods.</note>

### The infinite.

What is that thing which is not given, and if it were given, it would not exist?
It is the infinite, which if it could be given <note>in the sense of being defined or bounded</note>, would be terminated and finite; because that which can be given has a boundary with the thing that surrounds it at its extremities, and that which cannot be given is that thing which has no boundaries.

<column-break/>

<term>lie</term> <note>original: "bugia" — a falsehood or deception.</note>
<term>winter</term> <note>original: "verno" — archaic form of "inverno."</note>
<term>hidden</term> <note>original: "occulta" — concealed or secret.</note>
<term>the infinite</term> <note>original: "lo infinito" — that which is without end or limit.</note>
<term>boundary</term> <note>original: "termine" — a limit, end, or defining border.</note>
<term>surrounds</term> <note>original: "circuisce" — to encompass or encircle.</note>
<term>its extremities</term> <note>original: "sua stremi" — its outer edges or limits.</note>

<summary>Leonardo presents two philosophical riddles: one comparing the inevitable exposure of lies to melting snow, and another defining the infinite as something that cannot be "given" or bounded without ceasing to be itself.</summary>
<keywords>truth, lies, nature, seasons, infinity, mathematics, boundaries, logic</keywords>

# MAXIMS AND THOUGHTS

---

### On the soul.

Now look, reader, at what we might believe of our ancients, who sought to define what soul and life are—things that cannot be proven—when those things which through experience can at any time be clearly known and proven have been ignored or falsely believed for so many centuries.

.... I leave the definition of the soul to the minds of the friars, fathers of the people, who by inspiration know all secrets.

I let be the "crowned letters" ¹, because they are the ultimate truth.

---

Our body is subject to heaven, and heaven is subject to the spirit.

---

¹ Sacred books. <note>original: "lettere incoronate" — literally "crowned letters," a respectful or perhaps slightly ironic reference to Holy Scripture.</note>

<term>soul</term> <note>original: "anima" — the vital principle or spirit of a human being.</note>
<term>life</term> <note>original: "vita" — the state of being alive.</note>
<term>experience</term> <note>original: "isperienzia" — empirical observation, which Leonardo valued above all traditional authority.</note>
<term>centuries</term> <note>original: "seculi" — long periods of time.</note>
<term>friars</term> <note>original: "frati" — members of religious orders.</note>
<term>fathers of the people</term> <note>original: "padri de' popoli" — a title for clergy.</note>
<term>spirit</term> <note>original: "spirito" — the non-material essence, placed here at the top of the cosmic hierarchy.</note>
<term>Sacred books</term> <note>original: "Libri sacri" — the Bible or theological texts.</note>

<summary>Leonardo expresses skepticism toward ancient definitions of the soul and life, preferring empirical experience over metaphysical speculation, which he sarcastically leaves to the clergy. He concludes with a hierarchical view of existence where the spirit governs the heavens and the heavens govern the body.</summary>
<keywords>soul, spirit, experience, skepticism, religion, cosmos, ancient philosophy, theology</keywords>

The senses are of the earth; reason stands apart from them when it contemplates.

->All our knowledge begins with the senses.<-

---

Every part has an inclination to reunite with its whole to escape from its own imperfection.
The soul desires to remain with its body because, without the organic instruments of that body, it can neither act nor feel anything.

---

->Naturally, everything desires to maintain itself in its own being.<-

---

->Nothingness is the deprivation of being.<-

---

The soul can never be corrupted by the corruption of the body, but acts within the body like the wind that causes the sound of an organ; if a pipe is spoiled, the wind cannot produce a good effect through it. <note>Leonardo uses the metaphor of a musical instrument to explain that the soul remains perfect even when the physical body fails, much like a musician cannot play well on a broken flute.</note>

And this man possesses a supreme madness: that is, he always struggles so as not to struggle, and life flees from him while he hopes to enjoy the goods acquired with such great labor.

---

Now see, the hope and the desire to return to one's homeland and to return to the first state is like the moth to the light; and the man who, with constant longing and joyful words, always awaits the new spring,

<note>The text breaks off here, capturing Leonardo's poetic observation on the human drive toward "returning home"—which he identifies as a return to the elements or the source of life, even if it leads to our destruction like the moth in the flame.</note>

always the new spring, always the new months, and the new years—it seems to him that the things he desires are too slow in coming—and he does not perceive that he is desiring his own destruction. But this desire is the quintessence <note>original: "quintessenza" — the fifth element or the purest essence of a substance</note>, the spirit of the elements, which, finding itself imprisoned as the soul of the human body, always desires to return to its sender. And I want you to know that this desire is that quintessence which is the companion of nature, and man is the model of the world <note>original: "omo è modello dello mondo" — a reference to the 'microcosm' theory, where the human body reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note>.

Why does the eye see a thing more clearly in dreams than with the imagination while awake?

The idea, or the imaginative faculty, is both the rudder and the bridle of the senses, insofar as the thing imagined moves the sense.

---

And in this case, I know that I shall acquire few enemies, because no one will believe that I could be speaking of him; for there are few whom their own vices displease. Rather, only those men are displeased by them who are of a nature contrary to such vices; and many hate their fathers and destroy friendships when they are reproached for their vices, and neither contrary examples nor any human counsel is of any use to them.

---

The water you touch in the rivers is the last of that which has passed, and the first of that which is coming: so it is with the present time.

->No counsel is more loyal than that which is given from ships that are in peril.<-

That which can be lost is not called wealth. Virtue is our true good and is the true reward of its possessor; it cannot be lost, it does not abandon us, unless life first leaves us. Goods and external riches are always held with fear; they often leave their possessor in scorn and mockery, losing their possession.

->Fear is born sooner than anything else.<-

From *Cornelius Celsus* ². The supreme good is wisdom, the supreme evil is the pain of the body. Because we are composed of two things, that is, of soul and of body, of which the first is better and the worse is the body; wisdom belongs to the better part, and the supreme evil belongs to the worse and most wretched part.

---

¹ These last six sentences are taken from the *Flower of Virtue* <note>original: "Fiore di Virtù" — a popular medieval moral compendium</note>, chapters XVIII and XXXII.

² AURELIUS CELSUS or AULUS CORNELIUS CELSUS was a Latin writer of the 1st century AD. He composed treatises on rhetoric, history, and military art, but only one remains, *On Medicine* <note>original: "De Medicina"</note>, published for the first time in Florence in 1478.

wisdom is in the soul. Thus, pain is the worst thing in the body; therefore, just as the supreme evil is bodily pain, so wisdom is the supreme good of the soul—that is, of the wise man—and no other thing is to be compared to it.

Many times a single thing is pulled by two forces, namely: necessity and power. The rain falls, and the earth absorbs it by the necessity of moisture; the sun dries it, not by necessity, but by power.

For if you multiply your garments according to the increase of the cold, that cold will not be able to harm you; in the same way, increase your patience against great injuries, and those injuries will not be able to offend your mind.

---

All animals languish, filling the air with lamentations; the forests go to ruin, and the mountains are torn open to snatch away the metals generated within them; but what more wicked thing can I say of those who raise praises <note>original: "lalde," an archaic form of "lodi" (praises).</note> to heaven for those who, with the greatest fervor, have harmed their country and the human species?

Acquire something in your youth that may repair the damage of your old age. And if you understand that old age has wisdom for its food, conduct yourself in such a way in your youth that your old age does not lack nourishment.

Men wrongly complain of the flight of time, blaming it for being too swift, not perceiving that its passage is sufficient; but a good memory, with which nature has endowed us, makes everything that has long since passed seem present to us.

O sleeper, what thing is sleep? Sleep has a likeness to death; oh, why then do you not perform such work that after death you may have the likeness <note>Leonardo leaves the sentence unfinished here, but the implication is "the likeness of life" or "immortality" through one's deeds.</note>

Naturally, good men desire to know.
I know that many will say this is a useless work, and these will be those of whom Demetrius <note>Demetrius the Cynic, a philosopher known for his contempt for the unlearned masses.</note> said he took no more account of the wind which caused the words in their mouths than of the wind which came out of their lower parts; men who have only a desire for physical riches and pleasure, and are entirely deprived of that of wisdom, which is the food and truly secure wealth of the soul; because as much as the soul is worthier than the body, so much more worthy are the riches of the soul than those of the body. And often when I see one of these take this work in hand, I wonder if they will put it to their nose like a monkey, or ask me if it is something to eat.

When fortune comes, seize her with a sure hand—from the front, I say, because behind she is bald. <note>A common Renaissance trope: Occasion or Fortune has a long forelock to be grasped as she approaches, but is bald behind so she cannot be caught once she has passed.</note>

Among the great things found among us, the existence of Nothingness is the greatest. This resides in time, and stretches its limbs into the past and into the future, with which it occupies all works past and those yet to come, both of nature and of the animals; and it possesses nothing of the indivisible present. This does not extend over the essence of any thing. <note>Leonardo reflects on the mathematical and philosophical concept of "nothing" or "zero," noting that the present moment is a point without duration, while the "nothingness" of the past and future consumes all of existence.</note>

--- Page 351 ---
<meta>This page introduces the scholarly context of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical sketches, identifying his primary literary sources and the cultural significance of emblems in the Renaissance.</meta>

# APPENDIX

## ON THE VINCIAN ALLEGORIES

---

For quite some time now, scholars of Leonardo da Vinci have been interested in the sketches and allegorical notes scattered throughout his manuscripts, whether to find their literary source, to provide an exact interpretation, or to indicate their purpose with certainty.

The main sources of the allegories taken from the world of beasts are, as is well known, the *Flower of Virtue* <note>original: "Fiore di Virtù," a popular 14th-century anonymous work on moral philosophy</note>, the *Acerba* <note>original: "L'Acerba," an encyclopedic poem by Cecco d'Ascoli</note>, and *Pliny* $^1$ <note>referring to Pliny the Elder's "Natural History"</note> which remained, throughout the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries <note>original: "Quattro e il Cinquecento"</note> and beyond, the traditional sources of all the chivalric and amorous "devices" <term>impresa: a heraldic symbol or motto used by an individual to express a personal aim or state of mind</term> that adorned arms, books, veils, capitals, clothing, doors, chests, and medals.

Whoever opens the treatises of Giovio, Simeoni, or Ruscelli <note>Paolo Giovio, Gabriele Simeoni, and Girolamo Ruscelli were prominent 16th-century authors who codified the rules of emblems and heraldry</note> finds many and many of the symbols collected by Leonardo—symbols of a general character, beautifully adapted to this or that purpose.

Here it is appropriate to observe the distinction that our ancestors made between *emblem* and *device*. First of all,

---

$^1$ See G. Calvi, "The manuscript H. of Leonardo, the Flower of Virtue and the Acerba" in "Lombard Historical Archive," 1898, p. 73 and following.

<summary>This page introduces an appendix on Leonardo da Vinci's allegories, tracing his animal-based symbolism to medieval and classical sources like Pliny and the Flower of Virtue. It highlights the Renaissance distinction between emblems and personal devices used in art and heraldry.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Allegory, Bestiaries, Emblems, Devices, Renaissance Art, G. Calvi, Heraldry</keywords>

--- Page 352 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical distinction between the "emblem" and the "device" (impresa) in Renaissance symbolism, specifically as they relate to Leonardo da Vinci's work and the court of Ludovico il Moro.</meta>

both the former and the latter could be expressed with or without words, but the first served "as a demonstration of a universal concept" (though it could also express the specific sentiment of the person who created it), while the second, by contrast, had to be something entirely personal to a single individual. In the emblem, the words were the pure explanation of the figures; whereas in the device <term>impresa: a personal heraldic symbol combining a picture and a motto</term>, the figures were meant to express only one part of the symbol and the words the other. The device also alluded to matters of political interest if the person who adopted it played a role in public life, and it could justify his conduct through allegory, explaining the hidden feelings and intentions that had guided him. $^1$

For devices, simple and—as we would say today—stylized designs were used, mostly drawn from the animal and plant kingdoms: Giovio <note>Paolo Giovio, a 16th-century physician and historian famous for his work on heraldry</note> considered it an error to mix human figures into them.

This is a capricious and rebellious subject matter when (as is almost always the case with Leonardo) certain factual data do not come to our aid, partly due to the instability of the symbols; for often the same animal was attributed both a virtue and a vice, and the interpretation therefore remains uncertain.

Having stated this, and having noted that we know of only one device with figures adopted by Ludovico il Moro <note>Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan and Leonardo's patron</note>: that famous one of the "Moor" <note>A pun on Ludovico's nickname "Il Moro" (The Moor) and the Italian word for mulberry, "moro"</note> who cleanses a noblewoman—Italy—with a small brush $^2$, and only one symbolic representation of his conduct toward Giovan Galeazzo <note>The young nephew whom Ludovico effectively usurped</note>: the miniature in the history of Francesco Sforza by Giovanni Simonetta, let us now turn to the symbolism of Leonardo, in which recently there has been a desire to—

---

$^1$ Cf. *The Illustrious Devices* <note>original: "Le imprese illustri"</note> by J. RUSCELLI. In Venice, published by F. de Franceschi, 1583, Chapter V, p. 12.
$^2$ Cf. *Dialogue on Military and Amorous Devices* <note>original: "Dialogo dell'imprese militari et amorose"</note> by Monsignor GIOVIO. In Venice, published by G. Giolito, 1565, p. 24. This device is contained in the Trivulziano Album Ms. 21618, a rich and imaginative collection of devices mixing allegory and heraldry. For Sforza heraldry in the Milan castle, cf. F. MALAGUZZI VALERI, *The Court of Ludovico il Moro*, Milan, Hoepli, 1913, I, p. 320, ff.

<summary>The author explains the Renaissance distinction between universal emblems and personal devices, noting how Ludovico il Moro used such symbols for political justification. It highlights the difficulty in interpreting Leonardo's symbols due to their inherent ambiguity.</summary>
<keywords>emblem, device, impresa, allegory, Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Paolo Giovio, Girolamo Ruscelli, heraldry, Sforza, Giovan Galeazzo, Francesco Sforza, Giovanni Simonetta</keywords>

--- Page 353 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci's involvement in the political symbolism of the Milanese court, specifically examining whether his allegorical drawings served as propaganda for Ludovico il Moro's political maneuvers.</meta>

<page-num>349</page-num>

to see a very broad political intention, so much so as to make the supreme artist an interpreter, or rather a justifier, of the Moor's <note>Ludovico Sforza, known as "Il Moro"</note> conduct toward his inept nephew <note>Gian Galeazzo Sforza, the rightful Duke, whom Ludovico effectively usurped</note>.

> "With political symbols, he commented on the principal events in the Duchy of Milan from February 2, 1489—the date of the unhappy marriage of Gian Galeazzo Sforza—to the descent of Louis XII <note>The King of France who invaded Italy</note>. The Moor placed Vinci alongside Gian Galeazzo and Isabella of Aragon to serve his ambitious designs for dominion" $^1$.

Thus it was affirmed by a scholar very well-versed in Vincian matters, the late Solmi, and the statement has special importance insofar as it would give Vinci a role, and not a small one, in the political intrigues of the Milanese Court—a Court in which (as has been willingly said and repeated) Vinci excelled for his high intellect, his beautiful person, his easy and persuasive speech, his sweet singing, and his refined manners, but in which—conversely—it seems certain that he did not live at all, and was not at all (from what can be gathered from the scarce traces we find of him in the documents and letters of that period) in great favor; rather, he was lost amidst the crowd of engineers, architects, and minor artists $^2$.

Bernardo Bellincione, a petty poet, buffoon, and flatterer, could indeed address the Duke or the Moor with insolent familiarity, but an artist was, in those days, still held as not much more than a craftsman. Leonardo's manifold aptitudes were certainly exploited by the munificent lord, but more than the artist, more than the imaginative organizer of lavish festivals, the Moor made use of the engineer.

Furthermore, Vinci—a lover of solitude to speculate at his ease upon the "sweetest truth" <note>original: "dolcissimo vero"</note>, a despiser in his secret heart—

---

$^1$ E. SOLMI, *The politics of Ludovico the Moor in the symbols of Leonardo da Vinci (1489-1499)*, in: "Various writings of erudition and criticism in honor of R. Renier", Turin, Bocca, 1912. An allusion to this study is already in the *Leonardo* published by Barbera, 1900, p. 71-72.
$^2$ Cf. F. MALAGUZZI VALERI, *Op. cit.*, p. 586.

<summary>This page debates Leonardo da Vinci's social and political standing at the Milanese court, questioning whether he was a high-level political advisor or merely a court engineer and craftsman. It references Edmondo Solmi's theory that Leonardo's symbols were used to justify Ludovico il Moro's political usurpation.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Milanese Court, political symbolism, Edmondo Solmi, Gian Galeazzo Sforza, Isabella of Aragon, court hierarchy, Renaissance artist status</keywords>

--- Page 354 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's detachment from the political turmoil of his time, contrasting his inner life of scientific and artistic contemplation with the occasional political allegories he was commissioned to produce for the Sforza court.</meta>

<page-num>350</page-num>

regarding the majority of men and the events of the world,
he took a very mediocre interest—not to say none at all
—(and the eloquent silence of his manuscripts proves it),
in the turbulent political life full of subtle hypocrisies and fe-
rocious violence that seethed around him; he observed it closely,
certainly with acuity, but his searching spirit did not lin-
ger there, entirely absorbed as he was in the calm contemplation
of nature and in his visions of truth and beauty ¹.

   A very particular interest is held, therefore, by the notes or
sketches that can show him—even if by order
of his lord—intent on imagining political allegories.
   Let us begin with the few notes (for frescoes or for the-
atrical performances?) that undoubtedly refer to the
Milanese Court. These have already been noted for some time by
scholars.

> *“The Moor <note>Ludovico Sforza</note>—Leonardo notes—in the figure of Fortune*
*with hair and clothes and hands forward. And Master Gualtieri*
*<note>Gualtieri de' Bottapetri, the ducal treasurer</note>, with a reverent*
*act, should grasp him by the clothes from below, coming to him from the*
*front” ².*

   Here, rather than an allegory—we note—it is a matter of a
representation with some symbolic detail, as
in the previously mentioned miniature of the history of F. Sforza; in
it, Master Gualtieri and the Moor would have been portrayed to
the life, as an imperishable record and glorification of the muni-
ficence of the Prince.
   This representation is quite distinct, and (curiously!) no
one until now has noted it, from this other one which follows imme-
diately in Leonardo's manuscript:

---

¹ *“What can I say—he bursts out once, departing from his usual reserve—of anything more wicked than those who raise praises to the sky for those who with more ardor have harmed their country and the human species?” <note>original: "Che potrò io dire... cosa più scellerata di quelli che levano le lalde al cielo di quelli che con più ardore han nociuto alla patria e alla spezie umana?"</note> Atlantic Codex 382 r.* He was certainly thinking, in writing this, of the warriors and politicians whom the fawning multitude bows to and glorifies.
² *Manuscript J., 138 v.*

<summary>The author argues that Leonardo was largely indifferent to the violent politics of Milan, preferring scientific contemplation, though he did record specific instructions for a symbolic portrait of Ludovico il Moro and his treasurer.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Gualtieri de' Bottapetri, political allegory, Milanese Court, Atlantic Codex, Manuscript J, Fortune, artistic detachment</keywords>

--- Page 355 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical sketches for the Sforza court, specifically debating the interpretation of a scene involving Ludovico "the Moor" and a young boy fleeing Poverty.</meta>

<page-num>351</page-num>

> “Again, let Poverty in a terrifying figure run after a youth; the Moor covers him with the hem of his robe and with his golden rod threatens such a monster.”

And it is distinct for a very simple reason: if in the first <note>referring to the sketch on the previous page</note> the Moor has *“both clothes and hands forward”* (the usual posture of Fortune who, pushed by a favorable wind, scatters goods), he cannot at the same time cover the youth *“with the hem of his robe”* and threaten Poverty with the golden rod: otherwise he would have four hands!

The treasure of Gian Galeazzo and Isabella <note>Gian Galeazzo Sforza and Isabella of Aragon</note> was forestalled by Ludovico; therefore, this Leonardesque trace—it was concluded—represents in the fleeing youth the Duke himself, whom his uncle saves from the clutches of poverty: Leonardo would thus be justifying the Moor’s conduct.

Truly, for a mind as subtle and complex as Leonardo’s was even in allegories (as we shall see), one must confess that the invention for such a delicate case would be quite crude.

The Moor, represented here not even symbolically but in person, would have as a necessary consequence required that the Duke also be portrayed from life: and if so, why does Leonardo not name him?

Furthermore, who in the ducal Court would not have laughed in their heart seeing Gian Galeazzo fleeing before Poverty? And when had he ever been threatened by it?

It would be a poor device even on the Moor’s part—a representation of this sort that is only partially symbolic!

A fine way to justify oneself!

It is much more natural to leave things as Leonardo himself wrote them: leave the youth anonymous, and see in this second fantasy of the artist simply another glorification of the Moor’s <term>liberality<note>The virtue of being generous with wealth and status</note></term>.

With the two traces just examined, this third one has nothing at all to do, and I really do not know how Solmi <note>Edmondo Solmi, a noted Leonardo scholar</note> could have made one single thing out of all three:

> *“A plant with the roots upward. For one who was at the end of his wealth or favor.”*

What does this have to do with it? Nothing at all! Leonardo noted everything that

<summary>The author critiques historical interpretations of Leonardo's allegorical sketches, arguing they represent general virtues like liberality rather than specific political justifications for Ludovico Sforza's treatment of his nephew.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Gian Galeazzo Sforza, allegory, Poverty, Edmondo Solmi, Sforza Court, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical sketches found in his notebooks, specifically Manuscript H. The author is critiquing various scholars' (like Solmi and Berenson) interpretations of Leonardo's cryptic notes regarding Ludovico "the Moor" Sforza and his political maneuvers.</meta>

<page-num>352</page-num>

he could use it or because it interested him as the idea came to him, without any order: so that it has often happened even to skilled readers to join together what should have been kept separate.

Here is another trace; but this one surely alludes to the sinister rumors that were circulating about the Moor: regarding his increasingly unmasked usurpation of power at the expense of his nephew, or regarding his invitation to the French to enter Italy?

> “The Moor with spectacles and Envy with false Infamy painted and Justice black for the Moor” <note>original: "Il Moro co’ gli occhiali e la Invidia colla falsa Infamia dipinta e la Giustizia nera pel Moro." From Manuscript H, folio 88 verso.</note>.

But it is very doubtful, at least, whether one should make a single entity of that and this other note:

> “Labor with the screw in hand.”

In any case, the "screw" <note>Italian: *vite*, which can mean both "grapevine" and "mechanical screw."</note> should never be understood as the plant, giving it the symbol sung by Zanella in *Egoism and Charity*, and interpreting it, as Solmi does, to mean: “the Moor works only for the good of others”; rather, it should be understood as the **mechanical screw**, which may well be an attribute of Labor <term>Fatica: physical or diligent toil</term>.

Quite sphinx-like are these other notes in which I cannot reconstruct either the connection of ideas that should bind them, nor the whole of a pictorial vision.

Solmi does not hesitate to call them an allegorical representation—a lying one, naturally—of the imprisonment of Filippo Eustachio and Luigi Terzaghi (1489), carried out under the guise of punishing machinations against Gian Galeazzo, but in reality to strip him of his friends:

> “The Ermine with mud. Galeazzo meanwhile tranquil. Image of Fortune. The <unclear>strugolo</unclear> <note>likely a dialect or archaic term for a bird, possibly a starling or a similar creature</note> with patience makes the children be born. Gold in bars is refined in the fire” <note>original: "L’ Ermellino col fango. Galeazzo fra tempo tranquillo. Effige di Fortuna. Lo strugolo colla pazienza fa nascere i figlioli. L’oro in verghe s’affinisce nel foco." From Manuscript H, folio 98 recto.</note>. Disconnected notes,

<summary>The author analyzes Leonardo da Vinci's cryptic allegorical notes from Manuscript H, debating whether they represent political propaganda for Ludovico Sforza or more general moral concepts. --- Page 357 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of allegorical interpretations of Leonardo da Vinci's notes, specifically questioning whether certain animal symbols and sketches were intended as political propaganda for Ludovico Sforza (the Moor) or as general moral meditations.</meta>

<page-num>353</page-num>
<sig>23</sig>

perhaps disjointed, certainly obscure in their ultimate purpose. Let us attempt to comment on them.

Leonardo writes: "The Ermine <note>The stoat or ermine was a symbol of purity and was also the emblem of the Order of the Ermine, which Ludovico Sforza belonged to.</note> would rather die than soil itself" $^1$. And then: "The Ermine, because of its moderation, eats only once a day, and would rather let itself be captured by hunters than seek refuge in a muddy den" $^2$.

And would Leonardo have written this in honor of Ludovico? This, when he should have been saying that the Moor's bad reputation was due to calumny, and not to true guilt?

In that case, it would have been much better to speak of the sketch reproduced by Colvin, in which Envy seeks to strike Virtue or Fame with an arrow shaped like a tongue $^3$; or of the other reproduced by Colvin and Richter in which Envy (or Calumny) riding a monster, pursues and takes aim, while Ingratitude, seated on the rump, chooses another arrow from the quiver: both fail to notice that behind them stands Death, a horrendous skeleton armed with an hourglass and a scythe $^4$. There would be here—if one wished to find it—even a death threat against the detractors of the Moor!

The fact is—instead—that the explanations Vinci left of these sketches and other related ones are of a purely moral character.

He took pleasure in complicating the symbolism with that search for the unusual, the subtle, and the difficult which is the predilection of Italian geniuses who are nonetheless lucid, synthetic, and powerful: see, for example, the minute description of the garments and symbolic ornaments of a knight, and of the Virtues that surround him $^5$, and furthermore the entire long explanation that accompanies the sketch of a twin monster, Sorrow and

$^3$ S. <term>COLVIN</term> <note>Sidney Colvin (1845–1927), English art and literary critic.</note>, *Drawings of the old masters in the University Galleries and in the Library of Christ Church Oxford*, Oxford, at the Clarendon Press, 1907, I, 17.
$^4$ Id. ibid. I, 19.
$^5$ <term>RICHTER</term> <note>Jean Paul Richter (1847–1937), scholar who famously compiled Leonardo's literary works.</note>, p. 351-52.

<summary>The author argues that Leonardo's allegorical sketches of the ermine and figures like Envy or Ingratitude are moral meditations on human nature rather than specific political defenses of Ludovico Sforza. He highlights Leonardo's tendency toward complex, "subtle" symbolism typical of the Italian Renaissance intellect.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Ermine, Envy, Virtue, Fame, Ingratitude, Death, Sidney Colvin, J. P. Richter, symbolism, twin monster</keywords>

--- Page 358 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion on Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical sketches, specifically refuting the political interpretations of Edmondo Solmi regarding Ludovico Sforza (the Moor).</meta>

<page-num>354</page-num>

Pleasure $^1$, or the other which illustrates that of Envy riding Death $^2$.

It appears clear from those passages (which I do not cite for the sake of brevity), how Leonardo's concern was entirely in rendering the symbol, by means of uncommon attributes, as complete as possible: the reflections that lead him to this are the reflections of a moralist, and do not contain the most distant hint of particular facts, and much less of political facts.

I cannot even find how, then, one can make a symbol "of the clever politics of the Moor which ensured for Galeazzo and Isabella the liberty threatened by external or internal enemies" $^3$, out of the small figure of a hunter who, accompanied by a dog, launches a falcon to seize a bird that flies free for only a little while longer. The sketch is commented upon by Vinci himself as: "Short liberty" $^4$.

And I can find even less how this other allegory could refer to the conduct of the Moor:

> "The goldfinch <note>original: 'calderigio'</note> gives spurge <note>original: 'tortomaglio' or 'titimaglio', a poisonous plant of the euphorbia species</note> to its young in the cage. Death before losing liberty" $^5$.

I omit, to be brief, more minute observations, all of the same kind, to speak of some uninscribed drawings <term>anepigrafi: drawings without explanatory text or titles</term> that have severely tested the curiosity of many.

In the Royal Library of Windsor, a beautiful red chalk drawing <term>sanguigna: a drawing medium of reddish-brown pigment</term> by Leonardo is preserved $^6$. A boat, plowing through a rather rough sea, has by now left a distant shore—

--- Page 359 ---
<meta>Continuing the description of Leonardo da Vinci's red chalk drawing at Windsor, specifically the allegorical boat scene previously introduced on page 354.</meta>

<page-num>355</page-num>

rocky and is about to land, amidst strong foaming waves, on a flat beach upon which towers a globe surmounted by a crowned eagle with spread wings and surrounded by rays.

The boat has its sail swollen by a favorable wind, and for a mainmast it has a real leafy tree, whose branching and leaf shape suggest the mulberry—the emblem <term>impresa: a personal heraldic device or motto</term> dear to Ludovico, because—says *Giovio*—"**the wisest of all trees**, it blossoms just as the frost and rime are about to flee and bears fruit very quickly, meaning to say that with its wisdom it knew the future times, but it did not yet know..." $^1$.

Seated at the stern is a large, hairy, humanized animal, moving in a way that recalls a monkey. All things considered, the beast it most closely resembles is a wolf, though I will not hide that the snout is somewhat pig-like.

It has one paw resting on a kind of compass-rudder and observes attentively, in the act of steering the boat.

An intense ray emanates from the eagle's breast, passes through the sail and the mast, and falls upon the compass needle.

*Berenson* <note>Bernard Berenson, a famous American art historian</note> interprets it thus: the eagle represents the Empire; the wolf, the Church... and the rest, the most difficult part, he leaves—as if it were obvious—to the easy imagination of the reader.

The drawing is also reproduced by *Braun*, and *Müntz* read the date 1516 on the globe, a date that would assign it to Leonardo's late old age.

That in France, when the Moor <note>Ludovico Sforza, nicknamed "Il Moro"</note> had already been dead for eight years and had lost his State sixteen years prior, Leonardo would think to draw—as *Solmi* believes—the allegory of the Milanese State which, supported by the Moor (the mulberry), and in turn guided by bad advisors (the wolf), goes to wreck itself against Imperial policy, seems difficult for me to admit. This is also because the boat has long since left the jagged shore and is now about to land, albeit in a rough sea, upon the flat beach.

Whether this allegory was a tribute to the memory of the—

---

$^1$ *Op. cit.*, p. 24-25. <note>original: "sapientissima omnium arborum"</note>

--- Page 360 ---
<meta>Continuing the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical drawing at Windsor, specifically debating the dating and political symbolism of the wolf, the mulberry tree, and the eagle.</meta>

<page-num>356</page-num>

Sforza and a piece of flattery toward the House of France seems to me, then, even less credible.

But the presence of the mulberry and the eagle certainly authorizes one to seek a political meaning. It is that blessed date 1516 that disconcerts everything!

It is true, however, that as much as I (and others with me) have carefully observed the globe, I have not been able to see any figure there, neither Arabic, nor Roman, nor in words... could it be a blindness of mine or a delusion of Müntz? Even Seidlitz seems to have seen nothing there¹.

If the drawing could, therefore, as I believe, be attributed to two decades earlier (around 1495), things would certainly proceed better. For at that time, in fact, Ludovico—disconcerted and frightened by the French—was turning to the House of Austria to drive them from the peninsula; Leonardo was still at the Court of Milan and could very well glorify—through the stormy seas of events—the stability of the State supported by the prudence and foresight (the mulberry <note>The mulberry tree, *moro* in Italian, was a punning emblem for Ludovico "Il Moro" Sforza</note>) of the Moor.

And even the wolf sitting at the helm of the compass (one of the emblems noted by Leonardo with the explanation "to direct well")³, and guiding the boat according to the direction of the light of the Empire, could be the Moor himself. In fact, in manuscript H. one reads:

> "Correction. When the wolf goes stealthily around some livestock stall, and by chance he sets his foot down wrongly so that it makes a noise, he bites his own foot to correct such an error"³.

Ludovico had invited (if one does not want to say precisely

From which it is understood that he leaves the responsibility for their assertions to both of them.

--- Page 361 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Ludovico "Il Moro" Sforza's political maneuvers and the interpretation of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical drawings, specifically moving from the Windsor "Wolf and Eagle" drawing to a new subject in Oxford.</meta>

<page-num>357</page-num>

called), the French into Italy; he himself confessed it and apologized for it to the Venetians: having realized his mistake, by turning toward the Empire, he was correcting himself.

If then, moving away from political interpretation, one wishes to find a moral one, it is not difficult to fish out more than one. For example: the boat and the mast (very ancient symbols) represent human life; the wolf represents avarice—the greed that governs men and which only intends to conquer wealth and the dominion of the earth (the eagle). This is an allegory that could be beautifully compared with another that represents a great confusion of diverse objects falling from above under a torrential rain, bearing the note: "O human misery, of how many things do you make yourself a slave for the sake of money!"

The final conclusion of this long discourse: that nothing can be concluded with absolute certainty.

Another drawing which Colvin, based on the workmanship, judges to belong to the Florentine period or to the early time of the stay in Milan, is preserved at Oxford. An almost exact description of it was made by Colvin <note>1</note>, and a very inexact one by Solmi, who therefore erred more grossly than his predecessor in the interpretation.

One cannot be satisfied with a "near enough" in such a subtle matter, nor carve out judgments that are too resolute: when certainty cannot be reached, one must be cautious.

I shall attempt a third—and I hope more accurate—description of this attractive drawing.

To the left of the observer, two figures are sitting. The first is a woman (with a proud little face), who holds a naked broadsword with her left hand, and with her right presents a mirror to her companion. This companion, who at first glance seems to be a graceful young girl, is instead a <term>doubled</term> <note>original: "binato" - meaning twin-like or having two parts joined together</note> being: it has two faces, one feminine and young, and one masculine and old. And it is this second face that is reflected in the mirror. This strange be—

--- Page 362 ---
<meta>Continuing the description of the allegorical drawing at Oxford, following the mention of the two figures on the left—one of whom holds a sword and a mirror.</meta>

<page-num>358</page-num>

being, who has a female body, sits upon a piece of furniture that is somewhat difficult to classify (it has been called a chicken coop), and leans forward with great grace, resting the right hand upon it. It seems to benevolently observe a pack of beasts that have the muzzles and forms of hounds and bushy, fox-like tails: one of them has placed its paws on the so-called basket and seems to bark at the great bird (a rooster?) which, atop the cage, with drooping wings, ruffled feathers, and open beak, shrieks with rage and terror at him; another seems to bite the tail of a large snake that is inside the cage and which turns back with its throat wide open to punish the reckless creature.

Behind the beasts—therefore to the right of the viewer—is sketched a horned man (a satyr?) who is half-crouched, and with his right arm stretched forward seems to offer something to the dual-natured figure, or to point out the dog threatening the rooster.

Above, an eagle, or a falcon, drops its wide flight toward the hounds, and further in the distance—barely hinted at, but recognizable by the breadth of the wings—another bird of prey heads toward the two-faced being.

The latter raises its right arm with some violence; this arm—unlike the left, feminine one—has masculine musculature, wrist, and hand, and clenches in its fist a bundle consisting of a snake, a branch, and something else that is not clearly understood.

The figure, thus, has a double movement reconciled in the appearance of a single one: of grace in the leaning of the soft feminine body, and of violence in the rising of the virile right arm which seems to want to strike the bestial pack.

On the ground, near the right foot, there are several small birds; the left foot, drawn back a little, seems to poorly hide a plate (?) upon which some coins are scattered.

What is the allegory of this sketch, so complicated and so attractive in its mystery?

Colvin <note>Sidney Colvin (1845–1927), a British art critic and curator known for his work on Leonardo.</note> interprets it thus: the two figures on the left are the Justice and Prudence of Ludovico <note>Ludovico Sforza, "Il Moro," Duke of Milan.</note>: the latter is represented as the ancient Florentine artists were accustomed to do. Prudence entices the enemies of the State (the dogs) by offering them food with her left hand (which truly cannot be seen), and in the...

<page-num>359</page-num>

at the same time, it threatens them by raising a whip with its masculine arm, composed of various Visconti-Sforza emblems. The rooster is Giovan Galeazzo; the satyr who goads the dogs (though whether he truly goads them is at the very least quite doubtful) is Cecco Simonetta; the bird of prey is the Empire, always ready to take advantage of Italy's turmoils, or Ludovico himself, also called the "*noble falcon*" in the verses of Bellincione <note>Bernardo Bellincione (1452–1492) was a court poet for the Sforza family in Milan.</note>. The dove, tied with a cord to the bundle of supposed Sforza emblems, is Bona of Savoy, who specifically favored the dove as her <term>device</term> <note>An "impresa" or device is a personal heraldic symbol or motto used by nobility.</note>.

This dove, unfortunately (as has already been seen), is not there: Colvin <note>Sidney Colvin (1845–1927), a British art historian and critic.</note> has mistaken the line of the large wing of the bird of prey sketched in the distance for a cord connecting the bird (seen thus as greatly diminished in its characteristic wingspan) to the symbolic whip. Therefore Bona—at the mercy of "Il Moro" <note>Ludovico Sforza, nicknamed "The Moor," Duke of Milan.</note>, and lashing out to avenge her offended favorite, Tassino, against the faithful Simonetta—disappears from the scene.

And then? And then, can one safely believe that Leonardo, having arrived at the Court when the condemnation of Simonetta was already a finished matter of several years—and which was better for Il Moro to bury in oblivion—would be commissioned for such an <term>allegory</term> <note>A story or image where characters and events symbolize deeper moral or political meanings.</note>?

Worse than Colvin’s explanation is the correction by Solmi. He believes that Leonardo represents here the alleged conspiracy hatched (1492) by Bona of Savoy (*the dove*) and by Bernardo da Cotignola (*the satyr*) against Galeazzo da Sanseverino and Il Moro: "*on the left, Truth—he adds—puts slanders to flight with a mirror reflecting the light of the sun*." These things are entirely inaccurate in every respect.

I believe that many other political interpretations could be attached to this mysterious <term>allegory</term> by making use of the tumultuous history of those years¹. I also believe that equally—

---

¹ Could not, for example, the "*satyr*" be G. Giacomo Trivulzio, threatened in vain by Il Moro so that he would leave the French army?

<meta>Continuing the critique of political interpretations of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical drawings, specifically moving from the Oxford drawing to one in the British Museum and discussing the subjective nature of iconographic analysis.</meta>

<page-num>360</page-num>

one could just as easily, or even more easily, find moral interpretations for them, without ever having the certainty of possessing the key to the allegory.

I will content myself, for those who might wish to amuse themselves with it, by noting that Prudence, in a passage by Leonardo, is represented as crowned, with three eyes and dressed in red <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "Prudenza... con tre occhi e vestita di rosso"</note>; that Perseverance is symbolized in a sketch by a sword hilt gripped in a fist <gloss>2</gloss>; that Pleasure and Pain are depicted as twins (with two heads, truly, and four arms on a single male body), and that Pain carries a bundle of dry branches in his left hand, while Pleasure lets coins fall <gloss>3</gloss>.

With these details, could one not construct a "top-tier" interpretation? The scene could thus symbolize the struggle between virtue and the vices.

Another drawing kept at the British Museum, reproduced by Berenson <gloss>4</gloss>, and described with sufficient accuracy by Müller Walde <gloss>5</gloss>—who called it a depiction of the world tearing itself apart—was interpreted by Solmi as a political allegory: "the slander against 'Il Moro' <note>Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan</note> advances, in the form of a host of nocturnal animals driven by a dragon (the House of Aragon), and by a unicorn (Gian Giacomo Trivulzio), toward a narrow gorge formed by two rocky ridges, to penetrate the Lombard State; but Truth, reflecting the radiant light of the sun, puts the nocturnal witches to flight."

A description of the drawing will suffice to prove that Solmi either saw it poorly or misrepresented it. The background to the scene is a wall ruined here and there, among which grow tufts of grass and bushes, as the painters of the <term>Quattrocento</term> <note>The 1400s, or the 15th century in Italian art.</note> often liked. At the top, on the left, a sun... like a Medusa. Near the largest crack in the wall sits (turning her back to it), a

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, allegory, iconography, Prudence, Perseverance, Pleasure and Pain, British Museum, Solmi, Ludovico Sforza, House of Aragon, Quattrocento art</keywords>

--- Page 365 ---
<meta>Continuing the detailed analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's allegorical drawing in the British Museum, specifically refuting Solmi's political interpretation by describing the actual figures and animals depicted.</meta>

<page-num>361</page-num>

woman (a monk, according to Müller Walde, perhaps deceived by the strange hairstyle). She, with a mirror in which she reflects the image of the sun, illuminates a struggle of animals engaged at her feet. This struggle converges at a single point: a feline (perhaps a lion) has fallen under the fangs of a winged dragon that bites its ear. But in turn, a wolf (?) bites the neck of the dragon. Both, dragon and wolf, (it is worth noting) turn their backs to the ruins.

To the left of the viewer examining the drawing, a unicorn rushes to help, it seems, the fallen one; and in the distance, on the same side, a wild boar emerges from the walls, running.

On the right, in the foreground, turning its back to the observer, stands another feline, with its back arched and tail erect, aiming and hissing with its snout low against the dragon.

"Truth, sun," writes Leonardo; and in certain allegorical medallions found near this note, he sketches a winged woman showing a mirror to another allegorical figure <gloss>1</gloss>.

There is no doubt, therefore, that here the sun represents Truth, and that the woman with the mirror is Virtue or Science <note>In the Renaissance context, "Science" often referred to organized knowledge or wisdom</note>. The rays of Truth illuminate a fierce struggle, in which the victor, though himself attacked, is a dragon, and the victim is a lion.

It would be natural to think of Charles VIII <note>King of France who invaded Italy in 1494</note> as the victor over the King of Naples, but himself attacked by the Italian States led by Ludovico himself (the wolf), who was the first of all to initiate a firm reaction against the French. Nothing, however, seriously authorizes us to consider the dragon a symbol of France, and the lion a symbol of the Neapolitan territory <gloss>2</gloss>.

It is certain that "Il Moro" <note>Ludovico Sforza</note>, in four medals attributed to Caradosso, wished to celebrate his new stance against the French under the transparent veil of allegory <gloss>3</gloss>.

In the one dedicated to the Kingdom of Naples, a crowned horse (France) rears up, and has placed its front legs—

<gloss>1</gloss> Cf. RICHTER, p. 336.
<gloss>2</gloss> For Leonardo, as for his contemporaries, it should be noted, the dragon morally was the symbol of prudence, and the lion of fortitude.
<gloss>3</gloss> See A. ARMAND, *The Italian Medallists, etc.*, Paris, Plon, 1883, II, p. 54-55.

<summary>The author provides a meticulous description of Leonardo's allegorical drawing, identifying figures like Truth and various animals to challenge previous political interpretations. He argues that while Ludovico Sforza used similar allegories on medals, there is no firm evidence to link this specific drawing to the French invasion of Naples.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, allegory, Truth, Virtue, Ludovico il Moro, Charles VIII, Caradosso, iconography, Renaissance symbols, British Museum drawings</keywords>

--- Page 366 ---
<meta>Continuing the critique of political interpretations of Leonardo's allegorical drawings, specifically addressing the symbols used in commemorative medals and the difficulty of assigning fixed political meanings to his sketches of animal combat.</meta>

<page-num>362</page-num>

right on the chest of a man (a Neapolitan), to whose aid another man rushes (the Moor <note>Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, nicknamed "Il Moro"</note>), brandishing a mace (or a scepter?): as one can see, this symbolic scene does not serve to illuminate Leonardo's sketch, and the same—unfortunately—is true of the other three medals commemorating the triumph over the French.

Later, in 1509, we find in a medal¹ and in a satire in Latin elegiac verse titled *The Hunt of the Lions* <note>original: "Venatio Leonum"</note>, Venice represented by the symbol of the lion². But it is a very poor indication, just like the other fact that a winged dragon and a crested dragon are found continually in the Sforza coats of arms.

It may be, yes, that in these battles of wild beasts the great artist intended to symbolize some political event: the trouble is that he has not left us the key. And if, moreover, we wanted to find interpretations of this kind not only for the drawing in the <term>Uffizi</term> <note>The famous art museum in Florence</note>, but for all the combats of monsters scattered throughout his manuscripts, we would have to employ a great deal of imagination.

That many of the notes and small sketches of a moral character could or should have served to decorate the ceilings, walls, doorposts, or chests of the <term>Sforza Castle in Milan</term> or of <term>Pavia</term> is highly probable; but that—maxims, sketches, drawings—were, at least in large number, political allusions does not seem to me (in conclusion) yet proven, nor easy to prove. And what little, after an accurate examination, authorizes us to conjecture a political interpretation, is cursedly difficult, intricate, and obscure.

And for all these reasons, it seems to me very risky to make Leonardo out to be almost the artist-accomplice of the <term>Moor</term> <note>Ludovico Sforza</note>, who—with his clever allegories—would have masked the fraudulent conduct of his Lord in the face of the young Dukes and the skeptical Milanese Court.

² Noted by ROSMINI, *op. cit.* <note>original: "op. loc. cit." - in the place cited</note>

<summary>The author argues against over-interpreting Leonardo da Vinci's animal sketches as political propaganda for Ludovico Sforza, noting that while some symbols appear in medals, a definitive political "key" is missing. He cautions against viewing Leonardo as a mere "artist-accomplice" who used allegory to mask the Duke's political maneuvers.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico il Moro, Sforza family, political allegory, Renaissance medals, Milanese Court, Uffizi, animal symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 367 ---
<meta>Continuing the critique of political interpretations of Leonardo's allegorical drawings, specifically addressing the symbols used in commemorative medals and the difficulty of assigning fixed political meanings to his sketches of animal combat.</meta>

## LITTLE VINCIAN GLOSSARY.

<page-num>362</page-num>

right on the chest of a man (a Neapolitan), to whose aid another man rushes (the Moor <note>Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, nicknamed "Il Moro"</note>), brandishing a mace (or a scepter?): as one can see, this symbolic scene does not serve to illuminate Leonardo's sketch, and the same—unfortunately—is true of the other three medals commemorating the triumph over the French.

Later, in 1509, we find in a medal¹ and in a satire in Latin elegiac verse titled *The Hunt of the Lions* <note>original: "Venatio Leonum"</note>, Venice represented by the symbol of the lion². But it is a very poor indication, just like the other fact that a winged dragon and a crested dragon are found continually in the Sforza coats of arms.

It may be, yes, that in these battles of wild beasts the great artist intended to symbolize some political event: the trouble is that he has not left us the key. And if, moreover, we wanted to find interpretations of this kind not only for the drawing in the <term>Uffizi</term> <note>The famous art museum in Florence</note>, but for all the combats of monsters scattered throughout his manuscripts, we would have to employ a great deal of imagination.

That many of the notes and small sketches of a moral character could or should have served to decorate the ceilings, walls, doorposts, or chests of the <term>Sforza Castle in Milan</term> or of <term>Pavia</term> is highly probable; but that—maxims, sketches, drawings—were, at least in large number, political allusions does not seem to me (in conclusion) yet proven, nor easy to prove. And what little, after an accurate examination, authorizes us to conjecture a political interpretation, is cursedly difficult, intricate, and obscure.

<summary>The author argues against over-interpreting Leonardo da Vinci's sketches of animal combat as political allegories, noting that while some symbols appear in period medals, Leonardo left no definitive key for such meanings.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Ludovico Sforza, political allegory, Uffizi, Sforza Castle, animal combat, Renaissance symbolism</keywords>

# LITTLE VINCIAN GLOSSARY¹

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| *abbondanzia* (n. r. C.), *ab-* | *accidental, non-essential,* |
| *bondanza.* <note>abundance; Leonardo often used archaic or regional spellings</note> | &nbsp; &nbsp; *not intrinsic to the subject.* |
| *acciarolo* (r. G. C.), *accia-* | *to water, to irrigate.* |
| *rino.* <note>steel-strike or flint-striker</note> | *to freeze, to turn to ice.* |

---

¹ The Lincean Academy <note>Accademia dei Lincei: the oldest science academy in Italy</note>, when publishing the *Codex Atlanticus* edited by G. Piumati (Rome, 1891-1904), intended to follow it with a Vincian dictionary, but this *did not* come to fruition; L. Beltrami, in the *Vincian Collection*, 1st issue 1905, p. 67 ff., later performed only a brief survey of *Words and terms of the Milanese dialect in the Codex Atlanticus*. It seems to me, therefore, *not* useless to provide this *Little Glossary* which has (I declare this to avoid misunderstanding) two distinct purposes: I. to list the words which, even while having an easily understood meaning, present some difference from the form that prevailed in our literary tradition, with the intention of showing the most notable elements of Leonardo’s linguistic blend; II. to give young scholars the explanation of difficult words or those used by Da Vinci in his own special sense.
&nbsp; &nbsp; I also note that I did not take into account orthographic variations (especially regarding double consonants) because, I repeat, the spelling is fluctuating, not only in the transcribers but in Da Vinci himself.
&nbsp; &nbsp; I have indicated with the abbreviation: n. r. C. all the voca- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "vocaboli" (words)</note>

The author explains that the glossary focuses on non-standard spellings and specific definitions rather than mere orthographic inconsistencies.</summary>
