<page-num>366</page-num>

*adirieto* (r. G. C.), behind, back. See: *dirieto* and *indirieto*. <note>original: "adietro, addietro"</note>

*admirazione* (n. r. C.), admiration. <note>original: "ammirazione"</note>

*adoprare*, in the sense of acting, operating, or practicing, and not in the more common sense of making use of something.

*adversario* (n. r. C.), adversary. <note>original: "avversario"</note>

*aggiongere* (r. G. C.), to add, to reach. <note>original: "aggiungere, giungere"</note>

*albo*, white. <note>From the Latin 'albus'</note>

*albusto* (n. r. C.), shrub. <note>original: "arbusto"</note>

*aldace* (r. G. C.), audacious. <note>original: "audace"</note>

*alditore* (n. r. C.), auditor or listener. <note>original: "uditore"</note>

*alia*, wing. <note>original: "ala"</note>

*alicorno*, unicorn; an 'a' has been added through epenthesis <note>the addition of a sound in the middle of a word</note>; a fabulous animal. <note>original: "liocorno, unicorno"</note>

*alluminato*, illuminated. <note>original: "illuminato"</note>

*altore*, one who nourishes or feeds; author. <note>original: "colui che alimenta, autore"</note>

*altrementi* (r. G. C.), otherwise. <note>original: "altrimenti"</note>

*altunno* (r. G. C.), autumn. <note>original: "autunno"</note>

*alturità* (r. G. C.), authority. <note>original: "autorità"</note>

*ammunizione* (r. G. C.), admonition. <note>original: "ammonizione"</note>

*amplio* (n. r. C.), ample. <note>original: "ampio"</note>

*anare* (r. G. C.), used as both feminine and masculine: nostril; plural: *anari*. <note>original: "narice"</note>

*anarise* (n. r. C.), nostril, nostrils. <note>original: "narice, narici"</note>

*ancudine*, anvil. <note>original: "incudine"</note>

*annegare*, to flood or inundate. <note>original: "innondare"</note>

*annumerare*, to count, to ascribe. <note>original: "annoverare, ascrivere"</note>

*antento* (n. r. C.), intent, purpose. <note>original: "intento, scopo"</note>

*anticaglia*, an ancient thing such as buildings, statues, or medals—without the derogatory sense <note>i.e., "junk" or "old rubbish"</note> that it commonly has now.

*antiguardia*, vanguard. <note>original: "avanguardia"</note>

*antiporre* (n. r. C.), to prefer or place before. <note>original: "anteporre"</note>

*apostema*, abscess, used in both masculine and feminine forms in the ancient language. <note>original: "postema, ascesso"</note>

*appontate* (n. r. C.), pointed or sharpened. <note>original: "appuntite"</note>

*archimia* (r. G. C.), alchemy; the vain art of refining metals, changing them from base to noble, and composing medicines capable of curing every disease. <note>original: "alchimia"</note>

words not registered by the Crusca <note>The Accademia della Crusca, the primary authority on the Italian language</note>, latest edition in progress up to the letter 'n' inclusive, then the Pitteri edition, Venice, 1741. The abbreviation: r. G. C. indicates: registered in the Glossary of the Crusca, which includes only the letters 'a' and 'b'.

*<term>archimista</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), alchemist.

*<term>arguire</term>*, to dispute, to oppose, to refute; used as an absolute neuter verb, to argue or reason.

*<term>arguizione</term>*, refutation, argumentation.

*<term>aricordare</term>* (not recorded in this form by Crusca), to remember.

*<term>aricordo</term>* (not recorded in this form by Crusca), a memory or record.

*<term>armadura</term>*, armor.

*<term>aroversiato</term>* (not recorded in this form by Crusca), overturned or upside down.

*<term>arriversare</term>* (not recorded in this form by Crusca), to pour out or spill.

*<term>assentito</term>*, cautious, wary.

*<term>assenzia</term>*, essence (not recorded in this sense by Crusca, which only recognizes it as "absence").

*<term>astendere</term>* (not recorded by Crusca), to extend, to participate.

*<term>astensione</term>*, extension (not recorded by Crusca in this sense).

*<term>attimo</term>* (not recorded by Crusca in this sense), atom. <note>Leonardo often used 'attimo' (moment) to refer to the smallest physical particle, similar to the Greek 'atomos'.</note>

*<term>audito</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), the sense of hearing.

*<term>aumiliare</term>*, to humiliate or humble.

*<term>ave</term>* (not recorded by Crusca), bee, bees. <note>original: "ape, api"</note>

*<term>avvenimento</term>*, direction, side. (Crusca cites Vinci himself for this usage).

*<term>avvertenzia</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), warning or attention.

*<term>baga</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), wineskin; a word from the Venetian and Lombard dialects. Derived from the Vulgar Latin *boaga* <note>original: "latino barbaro"</note>, and this from Celtic. Gherardini and Crusca cite Vinci himself.

*<term>balatro</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), abyss or pit. (Both forms, *balatro* and *baratro*, are used).

*<term>ballotta</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), bullet, musket ball.

*<term>bareria</term>*, trickery or cheating.

*<term>basa</term>*, plural *base*, base or foundation.

*<term>battimento</term>*, a beat or throbbing.

*<term>berrettino</term>*, a grayish or ash-colored hue.

*<term>bilicare</term>*, to balance or place in equilibrium.

*<term>bilicazione</term>* (not recorded by Crusca), equilibrium.

*<term>bisognioso</term>* (not recorded by Crusca), that which is needed, necessary.

*<term>bo</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), ox. <note>original: "bove"</note>

*<term>bocchello</term>*, plural *bocchelli* and *bocchegli* (not recorded by Crusca), a water outlet or nozzle.

*<term>botare</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), to vow or to vote. <note>original: "votare"</note>

*<term>bono</term>*, good.

*<term>bozzacchio</term>*, a plum that is spoiled by insects laying eggs in it during the budding stage, causing it to swell excessively. More commonly called a *bozzacchione*.

*<term>briccola</term>*, a military engine used by the ancients to hurl stones or other projectiles during sieges.

*<term>brieve</term>*, brief or short.

*<term>bugiara</term>* (not recorded by Crusca), liar (feminine). — Venetian: *busiara*.

*<term>buso</term>* (recorded by Gherardini and Crusca), hole. A form from the Venetian dialect and some parts of Tuscany. Lombard: *bus*.

*<term>busso</term>*, a loud noise or din.

<term>*calcolazione*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), calculation.

<term>*cavagli*</term>, horses.

<term>*cecero*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), swan.

<term>*celabro*</term>, cerebrum, the brain.

<term>*centina*</term>, the curvature that carpenters and blacksmiths give to wood or iron, bending it in the same manner as the tool of the same name used by masons to support vaults and arches. Leonardo probably uses this word to refer to the <term>*oval lathe*</term> ("have George the German make the oval lathe"), a discovery by which 
> "he described an ellipse by means of a fixed point, impressing its trace on a moving plane, which was given the movement of an angle of constant size, the two sides of which moved on two fixed points" (Solmi). <note>Edmondo Solmi was a prominent scholar of Leonardo's manuscripts.</note>

<term>*centinara*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), hundreds; also a unit of weight used in Lombardy in the 15th century, corresponding to 100 pounds.

<term>*cerebro*</term>, see *celabro*; the brain.

<term>*cervero*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), "stag-like," an adjective for a species of wolf, commonly called a lynx. <note>original: "lupo cerviere"</note>

<term>*chiromanzia*</term>, palmistry; a vain science that claims to read the future on the palm of the hand.

<term>*cineruleo*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), ash-colored or ashen.

<term>*cingniere*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), to gird or encircle.

<term>*circuizione*</term>, a fold, a winding, or a circuit.

<term>*circundare*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), to surround.

<term>*circundato*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), surrounded.

<term>*circundatrice*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), she who surrounds.

<term>*circunscrivere*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), to circumscribe.

<term>*circunstante*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), surrounding or circumstantial.

<term>*circustante*</term> (not recorded by Crusca), surrounding. (Both forms are used).

<term>*ciriegio*</term>, cherry tree.

<term>*cirimonia*</term>, ceremony.

<term>*co*</term>, with.

<term>*cogitazione*</term>, meditation, contemplation, or thought.

*similitude*, image (the Crusca does not record it in this sense, but only as resemblance, conformity, or comparison). <note>original: "similitudine"</note>
*to slongate* (n. r. C.), to lengthen. <note>original: "slongare"</note>

<meta>This page concludes the glossary of Leonardo da Vinci’s vocabulary by listing specific geographical names and providing a philological analysis of his linguistic habits, focusing on his Florentine roots and deviations from standard Italian.</meta>

<page-num>379</page-num>

## Geographical Names.

*Adriano*, the Adriatic; also found in Dante: "on the Adriatic shore." <note>original: "in su' lito Adriano"</note>
*Attalante*, Atlas.
*Ceceri*, a mountain near Fiesole. <note>Monte Ceceri is famous as the site where Leonardo tested his flying machine.</note>
*Golfolina*, Gonfolina, a gorge at the final branches of the Pistoiese Sub-Apennines.
*Momboso*, Monte Rosa. <note>Leonardo describes climbing this mountain in his notebooks.</note>
*Talas*, Thala Mons, mountains of Interior Ethiopia, in the geography of Ptolemy.
*Taurus*, the Taurus mountain range.

---

After this brief sketch of the examination of Leonardo's linguistic material—antiquated, unusual, and dialectal—among the facts most worthy of observation one may note: the frequent change of *r* into *l* (e.g., *albusto* <note>shrub; standard: arbusto</note>), and vice versa (*refresso* <note>reflex; standard: riflesso</note>); of *au* into *al* (*aldace* <note>audacious; standard: audace</note>); of the digraph *sc* into *ss* (*lassare* <note>to leave; standard: lasciare</note>); the constant use of *o* instead of the diphthong *uo* (*omo* <note>man; standard: uomo</note>); the frequency of metathesis <note>the transposition of sounds or letters in a word</note>; the plural in *e* for nouns and adjectives ending in *e* in the singular; and *mia*, *tua*, *sua* used for the plurals *miei*, *tuoi*, *suoi* <note>my, your, his/her</note>; all of which are phenomena of the popular Florentine speech.

From this little glossary, the irregularities in the conjugation of verbs do not appear: often Leonardo <note>referred to here as "V." for Vinci</note> errs in the indicative (e.g., *concedano* for *concedono* <note>they grant</note>), the subjunctive (*pianghino*, *nascessino* <note>they might weep, they might be born</note>), the conditional (*astenderebbono* <note>they would abstain</note>), and other forms, such as those of common Florentine usage—for example, *saperrai* for *saprai* <note>you will know</note>, and *po* for *può* <note>he/she can</note>, the latter having already been used by Petrarch.

Alongside these Florentine localisms, there are very few Venetian-Lombard influences; frequent, however, are the remnants of Latin.

A curious peculiarity—perhaps entirely unique to Leonardo—are certain adjectives ending in *ente* (*fumolente* <note>smoky</note>) instead of *oso* <note>standard: fumoso</note>.

Much else could be observed, but in conclusion, I will content myself with noting how meager the Vincian linguistic material fallen into disuse is in comparison to the great mass of vocabulary that is still alive and green.

## Geographical Names.

*Adriano*, the Adriatic; also found in Dante: "on the Adriatic shore." <note>original: "in su' lito Adriano"</note>
*Attalante*, Atlas.
*Ceceri*, a mountain near Fiesole. <note>Monte Ceceri is famous as the site where Leonardo tested his flying machine.</note>
*Golfolina*, Gonfolina, a gorge at the final branches of the Pistoiese Sub-Apennines.
*Momboso*, Monte Rosa. <note>Leonardo describes climbing this mountain in his notebooks.</note>
*Talas*, Thala Mons, mountains of Interior Ethiopia, in the geography of Ptolemy. <note>Ptolemy was a 2nd-century Greek mathematician and geographer whose works were rediscovered in the Renaissance.</note>
*Taurus*, the Taurus mountain range.

---

After this brief sketch of the examination of Leonardo's linguistic material—antiquated, unusual, and dialectal—among the facts most worthy of observation one may note: the frequent change of *r* into *l* (e.g., *albusto* <note>shrub; standard Italian: arbusto</note>), and vice versa (*refresso* <note>reflex; standard Italian: riflesso</note>); of *au* into *al* (*aldace* <note>audacious; standard Italian: audace</note>); of the digraph *sc* into *ss* (*lassare* <note>to leave; standard Italian: lasciare</note>); the constant use of *o* instead of the diphthong *uo* (*omo* <note>man; standard Italian: uomo</note>); the frequency of metathesis <note>the transposition of sounds or letters in a word</note>; the plural in *e* for nouns and adjectives ending in *e* in the singular; and *mia*, *tua*, *sua* used for the plurals *miei*, *tuoi*, *suoi* <note>my, your, his/her</note>; all of which are phenomena of the popular Florentine speech.

From this little glossary, the irregularities in the conjugation of verbs do not appear: often Leonardo <note>referred to here as "V." for Vinci</note> errs in the indicative (e.g., *concedano* for *concedono* <note>they concede</note>).

<meta>This page follows the discussion of Leonardo's Florentine dialectal traits, concluding the formal linguistic analysis with a section on corrections and orthographic choices made during the printing of this volume.</meta>

## ERRATA CORRIGE <note>Latin: "Errors Corrected"</note>

---

The note on page 88 is incorrect. By *Momboso*, da Vinci—like other writers of the 15th century—did not mean Monviso, but rather Monte Rosa. The "four great rivers" would be the Rhone, the Rhine, the Danube, and the Po, according to what Leonardo himself says in a passage of the *Leicester Codex*. <note>The Leicester Codex is a collection of scientific writings by Leonardo, famous for its observations on water and hydraulics.</note>

But if that point is inaccurate, one finds marvelous, instead, the accuracy of his observation on hail or *grésil* <note>French: fine hail or sleet</note>, and his observation on the greater darkness of the sky at high altitudes. (Solmi.) <note>Edmondo Solmi was a prominent turn-of-the-century scholar of Leonardo's manuscripts.</note>

---

During the course of printing, a few words with the ancient spelling of *ct* for *tt* escaped notice at the beginning; for the sake of uniformity, these were subsequently preserved whenever the transcriptions contained them: *socto* <note>under</note>, *octavo* <note>eighth</note>, *sectimo* <note>seventh</note>, *tucta* <note>all</note>, *rocte* <note>broken</note>, *bructure* <note>ugliness/filth</note>, *benefactori* <note>benefactors</note>, *actioni* <note>actions</note>, *disfacte* <note>undone</note>, *soctosopra* <note>upside down</note>, *predecte* <note>aforementioned</note>, *facti* <note>facts/deeds</note>, *pictore* <note>painter</note>, *effecto* <note>effect</note>, *berrectino* <note>small cap/beret</note>, *socterane* <note>subterranean</note>, *decta* <note>said</note>, *dicti* <note>said (plural)</note>, etc.

And likewise, some purely graphical uses of *i* were kept, as in the words: *compagnio* <note>companion</note>, *montagnia* <note>mountain</note>, *signiore* <note>lord</note>, *vergognia* <note>shame</note>, etc.

<summary>This page provides corrections regarding Leonardo's geographical references to Monte Rosa and explains the editorial decision to maintain archaic Renaissance spellings (like "ct" and extra vowels) found in the original manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Monte Rosa, Leicester Codex, Edmondo Solmi, paleography, Renaissance orthography, Florentine dialect, errata</keywords>
